# Under the Palm Tree — Full Manuscript (36 Palms)



# Palm One — Thuraya's Book of Thoughts



## Scene One — Turkey  /  The Notebook


The morning had settled over the city the way certain mornings do — not with noise, not with light, but with a kind of held breath. As if the world had agreed, without speaking, to pause. To wait. To let something begin.

Thuraya sat on a wooden chair near the window, her back straight, her hands still, her gaze somewhere between the glass and the water beyond it.

The Bosphorus lay before her like a long, slow thought — its surface shimmering in the thin spring light, moving without urgency, carrying the morning on its back.

A breeze moved through the open window — slow, unhurried — gathering the scent of the sea as it came, and weaving it gently into the deep, warm aroma of Turkish coffee that rested, untouched, on the table beside her.

The coffee had grown cold. She had not noticed. It sat where she had placed it — patient, faithful, slightly forgotten — as if it had accepted its role long ago: not to be drunk, but to be present. To fill the silence with warmth. To remind her, quietly, that she was still here. Still in this room. Still in this year.

Her posture was calm — the kind of calm that is not absence, but presence. The kind that comes from having sat with oneself long enough to stop performing stillness and simply become it.

In her hand, a blue Cartier pen.

Her father's gift.

Professor Mohammed Al-Naabi.

He had pressed it into her palm the afternoon she received her scholarship — the afternoon she stood at the edge of one life and the beginning of another, not yet knowing which direction the wind would take her.

He had not made a speech. He was not a man who filled important moments with words. He had simply taken her hand in both of his — her small hand disappearing inside his — and held it. Just held it. Long enough for something to pass between them that neither could have named. Then he let go. And she understood that some things are not said because they are too full to fit inside language.

She could still hear his voice when she tried — low and unhurried, steady as stone.

Not loud. Not searching. Not asking anything of the moment except that it be real.

And it had been. It still was.

Her fingers moved across the notebook's cover.

The leather was soft — worn at the corners in the way that only time and hands can wear things — as though the notebook had been waiting to be written in long before she had thought of writing.

On its cover, in her own hand —

"A story that might have been in Thuraya's mind."

She had written that line herself. She was certain of it. And yet, reading it now — in this morning, in this light — it felt as though she were reading something left behind for her. A message from a version of herself she hadn't yet become. She read it the way you read something that was always yours but that you are only now, for the first time, ready to understand.

She opened it.

The paper breathed — delicate, dry, holding its silence the way old pages do.

She raised the pen above the page.

Did not write yet.

Simply held it there — suspended — as though the words were already present in the air above the paper, waiting only for her to lower the pen and let them arrive.

Then, slowly and without ceremony —

She began.


> التاريخ: 10 / 09 / 2027
اليومُ هو يومُ عيد ميلادي.
أبلغُ من العمر ثمانيةً وثلاثين عامًا،
وأنا في تركيا حيث أتابع دراستي لمرحلة الماجستير
في تخصّص تدريس اللغة الإنجليزية باستخدام التقانة والذكاء الاصطناعي لغير الناطقين بها.


She stopped. The pen rested against the page, its tip barely touching the paper — caught between one sentence and the next, the way breath catches between one feeling and another.


> اليوم… رأيتُ حلمًا غريبًا.


Her eyes lifted from the page.

Not to the window. Not to the water.

To somewhere further — somewhere the room had no wall for.


> حلمٌ أعادني إلى زمنٍ بعيد…


A long pause settled between one breath and the next.

Her fingers tightened almost imperceptibly around the pen.

Not from tension — from recognition.


> كيف يمكن لذلك العام أن يبدو قريبًا إلى هذا الحد؟


Her hand moved slowly toward the corner of the page.

She turned it.

And just like that —

the room in Turkey grew quiet in a different way.

The coffee, the curtains, the water — all of it softened at the edges, like a painting left too long in the sun.

And then, gently, it was gone.


## Scene Two — Transition


How can that year feel this close?

It was not a thought exactly. It arrived differently — not through the mind but through the chest. The way certain memories do not announce themselves but simply appear, already inside you, already warm, as though they had never truly left.

As if time had folded back on itself — quietly, without warning — pressing something distant against something present until the distance ceased to feel real.

Thuraya's pen slowed. Did not stop entirely — just lost its direction, the way a river loses speed before it widens into something larger.

Her fingers still held it. But the words were no longer coming from the page. They were rising from somewhere deeper — from a place that had no name in any language she had studied, a place that preceded language altogether.

She did not look at what she had written. She looked past it. Past the notebook, past the table, past the glass of the window — into something the room could not contain.

The Bosphorus was still there. She knew it without looking — still moving, still shimmering, still carrying its quiet freight of light. But she no longer saw it. Her eyes were open and yet entirely elsewhere. Turned inward. Turned backward. Looking at something that existed only in the part of the mind where time does not flow in one direction.

Her expression softened — not into sadness, not into joy — into that particular stillness that comes when a person is no longer entirely in the present.

How can that year feel this close?

Her thumb found the edge of the page. She turned it — slowly, with a kind of care that was almost ceremonial, as though what lay beneath was not paper but a threshold. As though turning it was less an act of reading than an act of crossing.

The room changed. Not visibly — nothing moved, nothing fell — but the air shifted its weight. The sounds outside seemed to step back. The warmth of the coffee, the rustle of the curtains, the low voice of the city beyond the glass — all of it drew away, like a tide going out, leaving the shore bare and still and waiting.

She lowered the pen. This time without hesitation — with the certainty of someone who has decided, quietly and completely, to go somewhere.

The number sat alone on the white page — stark, patient, absolute.

Then below it, like an address, like a destination —

And the moment that word touched the paper, the room answered.

Something shifted — in the light, in the air, in the quality of the silence itself.

The room was no longer the same room. The morning was no longer the same morning.

And she — without moving from her chair, without standing, without crossing any visible threshold —

was no longer in Turkey at all.


## Scene Three — London Apartment


The sky over Britain was the colour of old linen — pale, flat, pulled tight across the rooftops as though someone had smoothed it down with a wide, cold hand.

It did not threaten rain. It simply was. Permanent. Inevitable. Entirely itself.

London in this season wore its grey like a coat it had owned for too many years — not uncomfortable, simply familiar. The cold was not sharp. It did not cut. It was the kind of cold that settles, that seeps slowly through walls and windowsills and the narrow gaps between old buildings, taking up residence in the spaces between things. It asked nothing of you. It simply stayed, the way certain presences do — not demanding, not leaving.

Inside the apartment, the light entered reluctantly — pale and thin, slipping through the glass as though uncertain of its welcome, touching the edges of the room without ever fully committing to it.

Sophia sat curled into the wooden chair by the window — her knees drawn slightly toward her body, her shoulders softened, her whole self gathered inward in that particular way of people who are not hiding but simply elsewhere.

She was entirely absorbed in her book.

The Palm Tree.

Her fingers turned the pages with a kind of reverence — unhurried, deliberate — as though the story beneath her hands was something that needed to be handled gently. As though it might bruise.

On the small table beside her — a box.

Omani frankincense. Closed. Unlit. Untouched.

It had lived on that table for weeks — longer, perhaps. She had never opened it. And yet its presence was not absent. There was something it gave to the room simply by being there — a density, a quiet gravity, the kind that certain objects carry when they hold within them a world the room has never seen. It sat among the ordinary things of the apartment like a word in a foreign language — meaningful, patient, waiting to be understood.

Beside it — a photograph in a plain frame.

A boy. Young. Standing between two people who were clearly his parents, his small body leaning slightly toward his father the way children lean toward the things that feel most solid.

John. Behind them — mountains. Palm trees. A sky that was not this sky. A place that was entirely not here.

She had not heard the time pass. It had moved without her — quietly, undemanding — the way time moves for people who are truly, completely inside something.

And so she did not hear him enter.

No footsteps. No sound of a key, or a door, or a coat being removed. Nothing that the ear could name.

Only a change — not in the room, but in the room's quality. In the texture of the air itself.

A scent. Arriving before he did.

Faint at first — just the edge of it, a suggestion. Then more present. Then unmistakable.

Oud.

Warm and deep — the kind of scent that does not simply enter a room but changes it, the way a single note can change the feeling of a piece of music. Unfamiliar to her, and yet not unwelcome — the way certain unfamiliar things arrive and make you realize, without knowing quite how, that you have been waiting for them.

It did not rush. It settled — the way dusk settles, gradually, without announcement.

Sophia's fingers went still against the page. The book remained open, mid-sentence, mid-breath.

She lifted her head — not sharply, not with surprise — but with the slow, deliberate attention of someone who has sensed something and is now choosing to look.

She breathed in first. Then turned.

John was standing just inside the room. Still. Watching her with an expression she had learned to read over time — the expression he wore when something had already changed inside him, when the decision had already been made, and he was waiting, carefully, for the right moment to let her know.

Not happiness exactly. Not excitement. Something held below the surface — something that wanted to rise but was being held, gently, until she was ready.

A letter was in his hand. Open. Read. Held the way you hold something that has altered the weight of the afternoon.

Sophia looked at him — really looked, the way you look when you have put the book down inside yourself as well as in your hands.

And in that silence, before a single word had been spoken —

she already knew.

Something had changed.


## Scene Four — The Decision


"Sophia…"

His voice was low. Measured. The voice of a man carrying something he does not yet want to put down.

She did not look up immediately. Her eyes remained on the page, though they were no longer reading — moving across the words the way eyes move across a landscape they are no longer seeing, their attention already somewhere else entirely.

"You sound serious."

Now she looked. She set the book down — deliberately, without haste — and gave him her full attention. The kind of attention that is not just listening but arriving. Being present.

He moved closer. The letter still in his hand, held the way you hold something significant — not tightly, not loosely, but with awareness.

"I got the job."

Four words. Simple, plain — the kind of sentence that takes less than a second to say and a long time to absorb. She felt them land before she fully understood them, the way you feel a stone enter water before you hear the sound it makes.

Her fingers tightened at the spine of the book.

"What job?"

He inhaled — a quiet breath, barely noticeable, the kind that is not nervousness but its opposite. The steadying breath of someone who has already made peace with what they are about to say.

"In Oman."

The word entered the room and stayed there. It did not echo. It did not fade. It simply remained, suspended in the air between them — two syllables that carried the weight of a geography she had never touched, a life she had never imagined, a direction she had not considered.

"Oman… as in the Middle East?"

"Yes."

He did not rush. He gave each sentence its own space, the way you give a room its own silence before you begin to speak inside it.

"They are opening schools."

A beat.

"Building roads."

Another.

"Starting something new."

He met her eyes directly — fully, without apology.

"And they asked for me."

She said nothing. She sat down slowly — not from weakness, not from shock, but from the instinct that the body has when the mind is processing something too large to hold while standing. Something inside her needed ground beneath it.

"I don't know anything about Oman."

"I know only a little,"

he said quietly. An honest admission, offered without embarrassment.

He did not reach for certainty he did not have. Did not dress the thing in a confidence that would have been false. He stood in the reality of it — incomplete, unknown, uncertain — and simply named what he did know.

"But I know this…"

"It's a chance."

"A real chance."

She studied him. Not quickly, not lightly — but in the careful, patient way that she had learned to look at him over the years. The way that went past the surface of a face and into what the face was holding. Past the words and into what the words were covering.

And then, without deciding to, her eyes moved — away from him, toward the table.

To the frankincense box.

To the photograph.

To the mountains behind the boy. To the palms. To that unreachable, particular sky.

And suddenly — without crossing any distance, without any physical movement at all —

she was no longer in the room.


## Scene Five — Memory  /  University


A lecture hall. Wide and high-ceilinged, with the particular acoustics of rooms that have held many years of voices — absorbing them, holding them somewhere in the walls.

Rows of students faced forward — notebooks open, pens moving, pages catching the quiet industry of learning. The afternoon light fell in long diagonals across the desks, indifferent to what was written beneath it.

On the board, in clean letters — Literature Around the World.

The professor moved through the material the way certain people move through a room they have known for a long time — with ease, with ownership, but also with something that had not grown routine. An Iraqi man with a voice that carried its weight without effort, each sentence arriving as though it had been considered long before it was spoken. He did not simply teach. He inhabited the material. He brought it forward like something retrieved from a place students had not yet visited and made them feel they almost had.

Sophia sat in the middle row. Her pen moved across her notebook — but more slowly than the others, falling behind, pausing at intervals that had nothing to do with pace.

She was not writing. She was listening to something the lecture had not put into the room.

A voice. Off to the side. Speaking in a language she did not know.

She could not have said when it had started. It was simply there suddenly — clear and steady and entirely at ease, moving through the syllables of Arabic the way someone moves through a city they grew up in. Without hesitation. Without a map.

Her pen stopped. Her head lifted.

At the side of the room — John.

Standing, not reading from anything, speaking fluently — not as someone performing a learned skill, but as someone for whom the language was already part of the body. Part of the way breathing works.

There is a difference that the ear can detect, even without understanding the words, between a person who has studied a language and a person who has lived inside it. One carries the language. The other is carried by it. John belonged to the second kind. You could hear the kitchen in it — the road, the early morning, the specific silence of a house in a country that was not this one. He spoke Arabic like a man returning to something, not arriving at it.

The professor responded — easily, warmly — and the exchange moved between them with the effortless rhythm of two people sharing the same native water. The class shifted. Some looked confused. Some looked impressed. Most looked briefly up from their notebooks and then returned to them.

But Sophia did not return.

She watched him. Not the words — she could not follow those — but the way the words came from him. The ease. The absence of effort. The quality of confidence that was not performance but simply — presence.

It was unexpected in a way she could not yet explain. Not just the language — but the person standing inside it.

She leaned back slightly. Still watching.

Who is he?

The question did not arrive with urgency. It settled — softly, without fanfare — and stayed, the way questions stay when they already know they will be answered. Eventually. In time.

The professor nodded. The moment passed. Pens resumed their movement. Pages turned. The lecture continued in the direction it had always been going.

But for Sophia — the lecture had already become background.

She lowered her eyes to her notebook. Stared at the half-finished sentence she had been writing before the voice reached her.

She did not finish it.

She was thinking about something else entirely.

And without knowing it — without any announcement, without any decision she could point to — that was the moment. The precise, unremarkable, entirely ordinary moment in which everything that came after had its beginning.


## Scene Six — Back to Present  /  The Choice


She blinked.

The lecture hall dissolved — voices, light, the long diagonals falling across desks — all of it drawing back like smoke pulled through an open window.

And she was back. Back in the apartment, in the pale grey light, in the particular quiet of a London afternoon.

Back in front of him.

John had not moved. He was still standing exactly where she had left him — still holding the letter, still patient, still waiting with the composed stillness of a man who has learned that some answers cannot be rushed. Who has learned, more precisely, that rushing them damages them.

This was something she had come to understand about him over time — not through what he said, but through what he chose not to say. He had an instinct for silence. Not the silence of uncertainty or avoidance, but the silence of trust — the kind that says: I am here. I am not going anywhere. Take the time you need to arrive. He was not afraid of a long pause. He never had been. And that, she had come to understand, was one of the rarer things.

Sophia looked at him carefully. The way she had looked at him in that lecture hall years ago, for the first time — trying to see past the surface of a face into the thing behind it. The thing that stays when all the words have gone.

Then her gaze moved — deliberately, slowly — across the room.

To the table. To the frankincense. To the photograph.

The boy between his parents. Mountains behind him. Palm trees. A sky she had never stood beneath.

A place that had always been part of him — long before she was.

She inhaled — not deeply, but with intention. The way you breathe when something inside you is settling into position. When the parts of a thing are finally aligning.

She looked back at him.

She closed the book. The sound it made — soft, final — seemed to mark something. She placed it on the table with care, as though setting something down for the last time.

"I'm coming with you."

Six words. Said quietly, without drama — the way the truest things are often said. Not performed. Not announced. Simply placed into the air between two people, where they belonged.

John went very still. Not the stillness of someone who hasn't heard — but of someone who has heard too completely, and needs a moment before the moment can move again.

"You're not even going to argue?"

There was warmth in his voice, barely contained — not teasing, but genuinely, quietly surprised. The surprise of a man who had prepared himself for a longer road and has just been told the distance is shorter than he feared.

She shook her head. Gently. Certainly.

"No."

A small pause — a breath, really. And then:

"Book the tickets."

The room filled with a different kind of silence. Lighter. As though the air itself had exhaled — released some weight it had been carrying since he walked in with the letter. The grey light through the window seemed, just for a moment, to soften.

John exhaled slowly — his shoulders dropping with it, the way a held breath drops when it is finally let go. Not from exhaustion. From the deep, specific relief of a man who no longer has to carry something alone.

"Together…"

The word came out quietly. Almost to himself.

It did not sound like a decision. It sounded like something older than a decision. Like the recognition of something that had already been true, long before it was spoken.

Sophia said nothing.

She didn't need to.

Some things require no answer. Only the willingness to begin.


## Scene Seven — Preparing


The apartment changed the way certain things change — not all at once, but incrementally, one addition at a time, until the original thing is no longer recognizable beneath what has been added to it.

First one bag appeared near the door. Then a second. Then a third. And then — the process seemed to accelerate on its own logic — many more, arriving from rooms and cupboards and places that had held things quietly for years, releasing them now into the light.

Clothes. Books. Shoes in their pairs. Objects that made sense alongside one another and objects that made sense to no one but their owner. The careful taxonomy of a life being boxed up, sorted, reconsidered.

The room, which had once held its empty space with a kind of dignity, had been entirely overrun. There was no longer a floor to speak of. There were only bags — arranged in no discernible order, stacked and leaning and spreading in every direction, filling every available surface of the apartment with their quiet insistence.

John stood in the middle of it all. Utterly still. His eyes moved slowly around the room the way a man's eyes move when he is trying to understand something that has exceeded his ability to understand it.

One bag. Two. Five. Eight. Ten. More.

He blinked.

"Sophia…"

A pause — long enough to suggest that what followed required careful phrasing.

"Are we moving to Oman… or relocating London?"

She did not turn. She was on the floor beside an open suitcase — her movements precise and unhurried, folding each piece of clothing with the attentiveness of someone who has already decided that this, at least, will be done properly.

"This is normal."

Her voice was absolutely calm. The voice of someone stating a fact they consider self-evident, slightly puzzled that it requires stating at all.

John looked at her. Then back at the room. Then at her again.

"For who?"

She zipped the bag halfway. Adjusted something inside with the methodical patience of a person entirely at peace with their process. Closed it with a finality that seemed to settle the question before she even answered it.

"For women."

A silence followed that was less empty than full — full of John's effort to formulate a response that was simultaneously accurate and survivable.

He moved carefully through the landscape of luggage — each step deliberate, the way you step around things you respect even if you do not understand them. He reached down and lifted a dress. Turned it in his hands. Looked at another. Then a third.

"…You told me you have nothing to wear."

The evidence surrounding him did not support this testimony. It could not have supported it with any more thorough contradiction had it tried.

A quiet smile pulled at the corner of his mouth — involuntary, helpless, the smile of a man who has lost a battle he did not know he was in. He set the dress down. Shook his head, slowly, once.

Then — without argument, without further comment, without the speech he had perhaps been composing — he crouched beside the nearest unopened suitcase, unzipped it, and began to help.

Not because he had been convinced. Not because he understood. But because the decision had already been made — the real one, the one that mattered — and everything else was simply detail.

Across from him, Sophia did not look up. But she noticed. She noticed in the way you notice the things you have been quietly hoping for — not with surprise, but with a warmth that settles somewhere low and steady. He was not resisting. He was here. Entirely, without reservation. With her.

She continued folding. But now, just barely visible at the corner of her mouth — a small, private smile.

And the apartment — buried under its mountains of luggage, impractical and overloaded and entirely itself — was no longer just an apartment. It was a beginning wearing the disguise of chaos.


## Scene Eight — The Flight


The plane climbed steadily, and Britain fell away beneath it — slowly at first, and then all at once, the grey rooftops dissolving into grey cloud, layer after layer swallowing what had been familiar until there was nothing left to swallow and the sky above was clean and clear and entirely indifferent to what had just been left behind.

For a moment — just one, before the last of the cloud closed beneath her — Sophia thought she could still see it. The rooftops. The particular density of a city's streets from above. The small bright squares of windows behind which other people were sitting in their own rooms, reading their own books, living their own unremarkable Tuesdays with no knowledge of this departure and no reason to have any. She pressed her hand lightly to the cold glass. As if touching it were a kind of farewell. Then the cloud came up like a curtain being drawn, and London disappeared, completely and without ceremony, into the white.

The cabin settled into its long hum — that low, persistent note that becomes, after a while, not a sound but simply the sound of being in motion. Of crossing. Of the space between one place and another.

John was asleep. Not lightly — completely. His head resting at a slight angle, his breathing slow and even, his face entirely unguarded in the way that sleep alone permits. His body had accepted the journey in a way his mind had not yet had the chance to consider. Some part of him, she thought, had already arrived.

Sophia remained at the window. Awake and still, her hands resting loosely in her lap, her attention entirely outward. Not tense. Simply alert. Present in the particular way that comes when you are in the middle of something you cannot yet name.

For a long time there was only the cloud. White and seamless and without feature — a nothingness so complete it became its own kind of landscape.

Then — gradually, the way certainties emerge from uncertainty — it began to break.

Shapes appeared through the gaps. Dark at first, formless, like shadows beneath very still water. Then definition came, and with it, the unmistakable geography of something massive and ancient.

Mountains.

Not the gentle, softened hills of the English countryside. Not the polite elevations of a landscape that has made peace with being inhabited. These were something else entirely — wide and dry and absolute, rising from the earth with the patience of things that have been rising for longer than there have been people to watch them. They did not welcome the eye. They simply were — present and vast and entirely indifferent to being observed.

Sophia leaned closer to the glass.

She had expected sand. She had carried, without fully knowing it, the flat and featureless image that distance creates — emptiness, heat, a landscape made of absence. What she was looking at was not that. What she was looking at was its opposite — terrain of enormous presence, of density and weight and depth, of something that had refused, across all the years of its existence, to be softened.

And the light. The light was different in a way she could not yet articulate. It did not filter through haze or cloud or the polite diffusion of a northern sky. It arrived directly — clean, intense, making everything it touched more sharply itself. It did not illuminate the mountains so much as argue with them. And the mountains held their ground.

A thread of that light reached through the glass and touched her face. She felt it on her skin — warmer than she had expected, more immediate.

She glanced sideways. John slept on, undisturbed, his breathing steady, his face serene. As though none of this — not the mountains, not the light, not the fact that they were crossing the sky toward a life neither of them had yet lived — required anything of him at this particular moment.

"How…?"

she whispered. Not expecting an answer. Simply unable to hold the question inside.

How could he sleep, when the world outside the window had already become so completely different?

She turned back to the glass. The mountains were closer now — more detailed, more present, more themselves. She looked at them the way you look at something you are beginning to understand you will need to learn.

And in that looking, something settled inside her. Not peace exactly. Not readiness. Something quieter than both. The recognition — arriving slowly, without drama — that this was not a journey with a return ticket built into its logic. That what lay ahead of her was not an adventure to be lived and then folded away.

This was a life. Beginning now. Irrevocably, and in the most ordinary possible way, beginning now.

Nothing after this would be the same.

She pressed her forehead lightly to the cold glass and let herself look, for a long moment, at the mountains below — at their stillness, their scale, their absolute refusal to be anything other than what they were.

And she held that.


## Scene Nine — Arrival in Oman


The airplane door opened — and the heat did not wait.

It entered the way only heat enters — not through the door but through every opening at once, filling the space immediately, completely, without introduction. Alive in a way that London's air had never been alive.

Hot. Immediate. Unavoidable. The kind of heat that does not politely offer itself but simply wraps around you, presses against you, makes itself known with the directness of something that has always been here and has no particular interest in your adjustment.

Sophia stopped mid-step at the top of the stairs. The heat hit her like a wall she had walked into without seeing — not violent, but total. Not cruel, but entirely without consideration for her expectation of something gentler.

She blinked. Drew a breath that was also, somehow, warmer than the last one.

"John…"

She reached for the nearest paper — a boarding pass, a customs form, something — and began to fan herself with it.

"This is not warm."

A measured pause.

"This is fire."

John came down behind her. He lifted his hand against the sun instinctively, squinting into the brightness with eyes that were not yet calibrated for this quality of light. And then — just for a moment — something moved across his face that was not a thought but a feeling. Something older than thought.

It was not a memory he could hold in his hands — not a specific scene or moment, nothing he could point to and say: there, that. It was more physical than that. The weight of this particular light, the specific pressure of this heat against the skin — something in his body recognised it before his mind had the language to follow. He had been very young the last time he stood beneath a sky like this. Young enough that the memory had bypassed the mind entirely and lived somewhere deeper. In the chest. In the hands. In the way the eyes knew, without being told, to narrow.

He looked ahead — at the tarmac, at the low buildings, at the shimmer rising from the ground — as though searching for something familiar in a face he had not seen for a long time.

Then, quietly — almost to the air itself:

"I don't remember it being this hot… when I was a child."

A pause. Something almost like a smile.

"Or maybe… I forgot."

"How do people live in this?"

The ground beneath them shimmered — the tarmac breathing its heat upward in slow, visible waves. The light here had no interest in being soft. It was full and complete and arrived without apology, asking the eyes to meet it on its own terms.

And beneath the heat, beneath the brightness, the air carried something else — something that had no precise name in any language she owned. Not the scent of food, not of the sea, not of anything mechanical. Something drier. Older. The particular smell of a place that has existed long before it was named. Something that belonged to the earth here, to the stone and the dust and the long accumulated heat of it — waiting, without impatience, to be breathed in.

The airport was smaller than she had imagined. Quieter. No wall of sound coming at her — no urgent announcements, no crowd moving with the collective, purposeful rush of people who believe they are all late. People moved here — but they moved without urgency. As though the day was long enough. As though time, in this place, had not been divided into the same sharp intervals it had been given elsewhere.

She tightened her hand on the strap of her bag. Gathered herself.

"John…"

A pause just long enough to carry the weight of what she was not saying.

"What if we get lost?"

John looked at the signs overhead. Read them carefully, with the particular steadiness of someone who solves problems by beginning with the first one in front of him.

"Exit."

"Taxi."

He looked at her. Calm. Without false reassurance — but without doubt either. Simply certain that forward was possible, even if the way was not yet entirely clear.

"We'll take it step by step."

Not fast. Not perfect. But forward.

Sophia drew a breath. Let it out. She was not convinced exactly. But she was willing — which, she was beginning to understand, was sometimes the more useful of the two.

And somewhere in the heat and the brightness and the air that carried its ancient, nameless scent — something inside her had already begun to shift. Slowly. Without announcement. The way all real changes begin.


## Scene Ten — Airport  /  Luggage & First Realization


The luggage belt moved in its slow, patient circle — indifferent, unhurried, offering its contents one at a time to whoever was waiting.

John stood at the edge of it. Focused. Watching each bag as it came into view, leaning forward slightly as the conveyor turned.

His bag appeared. He reached for it, lifted it down, set it beside him.

Then another. Then another.

Sophia stood just behind him. Watching. Not the belt — him.

Then more bags came. Then more still. The pile beside them grew with a kind of inexorable momentum — each addition requiring a slight rearrangement of what had come before, a subtle expansion of the perimeter of their claimed territory on the airport floor.

John stepped back. Looked at what had accumulated. Took inventory.

One bag. Two. Five. Eight. Ten.

A long silence.

Then, in a voice pitched precisely at the threshold between being heard and not being heard — the voice of a man muttering to himself while fully intending to be overheard:

"Every day you say you have nothing to wear…"

His gaze moved slowly, almost ceremonially, across the mountain range of luggage surrounding them.

"…and yet… you brought London."

Sophia heard him. She processed the statement. She elected, with the serenity of someone who considers the matter entirely settled, not to engage with it. Because something else had taken her attention completely.

Across the arrivals hall — at a distance that made the details soft but the impression clear — a figure stood waiting.

A woman. Still. Not searching the crowd, not checking a phone, not leaning against anything for support. Simply standing — with the quality of presence that belongs to people who are entirely certain of where they are and have no need to demonstrate it. Her hijab was neat, her posture composed, her stillness not passive but active — the stillness of someone who has chosen it.

She was not performing waiting. She was simply there — the way mountains are there, the way water is there — occupying her space with a completeness that had nothing to do with effort. There was something in her bearing that Sophia noticed immediately, the way you notice something that is not common. A steadiness that did not come from certainty about the future but from deep familiarity with herself. The kind that is not performed. The kind that simply is.

The woman raised her hand — a single, unhurried gesture — and waved.

Sophia's eyes narrowed slightly. Recognition moved through her — slow and quiet, like light filling a room when a curtain is drawn back.

"That's her…"

John turned — following the line of her gaze — but before he could respond, Sophia was already moving. Walking through the crowd with the particular certainty of someone who has seen where they are going and no longer needs to think about how to get there.


## Scene Eleven — First Meeting  /  Thuraya


They walked toward her — the crowd thinning between them with each step, the distance closing in the unhurried way of things that are meant to meet.

As they drew close, Sophia extended her hand — and then, almost imperceptibly, paused.

Something had reached her before the woman had. Something in the air immediately around her — arriving softly, without announcement.

Oud. But different from John's. His carried depth — dark, resinous, the kind that settles into a room and stays. This was something lighter in register, less dense, moving the way good things move when they are not trying to be noticed. Balanced. Quietly feminine. Present for only a moment — then gone, leaving behind it the faint impression of something considered. Something chosen carefully.

"I'm Thuraya."

Her voice carried the same quality as her presence — unhurried, clear, entirely without pretence. The voice of someone who has never needed to fill space with unnecessary volume.

"Welcome to Oman."

The words were simple. But the way she said them — without ceremony, without performance — gave them a weight that ceremony would have diminished. This was not a greeting. It was an opening.

John stepped forward.

"I'm John."

A slight gesture toward Sophia.

"This is my wife, Sophia."

Sophia smiled — genuine, a little uncertain at the edges, the smile of someone who is still orienting herself but is willing to be present anyway.

"Nice to meet you."

Thuraya nodded — warmly, simply.

"Nice to meet you too."

Her gaze moved — briefly, almost casually — to the sprawl of luggage that had accumulated behind them. It was a great deal of luggage. It was, in fact, an impressive amount of luggage for any reasonable calculation of what two people required for a new chapter of their lives.

A small smile — knowing, unhurried — moved across her face.

"Do you need help with the bags?"


# Palm Two — The First Walk in the Village



## Scene One — The First Night


The room did not announce itself. It simply was — quiet in the way that unfamiliar places are quiet before they have been lived in, before a life has settled into them and made them its own. The stillness had weight to it. Not emptiness. Expectation.

The walls were clay — uneven, warm at the surface, their imperfections left exactly where they were, as though someone had decided long ago that a mark made by a hand was worth keeping. In the corner, a small qandeel gave what light it had — soft, trembling slightly, making the shadows on the walls breathe rather than stay fixed. On the floor, a bisat, pressed thin from years of use. No bed. Nothing assembled from catalogues. Only what was needed — and nothing more.

The window was open. The night entered freely.

Sophia stood near it, her hand resting lightly against the wooden frame — not gripping, just touching, the way you touch something when you need to be sure of the ground beneath you. The air came in layers she could not immediately separate. Oud first — deep, unhurried, the kind that changes a room. Then something lighter beneath it — musk. And below both of those — something she recognised without knowing its name. Luban. She had smelled it once in London, inside a small box that had lived on a table and been left, carefully, untouched.

She stood with the window and breathed it in — not trying to name what she was inhaling, simply receiving it. Some things arrive too complete to be taken apart. You can only let them in and hold them, for as long as they stay.

Behind her, John was asleep. Entirely, without resistance — the journey had settled into him the moment the door closed. His breathing was slow and even, punctuated now and then by a sound that was almost a snore. Almost. Sophia glanced at him once. Something pulled briefly at the corner of her mouth. Then she turned back to the window.

The village was not silent. But it moved differently than the cities she had known. Footsteps passed somewhere below — unhurried, purposeful. Voices drifted low and calm through the air. And then, from a distance that might have been a street or a hillside —

The adhan for Fajr.

It rose — not breaking the quiet, but becoming part of it. Deepening it, the way a voice that knows how to enter a room does not disturb it but adds to it. Sophia went still. Not from fear. From the simple fact that she did not yet have a place to put this sound. It arrived the way certain things arrive when you are in a country you have not yet learned to read.

Then — movement. Footsteps, faster now, multiple. Small.

Children.

Sophia leaned forward slightly, her eyes searching the path below. Small figures moved through the dim light, their voices carrying up —

"يالله يالله صلااااه!"

"أنا أسبق! أنا أسبق!"

She watched them. They were running toward the mosque — not being pushed, not being called by a voice behind them, but moving under their own urgency, with the energy of children who have somewhere to be and have decided, entirely on their own, that it matters. There was no obligation in their voices. No complaint. Only the particular joy of people who have somewhere to go in the dark.

She watched them until the path below was empty again. Then stayed at the window — thinking.

If they wake before the sun does — if they move through the dark this willingly —

She did not finish the thought. But something settled inside her — quietly, without announcement. The specific feeling of a question that has decided it wants to be answered.

She looked once more at John, entirely absent from this dark and moving world outside. She almost said something. She didn't.

"Where are the girls?" she murmured, to no one. "And the women?"

Not concern. Curiosity — the precise, alert kind. She knew she would ask Thuraya. She would ask everything.

At some point, without deciding to, without noticing when it happened — she sat down beside the wall. And sleep reached her at last.

Because tomorrow — she would walk this place.


## Scene Two — Letj


The sun had already decided it was morning by the time Sophia stepped outside. It offered no gradual transition, no northern sky's gentle easing of dark into light. It simply was — present and certain, spreading across the village without apology, revealing everything it touched exactly as it was. No flattery. No softening.

Thuraya stood waiting near the path. Entirely still. At ease in herself the way water is at ease in a vessel — filling the space completely without effort or announcement. She did not check the hour or glance at the road. She simply waited, as though time here had agreed to move at a different pace and she had accepted the arrangement long ago.

"Ready?" she asked. Sophia nodded. "Yes."

They began walking. John came beside them, his attention moving freely between the path and everything it offered. The ground was uneven in the way that earth is uneven when it has not been forced to pretend otherwise. Not rough. Simply honest.

The houses stood close to the land — not placed upon it but growing from it, the way things grow when they have been in one place long enough to forget the difference between building and belonging. Clay walls held the same colour as the ground around them, the same warmth, the same refusal to be anything other than what they were. To walk between them was less like moving through a settlement and more like moving through something that had always been here and had simply, finally, been given a name.

"Everything feels very open," Sophia said. "And very quiet."

"It's like the village is breathing slowly," John said. Thuraya glanced at him. "That is a good way to describe it."

Then — from ahead — laughter. A group of girls, gathered in a loose circle at the side of the path. One of them held a stone. She threw it — a short, practiced arc — and it landed in a shape drawn on the ground. Precise rectangles, some single, some paired.

"What are they doing?" Sophia slowed. Thuraya stopped beside her. "This is letj." Sophia repeated the word softly. "Letj…"

Thuraya explained — the stone must land cleanly, the player must remember where it falls, then move through the squares without touching a single line. One foot in the single squares, both feet apart in the paired ones. Every step chosen. Every position held.

The girl moved through the shapes with the focus of a child who has played something so often that the body has absorbed it entirely — no hesitation at the edges, no second-guessing the weight of a foot. She bent, without losing her position, and lifted the stone from the square it had chosen. Then returned the way she had come, equally precise, equally certain. The ground keeping score. She had never given it reason to mark her.

Sophia exhaled without knowing she had been holding anything. "That's not easy." "No," Thuraya said. "It isn't." "They don't need toys," Sophia said quietly. "They make their own," Thuraya replied.

Sophia stepped forward. "I want to try." John turned immediately, a smile already arriving. "Of course you do."

One of the girls handed over the stone without ceremony. Sophia took it. Studied the ground. Threw. First square — good. Second — steady. Third — her foot found the edge of the line. She stopped. Looked down.

The girls burst into laughter — immediate, honest, entirely without mockery. The laughter of people who have failed this game themselves, a hundred times, and carry that knowledge warmly. Sophia laughed with them. "I lost." "They are very good," she said, looking back at the girls. "Better than us," John agreed.

And then — without announcement — Malood. He came from somewhere, direct as always, and ran straight through the game. Through the lines. Over the shapes. The carefully drawn rectangles blurred and scattered in a single pass. The girls shouted — "هيييه! مالود!" — furious and delighted in equal measure.

Thuraya shook her head with patient affection. "He doesn't follow rules." John crossed his arms, watching Malood with an expression that could only be described as admiration. "I respect that." Sophia looked at him. "You would."

The girls were already redrawing the shapes — fingers in the dust, recreating what had been erased with the same care that had made it the first time. No debate about whether to continue. No decision required. They simply returned to it. And perhaps that — Sophia thought, watching them — was the thing most worth learning. Not the jumps. Not the stone. The returning.


## Scene Three — The Falaj


They walked further. The village continued to open in front of them — not all at once, but in the way a person opens: a little at a time, each layer offered only after the previous one had been received.

The sound reached them first. Water. At first barely there, almost lost beneath footsteps and distant voices. Then clearer — steady and continuous, carrying a rhythm that did not change with direction or distance. "Do you hear that?" Sophia slowed. John nodded. "Yes."

They followed the sound. And then — the falaj.

Water moved through a narrow channel of stone — clear, uninterrupted, making no particular effort and wasting none. It did not rush. It did not pause. It simply flowed, with the calm certainty of something that has been doing this for longer than any person watching it has been alive, and that will continue long after the last person stops to look. The surface caught the light in small shifting patterns — not still, not turbulent. Something between the two. Something alive.

But the falaj was not alone. Women sat along its edges — hands steady, movements practiced. Some washed clothes, rinsing and wringing with the ease of people for whom the task has long since become simply the rhythm of a morning. Others scrubbed large metal pots, voices moving between conversation and laughter without interruption. Children played in the same water. Splashing freely. And just to the side — Malood. He stepped forward, lowered his head, and drank. Without consulting anyone.

"They are washing clothes there," Sophia said slowly. "And dishes," John added. "And the children are inside the water." A pause. "And the goat is drinking from it."

Malood lifted his head briefly. Blinked. Returned to drinking. As though none of this required comment. Sophia exhaled. "They are using the same water for everything."

Across the channel, Nasser stood beside the pickup truck, pouring water over it in long arcs, scrubbing with focused patience. Sophia turned toward John. "Everything," she said again — not as complaint, but as the word for something she was still trying to hold.

Then John crouched at the edge. Placed both hands in the water. And washed his face. Cold. Immediate. He looked up — entirely refreshed, entirely unconcerned.

"John!" "What?" "You just washed your face in that!" "Yes." "People wash clothes there. And the goat is drinking from it." He wiped his face. "They wash everything in it. And the children drink from it."

Sophia froze. "They what?" He didn't look concerned. He looked — thoughtful. As though the falaj had just offered him a question he hadn't expected to find interesting.

One of the women had noticed them watching. She smiled and spoke in Arabic — easy, warm. Another joined in, adding something that made the first woman laugh. Sophia looked at Thuraya. "They're talking about us, aren't they?" "Yes." "What are they saying?" "Ahlan… welcome." A pause. "And they are asking who is your husband."

Sophia's eyes widened. "Already?" Thuraya laughed softly. "It is normal here."

Sophia gave a small, uncertain wave. The women waved back, still smiling — with the warmth of people who have no reason to be anything other than exactly that.

She turned back toward the falaj. But her face had changed — the reactive expression of the early morning had shifted into something quieter. She was no longer reacting. She was looking. And there is a difference between those two things as large as the distance between watching a river and understanding that the river is what makes the valley possible. The same water. Different uses. No order imposed from outside. She didn't fully understand it yet. But she could see it working. And that — she was beginning to think — was always how understanding begins.


## Scene Four — Sadoo's Shop


They left the falaj behind — but it did not entirely leave them. Its sound continued somewhere beneath the village's other sounds: steady, present, like a sentence that continues below conversation. "It's still there," John said, glancing back. "The falaj. It feels like it's moving with us. Like a long line running through everything."

"That sounds unsettling." "No. More like… it's watching." Sophia looked at him. "You're making friends with water now?" John nodded — completely serious. "Yes."

They walked a few steps further — and then something hit him. Fast. Entirely without warning. John lost his balance entirely. The ground slipped. He fell straight into the falaj. The water received him with cold, democratic completeness.

A moment of silence — the kind that arrives before laughter. Then Thuraya laughed first. Then Sophia, helplessly. Then John himself, sitting in the water, still trying to understand what had happened. Beside him — Malood. Standing at the edge. Entirely unbothered. Regarding the situation with the golden, philosophical expression of a creature who has long since decided to accept whatever the world offers.

Thuraya pointed at John, still smiling. "Your friend just hugged you." "I think… I chose the wrong friend." "No. Now you have two." Sophia added — "The falaj… and Malood." Malood stepped closer. As if agreeing. "No. Not Malood." But Malood followed — one step, then another. Persistent. Certain that John was the correct direction. "Can I return him?" "No," Thuraya said calmly. "He stays."

They continued — John still wet, Malood still following — until around a bend in the path a small shop appeared. Simple. Open-fronted. Alive in the way that small shops in small places are alive — not with the curated energy of commerce, but with the specific warmth of a place that knows every person who walks through it. "This is Sadoo's shop," Thuraya said.

Before they reached the entrance, children arrived from every direction —

"Sadooooo!"

"أنا باغي مينو!"

"أنا باغي داغوص!"

"أنا باغي ديو!"

The voices overlapped — all of them the most important person in the room simultaneously. "Are they shouting?" John listened. Then smiled. "No. They're ordering."

Inside, Sadoo moved between them with the composed efficiency of a man who has never been overwhelmed by any of this, because all of this is simply Tuesday. "What is 'minu'?" "Chicken-flavoured snacks." "And 'daghoos'?" "Spicy sauce." "And 'Dew'?" "A drink." Sophia turned toward John. "Chicken… spicy… and soda?" "Balanced diet."

A boy stepped forward proudly — barefoot, dusty — and dipped his snack into the daghoos without a moment's hesitation. Sophia watched. "Children eat spicy food?" Thuraya nodded. "Yes. They start early." Sophia looked at John. "Our children would cry."

Sophia picked up a colourful bottle. Read the letters slowly. "Bo… ba?" "Boba," Thuraya said. "A drink." Sophia shook it slightly. "It's gas?" Before anyone could answer, a boy ran out, opened his bottle, and drank it in three enormous gulps. Sophia looked at John. "This village will kill me."

And then — something small and fast shot between their legs.

"Malood!"

He was inside Sadoo's shop before anyone had time to respond — moving through the space with the single-minded energy of a creature who has located something interesting and intends to investigate it completely. He went at the shelves. He smelled everything. He jumped onto a low box, then onto another. Children shouted — "يالله! مالود! برا!" — and then followed him, which meant half the village was now involved. Malood ran outside, pursued by the children, the children's laughter, and the particular chaos that only he could generate from a standing start.

Sophia laughed so hard she had to hold the door. John stood beside her, still wet, still followed at a careful distance by Malood upon his return — and somehow, in all of it, looking entirely at home.

From across the road — a figure. A boy. Standing still. Not laughing. Not running. Not joining any of it. He only watched. Quietly. Carefully. His eyes moving from John to Sophia to Thuraya and back — taking in everything without giving anything away.

Thuraya noticed him. Her voice became softer. "Who is that boy?" John asked. "That is Saeed." "Why doesn't he come closer?" Sophia asked. Thuraya's answer was simple. "He is not allowed to go to school."

Sophia looked at the boy — at his stillness, at the way he watched, at the quality of attention in his face. She did not ask another question. She simply looked. And something in her, quietly, filed the moment away.


## Scene Five — Thursday  /  Khamis


They stepped away from the shop. The voices of the children faded gradually — not disappearing, but drawing back into the village's deeper register, becoming one thread among many in a place that never entirely goes quiet. The path stretched forward, carrying them further into something they had not yet learned to name.

For a moment, no one spoke. Sophia walked slightly slower than before — her eyes no longer jumping from surface to surface but settling, taking longer. She was watching differently now. With more of herself in it.

Then she saw them. A man walking ahead. Tall — his posture firm, his steps heavy and certain against the ground. His dishdasha simple, marked by use rather than display. A turban wound tightly around his head. In his hand, a ma'ool, the metal edge darkened from years of actual use.

Behind him — a boy. Walking in the same rhythm as the man — same pace, same weight, same angle in the shoulders. Not the imitation of a child performing what he has seen. Something more absorbed than that — as though the pattern had moved past the mind entirely and settled into the body itself, the way habits settle when they have been practised long enough to stop being habits and become simply the way things are.

"Who is that?" Sophia asked quietly. "That is Khamis." She repeated the name. "Khamis…"

John stopped. "Wait. Like… Thursday?" Thuraya smiled. "Yes." "You name people after days of the week?" "Sometimes. If the child is born on Thursday, he can be called Khamis. Friday — Juma." John let out a small laugh. "So you don't try to avoid Friday?" Thuraya shook her head, amused. "No." "Because it comes every week," John added. Thuraya laughed softly. "Ya rabee…"

But Sophia had already turned back to the boy. Khamis — still walking behind his father, step for step, without distraction. Without being a child in any of the ways she had expected children to be.

"This doesn't feel like a child," she said quietly. "He looks older." John nodded. "Like he's thirty."

"He's copying everything." "Yes," Thuraya said. "And his father?" "A farmer." "He works with him?" "Yes." The answer was simple. But it stayed — the way simple answers stay when they are carrying more than their words.

The man and the boy continued ahead, their rhythm unchanged. Not hurrying. Not slowing. Simply moving — the way the falaj moved, the way the morning moved — as though time here had made its own arrangements and the people had simply agreed to keep them. Sophia watched them until they had become small in the distance. Then said — not to anyone in particular — "I want to understand." Thuraya met her gaze. "You will."


## Scene Six — Nahsher  /  Another World


They continued walking, leaving Khamis and his father to their path. The ground softened slightly underfoot — the dust giving way to shade, the air holding a different quality. Greener. Cooler at the edges. The particular temperature of a place where something is growing.

"Look!" Sophia said, lifting her eyes. Trees — a loose collection of them, their branches wide and heavy with clusters of fruit. Different shapes. Different colours. "Those are farsadh and lamba," Thuraya explained.

A group of boys stood near the trees. Already decided. One picked up a stone and threw it — a sharp arc, a crack of contact, leaves moving, fruit falling. Another stone flew. Another fruit came down. The others ran forward, collecting before the next throw was even in the air. No wasted movement. A system arrived at through practice, not planning.

"They're attacking the trees!" Sophia gasped. Thuraya laughed. "They are doing nahsher. They throw stones so the fruit falls." John repeated the word immediately, the way he always did with new words — carefully, giving each syllable its proper weight. "Nah… sher." Sophia smiled. "You are learning fast." John nodded seriously. "Let's go and… nahsher." Thuraya burst out laughing. "Maybe another day."

The boys had collected their fruit and were eating now — and with their fruit, something else. Something bright orange. Something that smelled sharp even from a distance.

Sophia blinked. "They put… salt and pepper… on mango?" Thuraya nodded. "And chili." Sophia looked at John. John looked at the mango. Then at Sophia. Then — because he was the kind of man who believed in trying things — he reached forward and took a small piece.

He stopped. Chewed slowly. His expression passed through several stages. Then, finally — a slow smile. "It's… strange. But good." Sophia shook her head. She did not try a piece. But she watched John with the particular expression of a person who is revising their understanding of someone they thought they knew entirely.

As the sky began to turn orange, they walked back toward the apartment. The village settled around them — Sadoo closing his shop, children drifting toward their houses, the sounds of the afternoon folding quietly into evening.

And somewhere behind them — still there, still standing, still watching — was Saeed. He had not moved. He had not joined. He had only watched, the whole afternoon, with the particular stillness of someone who is taking in everything and giving nothing away yet. Still silent. Still thinking. Dreaming, perhaps, of a place called school.

That night, Sophia sat on the bisat and looked at the room around her — the clay walls, the qandeel, the open window, the warm dark beyond it. Then she looked at John.

"John… I think we didn't move to another country."

John looked at her. "Where did we move?"

She smiled. "We moved to another world."

And somewhere in the village, Malood bleated. Sadoo's door clicked shut. Children went home. And Saeed dreamed quietly of a place called school.


# Palm Three — The First Day at School



## Scene One — The Assembly Yard


The school did not announce itself. It appeared the way the village had — without fanfare, without any effort to be distinguished from what surrounded it — offering itself simply as the next thing along the path. Stone and clay. Uneven walls that carried the marks of their own making, not hidden but held, the way a face holds the years that have crossed it. Nothing polished. Nothing pretending.

Two long classrooms made of mud brick. A small yard in front. A wooden door that made a loud sound every time it opened — as though it wanted to be heard, as though it considered itself part of the announcement. Inside, wooden desks, some old, some newer, all with scratches and names carved into them by children who had wanted to leave something of themselves behind.

The blackboard was not very black. And the chalk was not very white. But for the children of Al-Qurawashiyah, this place was something new. Something important.

That morning, the village moved differently. Mothers walked with their children. Some boys ran. Some girls walked slowly, holding their books like treasures. Some children were excited. Some were scared. Some wanted to go home before they had even arrived.

Inside, Thuraya stood near the board, arranging the chalk, cleaning the table with her hand — the particular preparation of a teacher who has been in this room before and knows that what happens in the next hour will matter. She heard footsteps. Then many footsteps. Then noise. A lot of noise. She smiled. "They are coming," she said, to herself.

John stood near the door, holding a book like a soldier holding a map — with more confidence than was entirely justified by the situation. Sophia stood next to him, her dress simple and long, her hair covered with a light scarf. She looked around the room and whispered — "I feel like I am about to meet one hundred small judges."

John smiled. "They are not judges. They are students."

"They look dangerous," she replied.

They stepped inside through the wide blue wooden doors — the same shade of blue that Sophia had noticed along every threshold in this village. A large open room. A bisat pressed flat across the floor. Walls that curved slightly, forming natural edges. A ceiling fan turning slowly above — more out of habit than necessity. Wooden tables. Simple chairs. Shelves carrying books and notebooks in the particular disorder of things that are actually used.

"This is the teachers' room," Thuraya said. Sophia moved through it slowly. "It's different," she said — not as criticism, but as the honest statement of someone still deciding what different means. John ran his hand along the edge of a wooden table. Felt the grain. "It's enough," he said.

The classroom was even simpler. Stone walls. A blue wooden door. A single window through which the light entered directly — not filtered, not softened, just present. A few wooden benches. Open space.

Sophia stood in the center and looked — at the walls, at the window, at the space. An empty classroom has its own particular quality. Not emptiness. The specific silence of a held breath — the feeling of something about to begin that has not yet been given permission to. She felt it. This room was ready. Whether she was, was another question.

They moved back outside into the assembly yard. Wide. Flat. Firm from years of feet. Near the front stood a wooden pole. A folded cloth at its base. "The flag," Thuraya said.

John and Sophia unfolded it together — slowly, deliberately — the colours emerging as the fabric opened: red, white, and green, the emblem at the centre standing clear. Against the clay and dust and earth tones surrounding it — the flag did not blend. It stood. For a moment, all three of them were still.

Then — the sound of running feet. Children pouring into the yard — their energy arriving before them, dust lifting behind their steps, voices rising. They were not being pushed or called. They were coming because they had decided to come. They reached the yard and slowed, arranging themselves with the loose, practiced order of people who know where they are supposed to be.

Sophia stepped forward — instinctively. "Why are you late?" The boys answered quickly, in Arabic, faces bright. Sophia turned to Thuraya. "What did they say?" "They had a Qur'an circle before school."

Sophia looked at them. Not reluctant. Not distracted. Not waiting to be entertained. Simply present — as though the decision to be here had been made long before this particular morning, and what remained was only to begin. This was not a school waiting to be given life. It already had its own. She and John had not been invited to start something. They had been invited in.


## Scene Two — The Students


The first children entered — and they did not enter quietly. They did not sit quietly. They came like birds: talking, laughing, pushing, some heading directly for the nearest seat, some heading for the one farthest from the front, some apparently heading for no particular seat at all and simply landing wherever the momentum of their morning had carried them.

Thuraya clapped her hands once. Not loud — but strong. The sound of it had a specific quality, the quality of something that has been practised until it no longer requires effort. "Sit down, please." Some sat. Some didn't. Some sat, then stood again.

Then came a boy. Small. Thin. Dusty. With eyes full of ideas that no teacher has ever been glad to see. Thuraya noticed him immediately. "Mansoor," she said — just the name, with the particular weight of someone who has said this name many times and is prepared to say it many more. Mansoor smiled. The kind of smile that means trouble. He sat — on the wrong chair. Then moved. Then moved again. Then turned around. Then whispered. Then laughed. Then fell from the chair.

John watched all of this like a man watching a film he cannot believe is real. Sophia leaned toward him. "That one will kill us." Thuraya nodded, without looking away from the room. "Yes. But slowly."

More children entered. A boy with very straight posture sat in the front row — his back straight, his book straight, his pencil straight. Everything about him arranged with the seriousness of someone who has arrived prepared and intends to remain that way. Thuraya smiled. "Ah… Ahmed." Ahmed nodded seriously. Next to him sat Ali — looking at the board as though it were the most interesting thing in the world, his eyes sharp, his face calm. Thuraya said quietly to John, "That one is very clever."

Then came Khalil. He smiled at everyone. Sat. Then stood. Then helped someone. Then whispered something. Then laughed. Thuraya knew this type very well. Helpful. But dangerous.

Then came the girls. Noura entered and immediately said, "Where do I sit?" Before anyone answered, she added — "Is this my book? Is this my chair? Is today a long day?" Sophia blinked. "She is fast." Then came Mathila — holding papers and pencils, distributing them to other girls without being asked. "Here. Take." Then Salima, Rahma, and Marwa arrived together, shy, quiet, sitting close to each other as though they had agreed in advance to face whatever this was as a unit.

The classroom was now full. Noisy. Alive — alive in the specific way that rooms become alive when they are full of people who have not yet been asked to be quiet and are using every remaining second of that freedom.

Thuraya went to the front. "Good morning." The children answered — in Arabic. She smiled. "Today… we will learn something new." Mansoor raised his hand immediately. "Are they the new teachers?" He pointed at John and Sophia. "Yes," Thuraya said. Mansoor looked at them for a long moment. "They look strange." The class laughed. Sophia put her hand on her chest. "Thank you?" John laughed. Thuraya said — "This is Mr. John. And this is Ms. Safiya."

Some children whispered the name back to themselves — "Safiya…" — trying the shape of it, the way children try new words: quietly, privately, before committing to them in public. The children of the village would not call her Sophia. They would call her Safiya. Only John and Thuraya would use the other name. And Sophia — hearing herself become someone slightly different in this particular room, in this particular place — found that she did not mind at all.


## Scene Three — Good Morning  /  The First Word


John stepped forward. He picked up the chalk. Wrote on the board — two letters, then more, then a complete word: HELLO. He turned and faced them. "Good morning."

Silence. The children stared at him. At the word on the board. At the word coming out of his mouth. He said it again. "Good morning." Thuraya said, in Arabic — "Say: Good morning." Some said it. Some said something close to it. Some said something else entirely. And then —

Mansoor raised his voice, with the confidence of a person who is entirely certain they have it right: "Good mourning!"

The class laughed. John laughed too, the laugh of a teacher who has just been reminded that language is also sometimes very funny. "Close enough," he said.

Sophia picked up the chalk. Wrote on the board: My name is… She pointed to herself. "My name is Safiya." The children tried. "My name is…" "My name is…"

Then Mansoor stood — with the full dignity of a person making an important announcement — and said: "My name is monster."

Khalil laughed so hard he almost fell off his bench. Sophia covered her mouth. Then composed herself. "No, no, no. Mansoor. Not monster. Mansoor." Mansoor considered this for a moment — genuinely, as though weighing the options. Then said — "My name is Mansoor." John clapped. "Very good!"

Then Ahmed said perfectly, precisely, with the posture of someone who had been practicing — "My name is Ahmed." Ali said, "My name is Ali." Mathila said, "My name is Mathila."

And then Noura stood — and said: "My name is Noura. And this is my friend. And this is my book." Sophia blinked. Then laughed — genuine, surprised. "Very good. Very… detailed."

The lesson continued. "I am a student." "I am happy." "I am here." Some children said it right. Some said it wrong. Some shouted. Some whispered. And Mansoor — inevitably, magnificently — stood on his chair and announced, "I am tall!" He was not. John tried to keep order. Sophia tried to smile. Thuraya tried to survive.

Then — a sound from the yard. "ماaaaaaع!" Malood — walking straight into the yard as though he had been expected. He stopped near the students, looked around with his characteristic composure, and then made the sound again. "ماaaaaaع!" For one second — silence. Then the students broke into laughter — entirely, completely, with the full-bodied joy of children who have been holding themselves together and have just been given an excellent reason not to.

Sophia blinked. Then laughed with them. John shook his head slowly. "Of course he came." Malood took a few steps forward. Entirely comfortable. Entirely at home.

Sophia looked at him. Then back at the students. And in the specific way that good teachers are gifted — the ability to find the lesson inside the chaos — she knew exactly what to do. She pointed at Malood. "This…" A deliberate pause. "…is a goat."

The laughter made room. The students quieted enough to listen. "Goat," she repeated. Once. Clearly. One boy tried — "Goot." Another followed — "Goat." Then more. The word moved through the rows, uneven at first, then stronger. "Goat!" Malood made his sound again at precisely the right moment. The students laughed — and repeated. Louder. "Goat! Goat!" Thuraya stepped forward sharply — "مو هوش! مو جاكم!" The students straightened. The smiles didn't disappear. They just moved inward. John leaned toward Sophia. "Well… that worked." She nodded. "Yes." It was not a perfect start. It was a real one.


## Scene Four — The Way to School  /  Marhoon and Khamis


The path to school was no longer the same path. Not physically — the ground was unchanged, the houses stood where they had always stood. But the path had been filled. Students moved along it in the particular way of children who belong to a direction: some ahead, some behind, some running for no reason and then stopping for no reason, their voices filling the space the way voices fill a space when no one has thought to limit them.

Behind them — Malood. Walking. Then stopping. Then suddenly running. "ماaaaaaع!" A few boys turned back and laughed. "جاء معنا!" Another shouted — "خلّوه يجي!" And Malood, receiving this as the invitation it was, followed. Not because he had been claimed. Because he had claimed them. There is a difference, and he understood it completely.

Ahead, near the edge of the path — two figures. Marhoon. And beside him, Khamis.

Marhoon stood with the stillness of a man who has no need to move in order to be present. One hand resting on his ma'ool, his eyes moving across the passing students with the careful attention of someone who reads the world through observation rather than conversation. His face carried its usual weight — not anger, something older. The gravity of a man who has decided how things should be and has been living that decision long enough to believe it completely.

Khamis stood just behind him. Same posture. Same stillness. But his eyes moved differently — watching the younger students with something between recognition and restraint. A group of boys ran past them, laughing, their steps uneven and joyful, failing entirely to look serious.

Marhoon's voice came — not loud, but heavy. "شفت؟ يعلموا الصغيرين يجيوا كما الهوش." Each word placed carefully. The voice of a man who does not need volume because he has something stronger than volume. "خطف قدامي… لا نشوف عليهم."

The boys felt it — not the words, but the weight behind them — and adjusted. Their laughter moved inward. Their steps became slightly less chaotic. Slightly less visibly chaotic. Which was the point.

Then Malood walked past them. "ماaaaaaع!" This time Khamis could not hold it. A small laugh escaped — quick, soft, gone almost before it arrived. Marhoon turned his head the smallest possible amount. Just enough. Khamis straightened instantly. His face returned to something approaching stillness. Approaching — but not quite reaching.

Sophia, John, and Thuraya watched from a distance. "What did he say?" Thuraya translated calmly. "He said they are teaching the younger ones to walk like chaos. And he told them to behave."

Sophia looked at the boys — still moving, still carrying their contained energy — and then at Khamis, straightening himself beside his father. This kind of discipline was not loud. It was not performed. It was everywhere — in the path, in the ground, in the way Khamis straightened when his father turned, in the way the boys slowed without being told twice. Everywhere. And entirely invisible to anyone who had not yet learned to look for it.


## Scene Five — Malood and the Morning Disaster


The morning arrived wearing the face of peace. This was its first and most significant lie.

Sophia was pulled from sleep not by light, not by the adhan, not by any of the sounds a morning in Al-Qurawashiyah had already taught her to expect — but by a sound that did not belong to morning. Too heavy. Too deliberate. Unapologetic.

Chewing. Slow. Rhythmic. Chrrrkkk… chrrrkkk… Entirely confident.

"John…" He shifted. Did not open his eyes. "Good morning… tea…" "John. There is something eating our house." One eye opened, barely, reluctantly. "If it is not a lion, I will sleep." The chewing grew louder. More certain. "JOHN." This time he sat up.

They both turned toward the small table near the wall. And there — balanced on John's notebooks with the composure of a creature who has surveyed the available options and arrived at a decision he is entirely at peace with — stood Malood. Chewing. The corner of a page disappeared into his mouth in a slow, methodical progression, as though he had a plan for the notebook and intended to see it through.

Silence. The specific silence of two people looking at something they are still in the process of believing.

Sophia broke first. "No — no, no, no! That is PAPER!" Malood looked at her. Paused — with what could only be described as consideration, as though weighing her objection against his own appetite. Then continued. With increased confidence, if anything.

John moved — out of bed, across the room, hand reaching for the notebook. "Malood! Stop!" He grabbed one end. Malood pulled the other. The paper tore — cleanly, decisively — half in John's hand, the other half completing its journey. John stared at what remained. The blank face of a man receiving news he already knew was bad. "These were my lesson plans." Sophia pressed both hands to her face. "We are going to die in this village."

Malood jumped from the table and ran. Naturally. John followed immediately. "Come back! You are not a goat — you are a criminal!"

They rushed outside — and then Sophia stopped. She looked at her hand. Empty in a way it had not been empty before. She looked at the ground. At the room behind her. At John.

"John…" "Yes?" "My ring…" He turned. Looked at her hand. The world went very still. "It is gone."
