They both looked at the ground. At the bed. At the table. At the door. At each other. And then Sophia said the sentence that changes everything — quietly, with the specific calm of someone who has just understood something terrible: "Malood was here." John went very still. There was only one possible explanation. There was only one creature in the vicinity who could be responsible for anything missing, broken, eaten, or otherwise unaccounted for.


## Scene Six — Commands, Chaos, and Control


They did not walk to find Thuraya. They ran — words coming out of Sophia in pieces, fast and breaking over each other: "Malood ate my ring — he destroyed the notebooks — he ran —" Thuraya stood. Her calm tightened — not disappearing, but compressing into something that moved faster. "First, breathe." They did not breathe. "We will find him," she said, and moved.

The village was already awake to it. A single word moving between houses faster than anything else — "The goat!" They followed it to a neighbour's house, where a man stood at the door with the specific energy of someone for whom the sight before him had exceeded available vocabulary. "Who owns this goat?!" Inside — Malood. In the center of the room. Continuing. "He took my food! My clothes! He broke my basket!"

Sophia whispered, with the eerie calm of someone who has passed through the far side of distress — "I want to disappear." John, beside her, entirely serious: "If he eats my passport, I will personally end this friendship."

Malood saw them. And ran. Again. What followed was a procession of disaster. Malood led. They followed. Children joined, shouting and laughing, turning the chase into something dangerously close to celebration.

Then Malood turned and ran into their own house. Into the kitchen. Toward the large pot — and jumped. The pot fell. Rice spread across the floor. Meat followed. Sauce covered everything. The room froze. Sophia sat down first. John sat beside her. Thuraya closed her eyes, once, slowly. Then Sophia said: "My lunch." Malood looked at her. Chewed what he had. Unbothered. Victorious.

Much later — the ring still missing — Thuraya chose her words carefully. "He probably… swallowed it." Something inside Sophia sat down very quietly. That evening she said — not dramatically, not with complaint — "I love Oman." John nodded. "We will never be bored." Thuraya smiled. "Welcome to village life." And Malood slept — not like an animal that had caused everything described above, but like a king who has finished his day's work and found it good.

By the time they reached the classroom again, the story had already arrived — through eyes trying not to show that they knew, through mouths pressing inward with the effort of it. Sophia walked in — this time without imagining anything. Simply entering it. The actual room, with its actual students, in the actual aftermath of an actual morning. John stood to the side, holding what remained of his notebook: half a lesson plan, and the complete dignity of a man who has decided to find this funny rather than otherwise.

She looked at the students. Took one breath. "Okay." They looked at her immediately. She pointed toward the door — firmly. "Stop." Silence. They understood the tone before the word. She repeated it. Thuraya translated. The meaning landed. Then Sophia acted it out — a step forward, then a complete stop. "Stop." A boy stood. Took a step. Froze. "Stop." Others followed — standing, stepping, stopping — the room full of small people discovering the particular pleasure of choosing not to move.

She raised her hand. Shook her head. "Don't." A student near the front reached for something. She pointed gently. "Don't." He froze. Smiled. The room responded — voices beginning to repeat. "Don't… Don't…" John watched, impressed despite himself.

Then — "ماaaaaaع." The class turned as one. Malood. At the door. Of course.

Sophia closed her eyes. One second. Then opened them — pointed directly at him — "STOP." The class held its breath. Malood did not stop. He walked in. The class erupted. Sophia raised her voice — "STOP!" — and the students repeated it, together, loudly, gleefully. "STOP!" Malood paused. One second. Then continued. John turned away. His shoulders moved. "DON'T!" — and the students, following — "DON'T!" Together. The room full of the word, the students full of it, the word belonging to them in a way it hadn't belonged fifteen minutes ago. Malood stopped. Actually stopped. And then — sat.

The class lost what remained of its composure. Laughter everywhere. Joy, full and unmanaged, filling the room to its ceiling. Sophia laughed too. Then pointed at the students — across the whole room. "Good." They repeated it immediately. "Good!" She nodded. "Yes. Good."

At the end of that day, the board was full. The floor was full of chalk. The air was full of voices. And everyone was tired — deeply, satisfyingly, completely tired.

When the children left, John sat down. Sophia sat beside him. They looked at each other. Then laughed. Then laughed more.

Sophia said — "We are not ready."

John said — "Yes we are."

Thuraya smiled. "This," she said, "is only the beginning."

Three words learned. Not from a textbook. Not from the lesson plan that was currently being digested somewhere in the village. From a goat who had apparently decided that if he was going to disrupt the first English lesson of the year, he might as well contribute to it. Stop. Don't. Good. Three words that had arrived through chaos, through laughter, through the specific combination of disaster and joy that Malood seemed to carry with him everywhere he went as his own particular form of pedagogy. The lesson had not gone as planned. It had gone as it went. And sometimes — not always, but sometimes — that is the better outcome.


# Palm Four — The Morning Departure to the Souq



## Scene One — The Morning Departure


The morning arrived in the village the way mornings here had learned to arrive — not with announcement, not with the noisy machinery of a city waking up, but by small degrees. The light slipped down the mountain first, washing the ridges in a pale gold. Then it reached the roofs. Then the walls. Then, finally, the paths — where it found the first pair of sandals already moving along the dust.

The air was cool — the kind of cool that only exists in the village before the sun decides to become itself. It carried the smell of wet earth from the places where the falaj had passed in the night, and beneath that, something quieter — the faint, lifting smell of bread being made in a house she could not see.

John stood at the wooden door of their small mud house, adjusting his watch and looking out at the dusty road. The watch kept perfect British time. The road did not. The road kept its own time, which was older, and which had never consulted anyone about how long a morning should take. "Safiya?" he called inside. "Thuraya said early. I think she meant… this early."

Inside, Safiya was bent over a large cloth bag, folding things into it with the particular concentration of someone who has been warned, several times, not to forget anything. Her hair was already tied back. Her scarf was already in place. She had been awake, in fact, since before the first of the three calls to prayer — though she would not have admitted this, because some mornings are private.

"I am coming," she said, without looking up. "I am only… confirming." "Confirming what?" "That we have everything." "We don't need everything." "We might." "Have you seen our luggage from the airport?" "That," Safiya said calmly, "was relocating. This is shopping."

John considered this distinction. He had, he decided, no safe response.

Before he could find one — something small, fast, and entirely without respect for the concept of closed doors shot between his legs and into the house.

"MALOOD." "Not again." "Yes again."

The little goat stood in the middle of the room — his legs slightly apart, his head raised, his entire posture the posture of a creature who had already decided, long ago, that this house was his, and that the two humans inside it were his reasonable tenants. He surveyed the room. He located the bag. He approached it with interest.

"No," Safiya said, without raising her voice. "No, no, no. We are not doing this today." Malood looked at her. Considered the statement. Then sat.

From outside, a woman's voice arrived — bright, exasperated, entirely familiar: "Maloooood! Ta'al hna, ya majnoon sagheer!" "That's Azizah," John said, recognising the voice of the aunt who lived three houses away and who spent, by Safiya's estimate, approximately seventy percent of her waking hours looking for this particular goat. "She sounds tired." "She is tired."

Malood, receiving his name through the wall, stood. Looked at the door. Did not move. "She sounds close," Safiya observed. "She always sounds close," John said. "And he always sounds confident."

There was something Safiya had begun to notice about Malood — something she could not yet put into words in any language she owned. He did not act like an animal who had been lost and was looking for home. He acted like an animal who had several homes, all of which were his, and who had not quite decided yet which of them deserved his presence this morning. This was not the behaviour of an animal being raised. It was the behaviour of an animal being shared. Which meant, Safiya was beginning to understand, that he was not anyone's goat. He was everyone's.

John tied the bag shut. "Time." Safiya lifted it, tested its weight, accepted the verdict of physics, and handed half of it back to him. "Share." "This is marriage," he said, accepting. "This is arithmetic," she corrected.

They stepped out. Malood followed — not rushed, not reluctant, simply taking up the position behind them that he had apparently assigned to himself. Azizah's voice came again, further away this time, no longer hopeful. "I think she has given up," John said. "She has not given up," Safiya replied. "She has only paused."

They set off down the road. Three figures and a goat — unhurried, unbothered, already entirely part of the landscape they had arrived in only weeks before. And somewhere ahead, at the edge of the village, a pickup truck was waiting — and the morning was about to become something else entirely.


## Scene Two — Dukkan Al-Hillah


Thuraya was waiting for them on the path — already arranged, already composed, looking at them with the patient amusement of a person who has watched foreigners arrive on this road many times before and has, by now, stopped being surprised by anything. "Good morning." "Good morning," John returned. "We brought… supplies." "Yes," Thuraya said, glancing at the bag. "I see."

She turned and began to walk. "First — Dukkan Al-Hillah. We need rope. And a basket. And qit." "Qit?" John repeated, carefully. "You will see," she said — which, he was learning, was Thuraya's preferred method of teaching: let the thing itself do the work.

The shop was small. Not small the way a shop in London is small — apologetically, conscious of its modesty, its square metres measured and priced. This was small the way a thing is small when it has never considered being anything else. Its door stood open. Its front held wooden boxes of rice, sugar, tea — the essentials arranged not decoratively but practically, the way things are arranged when people are going to reach for them many times a day. On one wall hung baskets and ropes. In one corner, strung up like dried flowers at a very different kind of harvest — awal and qashie', hanging in their salted, sunlit patience.

Behind the wooden counter — Sadoo. Smiling the particular smile he always smiled when customers entered: a smile that had nothing to do with commerce and everything to do with the simple, habitual pleasure of seeing faces he knew.

And then, before any human had entered — Malood. Who had gone ahead. Who had entered first. Who was already inside, examining the shelves with the unhurried, proprietary air of an inspector arriving on scheduled rounds.

Sadoo laughed — a full, warm laugh that filled the small shop entirely. "Malood! shuftak! What do you want today, ya habibi?" Malood looked at him. Did not answer. "He does not answer," Sadoo explained, cheerfully, to no one in particular. "He only takes."

John, who had been looking for a word to start with, pointed to a bundle of green leaves resting on the counter. "I… I want this." A small silence. Sadoo looked at Thuraya. Thuraya looked at the leaves. Sadoo laughed again, more quietly this time. "Tell him — this is qit."

John repeated. "Qit." "Qit," Thuraya corrected, her smile widening. "Not 'kit'. Qit." He tried again. The sound was slightly better. "And what is qit?" Safiya asked. Thuraya turned to her. Gentle. Careful. Letting the joke land without cruelty. "Food for goats."

Safiya looked at the leaves. At John. At Malood, who had suddenly become interested in the conversation. "John. You just requested goat food." "With confidence," Thuraya added, generously. "Which is important in a new language." John took this on the chin. "Then I will buy it. For Malood. So the lesson is not wasted." "That," Thuraya said, "is teacher thinking."

Sadoo wrapped the qit in string. John paid. He lowered the bundle toward Malood, who took it with an expression that could only be described as smug.

Safiya, meanwhile, had stopped in front of the hanging awal. She tilted her head. She tilted it the other way. The fish did not become more appetizing from either angle. "Thuraya… how do people eat this?" Thuraya's eyes were patient. "Slowly." A pause. "And with rice." Another. "You will like it later." "Later when?" "Later when you are hungry enough."

They bought rope. They bought the basket. They carried their purchases out into a morning that had grown slightly warmer, slightly brighter, slightly more itself. Malood walked behind them with the qit in his mouth, proudly — as though he, personally, had conducted the transaction and was now escorting his purchase home.


## Scene Three — Uncle Nasser's Pickup


At the end of the road — the truck.

It rested at the edge of the path the way a working animal rests: not idle, but pausing. The kind of pause that is not absence of motion, but preparation for it.

Safiya looked at it, and then looked at it more carefully, because her eyes had arrived faster than her understanding. She had seen old vehicles in England. She had, once or twice, ridden in them. But those had been old in the sentimental way — kept because someone loved them, polished, driven on Sundays. This was old in a fundamentally different way. It was old because it worked. It had worked for so long, and through so much, that age had ceased to be a thing that could be used against it.

Its paint had faded past the point of being paint. It was now a surface — pale, warm, touched by a hundred sun-years — the colour of something that had negotiated, quietly, with weather. Along the doors, rust moved in thin, deliberate lines, as though the road had been drawing on it in slow, patient ink. The mudguards were bent — not damaged, shaped, the way hands become shaped by what they carry for years. And even at rest, the truck made sound — a small, intermittent tick from somewhere beneath the hood, a slow settling in the suspension, the soft creak of metal that had learned to speak.

And beside it — Uncle Nasser.

He was the kind of man Safiya had not seen often, because he was not the kind of man a British girlhood had prepared her for. He was not tall. He was not broad. He was not, in any of the obvious ways, imposing. But he had a density — she could think of no other word — a quality that made the space around him pay attention. His dishdasha was clean but not new, the hem slightly dark from the dust that every Omani hem eventually accepts. His turban was wound with the tightness of a man whose turban would stay on through weather. His eyes, she noticed, rested rather than looked — they settled on faces one at a time, gave each one its portion of attention, then moved on. This, she thought, was what authority looked like when it had stopped needing volume.

And in his right hand — not raised, not waved, simply held — the bamboo stick. Smooth at the grip. Dark where years of hands had darkened it. Every child in the yard registered it — not with fear, not with resentment, but with the same automatic awareness that comes with any agreed ritual.

The children were already gathered. Ali sat on a low stone, bag between his knees, looking at the truck the way some boys look at horses — with the specific steadiness of someone who respects the thing that is about to carry him. Ahmed stood behind him — back straight, pencil already in his shirt pocket, as though a pencil were the correct thing to have at the ready on any journey of any kind. Khalil was helping Noura lift her small cloth bundle into the truck, then helping Mathila with hers, then helping Rahma — helping without being asked, because helping, for Khalil, was not a decision. It was a reflex. Marwa, Salima, and Rahma stood in their usual triangle — slightly apart from the others but never from each other, speaking in the low, half-whispered voice of girls who have agreed, without ever discussing it, to face the world as a unit.

And Mansoor, inevitably, was orbiting. Around the truck. Around the children. Around Uncle Nasser, at a calculated, prudent distance. Even when he stood still, something about him was already moving — a foot, a shoulder, an eyebrow.

Uncle Nasser lifted the stick. Not high. Not fast. He lifted it the way a conductor lifts a baton — to mark a beginning. The children stopped moving. Even Mansoor stopped — partly. "Listen," he said, in Arabic. The word was not loud. It did not need to be. His voice had the same carrying quality as the adhan. Not in tone. In weight. "Today, we go to Samail. Today, no trouble. Today — who makes trouble meets this."

He did not even lift the stick higher. He only indicated it, with the smallest turn of his wrist. The children nodded, as one, with the gravity of people ratifying a treaty they had signed many times before. Mansoor's eyebrow stopped moving.

Safiya leaned toward Thuraya. "Does he really use it?" Thuraya did not answer immediately. She watched the children. She watched Uncle Nasser. Then — without looking at Safiya, only at the children — she said quietly: "He has never needed to."

And Safiya understood, without having it explained, that this was the entire philosophy of the stick. It was not an instrument of punishment. It was an instrument of agreement — made visible, in wood, so that the agreement did not need to be spoken each morning.

She did not know, at that moment, that she would think about this many times in her life. That she would think about it when she returned to London, years later, and watched teachers trying to explain, through many long sentences, what Uncle Nasser had communicated with a single unraised piece of bamboo.

John looked at the back of the truck. Then at Safiya. Then at the back of the truck again. He did this three times — a small, slow oscillation — which was John's way, Safiya had learned, of negotiating with reality when reality had failed, in some small but significant way, to match his expectations.

"Where do we…" "There," Thuraya said, indicating the flatbed. "Hold the metal sides." "There are no seats." "Yes." "There are no… belts." "Yes." A long pause, during which John's face moved through several distinct philosophical stations. "Is this safe?" "It is safer than walking to Samail." A longer pause. "Is walking to Samail prohibitive?" "Yes."

John turned to Safiya. His face had arrived, after its journey, at a kind of resignation that was, Safiya thought, very becoming on him. "Adventure?" "If we die," she said, and placed one hand on the side of the flatbed, "tell my mother it was your idea." "That was our understanding," John said, and offered her his hand.

She took it. She took it more firmly than she usually did. She climbed into the back of the truck, and the metal was warm — already warm, the sun having spent the morning on it — and the warmth went through her palm into her arm like a small private message.

The children arranged themselves with the unthinking grace of passengers who had, between them, made this journey a hundred times. Some at the cab end, close to the window. Some at the back, legs dangling. Some in a small central cluster, safest, most shared. Ahmed sat beside Safiya without discussion — as though he had been assigned to her, though nobody had. And Malood, last, jumped up onto the tailgate with the entitlement of a creature who had decided, long ago, that he was on the staff.

"Oh no," Safiya said. "Yes yes," the children said, in unison, already laughing.

The engine turned over. It did not start cleanly — it started the way an old man stands up: with complaint, with a small shudder, and then with the steady resumption of a duty long practised. Dust rose from beneath the wheels in a pale, opinionated cloud. Uncle Nasser leaned his head out of the cab window, shouted something cheerful to a man leading a camel on the other side of the road, received a shouted answer, laughed — a short, surprised laugh, as though the answer had been a good one — and released the brake.

The truck began to move. Safiya's body understood what was happening before her mind did. Her grip on the metal side tightened — she had not told it to — and her other hand went, without consultation, to the inside of John's elbow. He did not look at her. He only shifted slightly, made room for her balance inside his, and held it there.

The road to Samail was not paved. The road to Samail had never been paved, and had, over the course of many decades, formed a stubborn personal opinion about what smoothness was and was not. It did not believe in smoothness. It believed in honesty. Every bump announced itself in advance, delivered itself fully, and moved on to the next one without apology.

The first real bump came about forty seconds in. Safiya felt it move up through the truck — through the metal, through the boards beneath her, through the base of her spine — and exit somewhere near the top of her skull. Her teeth met. The scarf lifted around her face. The dust entered her mouth in a fine, gritty layer that settled immediately on the back of her tongue.

"Oh," she said. "I see." "It gets worse," Ahmed said, quietly, beside her. Not warning. Just informing.

"Worse than this?" He nodded — not unkindly. Almost reassuringly. "In the valley part."

She laughed. Not because it was funny. Because there was nothing else a person could reasonably do, in the back of an unbalanced truck, with dust in her teeth, being warned by a nine-year-old boy about the valley part.

The next bump. And the next. She stopped flinching. She began, instead, to ride them — to let her knees soften, to let her spine absorb what her arms could not brace against. Ahmed, beside her, was doing the same thing — had been doing it his whole life, possibly since before he could walk — and had never once thought about it.

She looked up. The mountains were closer than she had expected, and larger, and redder — a deep, iron-coloured red that she had not seen in any painting, any photograph, any book. The road turned. A small stream — a narrow wadi — opened to their right and closed again, briefly, like a page being turned.

"John…" she said. He leaned toward her. "Yes?" "I stopped being afraid." He smiled. "When?" "I don't know. Somewhere around bump eleven."

He laughed. Ahmed, listening, understood only the cadence of the English, not the words. But the cadence told him enough. He smiled too.

Then Mansoor — because the laws of Mansoor are eternal — stood up. In the back of a moving truck. At full height. His arms out, like a bird. "I AM FLYING!" Thuraya, from the front, turned sharply. "MANSOOR, IGLUS!" Mansoor sat. Fast. Like a puppet whose string had been severed. The children laughed. He laughed at himself — Mansoor's one redeeming feature, Safiya was beginning to realise: he was always the first to laugh at himself.

They passed a man on a donkey. The man raised his hand. Uncle Nasser, without turning, raised his hand back — an economical gesture, no larger than was needed. A greeting that had, by now, been made many thousands of times, and had been polished down to the exact size of itself. Safiya watched the exchange — one second, no more — and felt, without quite being able to name why, that she had just seen something sacred.

They passed palm groves. They passed a flock of goats that moved, without urgency, to the edge of the road as though the truck were a rumour they had heard but not quite believed. They passed a woman walking with a bundle on her head — perfect balance, perfect carriage — and the woman looked up, saw Thuraya in the front seat, and waved.

"Who is that?" Safiya asked. Thuraya, turning, had to raise her voice over the engine. "Umm Salim. She teaches the girls in Misfah how to make bukhoor." "From what?" "From everything. You will see."

You will see. Safiya was beginning to suspect that this was Thuraya's national phrase. You will see. Four syllables. Used in place of explanation. Used, in fact, as explanation — because in this country, as she was slowly understanding, seeing was not an extra step after hearing. Seeing was the primary method. Words only prepared the eye.

Somewhere around the twentieth minute — or was it the thirtieth; time in the back of the truck had become unreliable — Safiya became aware that she had stopped holding the metal side with both hands. One hand was loose at her side. Her body had made a decision she had not been consulted about. It had decided to trust. To trust Uncle Nasser, who was at the wheel. To trust the children, who were all still in the truck and laughing. To trust the road, which, against her every expectation, was becoming, in its honest brutal way, a kind of teacher.

She looked at John. His hair was white with dust. His eyebrows were white. A small crescent of dust had settled along his lower lip. He was beaming. Completely. Without reservation.

"John…" "Mmm?" "I think you have been waiting for this your whole life." He looked at her. Considered it, seriously. "I think you might be right."

Thuraya, from the front, turned her head once and saw them — white with dust, laughing, leaning slightly into each other the way people lean when they are no longer concerned with looking like themselves. She smiled — the widest smile Safiya had yet seen from her. The smile of a person who had known, from the moment she met these two, that the road would rearrange them, and who was now watching the rearrangement happen. "You are learning," she said, over the engine.

The truck rolled forward. The mountains drew closer. The dust behind them trailed in a long pale cloud that wrote their direction on the morning air. And Safiya — held between a British husband who was beaming, an Omani boy who was calmly balanced, a teacher who knew exactly what she was doing, and a goat who had no business being here — understood, without quite being able to explain it, that she was no longer a visitor. She was not yet a resident. She was something in between. Something the English language did not quite have a word for. Something the road, perhaps, had just named.


## Scene Four — Samail Market


Samail did not arrive. Samail accumulated.

First — more walls. Then walls that began to meet at corners, forming something that could be called, if you were generous, streets. Then a small herd of goats being driven along the edge of the road by a boy of maybe seven, who did not look up as the truck passed because he had a far more serious task in front of him. Then a woman carrying water in a metal container balanced on her head, moving with a stillness that was not the absence of motion but the mastery of it. Then more shops. Then more voices. Then — without anyone having announced it, without any sign — they were in the market.

Uncle Nasser pulled into a patch of dust beside a low wall. The engine protested once, as though it had one more opinion to express, and then went silent. The dust did not go silent. The dust continued its slow descent onto their clothes, their hair, their skin, with the unhurried precision of a thing that had all the time in the world. "We arrived," Thuraya said, from the front. "We survived," Safiya corrected.

The children climbed down first. They did it the way they did most things — without consultation, without ceremony — and arranged themselves in the loose, flexible shape of a group that already knew, internally, where its edges were. Safiya placed both hands on the warm metal side of the truck. She looked down. The ground was a long way below her, in the way ground is always a long way below a person who has been in a moving truck for forty minutes and whose legs now have opinions of their own.

John jumped down first — landing awkwardly, catching himself, laughing. Then he raised his hands to her. "Come." She came. Her feet touched the ground and the ground felt, for one very strange second, suspiciously still. The body did not believe in stillness yet. The body was still being rattled by a truck that was no longer moving. She stood for a moment, palms flat against her skirt, waiting for her legs to catch up with her arrival.

Then she lifted her head. And stopped.

Samail market did not open onto her in a single view. It opened in layers. First, sound — a complicated, unbroken texture of many voices going in many directions at many speeds, none of them in English, most of them not even in the Arabic she had begun, very slowly, to recognise. Then colour — reds she had never seen on a cloth, yellows she had not thought were available to human pigment, greens that looked less like colour and more like an argument. Then smell — and smell arrived last, because smell was the most difficult to catalogue. Spice. Bread. Animal. Sweat. Earth. Water on stone. Something burning somewhere that might have been incense and might have been breakfast. Citrus. Goat. Coffee. Dust. And behind all of them, older than all of them, the faint, unmistakable tang of salt and cured fish.

She understood, in a way that could not be put into a sentence, that she was standing inside a piece of time that had not changed in perhaps three hundred years. Adjustments had been made, of course — a metal container where once there would have been a clay one, a cloth dyed with a newer red. But the architecture — of motion, of exchange, of how a person asked for a thing and how the thing was given — had not moved.

"John…" He was beside her. He had been watching her face, which is to say, he had been watching her arrive. "Yes?" "This is not a market." "No?" "This is a country that has been compressed into three streets." Thuraya, beside them, smiled the quiet smile of a person whose pupil has, without assistance, reached the correct answer.

They began to move through it. Thuraya led — not briskly, not as a guide, but at the particular pace of a person who has been here a hundred times and has learned, a hundred times, that there is no advantage in hurrying. The children walked around them in their loose constellation — Khalil already helping a small girl who was not one of ours gather something she had dropped, Ahmed keeping careful watch over Mansoor, Noura three steps ahead and asking the first shopkeeper she reached what everything was.

A man passed them carrying a live chicken under each arm. The chickens did not seem to mind. Another man called out a price for dates in a voice so musical that for a moment Safiya forgot he was selling anything and simply listened. The intonation rose and fell like a small verse — his price at the top, his justification at the bottom, and a small, persuasive pause between them. This, she thought, was not selling. This was persuasion as a small spoken art form, practised daily, by men who had never heard of the word art and who had mastered it anyway.

Safiya stopped, without meaning to, beside a woman with a large mat of frankincense. The resin was in piles — different colours, different sizes, different grades. A small hand-written card beside each one, in Arabic, with a number. The woman looked up. She was perhaps seventy — a face weathered in the specific way of women who have worked outside, patiently, for decades — and in her eyes was the particular appraisal of a merchant who has seen every kind of customer in forty years and has already, in the first second, sorted this one.

The woman picked up a small golden stone between her thumb and forefinger. Crushed it, lightly, with the confidence of someone who could tell the grade by smell alone. She held it out to Safiya. Not offering to sell. Offering to share.

Safiya leaned down. She did not know why. Something in the gesture had been — a small invitation, wordless and complete. She smelled. Her eyes closed involuntarily. The smell was nothing she had smelled in London. Nothing she had smelled in the village. It was older than either. Sweet and dry and high in the back of the nose, and underneath, something resinous and deep, like a tree giving up its slowest secret.

She opened her eyes. The woman was smiling — a small, private smile. She said something, in Arabic. Thuraya, who had paused beside them, translated quietly. "She says — this one is hojari. The best. The frankincense of kings." "…I smelled frankincense of kings." "Yes." "How much?" Thuraya translated the question. The woman named a price. Safiya paid it without bargaining. Thuraya — mildly — said afterwards, "You could have haggled." "I know." "But you did not." "No." "Why?" Safiya thought about it for a long moment. "Because she let me smell it first. Before she named a price. That is not a transaction. That is an offering. You cannot haggle an offering." Thuraya looked at her for a long second. Then nodded — slowly, approvingly. "Good," she said. "You are reading the market correctly."

They moved on. Safiya placed the small paper parcel of hojari into her bag — and noticed that she placed it not in the top, where she would have to find it again, but carefully at the bottom, against the soft folds of her scarf, where it would be protected. She did not examine why she was doing this. Some actions, she was learning, arrived before explanation, and did not require any.

A few stalls further on, John had stopped. He was standing in front of a small wooden table on which, on a single flat cloth, lay the formidable body of a large awal. It was perhaps the length of his forearm. It was the colour of old steel. It did not gleam in any decorative way. It simply lay there, patient and complete, in the dignity of something that had been a fish and was now, with some dignity, something else.

The fish seller — an older man with enormous, patient hands, and the kind of white beard that begins along the edge of the jaw and arrives, eventually, at the chest — looked at John. "Awal?" "Yes. Awal. I would like… this one," John said, pointing. The man raised one eyebrow. Not in mockery. In assessment. "Big one?" "Big one." "For family?" "For…" John glanced at Thuraya. "For experience." Thuraya translated this. The fish seller laughed — a full, deep laugh, the laugh of a man who has waited his whole working life for a foreigner to give exactly this answer. "Tayyeb," he said. "Good."

He wrapped it. First in a clean paper, then in a cloth, then tied with a piece of palm twine the economy of whose motion suggested he had tied perhaps ten thousand such knots in his working life and had no intention of wasting any of them now. He handed the parcel across.

John received it. The weight of it surprised him — heavier than he had expected, more substantial. And then the smell. The smell hit him after the weight — a complicated, layered, deeply unapologetic smell of fish that had been salted, then dried in direct sun for weeks, then hung patiently in the shade, and had now, after months of this slow curing, concentrated the entire ocean into one small, patient, odorous package.

Safiya took one large, thoroughly diplomatic step back. "John." "Yes?" "It smells." "I know." "No. You do not. It smells with conviction." "I see the distinction." The fish seller, understanding the tone if not the words, laughed again — warmer this time. He said something to Thuraya. Thuraya, smiling, translated. "He says — the fish has character. Not every fish has character. This one does." "That is a generous way to describe it," Safiya said. "You will get used to it," Thuraya said. "When?" "When you stop holding your breath."

John, carrying the parcel at arm's length, looked at it — not the length of his arm, but almost — and said to it quietly, as a man might address an animal he is not sure is asleep: "It is not personal." The fish, being dried and salted and entirely beyond the affairs of this world, did not reply.

They moved on. Safiya continued walking at a slight, measured distance from John and his purchase — a distance that was mostly affectionate, slightly precautionary, and entirely sensible. But her attention had shifted. She was no longer reacting to things. She was beginning to anticipate them. Which stalls would be crowded. Which sellers would speak first, and which would wait. Where the goods flowed, and where they pooled. She was, without having learned it, starting to read the market the way Thuraya read it — as a living organism with currents and eddies and rules.

She passed a cloth merchant unrolling a long piece of deep green for a customer. She passed a man brazing a small metal pot with a hand-held flame, the specific smell of hot metal joining the market's other smells without apology. She passed a date seller arranging his dates by variety — darker ones on the left, smaller paler ones on the right — each heap a small, shining hill.

A boy of maybe five ran past her, chasing a tethered goat kid who did not understand the rules of the race but was running anyway. The boy nearly collided with Safiya, stopped short, looked up, said "sorry" in very careful English — clearly a word a teacher had taught him — and ran off before she had recovered enough to answer. She stood for a moment. Then laughed. Out loud. Without reservation.

It was the laugh of a person who had, without noticing, crossed a small inner threshold. She was no longer inside this market as a visitor. She was inside it as a person. The distinction mattered. She did not know why it mattered yet. She only knew that it did.

Thuraya had, very quietly, been watching. She had seen the frankincense moment. She had seen the laugh. She said nothing. She only, for a single second, placed her hand on Safiya's arm — lightly, briefly, without comment — and then let it fall again. Some things, Safiya was learning, were affirmed not through speech but through small, witnessed gestures.

Thuraya clapped her hands once — lightly, just enough. The children — who had been dispersed across three different stalls, each one making, in their own way, their own small explorations — gathered. John turned. Safiya turned. Even Malood — who had, in the intervening minutes, located someone's cabbage — lifted his head.

"Now," Thuraya said, "we learn."


## Scene Five — I Want


Thuraya looked at the students. "Today we will learn one sentence." The children stilled. Even Mansoor, momentarily. "Only one?" Noura asked. "Only one," Thuraya confirmed. "But one that will work a hundred times."

John took a piece of paper from his pocket. He had folded it very carefully that morning — carefully in the way a teacher folds a thing when he knows the thing will be unfolded in front of children, and children, as every teacher knows, can read creases. He held it up. He wrote, in clear capital letters, what Thuraya had asked him to write.

I WANT…

Two words. A pause afterwards. A small, open space where anything in the world could go.

And looking at those two words on that small piece of paper, standing in the middle of the largest, loudest market she had ever tried to be calm inside, Safiya felt something that was almost recognition. Not for the sentence — she had known that sentence her whole life. But for the act of teaching it. For the quiet generosity of handing someone a sentence they could now use to enter any shop in the country. That, she thought, was what languages really were. Not grammar. Not vocabulary. Doors. Given to people. So they could walk through.

Thuraya said — clearly, slowly — "I want…" The children tried. "I want." "I want." "I… want." "Very good," Thuraya said. "Now — we use it."

She pointed toward a nearby stall. A small pile of dates. A kind but curious shopkeeper, already half-listening. "Who will go first?"

Ali stepped forward without hesitation. He walked to the stall. He looked at the dates. He straightened his small back. "I want… this." The shopkeeper blinked — just once. Then smiled, hugely, the smile of a man who has not been spoken to in English in his own market for a long time, and who has been gifted the novelty of it by a boy who looks, for all the world, entirely serious. "Tafadhdhal, ya habibi," the shopkeeper said, handing him a date. "Take. Take."

The children cheered. Ali walked back with the date in his hand — and on his face, the quiet pride of a person who has just, for the first time in his life, used a foreign language to acquire an object. "It worked," he said, amazed. "Yes," Thuraya said. "It worked."

Mansoor pushed forward. Of course he did. "Me now! Me me me!" Thuraya placed a gentle hand on his shoulder. "Say: I want." Mansoor took a breath — a large, dramatic, theatrical breath — walked up to a cloth of oranges, and announced at full volume: "I WANT THIS! THIS! THIS!" The orange seller looked at him. Then at Thuraya. Then at John. Then, with the slow gentleness of a man who has seen everything and has decided not to be surprised by any of it — handed Mansoor three oranges. The children collapsed into laughter. Safiya, who had covered her mouth, uncovered it, and laughed too.

"He understood," Thuraya explained, to Safiya. "Three 'this' — three oranges." "The grammar was wrong," John observed. "The result was three oranges," Thuraya said. "The grammar can come later."

Noura went next. She had, of course, prepared a speech. "I want…" — she paused, she considered, she let the whole market wait for her — "…to know the name of every fruit in this market." The seller blinked. Thuraya translated, softly, in Arabic. The seller roared laughing. And then, patiently — because she was ten years old and she had asked honestly — he began to point to each fruit and say its name, while Noura nodded very seriously, as though she were conducting an interview for a newspaper no one had asked her to write for.

One by one, the children went. Ahmed, precisely. Khalil, charmingly. Mathila, already distributing what she had bought to the others before she had even finished buying it. Rahma, Marwa, and Salima, together — as always — asking, at the same time, for the same small thing, and receiving it in three identical bags.

And Safiya watched. She watched with an attention she had not known she possessed. These were not students reluctantly performing a lesson. These were children discovering that a sentence was a key. That once you had the key, doors opened. That the doors had been there all along. This was not language learning as she had known it in London. This was something older. Something more immediate. Something that would stay with these children long after any textbook she could bring would have been forgotten.

She turned to John. "We have a lot to learn." He nodded. "Not just the children." "No. Not just them."


## Scene Six — Habbabouh Saif


They were standing near a stall of textiles — Safiya had become helplessly fascinated by a deep crimson cloth — when a voice arrived from behind her.

It arrived, she would later realise, before she turned. It arrived in the way that certain sounds arrive — not through the ear first but through recognition. The voice spoke flawless English. Not careful English. Not the precise English of a second-language speaker. Something else. Something fuller. English spoken the way a person speaks a language when it has lived in their mouth for a very long time.

"Good morning. You must be the new teachers."

Safiya turned. John turned. Thuraya smiled, slightly, as though she had been waiting for this moment.

A man stood in front of them — aged, but not frail. The word 'old' did not quite fit him; a better word would have been settled. A white beard reached partway down his chest. His eyes were kind in a way that seemed to be a structural feature of his face, rather than an expression he was choosing. His dishdasha was simple. His kummah was embroidered, and the embroidery was very old.

John's mouth opened. Closed. Opened again. "You… you speak English." "I do." "Very well." "I had time."

Safiya, recovering slightly faster, found her voice. "Where… where did you learn?" The man smiled. A small, contained smile, the smile of a person who has been asked this question before and has his answer ready, but who still, each time, enjoys delivering it.

"My name is Saif. But here — the people call me Habbabouh Saif." He gestured slightly — not with importance, only with explanation. "I worked many years with the Omani fleet. And I lived — for many years — in " data-m="An island off East Africa that was part of the Omani Empire for centuries; a deep and living part of Oman's international, Swahili-influenced history" data-s="Omani Zanzibar, East Africa island" data-x="Zanzibar was not foreign to Oman. It was Oman, across the sea.">Zanzibar."

The word arrived the way words do when they have not been expected. Zanzibar. Safiya had read the name on maps. She had, perhaps, seen it written on the side of one or two very old tea boxes in British shops. She had never once thought of it as a place where an old man in a kummah might still live in the memory. And yet — here he was. An entire life she had not known existed. An entire geography folded inside one person. An Oman she had not yet realised was an Oman of oceans, not just mountains.

"Zanzibar," she repeated softly. "Yes." "We… I did not know." "Most people who come here do not know," Habbabouh Saif said. Kindly, with no judgment. Simply reporting. "But this country is very old. And it has been in many places. Before the places knew they were places."

John found himself sitting down — on a wooden box beside the stall, without having quite decided to. "You — you must have stories." "Many." "Will you tell us?" "Yes," Habbabouh Saif said. "Slowly."

And he did. He told them, quietly, with the patience of a man who had spent years at sea, about Zanzibar. About the market in Stone Town. About the smell of cloves on ships. About the Swahili he had learned and the English he had acquired, slowly, from English captains and Indian clerks and other sailors of a dozen nations who had met, briefly, on decks and then gone their separate ways. "Language," he said, looking at John and Safiya very directly, "is not a thing you carry. It is a thing that carries you. You will see."

The children had gathered, silently. Thuraya had not interrupted. Malood — improbably, impossibly — sat at the man's feet, still, attentive, as though he, too, understood that the moment did not require any of his usual interventions.

The market continued around them. Voices rose and fell. Goods moved. Prices were made and unmade. But inside that small circle, around that wooden box, time did something it rarely did — it paused. It made room. It held itself still for the length of a story.

When Habbabouh Saif finally rose and said goodbye — with the same small smile — Safiya stood for a long time without moving. "Thuraya," she said, "how many more people like him does this village have?" Thuraya smiled — the slow, long smile of a person who has been waiting for this exact question. "Every one of them," she said. "You have only met three."

That evening, as Uncle Nasser's truck rumbled back toward Al-Qurawashiyah — slower now, under a sky that was beginning to turn orange, the children quieter, Mansoor asleep with his head on Ahmed's shoulder, Malood tucked against John's boot, the day's purchases stacked between them — Safiya took out her small notebook. She wrote, in careful handwriting, and not to herself, and not quite to anyone:

Today, we did not only go to the market.We learned a sentence.We met a man with an ocean inside him.And we began — very quietly, without announcement — to understand that this country is not a small country.It is simply a country that keeps itself in very small places.

John read the page over her shoulder. He did not say anything. He only placed his hand, briefly, on hers.

Ahead of them, the mountains of Samail darkened into evening. Behind them, a long trail of dust marked the road they had crossed. And somewhere between — two teachers, one guide, ten children, a goat, and the beginning of a life that no textbook they had brought from London could ever have prepared them for.


# Palm Five — The Souq, the Children, and the First Pickup



## Scene One — The Village Before the Village Woke


She had not intended to wake this early.

The intention had been to sleep until the light made sleeping impossible — which, in this house, with its small windows and thick clay walls, would have been an hour yet. But something had woken her, and it had woken her gently, the way the village woke people who were paying attention.

She lay still for a moment, listening. And slowly — one sound at a time — the morning assembled itself around her.

The first sound, low beneath everything else, was the falaj. She had heard it every morning since they arrived, but she had not always registered it. It was a continuous sound — not quite water, not quite silence, the particular hush that moving water makes when it has been moving through the same narrow stone channel for longer than anyone in the village has been alive.

Above it — the soft cooing of doves from somewhere in the palm grove. A rooster, further off, beginning his long and unpersuaded argument with the idea that morning had arrived. A woman's voice, somewhere to the east, humming — not a song exactly, more the rhythm of a woman doing something with her hands.

She got up. Carefully — John was still asleep, his breathing deep and even, his arm across the space where she had been. She tucked the sheet gently around his shoulder and stepped onto the bisat.

The floor was cool. The mud walls held their cool carefully, the way they had been holding it since the last hours of the night. She walked to the small window and stood there.

The village was not yet light. It was in the long, in-between time that English did not quite have a word for but that Arabic did — fajr. Not night. Not morning. Something in between that was its own thing, with its own colour and its own quality of quiet. The sky was not yet blue. It was not yet pink. It was a pale, undecided silver, the colour of a decision that had not yet been made.

She lifted her shawl from the hook beside the door. She pulled her scarf over her head, quietly, not out of ceremony but because the pre-dawn cold in the village had a specific character — it did not hurt, but it insisted on a scarf. And then, without quite deciding to, she opened the door and stepped outside.

The air was different from the air inside. Cooler — she had expected that — but also clearer, in a way she had no word for. It carried the smell of the falaj more strongly out here. Water on stone. Wet earth. And beneath both of those — distant, carried on the current of dawn air — the faint smell of a wood fire beginning somewhere.

She walked, slowly, to the end of the short path that led from their door. And she saw the village awake in a way she had not seen it awake before. Not the bustling village of mid-morning. Not the children-running, Sadoo-shouting, market-day village of afternoon. Something earlier. Something the village reserved, perhaps, for itself.

A woman — Umm Hamad, she thought, though she was not yet sure of all the names — walked along the narrow path carrying an empty clay jar on her shoulder. She was going toward the falaj. Her steps were unhurried and exact, and the empty jar did not rock on her shoulder. It simply sat there, as though it understood its role.

From a house further up the path, the rhythmic, dull sound of a woman pounding something in a mortar. Cardamom, perhaps. Or coffee beans.

A man — she did not know his name — walked past carrying a bundle of qit on his shoulder. He saw her, inclined his head slightly — a greeting that contained no unnecessary motion — and continued past. He did not stare. He did not stop. He simply acknowledged her and went on.

From the small mosque near the centre of the village, the adhan began. Not loud. Not amplified — there was no loudspeaker here, only a man's voice, steady and clear, unaccompanied. The voice rose and fell in the old patterns, carried the old words, and the village, without any single person stopping, seemed to reorient itself around the voice.

She stood and listened. She did not pray — the prayer was not hers — but she understood, standing in that cool pre-dawn air, that she was being given something. Not instruction. Not even invitation. Simply presence. The right to stand here, at this hour, and witness a village being the version of itself that existed only before the children came out, only before the shops opened, only when the falaj and the doves and the adhan had the air entirely to themselves.

She heard a soft footstep behind her. She turned.

Thuraya. Already dressed. Already scarved. A small cup of something warm in her hands. She did not look surprised to see Safiya standing outside her door at fajr. She looked like a person who had guessed, the evening before, that this might happen.

"You are up." "I did not mean to be." "The village wakes people sometimes." Thuraya held out the cup. "Qahwa." Safiya took it. It was warm. It smelled of cardamom and something else she could not name yet. "Thank you." "Drink it slowly." A small pause. "And come. Today I will show you the falaj." "I have seen the falaj." Thuraya smiled. "You have seen the surface of it," she said. "Today you will see the rest."


## Scene Two — The Women at the Water


By the time they walked toward the main channel, the sky had committed to a colour. It was pink now — not a romantic pink, not a painting pink, but the working pink of sunrise, a pink that was going to turn into ordinary blue as soon as it had finished with its brief assignment.

Behind them, the village had begun to properly wake. Men's voices — a short Arabic exchange between two neighbours, the specific low register of men who had just finished prayer and were now discussing something about one man's brother's truck. The clinking of a tea glass against a metal tray. The scrape of a wooden door.

And ahead — the falaj. But not the falaj she had seen her first week. Not the falaj of midday, loud with children, with women scrubbing large pots, with the chaos of laundry and life. This was the falaj at its women's hour — the first pale hour, when the water was coldest and cleanest, when the village was still a place of women before it became a place of everyone.

Six women were there. Safiya had not expected six. She had expected one, perhaps two — the morning was young, and she would not have guessed there would be this much activity already. But here they were, each at her own station along the channel, each doing her own task.

The woman nearest them — older, perhaps sixty, with hennaed hands — was filling a clay jar. She lowered it into the channel by a rope, let it sink, let it fill, lifted it, set it on the stone. She did this without watching her hands. She watched, instead, the channel further down — where another woman was washing something, and where the woman below her was washing something else, and where the child at the end was rinsing a cloth. The older woman watched to make sure the child's rinsed cloth was not sending anything upstream.

"Thuraya," Safiya said quietly, "how do they know the order?"

Thuraya looked at her — warmly, without hurry. "The falaj has laws." "Laws?" "Yes. Old laws. Older than any of us." She gestured — a small, economical movement of the wrist. "Drinking water is first. Before anything. Then the water for prayer — for wudu. Then the water for cooking. Then washing yourself — and the women have their time, and the men have theirs. Then washing clothes. Then washing pots. Then — last — the water goes to the " data-m="Plural of falaj; here, the channels that distribute the falaj's water to the individual palm plots and farms" data-s="water channels, irrigation branches" data-x="The aflaj carried the used water to the farms — nothing was ever wasted.">aflaj for the palms and the farms."

Safiya looked at the channel. At the six women. At the ordered, quiet procession of water. At the child at the end, rinsing a cloth that had already been washed further up, into a place where the water would flow next to the farms and not backward to the drinking stations.

"So this —" she said, slowly, "is not the same water. It is the same channel. But it is not the same water." Thuraya looked at her with the approval of a teacher whose student has just answered, on their own, the question that has been stuck in the back of the class for a week. "Yes." "The water is always moving. So the woman upstream is never drinking what the woman downstream has washed." "Yes." "And the laws — they are not written." "No." "They are just… known." "They are known," Thuraya agreed. "By every child, before they are old enough to ask."

Safiya thought back, with something close to embarrassment, to her first week here. To the moment she had looked at the falaj in the middle of the day — women scrubbing pots, children splashing, a goat drinking — and had thought, quietly but completely, this is not clean. She had been wrong. She had been wrong in a way that was bigger than being wrong about cleanliness. She had been wrong about what cleanliness was. The falaj was not dirty. The falaj was the single most disciplined water system she had ever stood beside. It had laws. Older than any law she had studied. Known by every child, before they were old enough to ask.

The older woman with the hennaed hands looked up. Saw them. Smiled. Said something.

Thuraya translated, quietly. "She says — welcome, ya Safiya. She has seen you from her window." "She knows my name?" "Everyone knows your name." A pause. "It is a small village."

The woman spoke again — longer this time. Thuraya translated after, smiling. "She says — you have English skin but your eyes are like an Omani. You look the way you should. And she says — when you are ready, come and learn to carry water. It is not as easy as it looks."

Safiya laughed — quietly, so as not to disturb the fajr hour. "Tell her I would be honoured." "She already knows." "How?" Thuraya looked at her. "Because you came here. At fajr. To watch."

They stood for a while longer in the silver-blue-becoming-pink light. Safiya watched — but differently now. She watched the way one woman dipped her cloth. The way another folded hers, wrung it, folded it again. The way the child at the end looked up every minute or so to check with the older woman that she was doing the rinsing correctly — a check that was not anxious, just reverent.

And she understood, without Thuraya needing to say it, that she had just been given a second threshold. The market had been one. This was another. And this one, she thought, was older.


## Scene Three — John Enters the Water


By mid-morning, the falaj had changed again. The women had gone. The sky was now a plain, committed blue. The children were out — and where children were out, in this village, the falaj was their kingdom.

John and Safiya walked down the path with Thuraya. John had, that morning, slept long and thoroughly and well, and had woken with the specific freshness of a person who had not been awake at fajr, and who was now vaguely aware that his wife had seen something without him. He did not mind. He was, in the morning light, extremely agreeable.

The falaj came into view. And as it came into view, so did the children.

Mansoor. Already in the water. Khalil. Already in the water. Three smaller boys she did not know, also in the water. Ahmed — predictably, precisely, Ahmed-ly — sitting on the stone edge with his feet in the water, reading a small exercise book that he had evidently carried down with him for this purpose. Noura, not in the water, standing at the edge with her arms folded, delivering what appeared to be a lecture on something. Mathila, Rahma, Marwa, and Salima, slightly apart, talking quietly in their usual cluster.

And in the middle of the channel, proprietorially — Malood. Drinking. Of course.

"Safiya," John said, quietly. "Yes?" "I am about to do something I should probably not do." "Yes." "I would like you to remember," he said, already bending down at the edge of the falaj, "that I have been warned."

"John —"

He dipped both hands into the water.

The water hit him with its absolute, uncompromising cold. Not cool. Not refreshing. Cold in the way that mountain water is cold when it has spent its whole brief life in stone channels, beneath the shade of palms, and has never once been warmed by anything. The shock went up his wrists and into his shoulders before he had time to decide how to respond. He responded by washing his face.

He lowered his face to his cupped hands and pressed the cold water against his skin. Once. Twice. A third time. The water ran down his chin and his throat and into the collar of his shirt, and he let it, because at that moment he was not capable of deciding anything else.

He looked up. Every child in the falaj was watching him.

The silence lasted exactly one second. Then Khalil said something in Arabic — fast, low, and clearly delighted — and the children erupted.

Mansoor, standing waist-deep in the channel, pointed at John and shouted: "MR. JOHN! YOU ARE ONE OF US!"

"What did he say?" John asked, wiping his face. Thuraya — who had been about to intervene, and had now decided that the moment was beyond intervention — translated with a small, helpless smile. "He said: You are one of us."

John looked at the children. At Mansoor, waist-deep, grinning. At Khalil, already inventing a song about this. At Ahmed, looking up from his book with the particular expression of a child whose dignity has survived because he has chosen not to be involved. At Malood, still drinking, unmoved.

"Safiya," he said, "I feel… fresh." "You feel wet," she corrected. "Those are not the same." "They are," he said, "today."

And the strange thing was — he was not wrong. There was a quality to the water on his skin that tap water had never had. A mineral cleanness. The faint, clean taste that water has when it has spent a long time inside mountains, talking to stone. He could feel it on his skin even after he had wiped it off. The skin tingled, faintly, the way skin tingles after something that was not cold at all.

A woman's voice — from further down the path, one he did not recognise — called out a long sentence in Arabic. Then another voice joined. Then a third. The older women on the path had seen him.

"What are they saying?" John asked. Thuraya listened. She smiled. Then she hesitated — which was unusual for Thuraya, who did not hesitate often. "They are saying…" "Yes?" "That the English man is tall. And that you have a kind face. And one of them is asking who is your wife." She paused. "And another is saying — that wife is very lucky, because that man has now washed in our falaj, and a man who washes in the falaj belongs a little to the village, and a little less to his own country."

John went very still. Not from discomfort. From the slow, sincere recognition that he had just been given something that was not easy to give. He looked at the women down the path. He raised his hand slightly — not waving, just acknowledging. The oldest of the women smiled, nodded once, and walked on.

Safiya, standing beside him, said nothing. She only, very lightly, placed her hand against the small of his back — the way one does, without fanfare, when one has seen that something has happened to a person one loves. He felt her hand. He did not turn.

Thuraya, watching all of this, said nothing for a long time. And then, quietly, more to herself than to either of them — "It is easier after this." "After what?" Safiya asked. Thuraya turned to her. Her face was entirely open. "After you become someone the village can tell stories about. Before that, you are a stranger. After that, you are a character." "And John is now a character?" "John," Thuraya said, "is now the man who washed his face in the falaj. The village will tell that story for ten years."

"For ten years." "At least." John, still wet, blinked. "Is that good?" "That is excellent," Thuraya said.


## Scene Four — Why We Say Good


The inevitable response to all of this — once John had dried himself with the cloth Thuraya, prepared for anything, had somehow produced from a small basket — was that a lesson must be given. Not because they had planned one. Because the children were now gathered, the morning was beautiful, and to let such a moment pass without teaching something would have been, to John, a professional embarrassment.

They moved from the falaj to the large tree that stood a short distance from it. A ghaf tree — old, patient, its branches wide enough to hold shade for twenty children. The ground beneath it was packed hard from decades of sitting. It had been, Safiya realised, a classroom long before any classroom had a wall.

The children sat. Mansoor, damp, sat too close to Ahmed. Ahmed moved away. Mansoor followed. Ahmed sighed — the sigh of a nine-year-old scholar who has, once again, been denied the dignity of solitude.

John knelt. He took a small stick from the ground. He looked at Thuraya. She nodded — a small, teacherly nod: go ahead.

He drew, in the dust, two words.

HELLOGOOD MORNING

He pointed to the first. "Hello." The children repeated. "Hello." He pointed to the second. "Good morning." "Good morning."

Mansoor said it too loud. Khalil said it in a funny voice. Ahmed said it exactly — as though he had saved up the words and had now chosen to spend them correctly. Mathila whispered it to Rahma first, to make sure she had it right, and only then said it out loud.

Then Noura, who had been waiting — Noura always waited, because Noura always had a question, and Noura always wanted the spotlight for the question — raised her hand.

"Yes, Noura?"

She considered. Her face took on the particular look of a ten-year-old who has thought about a thing long enough to know that the thing is worth asking about. "Why we say good?"

Silence.

John blinked. "Why…?" "Yes. Why we say good? Why not just — morning?"

He had, he realised, never been asked this question. He had taught English to adults in London, to children in Sheffield, to two very tired businessmen in Frankfurt. Not one of them had ever asked why one said good morning. It was one of those phrases that survived in the language the way certain stones survive in a wall — nobody remembers who put them there, and everybody has agreed, without agreeing, that the wall needs them. But here was a ten-year-old Omani girl, sitting under a ghaf tree, in the dust, at ten in the morning, asking him the single best question about English that any student had ever asked him. And he did not know the answer.

He looked at Thuraya. Thuraya smiled. The smile did not help him. The smile said — this is your question now.

He turned back to Noura. "That is," he said, slowly, "a very good question."

A murmur of appreciation went through the children. Mansoor said, "She asked a good question!" — and was gratified by his own cleverness for noticing it.

"But," John continued, honestly, "I do not know the answer."

The children went quiet. Teachers, they had been taught, knew answers. Teachers who did not know answers were an unexpected category. Ahmed looked at him, specifically, with something close to alarm.

"But," John added, "I will tell you what I think."

He looked up — at the tree, at the village, at the water threading between the houses, at Thuraya, at Safiya, at the ten small faces who were now watching him and waiting for him to be honest with them.

"I think," he said, "we say 'good morning' because saying only 'morning' is not enough. Morning is just — a time. A fact. It says: the sun is up. But good morning says: the sun is up, and I am glad about it. And I am glad that you are here too."

He paused. He was not sure if he had done this well. "So when you say 'good morning' to someone," he continued, "you are not telling them the time. You are wishing them something. You are wishing that their morning will be good. You are giving them — a small gift. Just at the beginning of the day. A small wish that things go well for them."

Silence. Noura considered. Her face went through the specific stages of a ten-year-old considering a philosophical answer: first skeptical, then thoughtful, then surprised, then — almost against her will — pleased.

"So every morning," she said slowly, "we are giving a small gift?" "Yes." "To everyone we say it to?" "Yes."

She nodded. She turned to the child beside her — a small boy she did not know — and said, very deliberately, "Good morning." The boy looked at her. He looked confused. She said it again, more firmly. "GOOD MORNING." Thuraya, stifling a laugh, translated, in Arabic: she is giving you a gift; you must take it. The boy, understanding suddenly, smiled — and said, in careful English, "Good morning."

The children went around the circle then — without being asked, without being prompted — saying good morning to the child beside them, one by one. Small gifts. Handed around the ghaf tree in the dust, at ten in the morning.

Safiya, watching from the edge, realised that she had just watched her husband become a better teacher than he had been twenty minutes earlier. He had not known an answer. He had said so. And then — without panic, without pretending — he had thought about it, and told the children what he actually believed. Which was not a grammatical rule. Which was not a textbook sentence. Which was a small, true thing about how language works, and why people say the things they say. This, she thought, was what teaching was. When it was any good at all. Not the answering of questions. The honouring of them.

She looked at Thuraya. Thuraya was already looking at her. They did not speak. They did not need to.


## Scene Five — Malood Takes the Page


It would have been, by any measure, a good place to end the morning. The children were quiet. Noura was still turning the idea of good morning over in her head. John had, for the first time in weeks, the look of a man who had surprised himself with his own usefulness. Safiya had her notebook open and was writing something. Thuraya was sitting cross-legged beside Ahmed, reading the exercise book over his shoulder — which Ahmed permitted, because she was Thuraya, but would not have permitted for anyone else.

A breeze moved through the ghaf. The leaves rustled — the small, dry, specific rustle of desert leaves, which sounded like nothing else in the world. Somewhere, a dove resumed its call. The morning, having handed over its gifts, settled into its middle hour.

And into all of this — like a small, horned comet — Malood.

He had been, at some point, drinking in the falaj. Then he had been, apparently, exploring the path. Then he had decided — through whatever mysterious calculus governed his small goat brain — that the most interesting object in the entire village was, currently, the paper on John's bag.

He arrived at a trot. He jumped — lightly, with improbable grace — onto John's bag. He seized the corner of a piece of paper in his teeth. He ran. The whole operation took perhaps four seconds.

For approximately two of those seconds, nobody moved. The children simply watched — with the specific stunned calm of an audience at a play who have just witnessed something unexpected, and who are waiting for the play to tell them how to respond. John looked at his bag. Looked at where the paper had been. Looked at Malood, now halfway down the path, paper flapping in his mouth.

"MALOOD."

The village, as one, came back to life.

Mansoor shouted something. Khalil burst into laughter. Ahmed sighed — a long, philosophical sigh — and closed his exercise book with the air of a scholar whose research has, once again, been interrupted by the arrival of a goat. Noura stood up with great authority. "I will catch him!" "Noura, no —" But Noura, at ten, was already running.

Then Khalil. Then the three smaller boys. Then Mansoor, who had been, until half a second ago, reclining. Then the Marwa–Rahma–Salima triangle, still in their triangle but now a moving triangle. Then Mathila. Then Ahmed, who had decided, with enormous reluctance, that scholarship could not keep him out of this.

John stood. "Safiya." "Yes?" "That paper had — I had written — it was my —" "Your lesson plan." "My lesson plan." "For what?" "For the afternoon." "John." "Yes?" "Did you learn nothing last week?"

He looked at her. "Apparently," he said, "I have not."

And he, too, began to run.

Safiya remained under the tree with Thuraya. They watched, the two of them — the goat ahead, the children behind, the Englishman at the rear, all of them in a long ragged line disappearing around the bend. The laughter carried back clearly. The sound of small feet. The occasional adult voice — John's — shouting, in English, words that Malood did not speak and had, by any reasonable measure, no interest in learning.

"Thuraya," Safiya said. "Yes?" "How does one small goat disrupt an entire village this consistently?" Thuraya smiled. "He is not one person's small goat." "No. I know." "He is the village's." "And a village's goat is everyone's problem." "And everyone's joy."

They watched the dust settle along the bend where the running crowd had disappeared. In the middle distance, a woman doing something in a doorway had stopped doing it — and was now laughing, hand over her mouth, at the procession that had just passed her house.

Safiya thought about this. About how the day had gone — the pre-dawn falaj, the women, the order of the water, John's cold-water baptism, the ghaf tree and the gift of good morning. And now this. A goat carrying an English lesson plan down a village road, pursued by ten children and one red-faced Englishman. There was a shape to the day that she had not chosen, that none of them had chosen, that the village had simply provided. And the day's shape was, on reflection, exactly what it should have been.

From the bend in the path, the first small figure reappeared. It was Ahmed. Walking back calmly. Carrying, in both hands, what remained of the paper — which was, admittedly, slightly less paper than it had been when Malood had first taken it.

He arrived at the tree. He handed the paper to John's place beneath the tree, without ceremony. He sat back down. He opened his exercise book. He resumed reading, precisely where he had left off.

Safiya and Thuraya looked at him. "Ahmed," Thuraya said quietly. "Yes, teacher?" "How did you get it?" Ahmed considered the question. He turned a page of his exercise book. "I walked," he said, "in the other direction."

"…The other direction?" "To where Malood was going. Not where he was." A pause. "Goats always go back to qit. There is qit at Sadoo's." "You knew he would go to Sadoo's?" "Every time," Ahmed said, "he goes to Sadoo's."

Thuraya looked at Safiya. Safiya looked at Thuraya. Both looked back at Ahmed — who had, without effort, just demonstrated the fundamental difference between the children who chased and the child who thought.

"Ahmed," Safiya said. "Yes?" "Will you be a teacher when you are older?" He considered. "I will be a teacher now," he said, "sometimes. And I will be a student now, sometimes. Then I will see."

Thuraya covered her mouth. Safiya shook her head slowly, smiling. And from around the bend came the first of the returning runners — hot, laughing, defeated — explaining in excited Arabic what had happened at Sadoo's shop, which, from the sound of it, had involved Malood, a pile of dried dates, an older woman with a broom, and, against all reasonable expectation, a second stolen piece of paper.


## Scene Six — The River That Is Not a River


The sun had moved. It was now the sun of late afternoon — lower, warmer in colour, slanting in long gold lines through the palm fronds and across the paths. The village, having spent its midday loudly, was entering its quieter hour.

Safiya and John walked back toward their house. Slowly. The way two people walk when they have done enough for one day and are looking forward, with quiet urgency, to a chair and a cup of tea. Thuraya had gone home — to her own mother, to her own rooms, to the private part of her day that Safiya had begun to understand she should never ask about but should simply respect.

Their path took them beside the falaj. They passed the spot where the women had been at fajr. They passed the spot where the children had been at mid-morning. They passed the ghaf tree under which the lesson had been given. Each spot, now, held the faint echo of the hour it had belonged to. The channel was quieter now. The water still moved — Safiya was beginning to understand that the water never, ever stopped moving — but its sound was now the only sound. The late afternoon falaj was the falaj alone with itself.

"John," she said. "Yes?" "I want to sit here for a moment." "Here?" "Here."

They sat. On the low stone edge of the channel. Close enough to hear the water, close enough for Safiya to dip the tips of her fingers in if she wanted to. She did not, yet.

The water in the channel had, over the course of one day, been many things. It had been drinking water for a child at fajr. It had been wudu for an old man before prayer. It had been the water a woman filled her kitchen with. It had been the water she bathed in at the majaza. It had been the water she rinsed her clothes in. It had been, briefly, the water in which an English teacher had washed his face and become — by an old village reckoning — a little less English. And it was now the water that would flow, in its last hour, out to the palms and the farms and the small green patches at the edge of the village, to feed the roots that would feed the trees that would feed the people who had, in turn, drunk and washed and prayed with it this morning. Nothing was lost. Nothing was wasted. The water simply circulated, patient and ordered, through the lives of everyone it passed.

"John," she said again. "Mmm?" "Do you remember what I said the first day we saw this?" "You said — it cannot be clean." "Yes." "And you were wrong." "Yes. But not in the way I thought I was wrong."

He waited. He knew this voice. This was the voice Safiya used when she had understood something that had taken her a while to understand, and she was about to try to say it out loud for the first time, which meant he should be quiet and let her.
