"In London," she said, slowly, "clean means — separated. The drinking water is in one pipe. The washing water is in another. The water for the toilet is in a third. The dirty water goes away, to a place we do not think about. We are clean because we do not see the cycle. We have hidden it."

She dipped one finger in the water. Lifted it. Watched a drop run back down into the channel.

"Here, clean means — ordered. The water is the same water. There is only one channel. But there is an order. The drinking comes first. The washing comes after. The farm comes last. And because everyone knows the order, everyone trusts it. The cleanliness is not in the separation. It is in the agreement."

John looked at the water. At the long narrow channel going down toward the palms. At the village at their backs, whose agreement this was.

"It is not dirtier than London," he said, slowly. "No. It is cleaner. Differently cleaner. We just had to learn what clean meant." "And the falaj taught us." "The falaj," she said, "has been teaching people this for three thousand years."

They sat for a while longer. The water moved. The light lowered. Somewhere far off, the first hints of a voice preparing to call Maghrib began to warm in the mosque below.

"John." "Yes?" "I thought we moved to another country." "We did." "No," she said. "We moved to another world."

He did not answer. He only reached for her hand and held it, the way he had held it the afternoon her father had given her the pen, long before any of this had begun — held it with both of his, and held it long enough for something to pass between them that did not need a word.

That night, back in the small clay house, under the single qandeel, Safiya opened her notebook. She did not write a list. She did not write a summary. She wrote three sentences. Slowly, carefully, with the Cartier pen her father had given her, in the handwriting she reserved for things she intended to keep:

Today, I saw a river that is not a river.I saw children who learn faster than they know.And I saw a goat who does not belong to us, but lives with us — and who, I am beginning to suspect, lives with the whole village, because the whole village belongs to each other in a way no one has yet explained to me, but which the falaj has already, patiently, begun to teach.

John read the page over her shoulder. He did not say anything. He only, gently, took the pen from her hand, wrote beneath her three sentences one more, in his own handwriting — and closed the notebook.

What he had written was: Tomorrow, we teach again.

And somewhere, on the bisat in the corner of the room, Malood — who had, improbably, been permitted inside for this one evening, and who had availed himself of the privilege completely — was already asleep. His small flank rose and fell. One ear twitched, once, as though dreaming of qit. The falaj, outside, continued. It would continue, Safiya understood now, whether anyone was awake to hear it or not. That was the point of it. That was always the point of it.


# Palm Six — A Fan, A Bird, and a Lesson on Tongues



## Scene One — The Dust That Follows


The afternoon in Al-Qurawashiyah had a temperature that words in English did not quite fit. Warm was not enough. Hot was too aggressive — this heat did not attack, it simply arrived and stayed, the way a guest arrives and stays. The sun was high. Even the shadows looked tired, shortened beneath the clay houses that threw them, as though even the shadows had decided to wait this hour out.

The small clay houses were sleeping under the heat. That was how Sophia thought of it now — sleeping, not empty, not abandoned. A house in this country at two in the afternoon was doing what a house was supposed to do in this hour: holding its cool inside its walls, keeping the family quiet, and breathing out through its open door only the low murmur of people who had agreed, collectively, to do nothing until the sun eased.

Only the sound of birds and faraway voices filled the air. Sophia stood in front of the simple house she shared with John, holding a thin carpet over a low wooden beam, and she was trying — with the particular persistence of a woman who had been raised to believe that carpets could be made clean — to beat the dust out of it.

The dust was winning.

Each strike of her palm against the carpet released a pale, opinionated cloud into the air around her. The cloud rose, considered its options, and — as though insulted — settled straight back down onto the carpet from which it had come. Some of it went higher. It found her hair. It found her neck. It found, improbably, the inside of her ear.

She lowered the carpet. She looked at her hands. They were grey-brown where they had been the colour of hands. She turned her palms up, slowly — as though they belonged to someone else, a woman she was meeting for the first time.

"This dust is not normal," she said. Not loudly. Mostly to herself. "I am sure it follows me."

John was sitting on a wooden box against the wall, a shoe between his knees, an awl in his hand, and an expression on his face that Sophia had come to recognise as the expression he wore when he was doing something that did not require the help of any woman, thank you very much, even if the woman was his wife and the task was clearly going badly.

He looked up. Slowly. With the considered calm of a man who had been waiting for exactly this sentence so that he could deliver his reply.

"This is not dust," he said. "This is history."

Sophia lowered her hand. She looked at him. She looked at the carpet. She looked at her hands again — which were, by any objective measure, dirty.

"John." "Yes." "I am not," she said, with the specific, measured, wifely patience of a woman who had been married long enough to know exactly how many seconds John's jokes deserved — "wearing history."

John considered this. He placed the awl down. He looked at her — at the dust in her hair, at the grey-brown hands, at the carpet that had just mocked her — and, for perhaps the first time that afternoon, made the correct decision. He did not reply.

A breeze moved through the compound. It was a hot breeze. It did not cool. It only redistributed the heat, the way a spoon redistributes hot soup without cooling it. From somewhere down the path, a dove called — slowly, with long patience between each call, as though it too was conserving energy.

And then, into this thick, heavy, dust-filled afternoon silence — a sound.

Laughing. Loud. From far away.

Then shouting. Then more laughing. Then someone screaming — a short, bright scream that had nothing injured in it, the specific scream that belongs only to play. Then more laughing again.

Sophia lifted her head. She looked down the road — the white road, the dust-road, the road that led from the small cluster of houses toward the open sandy area between the village quarters.

"Something is happening."

John stood up. He set the shoe aside. He brushed his hands on his trousers — which did nothing, since his trousers were also dusty — and looked in the same direction.

"In this village," he said, with the quiet, settled certainty of a man who had lived here now for several weeks and had learned one thing about it beyond question, "something is always happening."

He offered her his hand. She took it — dusty hand in dusty hand — and together, slowly, with the specific pace of two people who had not been going anywhere ten seconds ago and were now going somewhere, they walked down the path toward the sound.


## Scene Two — The Girls Who Were Flying


The sound grew as they walked. It did not grow the way sound grows when you approach a single person speaking — in an even, predictable curve. It grew the way sound grows when you approach a flock. In bursts. In overlapping voices. In the particular layered texture of collective joy.

They reached the edge of the open sandy area between the houses. Sophia stopped.

Perhaps eight girls — she did not count them accurately; they were moving too fast — between the ages of thirteen and sixteen, she guessed. Their dresses were simple and colourful — reds, greens, deep oranges, one a particular shade of saffron that caught the high sun and seemed, for an instant each time its wearer turned, to be on fire. Their hair was tied back in different ways — some in long single braids, some in two, some simply gathered loosely. And their energy was — Sophia could think of no other word — abundant. It did not fit in the space. It spilled out, loud and long, in every direction.

Two long lines had been drawn in the sand — simple parallel lines, perhaps five steps apart. One girl stood in the middle between them. The others were moving — running, pivoting, jumping, feinting — trying to cross from one side of the lines to the other without being touched by the girl in the middle. The girl in the middle was very fast. She was also laughing. Everyone was laughing.

"Turn! Turn! Jump!" one of the girls shouted — in Arabic, but with a cadence so clear that Sophia, who did not understand the words, understood the instruction.

Another girl — slightly older, a long green dress, braided hair — ran. She jumped. She almost fell — her body went sideways in a wild, improvised recovery — caught herself with a hand on the sand — laughed out loud at her own near-disaster — and sprinted away before the girl in the middle could touch her.

Sophia watched with wide eyes. The game was moving faster than she could decode it. She could see the two lines. She could see the girl in the middle. She could see the others crossing. But the rules — the specific rules — seemed to be known by everyone except her.

"This looks dangerous," she said.

John, beside her, tilted his head. He watched for several seconds. He watched with the particular quality of attention that Sophia knew he reserved for things he was about to call educational — a bad sign, in her experience.

"No," he said. "This looks educational."

"John." "Yes?" "Everything you find educational, I find dangerous." "I have noticed."

Behind them — a small sound. The soft, specific shuffle of sandals on packed sand.

Thuraya. A small woven basket on her arm. The same unhurried, composed arrival that she had been practising since Sophia had known her.

"You found the girls," she said, smiling. The smile had something in it — affection for them, some old memory of her own — that Sophia would understand only later.

"They are flying," Sophia said. It was the only accurate word she had.

"They are playing Leteg," Thuraya said. She said the name with the soft warmth of someone returning a word to a familiar mouth. "We all played this when we were young."

The phrase when we were young moved through Sophia more slowly than Thuraya had intended it to. It was not when I was young. It was when we were young. A plural. A whole generation of women, somewhere in their shared past, running between these same two lines, being told turn! turn! jump! by each other. Thuraya had been one of them.

One of the girls — slightly taller than the others, with a confident face and a gap between her front teeth — saw them. She stopped in the middle of the game, unbothered, and called out: "Come! Come play!"

The other girls paused, turned, and immediately took up the call. "Come! Come! Come!" — a small, cheerful chorus.

Sophia looked left and right as though perhaps they were calling someone else. She pointed at herself.

"Me?" "Yes, you!"

She looked at Thuraya. A silent, pleading look.

"I think this is a bad idea."

Thuraya considered this. Her face took on the particular composed expression of a person who has already decided the matter.

"It is a very good idea."


## Scene Three — The Game is Honest


Sophia bent down and took off her shoes.

She did it slowly. She did it with the particular slowness of a woman who has agreed to do something she has not agreed to want to do. The sand was hot against the soles of her feet. Not painfully hot — the hour had passed its worst — but warm enough to register, to tell her, every second, exactly where her feet were.

"This," she said, straightening, to no one in particular, "is a very bad decision."

The girls laughed. The girl with the gap-tooth smile came forward — Safiya did not yet know her name, but she had the clear bearing of a girl the other girls looked at first — and took Sophia gently by the elbow. She led her to the line. She pointed. She said something in Arabic — fast and warm and absolutely incomprehensible — but her hands did most of the work. Stand here. Wait. When she moves, you move. Go there. Do not touch the line.

Sophia nodded. She understood perhaps thirty percent of what had been communicated, and had decided, with a kind of resigned bravery, that thirty percent was more than she had received in most of her interactions in this village and would have to be enough.

"Now jump," one of the girls said — and the word jump, in English, landed on Sophia like a rescue.

"Oh — yes. Jump. I can jump."

She jumped.

Wrong.

The moment her feet left the ground, seven voices around her shouted at her — not angrily, but with the specific, cheerful urgency of people trying to rescue a novice in real time. "No! No! Not yet!" "Wait!" "Not like that!"

She landed. She had jumped too early. She had also, somehow, landed on the line — which was apparently a significant offence, the specific gravity of which was not clear to her but was, from the volume of the response, unambiguous to everyone else.

She jumped again. Determined this time. Full commitment.

Wrong again.

This time the laughter was unconcealed. The girls around her collapsed — into each other's shoulders, onto the sand, one girl actually sat down because she could no longer stand upright. But there was no cruelty in it. Only the specific delighted laughter of girls watching an adult attempt something a child would do better. It was a tender laughter. Sophia understood the tenderness even as she understood the laughter.

"This game hates me," she said — not really complaining, more announcing a fact to the sky.

John, from the edge, arms folded, watching with the insufferable calm of a man whose wife was losing publicly and who had had the sense to stay off the sand himself, said — with the quiet, settled clarity of a man who had just thought of the correct sentence — "No. The game is honest."

Everyone heard it. Even the girls, who understood only half. Thuraya looked at John with surprise — and then with the small, approving nod of a teacher who has heard her student say something unexpected and correct.

The game was honest. It did not reward effort alone. It did not reward good intention. It rewarded only the thing it was built to reward — the exact timing, the exact weight of foot, the exact reading of the girl in the middle. Every one of Sophia's failures was a completely fair failure. The game had explained itself to her, each time, without words. It was, she understood now, a teacher.

She tried again. She concentrated this time. She watched the girl in the middle — not the line, not her own feet, but the middle girl. She watched the middle girl's eyes, her weight, the tilt of her shoulder. The middle girl leaned left. Sophia went right.

Her foot cleared the first line. Her body turned. Her other foot landed cleanly between the lines. She pivoted — not gracefully, but genuinely — and her third step crossed the second line.

She had done it. She had, against any reasonable probability, crossed the lines without being touched, without touching the line itself, and without falling over.

She stopped. She looked down. She looked up.

She raised both her hands.

"I did it!"

The girls clapped. Not politely — genuinely, loudly, the way children clap when a child younger than them succeeds. The girl with the gap-tooth smile came forward and placed her hand briefly on Sophia's arm. "Good. Good."

Sophia looked at John. John was smiling — not insufferably this time, but plainly. Thuraya was smiling the particular proud smile of a teacher.

And Sophia, standing in the sand, her hair coming loose, a thin line of sweat along her hairline, dust on her skirt — understood, not in her head but somewhere lower, that she had just been admitted to something.


## Scene Four — Darwazat Mutrah


Then one of the girls — a smaller one, perhaps thirteen, with very quick feet — shouted into the aftermath of Sophia's triumph:

"Now we play Darwazat Mutrah!"

The name entered the afternoon like a starting pistol. Before Sophia could ask what that was, the girls were already moving.

They began to run — in no direction that a newcomer could follow. One girl ran toward a house. One ran toward a tree. Two ran in the opposite direction from each other and then, apparently, from themselves. Someone shouted something. Someone else answered. The middle girl — who had a moment ago been between the Leteg lines — suddenly had the role of pursuer.

Someone fell.

Someone screamed.

Someone laughed.

All three of these things happened to three different girls, simultaneously, with no apparent connection between them.

And then, from nowhere — Mansoor.

Sophia did not see where he had come from. He had not been in the sandy area. He had not been walking down the path behind them. He had been somewhere else in the village, presumably, moments ago, and now, without any intermediate step — no approaching, no calling out — he was among them, already running, already shouting, already integrated into the game as though he had always been part of it.

"I AM FAST!" he shouted, to no one and everyone.

He ran perhaps three seconds. The sand was deep. His feet were small. His enthusiasm exceeded his coordination by a clear margin. On the fourth second, he fell — spectacularly, sideways, with his arms out, landing in the sand in a small soft explosion of dust and shouting.

Two of the girls rushed to him — not worried, just checking. He sat up. There was sand in his hair. There was sand in his mouth. He spat — dramatically — and then laughed at himself, which was, Sophia had noticed before, his one reliable grace.

The game continued around him. He rejoined it. He fell again. Nobody was particularly surprised.

Sophia, meanwhile, had made a tactical decision. She had seen the direction of the running — seen the chaos spreading outward — and she had, with the good sense of a forty-year-old woman who had already that afternoon won a game by reading another girl's shoulder — decided not to run anywhere in particular.

She ran to a tree at the edge of the sandy area. She put her back against it. She folded her arms.

"I am not a teacher today," she announced, in the general direction of the game. "I am a tree."

The game stopped. Every girl stopped. Mansoor stopped. Even the dust paused, briefly, in the air around her.

And then — very slowly at first, then completely — every person in the sandy area burst out laughing. The laughter was not at her. It was with her. It was a specific kind of laughter that welcomed the joke into the game. The girl with the gap-tooth smile said something in Arabic that Thuraya, standing at the edge, translated an instant later — "she said: then we will play around the tree."

And they did. They played around her. Sophia stood against the trunk with her arms folded, and the girls and Mansoor wove around her, past her, almost through her, running and shouting and falling and laughing, and she — still — did not move.

She had become part of the game without playing it. She had found, without meaning to, the exemption the game always secretly has. She was the tree.

After what felt like a long time — and had probably been ten minutes — the girls' energy finally began to ebb. One by one, they slowed. One by one, they walked to the edges of the sandy area and sat down on the warm ground. Mansoor sat last, on his back, his arms and legs out like a small dusty star. The game was over — because the afternoon itself had decided it was over.

Sophia detached herself from the tree. She walked, slowly, to the edge where the girls sat, and lowered herself onto the ground beside them. Her dress, when she sat, puffed a small cloud of pale dust into the air.

She looked at it. She looked at her hands. She looked at her legs, which were — it was clear now — thoroughly, comprehensively dirty.

"I am not a tree," she announced, to the same audience, with the same dry delivery. "I am a very dirty tree."

The girls laughed again. One of them — she had not seen which — said, in clear, practical English: "You must wash."

"Where?" Sophia asked — because she lived, after all, in a mud house with a jug of water and a small basin, and none of those had the power to solve what had happened to her.

The girls all turned and pointed, as one, in the same direction.

"In the falaj."


## Scene Five — The Majaza


Sophia's eyes — despite the dust, despite the heat, despite the small tiredness settling into her legs — shone.

"Nice! I want to swim!"

The response was immediate. Instant. Total. Every girl, as one, in one unified voice of horror:

"NOOOOO!"

Even Mansoor, still on his back, lifted his head and contributed an "Ooooh!" of his own, though he was clearly not sure why.

Sophia, taken aback, turned to Thuraya — who had, as she so often did, arrived at exactly the moment her services were needed. Thuraya was laughing. She laughed properly — head tipped slightly, hand over her mouth, the small shoulders shaking.

"Not like that," she said, when she had recovered. "Women do not swim in the falaj. Women go to the majaza."

"The majaza." "Yes." "What is it?" "You will see."

And Sophia, who by now had learned what you will see meant in this country — that no explanation was going to be offered, that the thing itself would do the explaining — put on her shoes, said goodbye to the tree, and followed.

They walked — Thuraya, Sophia, and three or four of the older girls who had elected themselves her escort — along a path that ran beside the falaj, past houses that Sophia had never quite looked at properly before. The path was narrow. It ended at a low, unassuming stone building — small, windowless at first glance, with a single wooden door painted a deep blue.

One of the girls pushed the door open. She gestured for Sophia to enter.

Sophia stepped inside — and stopped. The room was not a room, exactly. It was a chamber, perhaps four steps wide, four steps long. The walls were made of the same warm clay as every other wall in the village. But the floor — the floor was stone. And running through the centre of the floor, in a shallow trough, was a small, swift channel of clear water. It entered at one wall, crossed the floor, and exited at the other. The falaj itself, routed through the room. And above — the roof was open. Not entirely — a low wall ran around the top for privacy — but open enough that the sky, still pale with the afternoon, was visible directly above.

It was, in a single elegant design, a women's bath-house older than any plumbing she had ever known.

Inside were three women. One was older — perhaps sixty. Two were younger — perhaps her own age, or slightly younger. They looked up. They did not seem startled. One of them said something to Thuraya — short, warm, the familiar exchange of women who had shared this space more than once before.

Then they turned to Sophia. They smiled.

And the older woman said, in Arabic — Thuraya translated in a soft murmur — "Come. We will help."

And Sophia, for the first time in several weeks, found that she did not know how to protest. She did not know how to be polite. She did not know how to say no, no, I can manage. The women were already moving — unhurried, practical — and they were moving with the quiet certainty of women who had done this for other women many times before.

They helped her wash.

They used sidr. The older woman had a small bowl of it — crushed, dried, pale green, smelling of something faintly floral and faintly medicinal. She worked it into Sophia's hands, into her arms, across the back of her neck. The sidr foamed gently — not like soap, more like a gentle, herbal lather that smelled like a garden more than a bathroom.

They used sandal. Small chips of sandalwood, ground to a fragrant paste, applied in a small circle to the inside of each wrist. Once it was on, the smell was there for the rest of the day. Sophia could feel it moving past her nose — deep and warm and sweet — every time she turned her head.

And then — while she was still holding her arms a little away from her body, not quite sure what came next — the older woman produced a small stick of kohl and held it up, questioningly.

Sophia looked at it. She looked at the women. She looked at Thuraya. They were all waiting — quietly, patiently — to see what she would say.

She nodded.

The older woman leaned in. She was very close. Sophia could see the small lines around her eyes, the steadiness of her hand. She drew the kohl, very carefully, along Sophia's lower eyelid. Then the upper. Then the other eye.

When she was done, one of the younger women produced a small mirror — a round hand-held mirror with a cloth-backed frame — and held it up.

Sophia looked.

A different woman looked back. Her eyes were darker — not in colour, but in presence. They had become the main thing in her face. The kohl ran in a thin clean line along each lid, slightly thicker at the outer corners, and the result was — Sophia did not quite know how to describe it — ancient. She looked like someone who might have lived in this village a hundred years ago and known exactly what she was doing.

"Do I look beautiful?" she asked.

The women looked at each other. Then smiled — in a strange, knowing, slightly ambiguous way that Sophia, even in the moment, felt she should perhaps have paid more attention to.

But they did not say anything.


## Scene Six — The Bee, the Kohl, and the Goat


Outside the majaza, John was waiting.

He had been waiting for some time. He had exhausted his patience for standing, had exhausted his patience for sitting, had exhausted his patience for leaning, and had arrived at a state that could only be described as idle. He was standing under a small tree — a neem, perhaps — watching the path, picking up a small stone from the ground, throwing it against a wall, picking up another.

Then, without warning —

"AAAAAA!"

The scream came from John. It was not a dignified scream. It was a stripped, unprepared, animal scream — the scream of a man who has just discovered, without warning, that something small and fast has found the inside of his trouser leg.

He began to run. Not toward anything. Not away from anything. Simply running, in a loose circle, slapping at his own leg, jumping occasionally, uttering a sequence of English sounds that had long since left any language he had taught.

He fell.

He stood.

He ran again.

A small nest — it had been there, apparently, all along, close to the ground, near the base of the neem tree — had been disturbed by his idle wandering. And a bee — possibly a wasp; the distinction, in that moment, was entirely academic — had disagreed with him, firmly, on the inside of his calf.

"MY LEG! MY LEG!" he shouted.

Thuraya, hearing this from inside the majaza, came out at a full run. "A bee! A bee? A wasp?" "I don't KNOW!" "Is it still in the trouser?" "IT IS NOT IN THE TROUSER. I HOPE." "You need the hospital!" "I am fine!" A pause. "I think."

He was, in fact, not fine. But he was attempting a kind of Britishness about it, which involved standing very still, pretending the leg did not hurt, and looking, with increasing urgency, at the door of the majaza.

At precisely this moment — the door opened.

Sophia came out.

John stopped.

Thuraya stopped.

Every girl on the path, every woman, every goat — everyone in any line of sight — stopped.

The kohl, which had been applied with such precision by the older woman in the majaza, had met the heat of the afternoon — and the heat of the heat — and the faint, fine sheen of sweat that Sophia had not yet wiped from her own forehead and cheeks. It had melted. Not a little. Completely. Two long dark streaks ran from the corners of her eyes down her cheeks. Two more thinner lines ran down toward her chin. The upper lines had been disturbed by her own hand — she had, unthinkingly, rubbed her eye — and now ran in a smudged dark mask across her forehead and the bridge of her nose.

She did not know this.

She stood in the doorway of the majaza in her washed dress, her sandal-scented wrists, her clean hair — entirely proud, faintly expectant, waiting for her husband's response.

She looked like a very angry ghost.

Or possibly a very tired monster.

John looked at her.

For one beat, his face held the question: is something wrong? Is she unwell?

Then he laughed.

Then he laughed harder. He laughed until he could not stand. The bee-stung leg, the burning pain, the whole question of the hospital — all of it, temporarily erased from his body — disappeared into the laughing. He sat down on the ground. He fell backward. He lay in the dust, laughing with his whole chest, laughing so hard that Thuraya, concerned about the bee sting, had to kneel beside him and check that this was not some new, laughter-related medical crisis.

Thuraya looked at Sophia. She looked at the kohl. She looked at John, wheezing on the ground.

Then she laughed too.

Sophia, alarmed, touched her own face. She looked at her fingers. They came away black.

One of the girls, quickly — kindly — produced the small mirror from the majaza.

Sophia looked.

She screamed.

"I am going back to wash! I wash my face! I wash my hands! I wash EVERYTHING!"

She turned, marched back through the blue door, and disappeared into the majaza for what Thuraya would later estimate to be approximately twenty additional minutes of thorough washing.

Later — much later, when Sophia had emerged, de-kohled, re-composed, and the afternoon had begun to tilt into evening — they walked home. Slowly. Past the falaj, which was quieter now. A small boy — they did not know him — was splashing in the open channel, entirely at home, supremely unworried about germs or the specific order of water or anything else.

John looked at him. "He swims in the falaj."

Sophia looked at the boy. She touched her clean face. She looked at her clean hands.

"I wash my hands in the falaj," she said, very firmly. "I do not swim."

They walked on.

They reached their house. The sun was lowering now — that specific long-gold hour that this country did so well. They opened the door. They stepped inside.

And into their small clean house, through the still-open door, ran a small horned body — fast, determined, and unmistakable.

"MALOOD."

John, forgetting even his bee-stung leg, spun on his heel. "WHY does this goat love our house?!"

Malood did not answer. Malood had already reached the small wooden table. Malood was already — with the practised efficiency of a thief who had done this before, and would do it again, and who had, apparently, no intention of ever explaining himself — eating a piece of paper.

Sophia ran after him. "MALOOD!" The goat, with the paper still half in his mouth, evaded her neatly and left the house the way he had entered it, trailing a small torn edge of paper behind him as he went.

Sophia stood in the doorway, hands on her hips, watching him go.

"This village," she said, to no one, "is not normal."

That night, after they had eaten — a simple dinner, mostly rice, mostly quiet — John lay on the bisat with his leg slightly raised on a folded cloth, and said: "Today we learned new words."

Sophia, across the room, brushing out her still-damp hair, nodded slowly. "Yes. Wash. Swim. Hospital. Bee."

John, wincing slightly as he touched the small red swelling on his calf, looked at the ceiling.

"And pain."


# Palm Seven — The Three Letters of John



## Scene One — Mr. John and the Invisible Pillow


Mr. John walked into the classroom the way a brave man walks — with his shoulders back, his chin forward, and the specific composure of a man who had very recently, very completely, lost a fight with a very small enemy.

His shirt was neat. It had been pressed that morning with Sophia's particular brand of evening-before precision, and it sat across his shoulders in a way that suggested no mishap had ever befallen the man who wore it. His hair was tidy. His trousers were the correct length. His shoes, although not polished — no shoes in this village survived polishing — were at least matched. His smile strained itself into position. It tried. It tried very hard.

But his eyes?

One eye looked normal. Mild. English. The eye of a man who had slept adequately and was here to teach grammar.

The other looked like it was wearing a tiny invisible pillow.

The wasp — the one from yesterday, the one that had risen from the small nest by the neem tree outside the majaza, the one whose memory John would carry in his leg for several days — had apparently not been satisfied with the leg alone. Something else had found him in the night. Something had located him in his sleep, in his sleeping face, and had left its mark along the socket of his right eye. The swelling had not been there when he went to bed. It had been there when he woke up — fully formed, fully committed, fully magnificent — as though an artist had spent the night working on it.

The students stared.

This is the specific silence a classroom makes when the teacher has entered the room with a visible injury. Pencils stop. Notebooks stop. Conversations stop. Even Mansoor, at the back, for one full second, stopped.

Then Mansoor did what Mansoor always did. He stood up.

Not beside his desk. On it. He climbed onto his small wooden desk in one smooth, confident motion — in the way a small man climbs onto a stage he has been rehearsing for — and pointed at Mr. John's eye with the full, ceremonial gravity of a herald announcing the arrival of something important — a royal wedding, perhaps, or the outbreak of a war.

"Teacher! Your eye is… BOOM!"

The class exploded.

It was not laughter that built. It was laughter that arrived — complete and full, as though every child had been saving it since the moment Mr. John had opened the door. Desks shook. Two boys at the back slid off their bench. One girl put her head down on her notebook and did not lift it for nearly a minute.

Noura, from her seat in the front, clapped her hands — twice, in small staccato celebration — and delivered her verdict with the crisp clarity of a small judge who had already reached her decision. "It's true! It's big!"

Khalil leaned forward with a serious face — a face so serious that everyone else in the classroom knew, immediately, that Khalil was about to be very funny. He placed one elbow on the desk. He held his chin in his hand. He looked at Mr. John with the thoughtful, sympathetic expression of a village elder diagnosing a long-suffering neighbour.

"Maybe," he said, in careful English, "he cried all night."

The class, which had been recovering, went down again.

Mr. John blinked slowly — carefully — as though blinking, if performed with enough delicacy, might persuade the swelling to change its mind. It did not. Blinking, in fact, made it worse. He felt the small tightness of the eyelid. He felt the throb — low, patient — in the bone around the eye.

Ms. Thuraya walked in behind him.

She was carrying a piece of chalk and a thick notebook. She was, by all appearances, prepared for a normal morning of teaching. She had not yet seen Mr. John's face.

She saw the faces of the students first. She saw the pointing fingers. She saw the wide grins, the half-covered mouths, the boy at the back making — with both hands — the precise BOOM gesture that had now, evidently, become the official gesture of the morning.

She stopped. She turned her head, slowly, toward Mr. John.

She saw the eye.

Her face — for one-quarter of a second — betrayed her. It was the small, involuntary widening of a teacher's eyes when a teacher has just understood, in a single glance, exactly how the next hour is going to go. Then she composed herself. She tapped the board twice — the small, specific taps that every student in every village in Oman understood.

"Good morning, everyone."

The students tried to behave. They tried with genuine effort. They tried for exactly two seconds.

Then Mansoor, still not entirely down from the desk, said — in a loud whisper to Ali — "He looks like he slept inside a beehive."

Mr. John, whose hearing was sharper than his swollen eye suggested, caught the word. His remaining eyebrow went up.

"Beehive?"

Ms. Thuraya did not answer. She was already writing on the board.

She wrote, in clear capital letters, two words:

WASPSTING

She turned to the class. "Class — what do we say when Mr. John comes?"

They remembered yesterday's lesson. They remembered the small gift. They remembered that good morning was something you gave a person at the beginning of the day. Some of them said it correctly. Some of them attacked it like a wild animal.

"Good morning, Mister Jhon!" "Good morning, Teacher!" "Good morning… and… sorry for your eye!"

Mr. John gave a small, courteous bow. "Good morning."

It was not, he thought, the start to the teaching week he had imagined. But he was — he had to admit — among friends.


## Scene Two — Yesterday I Felt Pain


Ms. Thuraya tapped the board again. The two words waited. She pointed to the first.

"Repeat after me."

"Wasp," the class said.

The word came out in many different ways. Ahmed said it with the clean precision of a boy who had been studying the shape of English mouths in a pocket mirror. Noura said it with her usual slight flourish, as though the word were part of a small speech. Mansoor said it with three extra syllables that English did not recognise but that he clearly felt were improvements.

"Sting," she said again — pointing at the second word — "louder."

The class shouted "STING!" with such enthusiasm that a small lizard on the outer wall of the classroom darted up and out of sight.

Mr. John pointed — carefully — to his swollen eye. He said, slowly, with the deliberate cadence of a man modeling a sentence for children: "Yesterday… a wasp… stung me."

Ms. Thuraya nodded, approvingly. "Good. Past tense."

She wrote on the board, in her clear hand — the two halves of the same room, present and past, lined up beside each other like mirrors:

Today: I feel pain.Yesterday: I felt pain.Today: My eye is swollen.Yesterday: My eye was swollen.

The children copied the sentences into their notebooks. Ahmed, of course, copied them with meticulous care, each letter formed slowly. Khalil copied them at an angle that suggested either a rebellion against lines or a general unconcern with them. Mansoor, predictably, drew a small wasp in the margin.

Khalil raised his hand. He raised it with the specific sweetness that tells experienced teachers to prepare for a trap.

"Yes, Khalil?"

"Teacher," he said, "your eye is swollen… or was swollen?"

Mr. John sighed. The sigh was not theatrical. It was the specific sigh of a man whose grammar had just been used against him by a ten-year-old boy.

"It is swollen," he said, carefully, "and it was swollen."

The class laughed again — a smaller, warmer laugh this time, a laugh that knew what it had just witnessed.

Ms. Thuraya smiled. Her smile was kind. But her eyes held a warning. The eyes said — enjoy this for one more moment. Then we work.

"Okay," she said. "Today, we practice past simple. Everyone will tell a funny story. From yesterday. Or from any yesterday."

The students made small, excited sounds. The idea of telling a funny story was, to any ten-year-old in any country in any century, approximately the best news that a classroom could contain.

Ali sat up straight, like a ruler. Ahmed sat even straighter — like a ruler that wanted a promotion. Khalil arranged his face into the expression of a boy who was ready to perform. Mansoor adopted the expression of a boy who was ready to destroy the world, and possibly the floor, and possibly both.

Ms. Thuraya surveyed her class. She considered her options. She pointed at Ahmed.

"Ahmed. You start."


## Scene Three — The Mijz


Ahmed stood up slowly. He held his hands behind his back — the small, serious posture of a man at a meeting who has been asked to address his superiors, and who has decided to rise to the occasion completely.

He spoke carefully. He used the English words he knew, and he filled the gaps between them with Omani confidence — which is a different kind of confidence than English confidence, and which believes, with unshaken certainty, that the listener will do half the work of the meaning.

"Yesterday," he said, "I… um… I went… to the farm."

Ms. Thuraya nodded. "Good. Went is past."

Ahmed continued. His voice gained a small momentum. "I played with my cousins. I ran. I climbed. I fell."

Khalil, who could not resist a good opportunity, whispered to the boy next to him — loudly enough to be heard by the whole front row — "He fell because he is too straight."

Ahmed ignored him with the dignity of a professional public speaker. He continued.

"Then… I got pain in my stomach."

Noura gasped. She could not help herself. She gasped loudly and fully, as though a very important piece of information had just been disclosed.

"Oh no!"

Ahmed lifted his chin. He was pleased with the reaction. He was, Thuraya realised, about to deliver the part of his story that he had, without question, been planning since the moment she had said the word yesterday.

"My grandmother said," he declaimed, "'You eat too fast.' My mother said, 'You eat too much.' My uncle said, 'You need…'"

Ahmed paused. He paused for a very long time — long enough for the pause itself to become part of the story. He looked at Ms. Thuraya. His face contained a small question. She knew the question. She had known this question was coming since the sentence began.

Ms. Thuraya sighed — once, quietly, almost imperceptibly. It was the sigh of a teacher who loved her culture, and who also loved peace, and who had just realised, in this specific moment, that the two were about to come into conflict.

Ahmed said it anyway.

"You need… mijz."

The class made a sound — a long, collective "Ooooooh!" — as though Ahmed had just spoken a spell. Every child in that classroom knew the word. Every child had, at some point, heard a relative speak of the mijz, either in warning or in memory.

Mr. John leaned — carefully, because of the eye — toward Ms. Thuraya. "What is… mijz?"

Ms. Thuraya's smile became small. Careful. The smile of a teacher choosing her words.

"It is… an old treatment. Traditional."

Ahmed, before she could add any useful softening, lifted one finger like a small lecturer.

"They heated it."

Mr. John's eyebrows jumped. The good one, at least. The other was immobilised.

"They heated… what?"

Ahmed's eyes shone with the specific brightness of a storyteller reaching the choice part.

"The mijz."

Ms. Thuraya, realising that the story was going to continue regardless, did what any good teacher would do. She absorbed the story into the lesson. She turned to the board. She wrote, in clear English:

heatburntreathurtcure (old belief)traditional medicine

Ahmed, receiving this as encouragement, continued. He was enjoying himself now. Every second of telling the story was delivering him the full attention of fifteen other children — and, for the first time in his academic career, the full attention of two actual English people.

"My uncle made fire. He put the mijz in the fire. It became red."

Khalil, incapable of silence, whispered to Ali — "Like Mansoor's face when he lies."

Mansoor sat up immediately. "I don't lie!" A small, indignant pause. "I just… change the truth!"

The class laughed. Ms. Thuraya did not — quite — laugh, but something moved across her face that was not unrelated to laughter.

Ahmed went on. He was not about to lose his audience now. "My grandmother said, 'Be strong.' I said, 'I am strong.'"

He looked at the boys around him with the specific expression of a small hero who had, in the past tense, been very brave.

"I sat. I closed my eyes. I said… 'Ready.'"

Mr. John — one good eye wide, one swollen eye doing its best — slowly began to shake his head. "No, no, no…"

Ahmed, at this exact moment, delivered the climax. He acted it out. He raised one hand, pinched fingers together as though holding a small heated rod, and brought it down toward his own stomach — and, instead of naming the moment, produced the sound of it.

"Then… TSSSSS!"

The students shouted. They laughed. Noura covered her mouth. Khalil fell off his bench — slightly, not fully, enough to be funny but not enough to be reprimanded. Mansoor pretended to collapse onto his desk in sympathetic agony.

Ahmed, unbothered, held his stomach — leaning back in his chair — like a man remembering the exact location of an old pain.

"I felt like…" he paused, searching, searching — and landed, in his triumphant English, on the best possible comparison — "…I became a chicken."

The class roared.

Ms. Thuraya pressed her lips together. She tried not to laugh. She failed — a little. A small smile broke through, and she corralled the class back with a single raised hand.

"Ahmed. Use past verbs."

Ahmed nodded seriously. He had been waiting for this instruction. He had prepared the exact past verb for the exact moment.

"I screamed."

Noura clapped. "Good verb!"

Ahmed concluded his story with the satisfied cadence of a storyteller approaching his final line. "After that… my stomach felt better. My uncle said, 'See? Good!' My mother said, 'Next time… eat slowly.'"

He sat down. The class applauded — not quietly.

Mr. John, still processing the entire sequence of events that had just been described to him, raised his hand like a student.

"Wait. This is real? You burned him?"

Ms. Thuraya spoke gently. She chose her words with care, the way she chose all her words when she was explaining her country to someone who had not grown up inside it.

"Many people used it in the past. They believed it helped. Now we have hospitals. Doctors. Medicine. But in the 1970s — in villages — traditional ways were used. Some people still trust them."

Mr. John touched his swollen eye. Gently. "I prefer," he said, with quiet sincerity, "hospital."


## Scene Four — One Sentence, Mansoor


The word hospital had barely left Mr. John's mouth when Mansoor was already on his feet.

He had leapt from his chair with the particular speed of a boy who had been holding onto a piece of personal information for approximately forty seconds and could no longer survive without releasing it into the classroom.

"Teacher! I went hospital one time!"

Ms. Thuraya — who had, across the course of her teaching career, developed a very specific radar for Mansoor's I have a story voice — turned immediately. She pointed at him with the exact authority that every student in the village had learned, by the age of six, not to argue with.

"Sit. One sentence only."

Mansoor sat. But his mouth did not sit. His mouth kept moving, as though his body had been given one instruction and his mouth had been given another, and the mouth was pretending not to have heard.

"Okay okay… I went hospital… I cried… I ran away…"

Ms. Thuraya's voice became sweeter. It also became, Safiya noticed from the back of the room, much more dangerous — the specific sweet-dangerous voice that teachers use when a child is about to be managed, firmly, with complete public grace.

"ONE sentence," she repeated.

Mansoor froze mid-sentence. He considered his options. He considered them with the genuine, visible gravity of a small boy who has been told he has only one sentence of a story he wants to tell in six.

He looked at the ceiling. He looked at the floor. He looked at Ms. Thuraya. Then, with the small decisive air of a boy who had just arrived at his final answer — he chose the sentence. He chose the best one.

"I cried."

The class laughed — completely, delighted, disarmed — by the elegant, devastating economy of one sentence. It was, they all agreed later, a better sentence than the full story would have been.

Ms. Thuraya clapped once — a small, efficient punctuation — and brought the room back under her command.

"Now. We fix Ahmed's grammar."

She turned to the board. She wrote, in clear capital letters, with a small ✅ beside each line:

✅ Yesterday, I went to the farm.
✅ My uncle heated the mijz.
✅ I felt pain.
✅ I screamed.
✅ After that, I felt better.

The class read the sentences together, in the unified, rising voice of children reading from a board. "Yesterday, I went to the farm…"

Safiya, watching from the back, saw how Thuraya had done it. She had taken the chaos — Ahmed's story, the branding iron, Mansoor's one sentence, John's visible discomfort with the entire cultural conversation — and distilled it, without spilling a drop, into five correct English sentences. Past tense. Clean verbs. The lesson she had come into the room to teach. Thuraya was, she thought, an extraordinary teacher. And an even better diplomat.

Ms. Thuraya turned from the board. She looked at Mr. John. "Your turn. One sentence about yesterday."

Mr. John cleared his throat. He looked at the class — at the many small faces, each of them waiting for him to deliver something they could laugh at or learn from or, ideally, both.

He composed his sentence. Slowly. Carefully.

"Yesterday," he said, "I walked near a tree. A wasp stung me. I ran like Mansoor."

Mansoor, who had just fallen silent in honour of his I cried sentence, sat up straight with immediate delight. He whispered to Ali, proudly — loud enough for Mr. John to hear, which was entirely the point — "See? I teach him."

Ms. Thuraya laughed — openly this time, without embarrassment. "Good. Past verbs — walked, stung, ran."

Noura raised her hand. Because of course she did.

"Teacher — can we say… 'He looked scary'?"

Mr. John blinked. His one good eye widened, which made the other eye look, by contrast, even smaller. "I looked scary?"

Khalil nodded. Seriously. With the specific, committed gravity of a boy who had considered the comparison carefully and now wished to offer his professional opinion.

"Like a tired camel."

The class burst again. This time Mr. John joined them — briefly, helplessly. His smile, however, came out crooked. Half of his face could smile. The other half was, at the moment, on leave.

"Thank you," he said, with as much dignity as the remaining functional half of his face would allow.


## Scene Five — The Ghoul


The class was beginning to settle. Ahmed was rereading his own story in his notebook with visible pride. Noura was adding a small decorative border to her sentence about yesterday. Mansoor was still, impressively, using his one sentence to its full capacity, by mentioning it, at careful intervals, to everyone within hearing. "Hey — yesterday, I cried." "Hey — yesterday, I cried."

The classroom was returning to its working rhythm — the sound of children thinking, of pencils moving against paper, of low talk about who had written what. Ms. Thuraya moved between the desks, checking, correcting, occasionally tapping a page to point out a missing past-tense verb.

Mr. John, eye still swollen but considerably more settled now — the class had absorbed his disfigurement into the day and moved on — wrote, slowly, his own sentence on a clean page. He was beginning to enjoy himself. He was, in fact, just about to ask Thuraya about the past tense of forget, a small grammatical curiosity that had just occurred to him, when —

Khalil leaned forward over his desk. His face was entirely casual. His voice was entirely calm. His manner was the manner of a man sharing weather news.

"Teacher," he said, "last night… we saw a ghoul."

The room went quiet.

Even Mansoor — an event of great statistical rarity — stopped. Stopped talking. Stopped moving. Stopped breathing, briefly, for approximately two seconds. Every child had turned to Mr. John to see how this information would land.

Mr. John froze.

The English word ghoul — which is what he had heard — entered his English imagination with the full catalogue of everything that word meant in English. A grave-robbing spirit. A monster from the stories of his boyhood. A creature from the dark pages of Arabian Nights, which he had, in fact, read, and which had, in fact, frightened him more than he had ever admitted to anyone.

"A… what?"

Ms. Thuraya, realising immediately what was happening — the gap between the Omani ghoul (snake) and the English ghoul (monster) was about to swallow her entire lesson — turned to Khalil.

"Khalil," she said, carefully, "do you mean snake?"

Khalil nodded. Entirely cheerfully. "Yes. Ghoul."

Mr. John's face — what remained of it that was still capable of expression — lost all colour. His one working eye grew more worried. The swollen eye attempted to grow more worried, but was prevented by its structural circumstances, and simply looked, instead, permanently worried.

"A snake?" he whispered. "In the village?"

Ali, proud of his English and eager to contribute, spoke up. "Yes. It came near the water."

Noura added — helpfully — "It moved like this." She waved her arm up and down in a long, ribbon-like motion. The demonstration was, unfortunately, extremely accurate.

Mr. John, without thinking, took one step back — and bumped the blackboard. A small cloud of chalk dust rose around him. He did not notice.

Ms. Thuraya did what good teachers do in a small crisis. She pretended not to be in one.

"Okay," she said, smoothly. "New vocabulary."

She turned to the board. She wrote:

snakeafraiddangerousnearoutsideinside

Mr. John read the words. He read them carefully. His eye — the good one — stopped at the last two. He swallowed.

"Inside?"

Ms. Thuraya paused. She considered her options. She calibrated — precisely — how much honesty this situation deserved.

And then — because she was Ms. Thuraya, and because she enjoyed tiny, surgical jokes, and because the morning had earned one more small moment of mischief — she said, calmly:

"Sometimes… yes."

The students watched Mr. John's face as though it were a film. His single working expression shifted through several phases. Disbelief. Calculation. Resignation.

He spoke slowly. With the considered calm of a man summarising his own situation for the record.

"So," he said, "yesterday… I was stung. Today… I am swollen. And tonight… I will be eaten."

Ms. Thuraya laughed — openly, warmly, the specific laugh of a teacher who has been waiting for the whole morning to crystallise into one perfect sentence, and has just been handed it by her victim.

"Not eaten," she said, kindly. "Just… frightened."


## Scene Six — Animals Do Not Knock


The bell rang.

It was not a modern electric bell. It was a small brass one that Abu Salim, the caretaker, rang by hand from the school yard, with the specific steady cadence that he had been using for roughly twenty years, and which every child in Al-Qurawashiyah recognised before its third chime.

The students rushed out of the classroom like small storms, their voices overlapping in the specific half-English, half-Omani excitement that end-of-lesson had developed in this classroom over the last several weeks.

"Bye, teacher!" "See tomorrow!" "Mansoor, your bag!" "I forgot! I forgot!" "Ahmed, wait!" "Good — morning!" — this last from a smaller boy who had apparently decided that good morning was the best English phrase he owned and intended to use it at all times of day, regardless of context.

And then, as quickly as they had entered, they were gone. The sound of small feet faded down the path. The yard, which had been full of shouting, held, for a long second, only the specific pause that classrooms hold when their children have just left and the dust has not yet settled.

Mr. John did not move.

He stood at the front of the classroom with the board still full of past-tense verbs and the chalk dust still faintly visible on the back of his shirt from his earlier encounter with the blackboard. He looked at Ms. Thuraya, who was, with the calm precision of a person closing one day and preparing for the next, gathering her chalk bag.

He looked at her with the beseeching expression of a man who is asking — without quite asking — for the secret of survival.

"Ms. Thuraya," he said, carefully.

"Yes, Mr. John."

He chose his words. He chose them as a man chooses his final sentence before a very important exam.

"In your village…" he began. He paused. He considered his phrasing. "…do snakes… come inside houses?"

Ms. Thuraya lifted her chalk bag. She hooked its small strap over her shoulder. She adjusted her scarf. She moved — unhurriedly — toward the door. And at the door, she turned.

She smiled.

"Mr. John," she said, kindly, "you live near farms. Near the falaj. Near trees. Sometimes… animals visit."

He nodded. Weakly. "Animals. Yes."

She opened the door. Then — just before she stepped out — she added, with a small grin:

"And sometimes… they don't knock."

Then she was gone. The door closed softly behind her. Mr. John stood for a long moment in the empty classroom. The sound of the falaj came faintly through the open window. Somewhere outside, a bird was calling. Somewhere outside, a child was laughing. Somewhere outside — his imagination unhelpfully supplied — an animal was, perhaps, on its way to his door. Not knocking.

He walked home slowly. The sun was still high, but John did not fully notice it. His mind was full of long, thin, ribbon-like shapes.

That night, in their simple house, Mr. John lay on his bisat and stared at the ceiling.

It was a simple ceiling. Wooden beams. Packed palm fronds. A single qandeel in the corner, its small flame moving slowly as the evening breeze entered through the open window.

The problem with a ceiling made of palm fronds, when one has just been told that animals visit houses in this country and sometimes do not knock, is that a ceiling made of palm fronds is full of very small sounds. The " data-m="The long feather-like leaves of a palm tree; the structure that makes palm roofs" data-s="palm leaves, fronds" data-x="The fronds shifted in the breeze — a small, entirely innocent sound that, at this moment, was not innocent at all.">fronds shifted, occasionally, in the breeze. A small insect moved somewhere in the wood. A larger insect — possibly a lizard; possibly something worse — moved in the corner. And every one of these sounds, to a man who was now primed to think about snakes, became a story.

Every small sound became a story.

A soft tick in the rafters became a small body moving across a beam. A distant rustle became something slithering along the outer wall. The gentle creak of the wooden door became, at one point, a small animal testing the latch.

Sophia, beside him, turned over. "John." "Yes." "Why are you not sleeping?" "I am thinking." "About what?" A pause. "About animals." A longer pause. "And knocking." "And knocking?" "Or not knocking."

She opened one eye. She looked at him across the small distance of the bisat.

"John." "Yes." "Go to sleep." "I am trying." "Try harder."

She closed her eye. Within two minutes, her breathing was slow and even. The specific, unfair deep sleep of women who did not know the Arabic word for snake.

Mr. John lay awake.

He lay awake for a long time. He listened to the ceiling. He listened to the breeze. He listened to the falaj, which, in the distance, continued its long, old, unbroken hush.

And every small sound — just as the narrator of his evening had truthfully observed — became, in its own small way, a story.


# Palm Eight — A Goat Named Malood



## Scene One — The Morning Walk to School


The morning sun was already warm — not yet hot, not yet the flat unforgiving brightness of midday, but warm enough, even at this early hour, to remind every person walking along the road that this country did not believe in gentle mornings.

Mr. John walked slowly toward the small school in Samail. He carried his bag in one hand — a canvas bag that had come with him from London, and which, in this dusty road, was beginning to take on the patina of a bag that had started a second life — and he rubbed his eyes with the other.

His eye, for the record, was still slightly swollen. The wasp had left a small, patient memory along the bone. It was a memory he intended to live with for a few more days.

"Why," he said, half to himself, half to the path, "does everything here wake up so early?"

Behind him, Sophia — Safiya, as everyone in the village now called her, with the specific warmth of a name that had been adopted, not imposed — was fixing her scarf. Her scarf had, by now, become a small daily negotiation. It was never quite in the position she had last left it. The village had a wind that worked against scarves. And her feet — which had been born in leather shoes and raised in them — were still learning what a sandy path required.

"This place is beautiful," she said, smiling — with genuine affection now, not the strained cheerfulness of her first weeks — "but my feet are not happy."

Ms. Thuraya, walking a step ahead, laughed without turning.

"Your feet will learn," she said. "Everything learns here."

And Safiya, walking behind her, felt the weight of the sentence settle. Everything learns here. She understood, without needing to ask, that Thuraya had not meant only the feet. She had meant the eyes, which were learning to read light differently. She had meant the ears, which were learning to separate the adhan from the breeze from the falaj. She had meant the skin, which was learning to accept dust as a cosmetic rather than an insult. And she had meant whatever part of a person was deeper than all of those — the part that, without being asked, had already begun to rearrange itself around this road, this village, this morning.

The school appeared at the end of the path. It was a simple building — white walls, a wooden door painted the specific deep Omani blue that doorways across Samail shared, a blackboard visible through the open window, a small collection of chalk on a small shelf by the door. No fans. No electricity. Only open windows and, through them, the sound of birds and the distant bleat of goats somewhere beyond the farm wall.

When they arrived, the children were already there. They were always already there. Safiya had begun to suspect, half seriously, that some of them slept at the school.

Ahmed sat straight like a ruler — bag on his knees, slate held at the exact correct angle, the posture of a boy who believed, with deep personal conviction, that good posture contributed to good grammar.

Ali was drawing something instead of writing. He was often drawing something instead of writing. He had the specific gift of a boy whose pencil, given any choice between a letter and a small animal, would choose the animal. Ms. Thuraya had, with the wisdom of experience, stopped fighting this.

Khalil was whispering and laughing — in that precise rhythm, whisper then laugh, that said a joke had been invented, had been delivered, and was now waiting to be improved. Noura was asking Salima a question. Knowing Noura, Salima had answered one and been immediately asked three more. Marwa and Rahma were sharing one pencil — a shared object that had, at this point, become the shared possession of everyone in their small triangle.

And Mansoor — because it would not have been a morning in Al-Qurawashiyah otherwise — was standing on his chair.

"Sit down, Mansoor," Thuraya said — not even loudly, not even as a command, more as a ritual. She knew the sentence. He knew the sentence. Neither of them expected the sentence to permanently alter his behaviour.

Mansoor sat down.

Five seconds later, he stood up again.

Mr. John clapped his hands — twice, sharply, the teacher's way of opening a morning. "Good morning, everyone!"

"Good morning, teacher!" they answered — in the layered, imperfectly-timed chorus of children who had learned the greeting but had not yet learned to arrive at it together.

"Today," Mr. John said, happily — the eye did not quite smile with him, but the rest of his face tried — "we will learn how to talk about people. We say: He is… She is… He has… She has…"

He did not get any further.

The classroom door slammed.

BANG.


## Scene Two — The Shadow on the Wall


All the children jumped.

Khalil's eyes became big — the specific round alertness of a child who has not, in fact, been frightened, but who has understood, in the same second as the rest of the class, that now is the time to be frightened. Mansoor, who had just stood up for the second time, relocated behind Ahmed with an efficiency that suggested he had, in his short life, practised this.

Noura, whose voice was usually the first voice in any situation, lowered it to a whisper — a whisper that carried, somehow, more weight than any of her earlier questions.

"It's him…"

Safiya looked at Thuraya. "Who?"

Thuraya's face — the face that was, by now, Safiya's main way of reading the village — changed a little. Not with fear. Not with alarm. With the small composure of a person who has just heard a familiar footstep on a day when she had hoped not to.

"Stay calm," she said quietly. "Stay calm."

Outside — heavy footsteps passed near the window.

THUMP.

THUMP.

They were not the footsteps of a child. They were not the footsteps of a small woman carrying water. They had weight, and the specific deliberation of a stride that had never been in a hurry in its life.

A shadow appeared on the wall. A long one. The shadow of something tall, thin, passing slowly across the open window — and then, without pausing, continuing on.

Ali said, in a small voice — the voice of a boy offering a piece of information he had not intended to offer in public — "The old man…"

Marwa whispered, from the triangle, "The magic man…"

Rahma, her voice very small, "My mother says he is scary."

And Mansoor — from somewhere behind Ahmed's shoulder, so that his voice seemed to come from Ahmed himself, which Ahmed found deeply objectionable — added, with the full gravity of a report from his own father's mouth:

"My father says he can talk to goats."

Mr. John blinked. His one good eye widened. The other eye, doing its best, strained upward.

"Talk to… goats?"

Khalil, who had by now recovered enough to speak, supplied the name. "Teacher… it is Khalaf."

"Who is Khalaf?" Safiya asked — and the answer that came was not one answer but many, all delivered at the same time, overlapping, interrupting, each child contributing the piece of the legend that they had inherited.

"He is scary!" "He lives alone!" "He has a big stick!" "He walks at night!" "He makes smoke!" "He is a magician!"

Mr. John attempted, with the specific courage of a teacher who has not yet been defeated by a morning, a smile.

"There are no magicians," he said, firmly. "Only teachers."

It was a good sentence. Calm. Rational. The kind of sentence that would have ended the conversation in a classroom in London.

Just then, something fell outside.

CRASH.

The children screamed.

"ABOOYAAAAAH!" — the specific, unlearnable Omani exclamation that means oh my father, and which emerges, unbidden, from the small chest of any village child who has just been genuinely startled.

Some of them ran under the desks. Mansoor, now fully committed to the hiding strategy, pulled Ahmed's bag over his own head. Ahmed, to his small credit, did not object. Noura's three questions briefly became one question — "WHAT WAS THAT?" — at a volume that overturned her earlier whisper.

Safiya — without having made any decision to do so — had grabbed Mr. John's arm.

"What was that?!"

Thuraya — calm in the way that teachers become calm precisely when their students cannot be — walked to the window. She looked out. She considered what she saw. Then — unexpectedly, in the middle of the small crisis — she started to laugh.

"It's just Khalaf," she said.

"JUST?" Mr. John said — with the specific incredulity of a man who has just been told that the source of a village-wide legend is, in fact, not significant enough to justify the legend.

She opened the door.


## Scene Three — The Man in the Doorway


Outside stood an old man.

He was very thin. He was very tall. And he was, in a way that Safiya would find herself still thinking about a week later, very quiet — and his quietness was not the absence of sound. It was a presence. A thing he carried. An atmosphere around him in which sounds — even small ones, even birds — seemed to step back out of politeness.

A long white beard reached halfway down his chest. His clothes were old — not ragged, but well-used, the clothes of a man who had long since stopped buying new ones because his old ones had become part of him. In his hand was a stick. Near his feet, on the dust, was a small cloth bag. Through its open neck, Safiya could see herbs — bundles of them, leaves she did not recognise, small brown stems tied with string — and, beside the herbs, a few old tools she could not immediately name.

He looked at Thuraya. His voice, when it came, was dry, flat, and completely calm.

"Your goat," he said, "is eating my plants again."

Behind him — visible now through the open door, chewing happily, entirely unrepentant — stood Malood.

Safiya's eyes became big. "Oh no. Not again."

Thuraya lowered her head slightly — the small, formal lowering of respect that Safiya had begun to notice among younger people when they spoke to older ones.

"Sorry, Uncle Khalaf. We will keep him away."

Khalaf did not nod. He did not wave the apology off. He simply accepted it, and allowed his gaze to move — slowly, unhurriedly — from Thuraya, past her, into the room, to the two foreigners standing behind her.

He looked at Mr. John.

He looked at Safiya.

He looked at them with the specific calm attentiveness of a man who had lived long enough to read a face once and not need to read it a second time.

"You are the new teachers," he said. Slowly. It was a statement, not a question. He already knew.

"Yes," Mr. John said. He cleared his throat. He found, to his own small surprise, that he was using his careful teacher-voice — the voice he used when a student was waiting for a correct sentence. "I am John. This is my wife, Sophia."

Khalaf's eyes moved to Sophia's face. They stayed there for a long time — not intrusively, not uncomfortably, but with the same careful reading he had offered Mr. John. It was the look of a man who had decided, quietly, to see who these people were.

Then — the smallest softening around the mouth, not quite a smile.

"You look afraid," he said.

Sophia laughed — nervously, the laugh of a woman who has just been seen more clearly than she had prepared to be seen.

"No… just… surprised."

He did not reply to this. He did not challenge it. He simply nodded, once, as though surprised were an answer he had heard before and found acceptable. He picked up his cloth bag. He turned. He walked away — with the same heavy, unhurried stride that had announced his arrival — and disappeared around the corner of the school wall.

The moment he was out of sight, the children came out.

Not carefully. Not cautiously. Explosively — in the way that children, who have been pretending to be terrified for approximately two minutes, now pretend to be entirely recovered. Mansoor emerged from under Ahmed's bag with the triumphant air of a survivor. Khalil, recovered from his initial alarm, immediately began to talk.

"See? He is strange!" "He doesn't smile!" "He talks to himself!" "My cousin says he makes medicine!" "My brother says he makes smoke!"

Mr. John — who was, by training and by temperament, constitutionally opposed to myth — walked to the board and picked up his chalk.

"Look," he said. "Look. We can describe him."

He wrote on the board, clearly, in the same capitals he always used:

He is old.He is thin.He has a stick.He walks slowly.

He turned back to the class.

Ahmed — the boy whose seriousness had been his identity since the first day — raised his hand. His face was composed. His posture was, as always, a monument to good spinal habits.

"Teacher," he said, in his careful, measured English. "He is not a magician. He is just… quiet."

Every head in the classroom turned toward him. Ahmed's verdicts were rare. When they came, they counted.

Khalil, slightly uncertain — challenged in his own mythology — said, "Are you sure?"

Ahmed nodded. Once. Without elaboration.

"My grandfather knows him."

Silence. The specific silence of a myth being slightly, gently punctured.

Mansoor, unwilling to let go of an excellent story without a fight, offered — "But he is still scary."

Sophia — Safiya, in this room, from this day on — laughed. Not at him. With him.

"Sometimes," she said, "people look scary. But they are not."


## Scene Four — The Missing Ring


That afternoon, in the small clay house at the edge of the village, Safiya could not find her ring.

She had been wearing it, she was certain, that morning. It was her mother's ring — a small band of gold, nothing showy, the kind of ring that a woman wears so long that she stops noticing it in the same way that a person stops noticing the weight of their own hand. She had worn it in London. She had worn it on the plane. She had worn it on the first day here and the second and every day since.

Now, looking down at her hand — her hand that was, she realised, slightly lighter than it had been at breakfast — she understood what had been bothering her, faintly, for the last half hour without being named.

"My ring!" she said, standing up abruptly. "It's not here!"

Mr. John, who had been half-asleep on the bisat, came fully awake in the way that husbands come fully awake when their wives produce that specific kind of sentence.

They searched the house. They searched every surface that a ring could plausibly be. The table. The small wooden shelf where Safiya kept her few books. Her bag. Another bag. The bed. Under the bed. Inside the bed. The small stone bowl where she sometimes placed keys. The corner where her scarf had been hung that morning. The floor of the kitchen, which they swept, twice.

Nothing.

"Oh no," Safiya said. She sat down on the bisat. The particular hollow where the ring had been felt, now, very noticeable. "It's gone."

Outside, on the path that ran past their door, two small figures were passing by — Khalil and Ali, carrying something that had clearly just been stolen from somebody's orchard. They heard Safiya's voice through the open door. They stopped.

Khalil — because his mind, like every ten-year-old mind, ran on a very particular logic inherited from his mother, from his grandmother, from every auntie who had ever told a story in the village — leaned in and whispered to Ali:

"Maybe Khalaf took it."

Ali's eyes became big. "The magician!"

And that was all it took. Within ten minutes — the village was small, and a theory moved through it at the speed of footsteps — every child was animated by the same story.

"He took it!" "He uses it for magic!" "He will make smoke!" "He will turn it into a frog!"

Mr. John, hearing all of this through the open door, sighed — the sigh of a rational Englishman being outnumbered — and went to the door.

"No one," he announced, carefully, to the assembled children, "is turning anything into a frog."

But his voice — Safiya noticed, coming up behind him — was not entirely as confident as the sentence required. Even John, she understood, was not, at this moment, entirely sure.

Thuraya had been summoned. She arrived within minutes — the village's speed being, once again, remarkable — and took the whole situation in with a single glance. The worried wife. The unsure husband. The children, already gathered outside, loudly organising themselves into a small expedition.

"We will go to his house," she said, simply. "We will ask."

"Do you know where he lives?" Safiya asked. "Yes." "Does everyone?" "Everyone."

They walked. Thuraya in front — slightly ahead, at the pace of a person who did not want to arrive too fast or too slow. Then Safiya and John. Then — because the village always came too — a small trail of children, keeping a prudent distance behind them.

The house was a small mud house near the farms. Simple. Low. Older than many of the houses Safiya had walked past. The door was a plain wooden door, painted the same blue that every door in this village wore, but faded — more than any other door she had seen.

Safiya, without meaning to, reached for Thuraya's hand. Thuraya took it without looking down.

"Thuraya," Safiya whispered. "Why is my heart beating like this?"

Thuraya looked at her. Her face was, as always, composed. But the answer she gave was one Safiya would remember for the rest of her life.

"Because stories are stronger than truth."

They knocked.

KNOCK.

KNOCK.

The door opened — slowly — as all good doors in a village do. Khalaf stood inside it. He had not been expecting them. He was also not, it seemed, surprised.

"Yes?"


## Scene Five — The Open Hand


Thuraya spoke first. In the calm, polite Arabic that one uses with elders — and which Safiya, standing beside her, could now recognise even when she could not fully translate.

"Sorry to disturb you, Uncle Khalaf. Sophia lost her ring."

Khalaf looked at them. He looked at them for a moment — not suspiciously, not defensively, simply looked, in the same careful way he had looked at them that morning through the classroom door. He did not ask them to come inside. He did not invite them to wait.

He turned. He walked back into the small dark interior of his house. The children behind them, who had been whispering, went silent.

He was gone for perhaps fifteen seconds.

He came back.

He stood in the doorway. And he opened his hand.

Inside it — bright and small and entirely impossible — was the ring.

For a long second, nobody spoke. Safiya looked at her ring. She looked at Khalaf's hand — thin, weathered, with knuckles that had seen many winters in a country that had very few. The ring sat in the centre of his palm, small and gold, exactly as she had last seen it that morning. She had been wearing it when she left the house. She had not noticed when it had left her. But it had, and now it was here — inside the hand of the man the children had, an hour ago, accused of stealing it.

And Khalaf, without any change of expression, explained.

"It was near my plants," he said — calmly, without pride, without reproach. "Your goat likes shiny things."

All of them — every human being present — looked at Malood.

Malood had arrived, at some point during the walk, without anyone noticing, and had positioned himself — with the specific proprietary air he applied to every place he entered — near the door of Khalaf's small house. He was currently chewing something. He looked up at the humans who had all, at the same instant, turned toward him. He considered them. Then he continued chewing.

He said nothing. He never said anything. He simply kept eating — whatever it was, whatever had once belonged to whoever — with the full, serene self-possession of a creature who had, a long time ago, decided that he was not going to be embarrassed by anyone.

Safiya laughed — the laugh of a woman whose afternoon had, in a single second, been re-explained to her from beginning to end.

"Oh…" she said. "Malood."

Khalaf, meanwhile, had placed the ring — carefully, precisely — into her palm. He did not linger. He did not smile.

He met her eyes. Just once. And delivered the only counsel he offered.

"Next time," he said, "close your door."
