Then he stepped back. He closed the door — slowly, without emphasis, simply closing it — and went back inside.

The small group outside stood for a moment, unmoving. Safiya looked down at the ring in her palm. She slid it back onto her finger. The small hollow that had been there all afternoon closed up.

They turned, slowly, and began to walk home.

The children — who had been watching the whole exchange from a respectful distance — were, for the first time that afternoon, silent.

Finally, Mansoor spoke.

"He is not a magician."

Khalil, a few steps behind, added — quietly, as though admitting something — "He is just… an old man."

Ali, the last to speak, offered the sentence that Safiya would later write in her notebook.

"A kind old man."


## Scene Six — The Notebook and the Thump


That night, in the small clay house at the edge of the village, Mr. John sat at the small wooden table with his notebook open in front of him.

The oil lamp burned low — its flame steady, its light the specific warm yellow that had, over the past weeks, become the light that John now associated with thinking. Outside, the village had gone quiet. The falaj continued, as always. A dog barked once, somewhere very far away, and then was still.

He picked up the Parker pen he had brought from London — a gift, years ago, from a student who had since become a professor somewhere — and opened the notebook to a fresh page.

He thought for a moment. He composed carefully. And then, in his even, slightly slanted English teacher's handwriting, he wrote:

Today, we learned something important.Not every scary story is true.And not every quiet man is bad.

He read it back. He read it twice. He underlined, almost unconsciously, the word quiet. Then he closed the notebook.

He stood. He stretched. He blew out the small flame of the oil lamp — and the room became, for one complete second, fully dark, until his eyes adjusted to the softer moonlight coming in through the open window.

He walked toward the bisat — toward Sophia, who was already lying down, who had pulled the light cloth up to her shoulder in the specific way of women who had decided that the day was over.

Then — he stopped.

He had heard a sound.

THUMP.

THUMP.

Outside. Near the path. Slow. Heavy. The specific weight of a stride he had, this very morning, heard pass beneath the classroom window.

A shadow passed the edge of the window — tall, thin, unhurried — and continued on.

Mr. John sat up. Not fully. Just enough to hear, more clearly, the rhythm of the footsteps receding — thump, a pause, thump, a longer pause, and then only the sound of the falaj and the breeze and the small night.

"Sophia…"

"Yes?" She did not open her eyes. Her voice carried the specific slowness of a woman being pulled back from the edge of sleep, and who was not going to be pulled back very far.

He considered his question. He considered it with the care of a man who had promised himself, just that afternoon, that he had learned his lesson. He had written it down. Not every scary story is true. Not every quiet man is bad. He had signed his name, in effect, to that idea.

And yet.

"…you don't think he really makes smoke, right?"

Sophia, eyes still closed, smiled — a smile John could hear, even in the dark. Her voice, when it came, was patient, wifely, and entirely unafraid.

"No."

A small pause. The smile, he could still hear, was growing.

"But if he does," she said, "please go and talk to him."

Mr. John pulled the blanket up to his chin. He considered the night. He considered the village. He considered the small, dry, unsmiling man who had, that afternoon, returned his wife's mother's ring without ceremony and without comment — and who was now, apparently, walking past their house at some late hour, carrying, for all he knew, herbs, or tools, or nothing at all.

"I will talk to him," he said, slowly, "tomorrow."

Outside, the second thump — the last one — faded, and the village returned to the steady hush of the falaj and the night.

Mr. John did not, for some time, sleep.

But when he finally did — and this was a small, important victory — he slept, for the first time in three nights, without dreams of ghouls, without dreams of snakes, and without any particular fear of his own ceiling.


# Palm Nine — The Smoke and the Sahir



## Scene One — The Truck at the School


The sun was still soft when everyone started to gather near the small school in Samail.

It was the specific kind of early morning that this country did so well — the sun had crossed the threshold into morning but had not yet committed to its full brightness, and every surface it touched came back in the colour of honey rather than gold. The air had the cleanness that it held only for these brief hours. The village was, for one small window, possible to breathe.

The pickup truck of Uncle Nasser was already there.

It was old. It was noisy — even sitting still, it made a small, dry settling noise, like an old animal getting comfortable on the ground. And it was strong. Its paint had been white once, perhaps — perhaps green — but the years and the dust and the wadi beds had settled onto it a uniform dun colour that was the colour of the roads themselves. It looked, unmistakably, like a truck that had carried half the village many times before. Safiya could see, just by looking at it, that its bed had held sheep, goats, wedding parties, men returning from market, women going to weddings, children going to wadis, firewood, furniture, and — today — a small expedition of schoolchildren and two English teachers.

The children were excited. They were, at eight in the morning, at a level of excitement that most adults could not have sustained for more than three minutes.

Mansoor was jumping — not toward anything, not away from anything, just jumping, the way small boys jump when the instruction they have been given (wait here) has proven, within seconds, to be incompatible with his body's plans for the morning.

Khalil — in the corner of her eye, because he was always in the corner of her eye — was already planning trouble. She could see it in his posture. He had that small, focused, scheming look, and the Ali-beside-him was listening to the scheme with the small happy face of a boy who had just been included.

Ahmed was holding his bag carefully. He had, Safiya had noticed, a specific way of holding bags — with both hands, close to his chest, as though the bag contained something important. The bag contained, she had once discovered, three pencils, a slate, a cloth, and a small round pebble he had found the previous year and had no intention of ever losing.

Noura was asking ten questions at the same time. Thuraya, to whom most of the questions were being addressed, was answering them in the specific rotation of a teacher who has learned not to panic when ten questions arrive at once.

"Where are we going?" "Al-Lajal." "Is it far?" "A little." "Will there be food?" "Yes." "What food?" "You will see." "Is there a falaj?" "Yes." "Can we swim?" "That depends."

Marwa, Rahma, and Salima were sharing dates from a small paper twist. They were sharing with the ritual care of girls who had been sharing food with each other since they had been small enough for dates to be a significant event in the day — one date for Marwa, one for Rahma, one for Salima, then the whole rotation again, without any of them needing to say whose turn it was.

Ali was trying to climb into the truck first. He had, by the look of him, already tried twice. The first time, he had not been able to reach the high side. The second time, a larger boy had lifted him partway and then, deciding the joke was over, had put him down. He was now, on his third attempt, recruiting a small stool from beside the wall.

Safiya — dressed in her longest skirt, with the scarf she had learned to tie properly, and carrying the small cloth bag that Thuraya's mother had given her last week — was fixing her scarf again. It had slipped slightly in the walk from the house. She was getting better at it. Not expert. But better.

She was smiling. She was, she realised as she caught sight of her own face in the dusty truck window, genuinely smiling — the small, unforced smile of a woman who had woken up that morning and been looking forward to this.

She looked at the back of the pickup. At the high metal sides. At the bare, open bed with no seats, no belts, no anything except the sky above.

"Are you sure this is safe?" she asked.

Thuraya laughed — the clean, warm laugh she always laughed when Safiya's English concept of safety collided with the Omani concept of how things actually worked.

"Very safe," she said. "You just hold tight… and don't fly away."

"Fly away," Safiya repeated, slowly, as though trying out the phrase in her mouth. "That is my new concern."

Mr. John, meanwhile — who had been standing beside the front tyre, hands on his hips — was looking at the truck with shining eyes. Actually shining. His eye was mostly recovered now, and he was using its full expression with the delight of a man who had, for many days, been limited to half his face.

"Adventure!" he announced. "I love adventure."

Thuraya looked at him. She smiled in a particular way — a smile Safiya had, in her weeks here, begun to identify as the Thuraya-knows-something-that-John-does-not smile. It was small. It was patient. It was waiting.

"Yes," she said. "You love it."

Uncle Nasser — a broad man with a sun-dark face, a generous laugh, and the specific, settled competence of a man who had been driving village trucks since before half the children in his bed were born — clapped his hands once.

"Yalla! Everyone up!"

The word yalla — Safiya had learned — contained, in its two small syllables, the entire Omani concept of let us go, quickly, but also gently, but also now. It was a word that did the work of several English sentences.

The children began to climb. Ahmed, composed. Mansoor, too fast, and nearly over the far side. Noura, still asking questions. Ali, with his small stool. Khalil, with a small, private, scheming satisfaction.

Soon, they were all in the back of the pickup — the children holding the high sides, John and Safiya sitting on the metal floor with them, trying not to fall. Thuraya — being Thuraya, who was not to be argued with — took the front seat next to Uncle Nasser.

The engine started. It was the loud, asthmatic, wholehearted cough of an old truck being asked, one more time, to do what it had always done. It caught. It held. It began to move.


## Scene Two — Al-Naabi and the Fenzuz


The pickup rolled forward, and dust rose behind them in a long, soft plume that did not settle quickly. The road out of Samail was long, brown, and full of stones. It was not the London concept of a road. It was the Omani concept — a path that had been worn into the earth by the decades of feet and hooves and tyres that had gone the same way before them.

Mansoor, from his spot near the side of the pickup, shouted — as though the noise of the engine required him to shout, and possibly also because he was Mansoor —

"Where are we going?"

Thuraya turned in her seat. She had to half-lean out of the window to make herself heard.

"To Al-Lajal. In Wadi Al-Maawil."

"What is Al-Lajal?" Ali asked.

Thuraya turned — not quite fully, because the road was rough, but enough to meet Ali's eyes. And she said the next sentence with the specific, quiet pride of a person who had been waiting for the question.

"It is a village. And it is full of my family. The Al-Naabi tribe."

"The tribe?" John asked. The word had surfaced in him with the mild puzzlement that he always carried when an Arabic-English word appeared. "What is a tribe?"

Thuraya smiled. The smile had affection in it — not the affection of correcting a mistake, but the affection of opening a door that she was happy to open.

"In Oman," she said, "people belong to tribes. Big families. Old families. We say: this is my tribe. This is my name. This is my history."

She said each of those three sentences slowly. With a small pause after each. As though handing over three pieces of the same picture, one at a time, so that John could hold them one at a time.

And Safiya, sitting in the back with her hands braced against the side of the pickup, felt the sentences land. They were three sentences that English, in its economical way, would have compressed into one. Arabic had decided, long ago, that they were three different sentences. And now, hearing them separated, Safiya understood why. My tribe was one thing. My name was another. My history was a third. They belonged together, yes, but they were also — each of them — its own kind of gift.

The truck bumped over a particularly deep ridge. Every child in the back rose, briefly, into the air, and came down again laughing. Safiya grabbed the side of the truck. John's bag slid across the metal floor and came to rest against Ahmed's feet — Ahmed, predictably, picked it up and held it carefully until John could reach for it again.

"In Oman," Thuraya continued, raising her voice a little over the engine, "we also have something called tagheera."

Safiya looked up. "Tagheera?"

"Yes," Thuraya said. She was warming to the subject now — her voice had the specific clarity that she used when she was teaching. "It is like… a family symbol. A sign. Every tribe has one."

She paused. A small, satisfied pause — the pause of a person who has arrived at the name they most wanted to say.

She smiled.

"For Al-Naabi, the tagheera is " data-m="The traditional tagheera of the Al-Naabi tribe; a word passed down through generations as the Al-Naabi's sign and call" data-s="Al-Naabi's call, tribal-word" data-x="Fenzuz was the Al-Naabi's word. It belonged to them the way a name belongs to a family.">fenzuz."

The word landed in the back of the pickup and — as words sometimes do — rearranged the air around it. The children who were old enough to know it looked up, nodding small nods. The ones who were younger were watching Thuraya's face for clues about how the word should be received.

John tried it. He tried it carefully, the way he tried every new Arabic word — sounding it out slowly, with his English mouth, which was forever a little too polite for this language.

"Fen… zoz?"

Mansoor — who had been silent for an unprecedented forty seconds, and who could bear the silence no longer — corrected him. Loudly. With the specific grave authority of a boy who had just been given, by the mispronunciation of a foreigner, the chance to be the expert.

"Fenzuz," he said — precisely, as though he were pronouncing the name of his own grandfather.

Everyone laughed.

John, with the good humour of a man who had accepted, some weeks ago, that his linguistic dignity was, in this village, permanently expendable, bowed his head to Mansoor.

"Thank you, teacher."

Mansoor, beaming, sat down with the satisfaction of a boy who had just been publicly promoted.


## Scene Three — My Name Is, I Am From, I Belong To


The truck jounced on. Mountains rose on the left. Farms appeared on the right — long narrow plots of palm trees and green, each one edged by its own small falaj, each one working quietly as the pickup rolled past.

Thuraya, turning in her seat — holding the side of the open window with one hand to keep her balance — said,

"Today, we will practice something very important."

She produced, from her small cloth bag, a piece of paper. She had prepared it the night before — Safiya could see the evenness of the writing, the care of the three lines — and she held it up so that the children could read it.

My name is…I am from…I belong to…

Safiya clapped her hands — a small, delighted clap, the kind teachers give when they have just been handed, by another teacher, the exact lesson they would have chosen themselves.

"Great! Let's practice!"

She turned to the boy nearest her — who, of course, was Ahmed, because Ahmed had positioned himself, Safiya had noticed, in the exact place he judged most likely to be called on first.

"You first."

Ahmed sat up straight — which, in a bumping truck, was a minor feat of posture. He adjusted his bag on his lap. He cleared his throat. He spoke slowly, with deliberation, pronouncing each word the way he had been practising pronouncing English words since the day Mr. John had first walked into the school.

"My name is Ahmed. I am from Samail. I belong to… my family."

Thuraya smiled. "Good. Very good."

Khalil, who had been waiting for his turn with the specific restlessness of a boy whose joke needed to be delivered while it was still fresh, spoke next.

"My name is Khalil. I am from here. I belong to… trouble."

Everyone laughed. Thuraya laughed too — with the small, resigned laugh of a teacher who had been waiting for exactly that sentence and could not, in all honesty, disagree with it.

Noura went next. Noura always volunteered. Noura had an opinion about this format and was going to honour it.

"My name is Noura. I am from Samail. I belong to my mother."

Safiya watched Thuraya's face as Noura said this. A small softness came into Thuraya's expression. I belong to my mother was, for a ten-year-old Omani girl, the specific, correct, most honest sentence. It was where she truly, physically, emotionally belonged. Every mother in the village owned, in this specific way, her small daughter. And the small daughter knew it.

Marwa — softer voice — "My name is Marwa. I am from the farms."

Rahma — even softer — "My name is Rahma. I belong to my grandmother."

Another small softness crossed Thuraya's face. Rahma's grandmother — Safiya did not know her story, but Thuraya evidently did — was the person who, in Rahma's world, held the central space.

Then Mansoor.

Mansoor stood up in the truck — which was dangerous, and which Safiya would have corrected, except that the sentence he was about to deliver was so obviously a performance that correction would have been beside the point — and shouted, over the noise of the engine:

"My name is Mansoor. I am from everywhere!"

John laughed — the real laugh, the tired-happy laugh that had been building since the truck had started. "Everywhere," he said, "is a big place."

Mansoor bowed. And sat down.

Then — quietly, in the middle of this lesson that she had orchestrated, without calling attention to herself, Thuraya said her own.

"My name is Thuraya. I am from Al-Lajal. I belong to Al-Naabi."

The three sentences landed in the back of the pickup with the weight of a small personal testimony. She had said them the way a person says their own name at a gathering — plainly, completely, without ornament. My name is. I am from. I belong to. Three different kinds of knowing. All of them true.

Mr. John looked at her. Looked at her differently, Safiya thought, than he had looked at her ten minutes ago.

"Thuraya," he said, quietly, "that is a beautiful name."

Thuraya smiled. It was a soft smile — softer than her teacher-smile, softer than the smile she used for jokes. It was the smile of a person being told something about herself that she had inherited.

"My grandmother's name was also Thuraya."

Safiya looked up. Genuinely surprised. "Really?"

"Yes," Thuraya said. And then, as the truck moved between two sharp hills and the light shifted across her face, she told them the story that she had, perhaps, been saving for this exact ride — on the road to the exact village — with a small cargo of children and two English teachers who had now, finally, earned the story.

"My grandfather, Ali, was a teacher of fiqh. He taught in the mosque in Samail. That is why he stayed there."

She paused — not uncertainly. Just with the small, deliberate pause of a person telling a family story and giving each sentence its space.

"He married my grandmother. And her name was Thuraya."

John's eyes became big. Both of them. The swollen one had fully, finally, recovered — which was a small, cosmic gift, because what he was hearing deserved two working eyes.

"So… your name is the same as your grandmother's."

"Yes," Thuraya said.

She looked out at the road. The mountains were closer now. A falaj, somewhere in the middle distance, caught the sun and sent back a small bright line.

"Names have stories here."


## Scene Four — Falaj Al-Lajal


The car moved between mountains and green places. Palm trees appeared. Farms appeared. The light — which had been the honey-light of early morning — was, by now, the full working light of late morning. Every leaf showed. Every stone on the path was visible.

Suddenly, John pointed.

"Is that the falaj?"

Below them, curving along the edge of the palms, was a long, bright ribbon of water. It ran between stone banks. It caught the sun. It moved with the specific, purposeful flow of water that had been moving along this same course for longer than the village had been measuring.

Thuraya turned. "Yes. Falaj Al-Lajal."

John grinned. His grin was the grin of a man who had, very recently, washed his face in the cold falaj of Al-Qurawashiyah and survived, and who now believed — with the specific confidence of a man who had survived something once — that he was equipped for any falaj that this country could produce.

"Swimming!"

Thuraya looked at him.

It was the same small strange smile she had produced that morning — the Thuraya-knows-something-that-John-does-not smile — only larger now, because the moment it had been preparing for was arriving.

"Don't be too excited," she said. "Not every falaj is… friendly."

John did not, it was clear, hear the warning properly. He was already measuring the water with his eyes. He was planning his jump.

The truck slowed. Uncle Nasser pulled it off the road onto a flat patch of hard earth beside a grove of palms. He cut the engine. The specific, sudden quiet that follows the cutting of a loud engine — when the ear, released, hears the small sounds again — arrived. Palm leaves. The falaj. A bird somewhere. Everything else that had been waiting.

When they arrived — when they climbed down out of the pickup and looked around — the place was beautiful. Green trees. Old houses — older than the houses in Samail, in that specific way older houses are older, with walls that had settled a little further into the earth, with doorways that had been patched and re-patched over generations. Water moving between stones.

The children ran. Of course they ran. They did not need to be told where to go — they were all young enough that water was, fundamentally, the thing one ran toward when one arrived at a new place with water.

"Wait!" Safiya shouted. "Careful!"

But John — John — was already taking off his shoes. He was taking them off with the brisk, cheerful efficiency of a man who had, at some point in the preceding three minutes, decided that this was the moment he had been waiting for since London.

"Cold water!" he announced, to no one in particular, his shoes now paired neatly on a flat stone. "I am ready!"

Thuraya opened her mouth.

"John —"

Too late.

John jumped.

He jumped with the abandon of a tall Englishman who had been waiting, all morning, for the exact moment when he could fling himself into a clear cold falaj and cool himself completely.

The falaj was not cold.

The falaj was not even cool.

The falaj was hot.

For approximately one-third of a second, John's body processed this new fact in silence. The water closed around his ankles, his calves, his thighs. His brain, working at the speed of light, compared expected temperature to actual temperature and produced, as its reply, a single Arabic-adjacent syllable that he had not previously known he possessed.

"AAAAAAAAAA! WAFAQRIYA!"

He launched himself out of the falaj like a man who had just discovered, mid-flight, that he was on fire. Both feet cleared the water. He landed, staggering, on the stone bank. He raised his arms. His face, which was still turning red for unrelated reasons — the heat of the water, the embarrassment of the situation, the sudden flood of adrenaline — settled into the specific expression of a man who had just been betrayed, at a deep level, by a country he had been learning to trust.

"It's HOT!" he shouted. "The water is HOT!"

The children, who had been standing a careful distance away — their mothers and aunts had, for generations, taught them about this falaj, and they had known, unlike John, exactly what would happen — fell on the ground laughing.

Not metaphorically. Literally. Mansoor — of course Mansoor — sat down first, rocked backward, and disappeared into the long grass by the falaj in a paroxysm of laughter that would last, by Safiya's estimation, approximately four minutes. Khalil followed him down. Noura put her hand over her mouth and giggled helplessly. Ahmed — Ahmed — allowed himself a single, undignified snort.

Mansoor, from the grass, managed to speak.

"Teacher is cooked."

Khalil, finding breath for exactly one sentence, added — "Falaj soup."

Thuraya — who had been watching the whole sequence from the stone bank with her arms crossed, and with the small satisfied patience of a person whose warning had been completely ignored — said, calmly:

"I told you not to be too excited."

John — dripping, red-faced, still hopping slightly on one foot — looked at her.

"You knew."

"Yes," Thuraya said. The smile she had been holding all morning finally arrived at its full size. "Falaj Al-Lajal is warm."

"Warm," John repeated, with the quiet emphasis of a man who felt that warm was an inadequate, possibly diplomatic word for what he had just experienced. "It is warm."

"Warm," she agreed. "Very warm."


## Scene Five — Tell Me About Yourself


Safiya — laughing, dry, still seated on the truck bed where she had sensibly stayed during her husband's immersion — climbed down now. She crossed to where the children had gathered. She arranged her skirt beneath her and sat on the stone bank by the water.

"Okay," she said, with the warm decisiveness of a teacher reclaiming her morning. "Lesson time!"

The children, still subsiding from their laughter at Mr. John, turned to her. They arranged themselves around her in the specific loose semicircle that children make around a sitting adult — a formation that required no discussion.

John, still standing on the stone bank, slowly put his shoes back on. He joined them — somewhat gingerly — and sat at the edge of the group, his dignity in slightly better condition than his feet.

"Now," Safiya said, her eyes bright, "tell me about yourself."

She looked around. She was going to choose someone. She needed — Thuraya's practice had produced a beautiful set of sentences, but they had cycled through the usual performers. Safiya wanted to hear the ones who had not yet spoken.

She pointed — gently — to the boy who had been the quietest in the pickup. The boy with the small stool that morning. The boy who had tried three times to climb into the truck and had, in the end, used engineering.

"You."

Ali — the small, careful Ali, the one who drew instead of writing — looked up. He had not expected to be chosen. He had been braced for Safiya to pick, as teachers usually did, one of the louder children.

He took a breath. He sat up — not as straight as Ahmed, because no one could sit as straight as Ahmed, but straight enough to honour the moment.

He spoke — slowly. Carefully. In the specific measured English of a boy whose mouth was still learning the shapes of a language that had, until recently, not been in it.

"My name is Ali. I am from Samail. I belong to my family."

He looked at Safiya. His eyes had the small, careful watchfulness of a child who has just done something difficult and wants to know if he has done it right.

"Good!" Safiya said. The word came out warmer than she had planned. The boy's small English had hit her more squarely than she had expected. She had, without meaning to, allowed her voice to brighten.

Ali smiled. Small. Relieved. Proud.

And then, around him, the others took their turns.

They practiced again. And again. Safiya went around the small circle and heard each sentence. My name is. I am from. I belong to. The sentences arrived in different voices — small voices, loud voices, Mansoor's definite voice, Ahmed's precise voice, Noura's rapid voice — and each sentence, with each repetition, became a little steadier in the child's mouth. Each time, something small was put right. Each time, a new mouth shaped the English of I belong to more confidently. And each time, another small piece of the Omani world made its way, politely, through three English sentences.

My name is Marwa. I am from Samail. I belong to the farms.

My name is Rahma. I am from Al-Qurawashiyah. I belong to my grandmother.

My name is Salima. I am from Samail. I belong to my father.

My name is Khalil. I am from here. I belong to trouble. (Again. He could not resist. Safiya let him.)

My name is Ahmed. I am from Samail. I belong to my family.

My name is Mansoor. I am from everywhere. (Also again. Also let through.)

And then, after a long, contented silence — in which the falaj continued its soft hush, in which somewhere nearby a small bird called in an unfamiliar pattern, in which a distant woman somewhere in the village was humming, faintly, in a rhythm that was not quite a song — Mr. John spoke.

He had been, for some time, quiet. His feet had dried. His dignity had, with the dignity of time, partially returned. He cleared his throat.

"My name is John. I am from London."

He paused. A long pause. A pause in which the children, and Thuraya, and Safiya, all looked at him — waiting for his third sentence.

He considered.

He looked around the circle of small faces. At Mansoor, grass in his hair. At Ahmed, bag still on his lap. At Noura, already preparing her next question. At Ali, still quietly proud of his three correct sentences.

At Thuraya. At Safiya.

And then — with the small, specific smile of a man who had, less than twenty minutes earlier, been boiled by a falaj, and who had somehow, since then, come out the other side into a clearer kind of day — he finished.

"I belong to… this lesson."

Thuraya laughed. Safiya laughed. The children laughed — Mansoor the loudest, because he did not fully understand the joke but was unwilling to be excluded from any laughter.

And somewhere, behind the truck, in the shade of a palm tree at the edge of the farm — a small, white, horned head appeared. Malood — whom no one had seen climb into the truck that morning, but who had, in the way of Malood, evidently done so — stepped out of the shade. He walked, with the exact, unhurried, proprietary stride that was his signature, into the middle of the circle.

He looked at the children. He looked at Safiya. He looked at John. He looked — unmistakably — at the stone bank, where John's small bag was open, and where a piece of paper was visible.

He began to walk toward it.

"MALOOD —"

But the children were already laughing again. And Safiya, watching — dry, safe, seated in the warm sun beside the warm falaj, in a village her grandmother would never have imagined her granddaughter visiting — understood that some things travel with you, and that the goat was apparently one of them.

The lesson continued, but with one extra student.


## Scene Six — The Practice That Did Not End


They practiced again and again.

That was the texture of the afternoon. Not one lesson that ended. A lesson that kept returning — in new mouths, with new variations, with small Arabic interruptions, with Mansoor standing up and sitting down and standing up again, with Malood being politely escorted away from John's bag on approximately four separate occasions — and with the falaj, the warm falaj, the kind, patient hot falaj of Al-Lajal, continuing its small hush beside them the whole time.

The sentences came out different each time.

My name is Ahmed. I am from Samail. I belong to my family.

My name is Noura. I am from Samail. I belong to my mother. And my father. And my grandmother. (She had, on the second pass, added people to her list. Safiya, who understood what she was doing, did not correct her.)

My name is Ali. I am from Samail. I belong to my family. And today… I belong to Al-Lajal a little.

That sentence — Safiya caught it. It was Ali's own. She had not fed it to him. He had extended the template on his own. She looked at Thuraya. Thuraya, watching, gave her the very smallest nod.

My name is Marwa. I am from Samail. I belong to the farms. And to my sisters.

My name is Rahma. I am from Al-Qurawashiyah. I belong to my grandmother. Who is sick this week.

Safiya's heart tightened slightly. Rahma had said this sentence without drama, without asking for sympathy, simply including her grandmother in the sentence about herself, because the sentence about herself would have been incomplete without her. Safiya did not correct her. She only said, gently, "I hope she is better soon."

"Thank you, teacher."

My name is Salima. I am from Samail. I belong to my father. And my mother. And my brother who is a fisherman in Sur.

My name is Khalil. I am from here. I belong to trouble.

"Khalil," Safiya said, with mock severity. "Can you try a new third sentence?"

Khalil considered this. He considered it for a long time. He was, behind the scheming, a thoughtful boy — Safiya had begun to notice — and he was now, visibly, trying to think of a better third sentence.

Finally —

"My name is Khalil. I am from here. I belong to… my friends."

He said it with the specific, slightly embarrassed voice of a boy who had surprised himself by meaning it. The other children, for one soft second, went quiet. Ali looked up at him. Khalil, noticing, went immediately red and looked away.

"Beautiful," Safiya said, with deliberate lightness — giving him back his room to be himself. "That is a beautiful third sentence."

My name is Mansoor. I am from everywhere. I belong to the truck.

That one got a laugh. Mansoor beamed again.

And John, on his second pass — hair dry now, shoes firmly on, dignity fully restored — said:

"My name is John. I am from London. I belong to… this country, a little, now."

He said it shyly. The way a man says a sentence he has not yet earned but hopes to earn. Thuraya looked at him — the long, considered look she gave to sentences that were, in her judgment, true enough to honour.

She nodded once.

She did not correct the grammar. The grammar was, in any case, fine.

The afternoon moved on. Uncle Nasser — who had disappeared, at some point, toward a relative's house in the village — returned with a cloth bundle of bread and dates and cold yoghurt, which he laid out on the stone bank beside the falaj. The children ate. John ate. Safiya, who had been hungry and had not known it, ate slowly and completely. Thuraya ate looking at the water. The water continued. The sun moved, slowly, across the palm fronds above them.

And at some point — Safiya did not know quite when — the lesson became something else. Mansoor was telling Ali about a cousin of his who had once fallen into this exact falaj. Ahmed was writing — yes, writing — the sentence I belong to my family in his slate, with three different decorative underlinings. Noura was asking Thuraya, quietly, about her grandmother — the grandmother who had also been called Thuraya, the one who had married the fiqh-teacher in the mosque. Thuraya was telling her. Noura was, for once, listening more than asking.

Safiya sat with her back against the warm stone bank. She let the afternoon be whatever it wanted to be. She did not pull out her notebook. She did not translate the moment into a lesson plan. She simply — for the first time since arriving in this country — let a whole afternoon pass through her without trying to catch it.

When the sun had lowered, and Uncle Nasser had tapped the side of the pickup, and the children — reluctantly, slowly — had climbed back in, Safiya walked to the falaj, knelt beside it, and — very carefully, fully aware this time of what she was doing — dipped her fingers into the warm water.

It was warm. Genuinely warm. Like a bath, but older. She held her fingers there for a long second, and felt the specific warmth that came, Thuraya had later explained, from somewhere deep in the earth.

She lifted her hand. She stood.

She walked back to the truck.

She climbed in. The children were, at last, quiet — the specific quiet of children who have had a full day and are now beginning to understand, in their small bodies, that they are going to sleep on the ride home.

The engine started. The truck rolled forward. The dust rose behind them, as always. Al-Lajal disappeared around a bend.

In the back of the pickup, Safiya — without meaning to, without planning to, because the day had earned it from her — leaned her head against the warm metal side of the truck and, quietly, to no one in particular, said her own:

"My name is Safiya. I am from London. I belong to…"

She did not finish the sentence. Not because she did not know. Because she did not yet want to decide.

Thuraya, in the front seat, heard her — somehow, over the engine — and turned, briefly, to look at her through the small back window.

She did not say anything.

She only — very slightly — smiled.


# Palm Ten — Smoke, Truck, and an Englishman Speaking Arabic



## Scene One — Before the Bell


The morning bell did not ring yet, but Samail already felt awake.

That was how this village worked. The bell was a formality, not an announcement. By the time its brass rang out of Abu Salim's hand, the houses had already turned over, the doorways had already exhaled their first people, the small lanes had already collected the first soft arguments of the day between mothers and the children they were trying to dress.

A pale sun climbed slowly behind the mountains. The air smelled like dust, like the farms waking up on the far side of the wall, and — Safiya could catch it, faintly, on the edge of the breeze — like something warm being cooked far away. A stew, perhaps. Someone's mother, generous in her kitchen.

Mr. John stepped out of the simple house, holding a small notebook.

He was squinting at the pale sun like a man who had misplaced his glasses somewhere in his sleep and had not yet gathered the energy to find them — though John, in fact, did not wear glasses. He simply had the specific face of a man who, in this country, was always almost about to.

Safiya — and she was Safiya now, properly, completely, everyone in the village called her that and she had stopped correcting them some time ago — stood behind him at the doorway, tying her scarf.

She tied it neatly. Carefully. With the specific small satisfaction of a woman who had, after several weeks of daily failure, finally learned a skill that her neighbours had been doing since they were six. She tied it, and then — unable to help herself — glanced down at the edges to make sure they sat right, in the way a child checks their shoelaces for the third time in the first week of school. She looked proud of it. Like it was a new rule she had finally learned perfectly on the first try.

Ms. Thuraya appeared at the end of the neighbour's path. Her steps were quick and confident. In one hand she carried a cloth bag. Behind her ear — held there the way carpenters hold pencils — was a small piece of chalk.

"Good morning," she said, in English, smiling.

John grinned. "Good morning! Today is… Monday?"

Thuraya laughed. It was her small, indulgent, teacher-laugh — the laugh she reserved for Mr. John's hopeful attempts at confidence.

"Mr. John," she said, "you still need to learn the days."

Then — before anyone could quite decide who would answer next — something small and white hopped into the yard.

It hopped like a sneaky cloud with legs.

It was, of course, Malood.

He marched — there was no other word for it — straight to John's bag. He stopped. He sniffed it seriously — the specific, unhurried, professional sniff of a goat who had, in his short life, learned that bags often contained interesting things. Then he tried to bite the corner.

"Hey! No! That is my teaching bag!"

John waved his hands — waved them the way airport men waved at planes, broad and slow and very important. The airport gesture had no effect on goats. This had been, by now, well established in the scientific record of the village.

Malood didn't care. Malood never cared.

Thuraya bent down. She did it easily — it was the specific low crouch of a village woman who had been squatting since she could walk. She patted Malood on the flank.

"He belongs to Aunt Azizah next door," she said. "But he loves your house like it is his own."

Safiya whispered, mostly to John — "He is… too confident."

Then came a sound that made the village shift its attention — the specific small shift of an entire place that has been, until that moment, reading its own page, and has now, all together, turned the page at the same time.

A loud engine. A truck. A man's voice calling from far away — calling the way a voice calls when it is used to a loudspeaker, even when it is not currently using one.

Thuraya's eyes narrowed. The small, quick narrowing of a person who had identified the sound before her mind had finished naming it.

"Oh no. The municipality truck."

John blinked. "The what?"

Before she could explain, the pickup appeared at the end of the dusty road. It moved slowly — slowly in the way a large insect moves slowly — like a giant beetle that knew exactly where it was going and had no interest in arriving quickly.

Two men sat in the back with a large metal tank. And behind them — rolling slowly outward, filling the dusty street with a pale foggy cloud — was the smoke.

Safiya's eyebrows jumped. "Is that… smoke?"

Thuraya pulled her scarf a little tighter across her face — the small, practised gesture of a woman who had seen this truck many times in her childhood and knew the correct response.

"Pesticide," she said. "They spray it to kill insects. People used to do it a lot."

Malood made a tiny noise — a small, disgruntled noise that sounded like a complaint — and then, for emphasis, coughed once.

John stepped forward, concerned. "Is it safe?"

Thuraya opened her mouth.

She did not get the chance to answer.

Because the children had arrived.


## Scene Two — The Smoke Chase


A group of boys came running behind the truck. They were yelling. They were laughing. They were chasing the smoke — chasing it as though it were a game that had been invented, specifically, for their benefit.

One of them was Mansoor.

Of course it was Mansoor.

He ran like he was being chased by a lion. His small legs were moving at a speed that his body could not quite sustain, and his face — his face was pure joy. Not the joy of winning a game. The older, simpler joy of running through a cloud of chemical smoke in a village street with his friends and pretending he was somewhere else entirely.

Thuraya shouted — the specific teacher-shout that carried across open spaces with a precision that surprised people who did not know her —

"MANSOOR! STOP!"

He didn't stop.

John jumped forward. The jump was sudden, entire, and had nothing English in it — the specific instant activation of teacher-mode that happens, in good teachers, without any decision being made.

He grabbed a thin stick from the fence — Safiya would later try to remember where the stick had come from, and would fail — and lifted it like a traffic sign.

"STOP!" he shouted, in full English. "GO INSIDE!"

The boys froze — for half a second, no longer — and then yelled back happily. They did not understand the English. They understood that the English teacher had joined the game, which was, categorically, even better than the game had been thirty seconds earlier.

One boy shouted something in Omani Arabic — short, fast, the specific teasing cadence of a ten-year-old who has just thought of an excellent insult. Another boy laughed. Mansoor, from the middle of the cloud, waved — waved the way fans wave to favourite actors in a play — as though John were now part of the show.

"NOOO!" John shouted again, louder. "INSIDE! INSIDE!"

Safiya covered her mouth with her hand. She was laughing. She was laughing properly — the undignified laugh that a wife permits herself only when her husband is, in her view, being magnificent at something and wrong about something at the same time.

"John," she managed, "you look like a British police officer."

John, ignoring her, took one firm step toward the smoke.

And slipped.

His shoe hit a wet patch — one of the small overflow trails from the falaj channel that crossed the path every few metres. His foot slid. His other foot, attempting a rescue, arrived too late. His arms went up. His notebook — the small, professional notebook that had been in his hand all morning — sailed, in a clean arc, into a small patch of dust and palm leaves.

Malood, from beside the door, jumped sideways with the specific self-preserving quickness of a goat who wanted, very clearly, no part of this. His posture said, Not my problem.

John fell.

Face-first. Into the dusty ground.

And then, as though the morning had been waiting for exactly this composition — the smoke rolled over him.

The pale fog passed across his body. Slowly. Completely. It filled the air around him. It settled on his shoulders. It settled on his hair. It settled in the long thin lines where his face had, a second earlier, been a healthy English colour.

When the cloud passed — when the air cleared, and the truck's tail-lights were receding down the road — John stood up slowly.

He looked… terrifying.

His hair was full of dust. His face was a pale, even grey — the grey of old walls, of old tombs, of plaster that had lost its paint decades ago. His eyes, watering from the chemical, were bloodshot and wide.

He looked, exactly, like a ghost that had lost a fight.

Safiya screamed. Then she laughed — so hard that she had to catch herself against the clay wall of their house, one hand flat against the mud, the other over her stomach.

Thuraya covered her mouth. She was attempting to be polite. She was failing. "Oh my…"

The children screamed too. But the children did not scream from fear of the smoke.

They screamed from fear of John.

"Aboooyaaah!" Mansoor shouted, dramatically — and half the kids ran away as though the ghost were real.

John wiped his eyes. He coughed. He coughed again.

"I am… fine," he said.

He was not fine. He was standing in a grey cloud of his own dust, with a split lip he had not yet noticed, in a village street, while a municipality truck disappeared around a corner. But he was upright. And for a man who had just been face-first in the ground, upright was a position he was prepared to defend.


## Scene Three — Shayb Khalaf


Then a shadow moved at the edge of the smoke.

Not dramatically — no village shadow ever moved dramatically. This one simply moved, the slow deliberate movement of a tall thin person emerging from a thinning fog. A walking stick. A worn shoulder. A white beard, partly visible first, then fully, then the whole man.

A thin old man. Wrinkled face — wrinkled like the earth after summer, like the specific deep cracks that formed in the wadis when the last rain had long since disappeared. Eyes that were, despite everything else, sharp.

The children saw him and froze.

It was a different kind of freezing than the freezing they had done when John had become a ghost. That earlier freezing had been fun. This freezing was not fun. This freezing had the specific reverent quality of children who had been told, since they were very small, about exactly this man.

Some whispered — not loud enough to be a sentence, just loud enough to be shared — "Khalaf…"

Someone hissed, "The wizard!"

Safiya leaned toward Thuraya. Quietly. She was not alarmed — she knew Khalaf now, she had met him, she had accepted her ring from his hand — but the children's reaction was its own kind of information.

"Why," she whispered, "do they look like they saw a monster?"

Thuraya whispered back, keeping her voice low and easy — the tone of a woman demystifying the whole village mythology in one sentence.

"They think he is a sorcerer. He lives alone in an old clay house. People fear him."

The old man — Shayb Khalaf — walked straight to John.

He did not stop. He did not slow. He crossed the small patch of dusty ground between where he had been and where John was standing, and he grabbed John's arm — not kindly, not unkindly, just grabbed, the way an old farmer would steady a small animal that had gotten turned around.

"Stand up properly," he said. Rough — but not cruel.

John, still dizzy, his head still thick with dust, his mouth still full of the chemical taste of the pesticide, suddenly — and without any decision that his English-trained mind could later account for — spoke in Arabic.

The Arabic that came out of him was not textbook Arabic. It was not the polite formal Arabic of the phrasebook Mr. John had carried on the plane. It was local Arabic — the specific Samaili phrasing of a man who had somehow, in the last few seconds, become a villager.


> يح… حسبتك جني! خوفتني… الله يصرفك صرفة!


Safiya gasped. Thuraya's mouth dropped open.

Khalaf stared at John. For a long second. Then — without warning, because Khalaf did not warn people — gave a short laugh.


> واحليلك باه… تفزع؟ خليك رجّال.


He lifted his stick a little. Just a little. A small, amused raising.


> والاّ بذي الخيزرانة ع ظهرك!


John's eyes widened.

And out of his mouth — still not English — came the answer. Fast. Almost polite. A villager's answer.


> هاعه… الشيمة تجمّل.


Then John stopped.

He blinked. Several times. Like his brain had just finished a process it had not been consulted about and was now — only now — arriving at the meeting to find out what had been decided.

He turned to Safiya. He whispered, in English, in the small, confused voice of a man who had just heard his own mouth say something his mind had not prepared —

"How did I… say that? I don't know those sentences."

Safiya collapsed — again, the wall, the stomach, the complete giving-way of a woman whose morning had produced, in fifteen minutes, more comic material than the previous three weeks combined.

"John," she managed, "you just threatened-corrected yourself in Arabic!"

Thuraya tried to look serious. Her shoulders shook with the effort. The effort was not sustainable.

"Mr. John," she said, with admirable composure, "you surprise me."

Malood, meanwhile — having noticed that no one was looking at him for the first time in forty seconds — was coughing, dramatically, once, twice, a third time, leaning his small body against the wall like an actor in a sad movie. Nobody paid attention.

The municipality truck moved on. The smoke thinned. The children, still partially scattered, returned carefully — peeking, first, from behind fences.

Mansoor stepped forward. Proud. "Teacher John… you are… ghost!"

John, still grey, still slightly unsteady, pointed at him with the firmness of a man who had decided that he had accepted enough indignity for one morning.

"No. I am teacher."

Thuraya clapped once. "Okay! Everyone… to school."


## Scene Four — The New Teachers


John walked into the classroom, later that day, with red, puffy eyes. He had washed — briefly, carelessly — but some of the grey was still lodged in his hair, and his eyes still carried the small chemical redness of a man who had been, very recently, face-to-face with a cloud.

The classroom was its usual self. The chalkboard was old and dusty — with the specific, faint ghost of yesterday's lesson still visible beneath the attempts to wipe it. The room smelled, as it always did, of chalk, of heat, and of the small, secret lunches hidden in pockets — dates, a piece of bread, a small twist of salt.

Near the door — and this was new — stood two teachers Safiya had never seen before.

A strong Egyptian man. Sharp moustache. Serious face. The specific upright stillness of a man who, in any country, could be instantly identified as the kind of man who does not tolerate nonsense.

"I am Mohammadeen," he said. Loud. Each syllable given its full weight. "Math teacher."

Beside him stood his wife — sporty, confident, wearing a tracksuit that had clearly never belonged to anyone but her.

"And I am Fatkat," she said. Equally loud. Equally unafraid. "Sports teacher."

The students sat straighter immediately. Not because anyone had told them to. Because the air in the room had, in the space of two sentences, shifted — and their small bodies had read the shift correctly.

Mohammadeen lifted a thin stick — not in anger, not as a threat, just as a symbol. A small, dry, clean statement of authority.

"Any noise," he said, "and I make you quiet faster than lightning."

Mansoor swallowed.

Visibly. Audibly. The specific swallow of a small boy who had just recalculated the morning's likely budget of mischief and found it considerably reduced.

Safiya whispered to Thuraya. "They look scared. And respectful. At the same time."

Thuraya whispered back. Matter-of-factly. Not judgmentally.

"That's how it was."

Three small words. That's how it was. Safiya felt the sentence settle. It was not — she understood — Thuraya defending a practice. It was Thuraya giving her a historical fact. A village school in 1973, in rural Oman, was a place where a stick on a desk was a convention, not a cruelty. It was what English classrooms had also been, once. And the children who sat up straighter when the stick appeared were not, in their own minds, being terrorised. They were being given the dignity of being taken seriously. That's how it was.

John cleared his throat — wincing slightly, because the cough from the pesticide had not yet fully left his chest.

"Today," he said, "we talk about… what happened."

He wrote on the board, in big careful letters:

PAST SIMPLE: Yesterday, I… / He… / They…

He turned around. "Repeat: Yesterday, I walked."

The class echoed — twelve small voices, layered, not quite perfectly together — "Yesterday, I walked."

John pointed at Mansoor. "Mansoor. Yesterday, you…?"

Mansoor stood up proudly. His small bodily re-calibration of five minutes ago had not survived the sight of his own name being called.

"Yesterday, I run."

John smiled. The smile was, for the first time that morning, unreserved. "Good! Say: I ran."

Mansoor frowned. The past tense of run was one of English's small betrayals — a verb that refused to follow the rule. He considered. He tried it.

"I ran."

John nodded. "Excellent."

Then — with the clean, ceremonial self-deprecation of a teacher who had decided to use his own disaster as the lesson material — John pointed at himself.

"Yesterday, I… fell."

The class burst into laughter.

Safiya, from the back — unable to resist — added: "And yesterday, Mr. John looked like a ghost."

John sighed. The sigh of a husband whose wife had, once again, been correct. "Yes. He looked like a ghost."

Thuraya raised one eyebrow. "He looked like a very dramatic ghost."

Mohammadeen watched — trying not to smile. Fatkat did not attempt the same discipline. She laughed openly. Her laugh was large, warm, and entirely unapologetic.

Then John lowered his voice. Teacher-serious again. The atmosphere of the room returned — almost — to lesson tone.

"Yesterday, the truck sprayed pesticide. What do we say?"

Safiya — who had, by now, become a regular part of the class — stepped in with hand gestures. She spoke slowly. Clearly. The way mothers speak when they are telling children the important words.

"We say: Don't run behind the truck. Go inside. Close the door."

Thuraya nodded. "Safety English."

John wrote more on the board:

Don't + verbDon't run. Don't breathe the smoke. Don't touch the spray.

Mansoor raised his hand. "Teacher… Malood… yesterday… Malood… cough?"

Safiya beamed. "Yes! Say: Malood coughed."

The students repeated. "Malood coughed."

John tapped the board. "Perfect."

He paused. He turned, slowly, toward Thuraya. His eyes had narrowed. Not with anger. With the specific investigative narrowness of a man who had not forgotten, despite the lesson, despite the smoke, despite the ghost, that something had happened that morning that had not yet been explained.

"I will ask you something," he said, "after class."

Thuraya's smile turned too innocent. The specific too-innocent smile of a woman who had been waiting for this conversation all morning.

"Of course."

John muttered to himself, in English, just loud enough for Safiya to catch — "Something strange is happening here."

And from the back of the class — from the seat against the far wall, the seat that was, Safiya had begun to notice, always occupied by the same quiet child — a boy watched everything with bright eyes.

Not speaking. Not joining. Just watching.

His name was Saeed.

And he was — she would only understand this later — already collecting secrets like they were treasure.


## Scene Five — The Forest of Fenzuz


Note: this scene belongs to Palm Nine — Al-Lajal — and is placed here because in the original manuscript it continues directly from the "practiced again and again" sentence at the end of that chapter. It shows what Mr. John was doing while Safiya taught the children their names. It is included now so that the full day at Al-Lajal is complete.

While Safiya had been sitting by the warm falaj teaching the children my name is, I am from, I belong to — over and over, in small patient rotations — Mr. John, some distance away, had been walking.

He was still, quietly, angry at the falaj.

The anger had the specific small flatness of anger that had been outlived by its own heat — reduced now to a small, patient, unreasonable grumpiness that had settled into his walking. Every step said: warm falaj. Every second step said: warm falaj. The word warm had acquired, for John, an entirely new and sinister meaning.

He walked between the palms at the edge of the grove. The light there was dappled. The ground was softer here — old fallen fronds, dry earth, the occasional small piece of — he stopped.

He had seen something in the trees.

He crouched. He examined. On the ground, half-hidden in the fronds, lay a scattering of small, distinctive shapes. Small. Particular. Exactly the shape of what Thuraya had shown them earlier, in the truck, when she had explained the Al-Naabi tagheera.

"Fenzuz," he whispered — to himself, because there was no one else.

His face illuminated. The grumpiness of the warm falaj, temporarily, departed.

He began to collect.

Quietly. One. Two. Three. Many. He moved around the fronds, methodically. He put them in his bag — the same bag that Malood had tried to eat that morning, the same bag whose paper Malood would also, later, eat.

"I will surprise Thuraya," he said to himself. "She will be pleased."

He was imagining, in his head, the small ceremonial moment of presenting her with the fenzuz. Look, Thuraya — I have brought you the mark of your tribe. It was, he felt, the kind of cultural gesture that would confirm, once and for all, that he was paying attention. That he had listened. That he understood.

When he walked back to the falaj, the children were beginning to finish their sentences. Mansoor was on his fourth I am from everywhere. Safiya was clapping, warm and tired. Thuraya was sitting cross-legged on the stone bank, her teacher-satisfaction visible.

John arrived with the specific quiet pleasure of a man about to present a gift.

He opened his bag. He held it toward her.

"Look!"

Thuraya looked inside.

For one full second, her face did nothing. Then the expression moved — through surprise, through recognition, through the specific amused disbelief of a woman who has just been given, by a well-meaning foreigner, exactly the wrong gift.

She laughed. A lot.

Not at him — with him. With that specific warm Thuraya-laugh that meant: you have done a thing so perfectly mistaken that I must, now, explain it to you, but I want you to understand first how much I appreciate the intention.

"You collected my tagheera," she said.

"Yes!" John said. Proudly. The specific proud tone of a man who had still not yet understood his own joke.

Thuraya shook her head. The head-shake was soft. It carried its own warmth.

"Only Al-Naabi," she said, "can use it."

John looked at the bag.

He looked at the small, distinctive, Al-Naabi-only shapes he had, with great care, for the last twenty minutes, been collecting for a woman who was the only person at the falaj who was, in fact, entitled to them.

"Oh."

The children — who had, in the last ten seconds, realised what had happened — laughed. Again. The specific laughter they had been laughing all day, at this tall Englishman who had, once again, found the most culturally inappropriate gift available in a fifty-metre radius.

John, with the weary grace of a man who had now been laughed at twice in one day by the same audience, bowed his head slightly.

"I will put them back," he said, "where I found them."

And later, as the sun lowered, and Uncle Nasser drove the truck slowly back along the dusty road, Mansoor — unable to let a whole afternoon of excellent material pass unused — leaned toward John.

"Teacher… will you swim again?"

John said, quickly — a sentence he had evidently prepared — "No."

Safiya smiled. Softly. "But you learned something today."

"Yes," John said. "Always ask before you jump."

Thuraya laughed. "And always listen to Omani teachers."

That night, at the small wooden table in their simple house, John wrote in his notebook. He wrote slowly, the specific slowness of a man committing a lesson to paper properly, so that he could not, later, pretend he had not learned it.

Today, I learned about names, tribes, and hot falaj.And I learned that not all water is friendly.

He paused. He looked at the sentence. Then — with the small amused honesty of a man who was, finally, finding his place — he added one more line, beneath the first two:

Also — never trust Thuraya's smile.


## Scene Six — The Boy Who Collected Secrets


Back in the classroom, on the day of the smoke, the lesson had ended. The bell — Abu Salim's bell, the brass bell that every child in the village had learned to answer — had rung. The small storms had rushed out, half-English, half-Omani, shouting about ghosts and lightning and Mohammadeen's stick and Malood's cough.

The classroom was, once again, that specific kind of empty that classrooms become when their children have just left — the air still holding the shape of them, the desks still warm, the chalk dust slowly settling.

John stood at the front, tapping the board once, idly, with the flat edge of his chalk. Safiya was gathering papers. Thuraya was cleaning the smaller slate by the wall.

Mohammadeen and Fatkat had already gone — to the teachers' small shared room where, Safiya had by now understood, the four adults in the school were going to have to share their tea and their working space for the foreseeable future.

John, with the specific seriousness of a man returning to an unfinished conversation, turned.

"Thuraya."

"Yes, Mr. John."

He pointed at his own mouth. His own English mouth, which had — that morning — produced Arabic sentences that his own English mind did not know.

"Explain."

Thuraya set down her slate. She sat on the small stool at the front. She considered her answer. Then she answered — simply, honestly, in the way that Thuraya always answered the questions she was not going to evade.

"You have been listening," she said. "More than you know."

"Listening to what?"

"Listening to us. Every day. In the market. At the falaj. In the street. In the classroom. When the children speak to each other. When Aunt Azizah shouts at Malood. When Uncle Nasser calls for passengers. When the women at the water scold each other. When the old men at the mosque greet each other."

She paused. She smiled — the small teacher-smile, not the mischievous one.

"Your ears," she said, "are smarter than your mouth. They have been collecting sentences. Today — when you were shocked, when your mind was somewhere else — your mouth finally used them."

John stared at her. He considered this. It was, he realised, a completely plausible explanation — and also, somehow, not entirely reassuring.

"So I will," he said, slowly, "keep doing that?"

"If you are lucky," Thuraya said, "yes. If you are lucky, every week, without noticing, you will speak a little more Arabic. And your wife," she added, turning to Safiya, "will too."

Safiya, who had been listening, smiled. "Good."

Outside, the village was settling into its afternoon hour. The bell had been rung. The children were scattered. Some had gone home. Some had gone to the falaj. Some had, without any particular plan, gathered in small groups near the mosque wall, where the shade was best, to continue talking about whatever it was that ten-year-olds in 1973 continued talking about when school ended.

One of those small groups was under a ghaf tree near the old wall.

Three boys. Two small. One — slightly older, slightly quieter — sitting between them, cross-legged, in the specific composed posture of a storyteller.

Saeed.

The quiet boy. The boy from the back of the classroom. The boy who did not speak, did not play, did not join — but who, all morning, had been watching.

He was not playing now. He was talking. Quietly.

The two smaller boys were listening — leaning forward, eyes already bigger than normal, ears fully committed to the reception of whatever it was that Saeed was telling them.

"Teacher John," Saeed said — his voice low, even, measured — "went into the smoke."

One of the boys whispered, "We saw."

"And," Saeed continued, "when he came out, he was grey. Like a ghost."

"We saw."

"And then," Saeed said — his voice dropping just enough to signal the important part — "Shayb Khalaf came. And grabbed him. And Teacher John… spoke Arabic."

The other boy — the smaller one, the one with the gap-toothed seriousness — whispered, "An English man? Speaking Arabic?"

Saeed looked at the two of them. He looked at them with the specific authority of a boy who had been watching, all morning, while everyone else had been panicking or laughing or running, and who had therefore accumulated a small private archive of the day's mysterious facts.

"Something," he said, quietly, "happened when Shayb Khalaf touched him."

The two boys went very still.

Saeed said nothing else. He did not need to. He had — with the precise, small economy of a natural storyteller — left the sentence unfinished. And the unfinished sentence, once it had been delivered, would now do its own work. It would spread. It would multiply. By evening, every child in Samail would be telling their mother a slightly different version of the same thing — Teacher John went into the smoke and came out speaking Arabic.

Inside the classroom, John did not yet know this.

He was looking at Thuraya. He was frowning slightly.

"Thuraya."

"Yes?"

"The old man. Khalaf. Tell me…" He hesitated. "Tell me about him."

Thuraya met his eyes. The small mischievous smile returned — the Thuraya-knows-something-that-John-does-not smile. The same one John had, in his notebook that afternoon, written the instruction never trust beside.

"Tomorrow," she said. "I will tell you tomorrow."

"Why not now?"

"Because," she said, "tomorrow, you will be ready to hear it."

And outside, beneath the ghaf tree, Saeed — eight years old, patient, quiet, holding the day's secrets in both hands like small stones — stood up. He brushed his robe. And he walked, without hurry, toward the next small group of children whose day needed, in his view, to be improved with the correct version of the story.


# Palm Eleven — Maghsoob — And the Boy Who Watches



## Scene One — The Night That Would Not Sleep


John did not sleep well that night.

The night air in Samail, in October of 1973, was warm and still. It was the kind of night that, on any other evening, would have been a blessing — the specific warm stillness that makes the bisat feel generous, and the breeze through the window feel deliberate, and the distant sound of the falaj feel like a lullaby.

Tonight, it was none of those things.

Every time John closed his eyes, he saw smoke.

Or a stick. Or a thin old man with sharp eyes. Or — in the unhelpful way that tired minds combine images — all of them together, at the end of a dark path, standing in a small row, saying something he could not quite understand.

He turned, in his simple bed. He looked at the ceiling.

The ceiling, he was reminded for the second time in two weeks, was made of palm fronds. The fronds, he was also reminded, contained small shifting things. Small shifting things, at night, when the mind was already occupied with smoke and old men and sticks, were not the soothing presence they had been three weeks ago.

"This place," he whispered, in English — quietly, because Safiya was sleeping beside him — "is doing something to my brain."

Beside him, Safiya was asleep. Breathing peacefully. Her scarf folded neatly near the pillow — folded the way Thuraya's mother had taught her, the small triangular fold that had to be done exactly right for the scarf to sit correctly again in the morning.

She had, John noted with some resentment, entirely the peaceful sleep of a woman who did not know the Arabic word maghsoob, and who had not, that morning, threatened-corrected herself in a language she did not speak.

Outside, the village of Samail was quiet. Somewhere far away, a dog barked — once, twice, then stopped, having decided the thing it had been barking at did not deserve the effort. Somewhere closer — from the small yard of Aunt Azizah's house — Malood made a small unhappy sound, the specific small sound of a goat arguing with his own dreams.

John closed his eyes again.

And immediately, he saw Shayb Khalaf.

Not a menacing Khalaf. Not a Khalaf with a stick raised. Just the old man, standing near a clay wall, holding his kheyzaran, looking exactly — exactly — like he had that morning.

The voice came, inside John's head, in the old man's Arabic.

"khallik rijal."

Be a man, the voice said. In John's own head. In perfect Arabic.

John opened his eyes.

"No. Enough," he said to himself. Out loud. In English. As though English itself would act as a kind of protection, a small firm sentence his mind could hold onto.

"Tomorrow," he said, "I will ask Thuraya. Everything."

He turned onto his side. He watched the soft outline of Safiya's breathing. He tried to match his breath to hers. He failed. He tried again. He failed again.

Somewhere, the dog barked once more. The goat made its small argument. The village continued its ancient hush.

And John lay awake. For a long time.


## Scene Two — Two Missions


The next morning, John walked to school with two clear missions.

One: teach English.

Two: understand why he had, the previous morning, suddenly spoken Arabic like a confused poet.

He had arranged the two missions in his head as a numbered list. He had considered writing them down. He had decided, on reflection, that writing down understand why I suddenly spoke Arabic like a confused poet was the kind of sentence that — if found by anyone in a notebook — would be very difficult to explain.

Safiya, walking beside him, noticed his serious face immediately. His face had, she had come to learn, three settings — the normal teacher setting, the amused-at-Mansoor setting, and the something specific is happening in my head and I am not going to tell anyone about it yet setting.

This morning, it was the third.

"You look," she said, with the affectionate teasing that had become the natural shape of their morning walks, "like you are going to arrest someone."

John did not smile.

"I am," he said. "A mystery."

Thuraya was already at the school. She entered the classroom a few seconds after them — cheerful, as always, carrying books and her small piece of chalk.

"Good morning," she said.

John looked at her carefully. The look was the same detective-look he had given Safiya on the path. "Good morning. We need to talk."

Thuraya's face — without any surprise, because she had been expecting exactly this — arranged itself into a smile. She set her books on the desk. She adjusted, very slightly, the chalk behind her ear.

"About your flying lesson yesterday?"

"About the old man," John said. His voice was flat. It had the specific flatness of a man who did not intend to be deflected. "And about what you did not tell me."

Thuraya raised her eyebrows — the small precise raise of a teacher who had been asked a broad question and had decided, strategically, to narrow it.

"I did not tell you many things," she said. "You must be more specific."

Before John could refine his question — and he had been refining it in his head since six in the morning — the door burst open and the students ran in. Filling the room with noise and dust and the specific energy of ten small bodies that had been, for approximately fifteen minutes, unsupervised on the path.

Mansoor came first. Of course he did. He nearly crashed into a desk — nearly, because he had developed a small instinct for missing desks at the last possible moment, a village skill whose origin Safiya had not yet traced.

Ahmed walked in quietly, in his usual careful way, and sat straight like a ruler.

Khalil whispered something to Ali. Both of them laughed the laugh of boys who were going to be, within three minutes, in some small form of trouble.

The girls — Noura, Marwa, Rahma, Salima, and Mathila — came in together. They were talking about something serious, and secret, and Safiya understood that she was not going to learn what it was. Girl secrets, in this classroom, had a specific protected quality that the boys' secrets — Saeed's aside — did not have.

And in the back, near the door, stood one boy.

He did not sit immediately. He stood for a moment first — as though he were, briefly, surveying the room before committing to a seat. Then he walked, calmly, to the far-back desk and sat down.

Saeed.

He did not talk much. He did not play much. He watched.

Always watched.


## Scene Three — Telling Stories


John, returning himself to mission one — which he had promised he would — turned to the board. He wrote, in careful large letters:

Today's Topic: Telling Stories

He turned back to the class. He had, in fact, planned this lesson before yesterday's smoke had happened, and he had kept it today because a lesson on telling stories had, for reasons he was beginning to understand, a very specific relevance to this particular village.

"In English," he said, "when we tell a story, we use special words. Small words. But they help us move through time."

He wrote:

FirstThenAfter thatFinally

Safiya — from her usual spot near the back — clapped her hands, the warm teacher-clap that she had been adopting more and more without noticing.

"Good!" she said. "Today, we talk about… yesterday."

The class reacted immediately.

"Teacher John fell!" Mansoor announced — proudly, with the specific triumphant announcement voice of a boy who had been waiting since dawn to reintroduce yesterday's disaster into the classroom record.

John closed his eyes. Just for one second. The specific one-second closure of a teacher accepting that a personal humiliation had now become, officially, part of the syllabus.

"Yes," he said. "Thank you, Mansoor. The village will never forget."

Thuraya smiled. "Okay. Who wants to tell the story?"

Ahmed — the boy who was always prepared — raised his hand. He had, Safiya could see, been composing this sentence in his head for the last two minutes. He had been waiting for his moment.

He stood up. He placed his hands behind his back in his usual small-man-at-a-meeting way. He spoke.

"Yesterday," he said, "first, the truck came. Then, we ran. After that, Teacher John fell. Finally, he became a ghost."

The class laughed.

John sighed. "Excellent grammar. Terrible story."

Safiya pointed at Noura. "Your turn."

Noura thought. Her face went through its usual stages — the serious consideration, the small arranging, the decision. Then she spoke, slowly, mixing her two languages the way all the children mixed them.

"Yesterday, first, smoke come. Then, Mansoor run like crazy. After that, Malood cough. Finally… teacher John… grey."

Safiya nodded happily. "Good! Very good!"

She did not correct the grammar. The grammar would become, with each telling, slightly better. The story itself — that was the point. The child had, for the first time in her short English life, successfully used three sequential time markers and arrived, correctly, at the ending.

The lesson continued. Khalil told a version in which he, himself, had personally rescued a small bird from the smoke — a version which nobody else remembered but which Khalil delivered with full conviction. Ali — quietly — told a version in which he had stayed inside with his mother. Mathila told one in which the pesticide man had waved at her, which was probably true.

And Saeed, in the back, was silent.

Watching.

Listening.

Collecting.


## Scene Four — The Maghsoob


After the class ended, John did not leave.

He waited.

When the last student had run out the door — Mansoor, predictably, shouting something down the corridor that was not strictly inside-voice — John turned to Thuraya.

"Now," he said. "Tell me the truth."

Thuraya smiled sweetly. The sweet smile. The one that, as John had written in his own notebook, should never be trusted.

"About what?"

"About Shayb Khalaf," John said. "And about me. And about why I spoke Arabic."

She looked at him. For a long second. Considering.

Then — with the small decisive nod of a woman who had decided, after all, to indulge him — she said, "Come. Let's walk."

They walked outside. Past the falaj. Past the trees. Safiya, knowing the conversation was not for her, stayed behind to organise books. She understood that Thuraya was going to tell John something specific, in the specific way Thuraya told things, and that the walk was part of how the telling would work.

They came to an old wall. The wall of someone's very old clay house — the kind of wall that had been patched so many times that the patches had their own patches. Thuraya stopped.

She leaned, lightly, against it. She looked at the ground. Then she looked at John.

"People here," she said, "love stories."

John nodded. "I noticed."

"And people here," she continued, her voice dropping just slightly, "love fear."

John did not like where this was going.

She lowered her voice further. The specific lowering that told a listener: what comes next is not for everyone, so listen carefully.

"Some say Shayb Khalaf is a sahir."

John blinked. "A… what?"

"A sorcerer," she said. Clearly. Slowly.

John laughed. It was not a confident laugh. It was the small, too-fast laugh of a person trying very hard not to take something seriously.

"That is not funny."

"I am not joking," Thuraya said. Her voice was serious. Too serious. He felt his spine make a small, involuntary adjustment.

"The story says: to become a great sorcerer — a strong one — a man must give one of his own children to the devils. He must hand the child over. The child disappears."

She paused. The pause was specific. Measured. A storyteller's pause.

"Years later…" she continued, "the child comes back. But not as a child. As a maghsoob."

John felt cold.

Not metaphorically. A real, small, specific cold that passed across the back of his neck and down his shoulders — the particular way that a body registers a story it does not want to be registering.

"They say the maghsoob comes back like a dead man," Thuraya continued, her voice even quieter now. "Dirty. His clothes are old. His mind is gone. The devils ate his mind.
