People do not accept him, because… he already died in their hearts, the day the father gave him away. So the maghsoob wanders. Between houses. Alone. For the rest of his life. A walking sign of what his father did to buy his sihr."

John swallowed.

"And they say Khalaf…" John whispered, "has one of these children?"

"That is what they say. A son. Given, long ago. And now — somewhere in the next village, or perhaps further — a maghsoob walks. Dusty. Silent. Recognised by nobody."

She lowered her voice one more notch.

"And they say that a sahir like Khalaf… sometimes… can put words in your mouth."

John stared at her.

He considered, for one full second, what he was being told. He considered, for a second second, the Arabic sentences that had come out of his own mouth the previous morning — sentences he did not know, conjugated correctly, delivered with the cadence of a village. He considered, for a third second, the possibility that the two things were connected.

Then he rallied. With the full force of his English rationalism, he rallied.

"…You are joking," he said.

Thuraya looked at him.

Her face, for a long moment, gave nothing.

Then — slowly, mercilessly — she smiled.

"Yes," she said. "Of course I am joking."

He did not relax.

His shoulders, which had been up near his ears for the last three sentences, did not come down. His breath, which had been shallow for the last thirty seconds, did not fully return. The problem with a joke told in a low, serious voice, against an old clay wall, about a village myth you had never heard before, is that the body receives it before the mind has had a chance to vote.

She laughed — properly now. The full warm Thuraya-laugh. "Mr. John, your face! You look like you saw the ghost again."

"You told me that on purpose," he said.

She nodded. Proudly.

"Yes."


## Scene Five — Meanwhile, Under the Trees


Not far away — near the trees, in the specific cool patch of shade behind the small school yard — Saeed was sitting with two smaller boys.

He was not playing.

He was talking.

Quietly.

The two smaller boys sat cross-legged in front of him. They were watching his face. They had the specific full attention of children who had been promised a story, and who had learned — by approximately the age of six — that adults in a village gave you news, but children of Saeed's particular kind gave you secrets, which were much more valuable.

"They say," Saeed said — beginning, as all good tellers of stories did, with the they say that relieved the teller of any personal responsibility for the claim — "that Shayb Khalaf is a sahir. A strong one."

The boys' eyes grew big.

"They say," Saeed continued, "that a long time ago… he gave one of his sons."

One boy whispered, "Gave him where?"

"To the devils," Saeed said. Serenely. "So he could become strong. A strong sahir."

The boys stared.

"And the son," Saeed continued, "came back. But not as a son. As a maghsoob. Dusty. Empty. His mind eaten."

"Where is he now?" whispered the smaller boy.

"Walking," Saeed said. "Somewhere. He never stops walking."

A pause. Then — the embellishment that Saeed had been preparing, his own invention, because Thuraya had not said this exact thing — "and some say, when a sahir like Khalaf touches you… he can put Arabic words into your mouth. Words you do not know."

One of the boys — the smaller one, the one with the wide alarmed face — whispered, "Did he put words in Teacher John's mouth?"

Saeed considered this question.

He considered it with the specific small deliberation of a storyteller weighing how committed he wanted to be to the claim. If he said yes, the story would be bigger. If he said maybe, the story would last longer.

He chose the more durable option.

He smiled. A small, clever smile. "Maybe."

And the two boys — nodding slowly, eyes still wide — sat with the maybe in their laps like a small, glowing, living thing. A maybe that would, by the end of the day, have travelled through their mothers to their mothers' neighbours to their fathers at the mosque to their cousins in the far fields.

Under the trees, Saeed sat back against the trunk. He closed his eyes briefly. He was eight years old. He was already — though no one in the village had yet said this — quite a good writer. He had not been taught to write stories, exactly. He had been taught to watch. But watching, done long enough and patiently enough, produced stories of its own.

He opened his eyes.

He looked at the two smaller boys. He considered what to add next. He had a small instinct, by now, for when a story had received exactly enough weight.

He stood up. He brushed his robe. He walked — without hurry, without announcement — out from under the trees and down the path toward the next group of children whose day, in his view, would benefit from the correct version of what had happened the day before.


## Scene Six — The Afternoon of Shadows


That day, John was… nervous.

The maghsoob story, despite having been officially retracted by Thuraya in the following sentence, had lodged. In his chest. Behind his breastbone. In the specific small place where stories, once they have entered at the correct angle, are very difficult to dislodge.

Every shadow, that afternoon, looked suspicious.

Every sound looked dangerous.

When Malood — who had, in the specific goat-magic way of Malood, positioned himself behind a water bucket in the school yard without John noticing — suddenly jumped out, John jumped like he had seen a lion.

An actual lion. The full, full jump. Both feet off the ground.

Safiya, who had seen the whole thing, laughed. "You are becoming very local."

"I am becoming," John answered, without pleasure, "very scared."

When they walked home — along the usual path, past the usual walls, past the usual trees — John looked at every old man.

Every wall.

Every tree.

It was exhausting, Safiya noted. She said nothing. A husband, she had long since learned, was owed the dignity of his own small, unnecessary fears, at least until the fear had given up on its own.

Then John saw someone, sitting near the road.

Old. Thin. Holding a stick.

Shayb Khalaf.

John stopped walking.

He did not stop with a plan. He stopped because his feet had simply stopped — without consulting him, without asking, without any permission from the English part of his brain. They had, it seemed, made a decision on his behalf.

Safiya continued walking a few steps. Then she noticed.

"John?"

She turned. She followed his gaze. She saw the old man. She understood, in a single second, the entire situation — the sleepless night, the Arabic sentences, the muttered maghsoob, and now this — and she had, silently, the small, unseen wife-thought of oh, John, really.

The old man looked up.

Their eyes met.

Khalaf said nothing. He simply watched.

Not hostile. Not welcoming. Just — watching — in the specific calm way that Khalaf had, that Safiya had already noticed was the only way he ever watched anything at all.

John nodded. Very politely. The small, restrained, English nod of a man who had been told a ghost story about someone and was now standing on a public road and had decided, in the space of two seconds, that he was going to be polite about it regardless.

Khalaf nodded back.

Nothing happened.

No words came into John's mouth. No Arabic. No prophecy. No ancient phrase rising from a place he did not remember having. His mouth stayed obedient. His feet agreed — after their small, unauthorised stop — to start walking again.

They walked away.

John did not breathe until they turned the corner.

Then, properly, at the corner, he exhaled — a long, complete exhale, the specific exhale of a man who had just survived an entirely imaginary encounter with an entirely imaginary danger.

Safiya put her arm through his. "Home," she said, gently.

"Home," John agreed.

That night, John could not sleep.

Again.

He looked at the ceiling. Then at the door. Then at the window.

"Thuraya," he whispered, to no one, "is enjoying this too much."

Outside, the village was quiet. Inside, his imagination was not.

He closed his eyes. And, somewhere in the small fold between waking and sleep, he dreamed.

He dreamed of a man made of dust, walking out of a grave at the end of a long path — the specific long path that ran from the edge of the village toward the farms. The man was tall. Thin. Holding a stick. His clothes were old. His face was exactly the face of Shayb Khalaf, only paler — only dustier — only, in the way of dreams, more present than real people are when they are actually there. And the man, in the dream, was speaking. Speaking calmly. In perfect English. "Good morning, teacher John. I have been waiting. I have something to tell you."

John woke up.

He sat up in the bed.

He looked at Safiya. Sleeping peacefully. Scarf still folded.

He lay back down. He stared at the ceiling. He watched the slow shifts of the palm fronds above him until the first call of fajr came from the mosque — and even then, he did not sleep.

The next day at school, John walked in with a small cup of coffee in his hand and red eyes.

Ahmed noticed immediately. "Teacher… you tired?"

"Yes," John said. "Very."

Mansoor grinned. The specific, knowing grin of an eight-year-old who had, somehow, already heard a version of yesterday's maghsoob conversation through the village information channels that no adult could quite map.

"You afraid?"

"No," John lied.

And in the back, near the door, Saeed — the boy who collected secrets like treasure, the boy whose maybe was, by now, travelling through the village in small glowing pieces — watched.

And smiled.

Just a little.


# Palm Twelve — A Ring, a Goat, and an Apology



## Scene One — Too Quiet a Morning


The day began quietly.

Too quietly.

John noticed this first, and that alone made him suspicious.

The village of Samail usually woke up with noise. It woke up with the specific layered noise of children running on sand, of goats arguing with the low walls that tried to contain them, of someone shouting for someone else from one courtyard to another, of women pounding grain, of doors opening and closing, and — always, reliably, without fail — Mansoor doing something, somewhere, that he was not supposed to be doing.

But this morning, there was only the sound of birds and the soft, continuous hush of the falaj water.

Nothing else.

Safiya was sitting near the small mirror, fixing her scarf. She was moving slowly. Calmly. The way a person moves when they want the day to stay peaceful — as though peacefulness were a small bird that might fly away if one moved too suddenly.

John was writing something in his notebook. He was writing the small observations of a man who had begun, inadvertently, to keep a journal. He had not planned this. The village had quietly, without asking, turned him into a person who wrote things down at breakfast.

Thuraya arrived a few minutes later, carrying books and smiling.

"Good morning," she said. Her eyes moved, briefly, from Safiya's careful scarf to John's half-filled notebook. "You both look… too relaxed."

John looked up. "Yes. And that worries me."

Safiya laughed. "You are becoming very local."

It had become, she realised, a small running joke between them. You are becoming very local. She had been using it for two weeks now. Each time, it meant something slightly different. Today it meant: you are now suspicious of peace itself. Which was, she had to admit, a very Samaili attitude.

They walked to school together.

Malood, the baby goat — who had, by some unexplained agreement that had never been formally signed, become the small fourth member of their walking group — followed them like a small white cloud with legs.

No one had invited him.

No one could stop him.

The road was quieter than usual. A woman greeted them from a doorway. A boy dashed past with bread in his hands, heading home. The falaj, as always, continued its hush. But the usual dense overlapping morning — the thing the village did every morning — was not happening.

And the quietness, for reasons John could not articulate but was beginning to accept, continued to feel like a promise of something.


## Scene Two — The Discovery


The first two lessons passed normally.

Too normally.

The students did their work. Even Mansoor only caused two small problems — a number so low that Safiya, when she noticed, actually kept a small count of them in her head, the way a doctor might count a patient's low pulse. One: he flipped his slate. Two: he told Ali that Ali's handwriting looked like an ant's argument. That was all. By Mansoor's standards, it was almost good behaviour.

Then, during the break, Safiya stood up to wash her hands near the water pot in the corner of the classroom.

She stopped.

She looked at her hand.

She looked again.

Then she froze — with the specific, small stillness of a person whose morning has, in the space of one glance, stopped being an ordinary morning.

"My ring," she said. Slowly. Each word separate.

Thuraya turned. "What?"

"My ring. It is not here."

Safiya checked her other hand. The fingers were there. The finger was there. The small, pale, slightly indented ring of skin, paler than the rest, was there. But the actual ring — the gold band — was not.

She checked her pocket. Then her bag. Then, with the specific small panic of a woman who had already lost this same ring once, she checked her bag again.

Nothing.

John stood up immediately. "What ring?"

"My ring," Safiya said. "The one from my mother."

Silence.

Even Mansoor felt it was serious. He had been, a second ago, preparing to offer a joke — Safiya could see the joke forming on his small mouth — and he swallowed it back down. The specific swallow of a ten-year-old who had, for once in his life, read the room correctly.

Thuraya walked closer. "Are you sure you wore it today?"

"Yes," Safiya said. "I remember. I fixed my scarf. I looked at it in the mirror."

She remembered the exact moment. She remembered thinking, as she had so many mornings since her mother's death, the ring. Still there. Good. It was one of those small registrations that happened under the surface of her mornings, every morning, automatically.

John's face changed. Not into panic. Into the specific investigative focus of a man who had, in another life, been quite good at finding things people had lost. "Okay. Nobody panic. We will find it."

Mansoor raised his hand. Slowly. Almost reluctantly. The gesture of a boy who had just thought of a solution he knew nobody wanted to hear.

"Teacher… maybe Malood eat it?"

Everyone turned. Slowly.

Malood was standing near the classroom door. In the small triangle of sun that came through the gap at the bottom. His posture was the posture of a goat who had been, all morning, minding his own business.

Chewing.

Something.

Everyone stared.

Safiya's heart jumped. "Malood! No, no, no…"

She walked toward him. Slowly. The specific slow approach of a woman trying not to spook a small animal whose cooperation she suddenly urgently needed.

Malood stopped chewing.

Then — with the specific, practised, unregrettable decision-making of a goat who had decided that safety was something other goats and other people could worry about — he ran.


## Scene Three — The Chase


Malood ran like he had stolen the crown of the king.

He did not run in a straight line. He ran in the specific zigzag that a small hunted thing runs when it wants, urgently, not to be caught.

The children ran after him.

All of them. As one. With the specific collective instinct of a classroom that had, in its pocket, every morning for two months, been secretly hoping that exactly this would one day happen.

"Catch him!"

"He ate the ring!"

"Stop, Malood!"

John ran too. He ran holding his trousers — because John's trousers, in the warm village climate, had a small, reliable tendency to loosen at the worst possible moments — and he was not, at this particular moment, about to be remembered in the village as the English teacher who had chased a goat with no trousers.

Thuraya shouted, from further back — her voice the specific calm teacher-shout that was heard clearly over any chaos — "Careful!"

Malood jumped over a small wall.

The children jumped too. Effortlessly. The specific short-legged leap of boys and girls who had been leaping over exactly these walls since the moment they could walk, and who had, long ago, absorbed the art into their bodies.

John tried.

He did not jump.

He climbed.

Slowly.

Carefully.

With the specific, dignified, cautious climbing of a tall adult Englishman who had, finally, accepted that his body was not a village body and that certain walls were, for him, mountains.

By the time he reached the other side — panting, sweating, trousers still technically in position — Malood was gone.

They searched near the trees.

Near the falaj.

Near the small fields at the edge of the village where Aunt Azizah's vegetables grew in small patient rows.

Nothing.

No white cloud. No chewing sound. No small proprietary silhouette on any wall.

Safiya looked like she wanted to cry. Not quite. Almost. The specific small pre-cry tightness of a woman whose one important inherited object was in the digestive system of a small horned thief.

Thuraya put a hand on Safiya's shoulder. The firm, warm, specific hand of a friend. "We will find it."

Then a small voice said — very quietly, from somewhere near the cluster of children standing at the edge of the path —

"…Maybe Shayb Khalaf."

Everyone turned.

It was one of the smaller boys. One Safiya did not yet know by name — a small round-eyed child with a solemn face and a dusty robe.

Mansoor's eyes became big. "Yes! Shayb Khalaf!"

Another boy whispered, "He likes shiny things."

A girl said, almost without meaning to, the word — "He is… sahir…"

Another girl added, "His son is maghsoob."

John felt cold.

The specific small cold he had felt the first time Thuraya had mentioned the word maghsoob along the old wall. The cold that was not really about the truth of anything — that was, at the end, Thuraya's joke — but about the way stories, once they had entered a small body, did not quite leave again.

Safiya looked at Thuraya. Quickly. "They don't really mean that… right?"

Thuraya hesitated.

Just a little.

The smallest hesitation in the world. A quarter of a second. Less.

John noticed.

That made everything worse.


## Scene Four — The Rumour


By lunchtime, the story was everywhere.

It had migrated — through the small channels that carried all important village information, which were the children's mouths, the mothers' doorways, the men's tea at Sadoo's shop, the women's conversations at the falaj — until the whole village had, by around half past twelve, the same set of sentences available in four or five slightly different versions.

"Shayb Khalaf took the ring."

"Malood works for him."

"He uses magic."

"His son is maghsoob. He sold him for sihr long ago."

Saeed did not say anything.

He only listened.

And remembered.

He was, once again, sitting in his usual quiet spot — this time beneath the small ghaf tree behind the school yard, with his bag on his knees and his eyes moving between the groups of other children as they rearranged the story, one small retelling at a time. He was, Safiya was beginning to understand, not just a collector of secrets. He was a cataloguer of them. He remembered, it seemed, every version.

Safiya tried to stay calm. She kept her face composed. She helped Thuraya continue the lesson. She even, once, smiled at Mansoor — a small quiet smile that required, in that moment, an actual effort.

But John could see she was worried.

Not panicked. Worried. The specific contained worry of a woman who was doing her best to perform composure while, underneath, privately measuring how badly she felt about the loss.

And John, meanwhile — John was incensed.

"This is crazy," he said, quietly, to Thuraya, as they stood at the edge of the yard. "We are accusing an old man because a goat is stupid."

Malood — who had, at some point in the last hour, returned, and was now sitting in the shade of the low wall with the specific complacent air of a goat who had nothing to confess — sneezed.

"See?" John pointed at the goat. "Even he agrees."

Thuraya sighed. It was a long sigh. Older than the morning. The specific sigh of a village teacher who had been watching this exact pattern — thing lost → old man blamed — all her life.

"People here," she said, "when something is lost… they look for stories before they look for facts."

Safiya, who had been listening, stood up.

"Then," she said, quietly but firmly, "let us look for facts."


## Scene Five — The Visit


They walked to the edge of the village.

The children did not come close.

They stayed far. Watching. Gathered in a loose, cautious line at the end of the path — Mansoor, Khalil, Ali, the small boys, the girls' triangle, Saeed at the back — all of them committed, as an audience commits to a play it has already heard the plot of, to witnessing the confrontation.

Shayb Khalaf's house was small.

Made of clay. The specific quiet clay of old Omani houses — the mud mixed with straw, the walls patched and re-patched, the door a plain worn blue that was, after many years, more grey than blue.

Quiet.

John felt his heart beating faster.

He did not want to be here. He actively did not want to be here. He had spent the walk over telling himself, with increasing firmness, that he was not afraid of old men. That he was an English teacher. That he had come to this country with his wife. That the ring belonged, in all probability, to the small pale village criminal sleeping by the low wall, not to an old man they had spent the morning slandering.

Thuraya knocked.

Nothing.

She knocked again.

The door opened — slowly. With the same quiet unhurriedness it had opened the last time. Khalaf stood there.

Thin. Quiet. Holding his stick.

"Yes?" he said.

Thuraya spoke gently. The specific careful Arabic voice of a younger person addressing an elder whose patience had already been, in her view, asked for more than enough today.

"Good afternoon, uncle. We are sorry to disturb you."

He looked at John.

Then at Safiya.

Then at Malood — who had, without permission, followed them, and who was now sitting, composed and unconcerned, beside John's ankle.

His eyes stopped at Malood.

"…This goat," Khalaf said. Flatly. "Always hungry."

John swallowed.

There was, he thought, something specific in the old man's voice — not annoyance, not blame, just the weary recognition of a man who had already, several times in his long life, traced a small problem in his yard back to a small white animal who did not belong to him.

Thuraya explained — carefully, respectfully. "Sophia lost her ring. The children… are afraid it came here."

Khalaf's face changed.

Not angry. Not scared. Not surprised.

Just… tired.

The specific, inherited tiredness of a man who had — Safiya understood, with a sudden, complete, painful clarity — been accused of many things over many years, for many items lost by many people in a village that had decided, a long time ago, that when something-went-missing happened, the first name to be reached for was his.

"I did not take anything," he said.

The sentence was calm. It was also, Safiya noticed, a sentence he had said before. Perhaps many times. A sentence that had, for him, the worn smoothness of a stone turned over and over in a pocket.

Safiya felt ashamed.

"We are sorry," she said quickly. "We only… wanted to ask."

Khalaf nodded. Once.

"You can look."

He stepped aside, without ceremony, and let them see inside.

They looked.

The house was simple. A mat on the packed earth floor — the bisat, worn smooth in the specific places where a tall thin man had sat on it for many years. A pot. A small collection of clothes hung on a hook. A clay jar of water. A small shelf with three books — Safiya could see, on the spine of one, the specific gold-threaded binding of an old Qur'an. Nothing else. No shiny things. No jewellery. No ring.

No ring.

John felt relief.

And guilt.

Both at once — the specific mixed feeling of a person who had, in the last four hours, been at least partially convinced by a story, and who now, standing in the calm, quiet room of the man the story had been about, was discovering that the room was just — a room.

An old man's room. With a mat, and a pot, and three books, and no ring.


## Scene Six — The Truth and the Apology


When they walked back along the path, Saeed was waiting.

He had not been with the main group of children. He had been, somehow, ahead of them — sitting on a low wall at the turn in the path, with his bag on his knees, watching the approach of the three adults with his usual composed quiet.

He did not smile. He did not joke. He did not — unlike Mansoor, who would have — demand attention.

He simply stood up. He waited until they had reached him. Then he spoke.

"Malood," Saeed said, "was chewing something white this morning."

Everyone froze.

John turned slowly.

"White?"

Saeed nodded. "Near the big stone. Then he ran."

Safiya and John and Thuraya looked at each other. For one full second, nobody spoke. Then all three of them, without consultation, without a word, turned and walked — quickly — in the direction of the big stone.

They ran.

They found Malood.

Sleeping. Very happily. In the specific after-lunch sleep of a small animal who had consumed, not long ago, something he had very much enjoyed.

John lifted him.

"Sorry, my friend," he said — and the word friend, applied to a goat, came out of his mouth with a small note of surprise, as though his vocabulary had made a choice without consulting him — "this is… an investigation."

They waited.

And waited.

And waited.

It was, Safiya would later recall, the longest fifteen minutes of her day. They had gathered in a small ceremonial circle around Malood — who was now awake, and who had been set down on the packed ground, and who was, with the air of a goat who had no idea what was happening around him and no intention of finding out, nibbling a dry leaf.

Finally —

Safiya screamed.

"MY RING!"

It was dirty. Very dirty. It had been — through processes Safiya would not describe in any letter home — thoroughly transformed by its passage through Malood. But it was there. It was, unmistakably, her ring.

John closed his eyes. His face held the expression of a man who had been, for the last four hours, privately writing, cancelling, and re-writing a new personal philosophy about goats, and who had, at this moment, arrived at its final draft.

"I," he said, quietly, with deep conviction, "will never say anything bad about goats again."

Malood looked proud.

He stood. He stretched. He walked, without hurry, in a small dignified circle. He looked, Safiya thought, like a small war hero receiving a medal.

The children had gathered. They had heard the scream. They were now standing in a cautious arc, watching.

No one laughed.

Not even Mansoor.

Safiya held the ring in her palm. She looked at Thuraya.

"We almost blamed an innocent man."

Thuraya nodded. Quietly. "That happens a lot."

John said, low — "We need to fix this."

They walked back, the three of them, to Shayb Khalaf's house. Safiya had, in her palm, the cleaned ring. She had washed it twice. She had washed it, unnecessarily, a third time. It was bright again. And it sat on her finger, where it belonged, and her hand was no longer lighter than usual.

They knocked.

Khalaf opened the door. His face, when he saw them, was the same face he had shown them two hours earlier — tired, quiet, entirely unsurprised.

Safiya spoke first.

"We are very sorry."

John added — clearly, carefully, so that the sentence would land — "We believed a story. Not the truth."

Khalaf looked at them for a long moment. The specific, calm long moment of an old man deciding what to say.

Then he said, "People always do."

He turned. He was, they understood, about to close the door. But — at the last moment, with the specific, small, surprising softening of a man who had not planned to give them anything further — he paused.

He turned back.

"But you came back." He met Safiya's eyes. Then John's. Then Thuraya's. "That is… something."

He closed the door. Gently.

That evening, John was quiet. Safiya was quiet. Thuraya was thoughtful. The village, around them, was normal again — the falaj continuing its hush, the evening settling into its gold hour, the small sounds of the dinners beginning in the other houses.

But something had changed.

That night, at the small wooden table, John wrote in his notebook. He wrote, in his careful teacher's hand, a single sentence:

Today, we learned something more important than English.

In another part of the village — under the same ghaf tree where he had delivered yesterday's stories — Saeed was sitting with the same two small boys who had, that morning, listened to his maybe. He was telling them something different now. He was telling them the correction. He was telling them, in the quiet voice he used for all his important sentences —

"Stories are strong. But truth is stronger."

And Malood? Malood slept.

Dreaming, Safiya imagined, of his next meal.


# Palm Thirteen — The Yellow Mask Disaster



## Scene One — A Personal Attack from the Sky


The sun in Samail did not wake up gently.

It attacked.

By eight in the morning, the village was already glowing — glowing, shining, and, in the specific soft menacing way that only a desert sun manages, quietly threatening everyone who had forgotten a hat, or a scarf, or common sense.

Safiya — and by this point in the story of her life, she was Safiya completely, everywhere, even in her own head — stood in front of the small mirror in their simple house, touching her face carefully.

She was investigating. Her fingertips moved from her forehead to her cheeks to the tender patch of skin just beneath her eyes. Each small touch produced a small small flinch. Her face, she realised, was no longer the face of a woman who lived indoors.

"I think," she said — slowly, to her own reflection, because John was outside — "my face is… cooking."

The word cooking was generous. Her face, in the small mirror, was the specific shade of red that tomatoes achieved only after they had been deliberately left in a pan on low heat for longer than anyone had intended.

Outside, John was already sweating and pretending he was not. It was a specific English talent — the art of pretending that one was not sweating when one was, in fact, sweating through the back of one's shirt. He stood in the small patch of shade beside the door and attempted, without success, to look cool.

"This is not sun," he said. Seriously. As though he were addressing a board. "This is a personal attack from the sky."

At the school yard, Thuraya arrived as usual. Calm. Prepared. Unbothered. As though — and it was impossible to confirm this, but Safiya had begun to suspect it — the sun had, at some point in the last forty years, signed a small private peace agreement with her, and had agreed to leave her alone.

Behind her came Fatkat — full of energy, wearing her sports clothes, carrying her whistle around her neck like a weapon of considerable importance.

"Ya binti," Fatkat said loudly, fanning herself with her free hand, "this sun is not normal. This sun needs discipline."

Safiya looked at her. Her own hand had drifted up, again, to her own cheek.

"My face feels like fire."

Fatkat touched her own cheek. Then, as though confirming a diagnosis, she did it a second time.

"Mine too. Look." She inclined her face toward Safiya. "We are… red."

They stood together in the small patch of shade beside the school wall. They inspected each other's cheeks — earnestly, gravely — like doctors without a single degree between them and a great willingness to diagnose regardless.

John passed by. He looked at them. He considered, briefly, whether to say something. He said it anyway.

"You both," he offered, "look like tomatoes that went to the beach."

Thuraya laughed. "You need," she said — to Safiya, helpfully — "something for your skin."

Fatkat's eyes shone — the specific sudden brightness of a woman who had been waiting for exactly this opening and had had her recommendation ready for approximately the last fifteen minutes.

"There is Marhoona. She makes good masks. Real traditional."

Safiya clapped. "Skin care!"

John raised one eyebrow. The specific eyebrow of an Englishman who had, at several prior moments in his life, witnessed his wife's enthusiasm for a new village beauty treatment and had reasons to be concerned.

"Be careful," he said. "The skin may not agree."

They ignored him.

That, it would turn out, was a mistake.


## Scene Two — The Salon of Marhoona


Marhoona's place was not really a salon.

It was a small room. In a small house. At the end of a small path on the far side of the village, just past the house of Aunt Azizah and just before the road turned toward the farms. The door was the same painted blue that every door in Samail wore. The room behind the door was the kind of room that had never been called a salon in its life, and had, until recently, been called a storage room, and had, before that, been called a back room, and had, at some very old point, been a corner of somebody's grandmother's kitchen.

Inside: a mat. A small round mirror with a silver frame that had lost most of its silver. And a lot of confidence.

Marhoona herself was a woman of about fifty — short, sturdy, with henna-darkened fingertips and the specific calm authority of a woman who had, for approximately thirty years, been the final word in the village about any matter involving hair, skin, bridal preparation, or the removal of unwanted hair.

She looked at Safiya's face. Then at Fatkat's face. She nodded — slowly — like a wise judge arriving at his verdict.

"Sun," she said. "Too much."

It was, Safiya would later note, not an incorrect diagnosis.

Marhoona began to mix things in a small clay bowl. She moved quickly — the specific practised quickness of a woman who had done this many times — and she named her ingredients as she dropped them in, the way a chef might name his spices for a visitor.

"Sandalwood. Saffron."

She smiled.

"Very good for skin."

She put the mask on Safiya's face — spreading it with the warm steady pressure of fingers that had applied this exact mixture hundreds of times. Then on Fatkat's face. They were instructed to keep their eyes closed. They were instructed to wait.

They closed their eyes.

They waited.

And waited.

And waited.

The smell was pleasant. The sandalwood warmed Safiya's skin. The saffron — she had not smelled saffron so strongly before outside a kitchen — was deep and aromatic. She could feel, she thought, the mask working — the small tightening of her skin, the small cooling effect, the small sensation of being treated.

After what seemed to be the correct length of time — Marhoona was keeping track of it by some internal clock that had no visible mechanism — Marhoona wiped the mask off.

She handed Safiya the small, silver-less mirror.

Safiya opened her eyes.

She looked.

She screamed.

Fatkat opened her own eyes. She looked at Safiya. "Ya rab," she said — quietly, with genuine awe. Then she grabbed the mirror herself. She looked. "Ya rab!"

Their faces were…

Yellow.

Very yellow.

Like lemons.

Like — Safiya would later refine the description in her head — very sad lemons. The kind of lemons that had been, at some point, enthusiastic about their lemon-career, and had then been informed that they were not going to be used in a cake, or a drink, or a marinade, but would instead be juiced and left in a bowl on a counter and forgotten for several hours.

Safiya touched her cheek. "Why," she said, slowly, to no one in particular, "am I the colour of a banana?"

Fatkat was louder. "Ya rab! I look like a warning sign!"

Marhoona said calmly, "It will go."

That did not help.


## Scene Three — The Return


They walked back to school.

Carefully.

Slowly.

Hoping nobody would see them.

This hope was, Safiya realised only after they had taken their first fifty steps, foolish. Everybody saw them. The village, in the middle of the day, in 1973, in a small agricultural settlement where the most interesting piece of news in any given week was usually whose cow gave more milk, was not the kind of place where two yellow women could pass unnoticed.

A woman at a doorway saw them. She did not speak. She only paused in her sweeping, leaned her broom against the wall, and watched them go past with the specific unblinking interest of a person mentally writing, in real time, the story she was about to tell her sister that afternoon.

A boy carrying bread saw them. He stopped walking entirely. He stared. He forgot about the bread. The bread dangled from his hand at a worried angle.

An old man, sitting on the low wall that marked the edge of the farms, watched them approach. He watched them pass. He watched them recede. Then, when they had turned the corner, he — without turning his head — called something to his neighbour in the next yard, a sentence that contained the word banana and a small interrogative inflection.

John was the first person at the school to see them. He was the first, as it happened, because he had been standing in the doorway of the classroom, waiting for them to come back, drinking a small cup of lukewarm water, and feeling — because his wife was at a skincare appointment with the sports teacher — an unusual sense of calm.

The calm lasted until the yellow appeared around the corner.

He stopped.

He looked.

He set the cup down very carefully on the windowsill. He did not want, if he were about to do what he was about to do, to spill it.

Then he fell into a chair laughing.

Not metaphorically. He walked two steps, located the nearest wooden teacher's chair, sat down in it, and laughed with his whole body — the specific full laugh of a man who had been holding in, for several weeks now, the accumulated comic material of living in a village that produced, on a regular schedule, situations that no English training had prepared him for.

"I am sorry," he said, between laughs — and he was, in some part of him, genuinely sorry, but not nearly sorry enough to stop — "I am really sorry… but you look like… a school bus and a lemon had a baby."

Thuraya came.

She looked.

She tried to be polite.

She failed.

The failure was dignified, at first. She pressed her lips together. She looked at the ceiling. She looked at the floor. She tried, visibly, to compose her face. Then she looked back at the two yellow faces in front of her, and the composure — like the mask — came off.

"This," she said, pointing at their faces, "is called… skin care?"

John wiped his eyes. "Yes. But…"

He stood up. With the specific dramatic posture of a man who had just been handed, by the comic gods themselves, the perfect sentence — and who intended, now, to deliver it.

"Some of you," he said, "do a lot of skin care… but the skin doesn't care."

Even Malood — who had wandered into the yard at some point, who had watched the whole return, who had then, for his own reasons, decided that the yellow women deserved closer inspection — made a small sound. A sound that, with the specific generosity a listener could grant him, could be interpreted as a laugh.


## Scene Four — Mohammadeen Arrives


Then Mohammadeen arrived.

The arrival of Mohammadeen was, as it always was, an event. He did not enter rooms. He arrived in them. The specific operatic way an Egyptian math teacher could arrive at a village school and, without lifting his voice, make it entirely clear that he had arrived.

He came through the gate. He saw his wife. He froze — mid-stride, one foot still lifted. His moustache, which normally had a confident upward cant, drooped slightly. His eyes travelled from the top of his wife's yellow forehead to the bottom of her yellow chin.

Then he said — in the strong, rich, unmistakable Egyptian Arabic that he used when his feelings had exceeded his ability to contain them —


> الله! إيه ده اللي في وشك يا فتكات؟ عاملة زي لون الليمون كده ليه؟


(God! What is this on your face, ya Fatkat? Why are you the colour of a lemon like this?)

Fatkat put her hands on her hips. The specific stance of a wife who had not asked for her husband's opinion and did not, at this moment, desire it.

"Don't," she said, in English — clearly, firmly, in the universal international language of an annoyed wife — "talk to me."

The students arrived.

In their small usual wave — Ahmed composed, Khalil already mid-laugh with Ali, the girls in their triangle, Saeed at the back — they came through the gate.

Then Mansoor arrived.

Mansoor saw.

Mansoor pointed.

Mansoor laughed.

Very loudly. With the full abandon that was Mansoor's one unshakeable personal talent. "Teacher Fatkat! Teacher Safiya! You are… YELLOW!"

The class laughed.

Every single child. Even Ahmed permitted himself a small dignified exhale that, in another child, would have been a giggle.

Safiya tried to stay calm. She attempted a small teacher-smile. It came out as something closer to the specific tight smile of a woman who had, that morning, agreed to have saffron applied to her face and was now, publicly, paying the price.

Fatkat, on the other hand, did not try to stay calm. Fatkat had never, in her life, tried to stay calm when she had been laughed at in public.

"OUT!" she shouted. "YOU! OUTSIDE!"

Mansoor stood there. His face went through several stages — first triumph, then slight confusion, then a small flicker of protest.

"But teacher," he said, "I just—"

"OUT!"

Mansoor went out.

Sad. But — because he was, and would always be, Mansoor — still smiling.


## Scene Five — In the Classroom


John walked to the blackboard. With the specific small ceremonial movement of a teacher who had, in that specific moment, just thought of exactly the lesson the day required.

He took a piece of chalk. He wrote, in his careful teacher's capitals:

Today's Topic: SKIN, SOAP, AND THE SUN

He turned around. "Today," he said, with the quiet satisfaction of a man using his wife's small disaster as pedagogical material, "we talk about… what not to do."

Safiya — from her seat, face still yellow, cheeks slightly flushed beneath the yellow — touched her own face one more time. "And what not to put."

Thuraya smiled. "Let's learn."

John wrote a list on the board. A simple list. The specific list that any English teacher in any country would write when he had decided to turn today's material into tomorrow's vocabulary.

skinfacesoapwatercleandirtysunburnwashcare

He pointed to Safiya. "Say: My skin is…"

Safiya — composing her face into the specific solemn teacher-composure that she had developed over the last few months — said, "My skin is… yellow."

The class laughed. Again.

John nodded. "Good sentence. Sad, but good."

He pointed to a student — one of the smaller boys, a child Safiya did not yet know by name. "You. Say: I wash my…"

The boy — with the specific concentration of a child preparing a short English sentence — said, "I wash my face."

"Good."

Safiya added — using gestures, because she had long ago learned that her English sentences landed more reliably when accompanied by small mime —

"I wash my hands. I wash my face. I use soap."

Each sentence came with its own small performance. For wash my hands, she rubbed her palms together. For wash my face, she made small circular motions at her yellow cheeks. For use soap, she mimed — carefully, deliberately — the lathering of imaginary soap between her fingers.

Thuraya said, "The sun is hot."

The class repeated, in their layered village-chorus voice — "The sun is hot."

John wrote on the board:

The sun is hot.My skin is red.I wash my face.I use soap.I take care of my skin.

The children copied. Quietly. Ahmed's copying was, as always, a small act of calligraphy. Noura's was decorated, by the time she had finished, with a small flower beside the sun. Mansoor's copying — which was happening through the open window, because he had not been allowed back inside — was loud, laboured, and accompanied by loud whispered readings of each word as he wrote it.

Then — when the copying had mostly subsided — there was a knock on the door.

Very quietly.

Very humbly.

Every head in the room turned toward the door. They knew whose knock it was. There were not, after all, many candidates.

Fatkat crossed her arms. "Say sorry."

The door opened, just a crack. Mansoor's small face appeared. He looked at the yellow faces — now slightly less yellow than they had been, but still significantly yellow. He looked at them gravely. Then, with the specific serious tone of a boy who had been preparing his apology for the last fifteen minutes, he said —

"I am sorry."

A silence. In which Fatkat, perhaps, let herself believe that the apology was done.

Then Mansoor, after thinking, added — with the specific honest frankness of a boy who could not, in a single apology, betray the truth —

"But… you still look funny."

Fatkat pointed at the door.

"Outside again."

Mansoor — with the specific sad acceptance of a small boy who had known, in his heart, that this was how the sentence would end — went out.


## Scene Six — The Skin That Didn't Care


By the end of the day, the yellow started to become… less yellow.

Not gone. Not yet. But fading. The specific soft retreat of a stain that was, like all stains, eventually persuaded by time.

Safiya touched her face, cautiously, in the afternoon light of the classroom — the late afternoon light, which came in low across the desks and gave everything the specific gold-warm colour of a day nearing its close.

"I think," she said, quietly, to Fatkat, "it is leaving."

Fatkat — whose yellow was retreating at the same rate as Safiya's, though Fatkat had spent considerable energy hurrying it through three separate washings during the lunch break — sighed the long sigh of a woman who had learned a lesson.

"Next time," she said, with the weary finality of a person who had seen the truth, "we use water. Only water."

John smiled. The specific small peaceful smile of a man whose earlier warning had been, by the course of the day, magnificently confirmed — and who had, to his credit, decided not to say I told you so.

"And maybe," he said, "a hat."

That evening, at the small wooden table in their simple house, John wrote in his notebook. His face — no longer the ghost-grey of the smoke day, no longer the bloodshot-red of the wasp day, just the plain pale-pink of a tired English teacher — was settled in the specific calm of a man who had lived through another small village comedy and was, as always, making notes.

He wrote:

Today we learned:The sun is strong.Soap is useful.And not all "skin care" cares about skin.

He paused. He looked at the last line. He smiled at it, privately — the specific private smile that a writer gives to a sentence he suspects might, one day, be quoted back at him.

He closed the notebook.

Safiya, sitting across from him, was — with the specific slow patient movements of a woman whose face still carried the faint suggestion of saffron — applying a small amount of plain water to her cheeks. No soap. No sandalwood. No anything else.

Malood, who had — through processes Safiya did not want to examine — somehow found his way into the house again, wandered over to Safiya. He considered her hand — the hand with the water on it — and, with the specific earnest concentration of a small goat who had decided that the hand looked interesting, tried to lick it.

Safiya moved her hand. Fast.

"No," she said — firmly, with the quiet authority of a woman whose morning had been expensive enough. "You already did enough."

Malood considered this. He considered it for perhaps two seconds — the specific two seconds a goat needed to weigh whether to press his case. Then, apparently accepting the ruling, he turned. He walked with the small injured dignity of an unappreciated guest to the corner of the bisat. He lay down.

He closed his eyes.

He slept.

And outside the small clay house, the sun — which had, that morning, launched its personal attack from the sky — finally, as it always did, lowered itself toward the mountains. The village air cooled. The falaj continued. Somewhere, a woman was beginning her evening cooking, and the faint smell of onions and ghee began to travel, lightly, along the path.

And Safiya — Safiya, with her fading-yellow face, with her clean ring back on her finger, with the village having, in the last two days, forgiven her, and having, in the last two hours, once again laughed with her — understood that she was becoming a person she had not been before.

Not yellow. Not English. Not Omani.

Something in between. Something the village was, slowly, steadily, patiently — without ever having been asked — in the process of making.


# Palm Fourteen — Sadou Climbs the Palm



## Scene One — The Man in the Sky


The morning in Samail was already warm — even before the sun had climbed properly high — with the specific early warmth of a day that had decided, in advance, that it was not going to be merciful.

The mountains looked sleepy. The village did not. Roosters were loud. Doors were opening. And somewhere — a sound that was, by now, simply part of the soundtrack of Samail mornings — someone was already shouting at a goat.

John and Safiya walked with Thuraya along the dusty road toward the school. Malood, the baby goat, followed them for a few steps — and then stopped. A leaf, to his left, had looked, for reasons Safiya could not ascertain, more interesting than they were.

Safiya fixed her scarf. "I still can't believe how quiet this place is in the morning."

John smiled. "Quiet is good. I hope the class is quiet today too."

Thuraya laughed — the specific warm laugh she reserved for John's most optimistic predictions about his classroom. "You always hope."

They were just passing near the farms — the small narrow plots of palm trees that ran along the edge of the village — when Safiya suddenly stopped walking.

"Wait… what is that?"

John followed her eyes — up.

On the tall palm tree near the road, a man was climbing. Not slowly. Not carefully. Not with the specific cautious technique of a person who had thought, even briefly, about gravity.

He was flying.

Up, up, up — using his hands and feet as though the tree were a ladder, as though the tree were his own staircase, as though the tree were, in fact, a floor on which he was merely walking in an unconventional direction. His small body moved in a rhythm Safiya could not follow. Her eye wanted to catch each placement of the foot, each grip of the hand, and her eye failed. By the time she was still finding his second position, he was on his fifth. The tree was, perhaps, fifteen metres tall. It did not seem to matter.

It was Sadoo.

The shop owner.

The man who sold potatoes, and sweets, and an unclassifiable assortment of small strange things that nobody had ever asked for and which, somehow, always sold.

And now he was on top of a palm tree.

John opened his mouth. "Is… is that Sadoo?"

Thuraya nodded. Calmly. As though this were, in fact, perfectly normal. As though everybody who ran a shop in Samail also climbed palm trees on Wednesday mornings — which, Safiya was starting to suspect, might be true.

"Yes."

Safiya blinked. "Why," she asked — genuinely, sincerely, the way one asks children questions about things in picture books — "is he in the sky?"

Thuraya smiled. "He is " data-m="Gathering a crop; here, cutting dates from the tree at the point where they are ripe" data-s="gathering, picking, collecting" data-x="Harvesting dates was a technical skill. You needed to know which fruit was ready. Sadoo knew.">harvesting dates. This is how people collect fresh dates. They climb. And cut."

John was impressed. His face had moved, over the last twenty seconds, through several phases — disbelief, admiration, cultural awe, and had now settled into the specific wide-eyed English expression that meant I am going to have to revise several of my opinions.

"So…" he said, slowly, carefully, "Sadoo sells things in a shop… and he climbs palm trees?"

"Of course," Thuraya said.

The of course was, in its specific gentle simplicity, more surprising than the climbing itself.

They watched Sadoo cut a cluster of dates. They watched him — improbably, casually — jump down with the bag of dates, as though the descent from a fifteen-metre palm tree were the kind of thing a small man did between errands. He landed easily. He brushed his trousers. He looked up toward the three observers at the road with the specific delighted awareness of a village man who had noticed his audience and was about to address it.


## Scene Two — The Multi-Talented Superhero


John shook his head slowly. He said, in English, with the specific ceremonial voice of a man pronouncing a formal title: "Sadoo is… a multi-talented superhero."

Thuraya laughed.

"You haven't seen anything yet."

She paused. The specific small pause she used when she was about to deliver information that would delight John.

"He also has a sewing shop."

John froze.

His brain — Safiya could see — stopped working for one second. The full second. A small, complete English pause while the sentence he also has a sewing shop made its way, through the dust and the sunlight and the still-settling fact of the palm-tree descent, into his already-overloaded understanding of what Sadoo was.

"A… what?"

"A sewing shop," Thuraya said. "He fixes clothes."

John looked at Sadoo — who was now walking toward them, bag of dates over his shoulder, shirt slightly dusty — the way one looks at a legend who had just been revealed to be three different legends in one.

Sadoo saw them. He smiled — a broad, unreserved village-shopkeeper smile. He hit his chest with the flat of his palm — the specific gesture of a man making a claim about himself — and called out to them, in his full Samaili Arabic:


> فكّر ما في معلوم أنا… سادو! أنا… سادو يعرف كل شي منيه!


(Think there's nothing I don't know! I am Sadoo! I, Sadoo, know every single thing!)

And John — without thinking, without permission, without any consultation with the English part of his brain — answered. In Arabic. Once again.


> أبوي، ما نرومه صوبنا!


(Father, we can't reach where you are!)

Safiya and Thuraya both stopped walking.

They looked at John.

Slowly. Carefully. The specific slow scrutiny reserved for men whose mouths had, for the second time in two weeks, produced Arabic sentences that their mouths had no business producing.

Safiya said, "What… did you just say?"

John blinked. The genuine, confused blink of a man whose own ears were catching up with his own mouth. "Did I say something?"

Thuraya crossed her arms. "Yes. In Arabic."

John suddenly felt cold. Again. The specific cold that had, over the last fortnight, become a familiar sensation along his back. The cold that came when he had, once again, found Arabic coming out of his mouth without ever having put it there.

"Oh."

He looked at the ground. Then at the sky. Then at Sadoo.

He pretended, with the specific dignified pretending of a man who had just embarrassed himself in front of his wife for the seventh time that month, that he had not heard anything. He walked faster.

In his head, meanwhile, a small anxious argument was taking place.

How did I say that? I don't know that sentence. Am I… learning Arabic in my sleep? Or… is this that maghsoob thing again? No. No. Don't think. Just walk.

Sadoo laughed behind them. A big, friendly, completely unconcerned laugh. He waved. They waved back. They continued to school.


## Scene Three — I Can, He Can, She Can


The classroom was noisy before the lesson even started.

Mansoor was making a paper airplane.

Khalil was — with the specific small patience of a boy who believed, deeply, that everything was better when stolen — trying to steal it.

Noura was talking. She had started talking before she entered the room, and she was continuing to talk at the same speed, with the same energy, to a slightly different audience.

Ahmed was sitting straight like a ruler. His ruler — his actual ruler — was out. He had aligned it, on his desk, parallel to the edge.

Mathila was sharing a pencil. It was, somehow, the same pencil that had been the shared pencil of the Marwa–Rahma–Salima triangle for three weeks.

And Saeed — the quiet boy at the back — was sitting in his usual position, watching everything. As always.

John entered. He cleared his throat.

"Good morning!"

"Good morning, teacher!"

He wrote on the board, in clear capitals:

Today's Lesson: TALENTSI can… / He can… / She can…

Safiya clapped her hands. "Okay! Today we talk about what you can do."

Thuraya smiled. "Your skills. Your talents."

Mansoor raised his hand — without waiting to be called on, with the specific irrepressible certainty of a boy who had already identified the best answer.

"I can run!"

John nodded. "Good. Say: I can run."

"I can run!"

Khalil, not to be outdone — "I can climb!"

Safiya smiled. "I can climb."

Ahmed — quietly, with the specific understated dignity he applied to all his contributions — "I can read."

The room went a little quieter. I can read. It was the sentence, Safiya understood, that was — of all the sentences in that classroom, of all the sentences in that village — the most consequential. A nine-year-old Omani boy saying I can read in English, in 1973, in a village where most of his grandparents could not, was a small revolution disguised as a grammar exercise.

Mathila — proudly — "I can help."

Noura — with the small satisfied air of a girl reporting what everyone had been wanting to hear — "I can talk."

The class laughed. It was not cruelty. It was recognition.

John wrote on the board:

I can run.I can climb.I can read.I can help.


## Scene Four — Sadoo Can Climb


Then John did something he had not planned. He took a fresh piece of chalk, and drew — quickly, with a small amateur flourish — a palm tree on the board. Tall. A few curved lines for fronds.

On the trunk, halfway up, he drew a small man. Also amateur. Also charming. Also, unmistakably, Sadoo.

He wrote beneath it:

Sadoo can climb a palm tree.

The class laughed. Happily. They had all seen it — some of them that morning, all of them at some point — and the specific pleasure of having your own village shopkeeper put on the board as a grammar example was a pleasure no textbook could have given them.

Mansoor shouted — because Mansoor always shouted — "Yes! Sadoo climb like monkey!"

John corrected him. Gently. With the specific small patient correction of a teacher who had decided not to fight the noun.

"Sadoo can climb."

"Sadoo can climb!" the class repeated.

Safiya pointed at John. "Teacher John — what can you do?"

John smiled. The specific small modest teacher-smile that he had been practising since he was twenty-two. "I can… teach."

The class clapped.

Thuraya — from her corner — "And I can explain."

Safiya — "And I can organise."

Mansoor — not to be left out, and never willing to allow a good sentence to pass without contributing — "And I can… make noise!"

At exactly that moment, Mohammadeen — who had been walking past the open door on his way to his own classroom, carrying his math books and his specific expression of Egyptian professional purpose — paused.

He looked inside. He surveyed the room.

He said, in his strong Egyptian voice — the specific voice that required, structurally, obedience — "No. You cannot."

The class went quiet immediately.

The silence was surgical. Not gradual. One full second of full complete classroom silence. Even Mansoor's mouth closed.

Safiya whispered to Thuraya, "Wow. He doesn't need English."

John laughed — softly, under his breath. "He is universal."


## Scene Five — The Quiet Challenge


John turned back to the class. He looked at Mansoor — still, for the first time that morning, quiet.

"Mansoor. Can you be quiet?"

Mansoor stood up. Proudly. The specific proud standing of a boy who had just been asked if he could do something, and had decided, with the full commitment of his small eight-year-old chest, to say yes.

"Yes. I can."

Thuraya raised one eyebrow. "Let's see."

Safiya looked at her watch. The small silver watch her father had given her on her twenty-first birthday. She had not needed it often, in this village, where time was measured by prayers and the falaj. But it still worked.

"Let's try. Two minutes."

John said, "Okay. Mansoor. Sit. Be quiet."

Mansoor sat.

He was quiet.

For ten seconds.

He was quiet in the specific concentrated way that small boys are quiet when they have decided that quiet is a thing they can win at. His hands were flat on the desk. His face was forward. His eyes were on a specific small mark on the blackboard that he had, in the last three seconds, decided would be his anchor. He was, for the first time since Safiya had met him, performing stillness.

Then — his foot began to move.

Small. Involuntary. The foot tapping, very lightly, against the leg of the desk.

Then his hand. A single finger, moving in a small rhythm on the wood.

Then he whispered to Khalil.

John looked at him.

Mansoor closed his mouth. Abruptly. Mid-whisper. Like a gate.

Thirty seconds.

He started, very quietly, making airplane noises. Brrrrrrm. Brrrrrrm. Small. Under his breath. As though the airplane were very far away.

Safiya said — calm, teacher-firm — "Mansoor…"

He stopped.

One minute.

He was — Safiya noticed with a small twinge of affection — shaking. Actually shaking. The specific small tremor of a small body holding back its own natural state.

Thuraya said, kindly — "You can do it."

Mansoor nodded. Seriously. Gratefully.

One minute and thirty seconds.

Then — all at once, in a small explosion of honesty that Safiya would later remember as one of the truest moments of her teaching year — he broke.

"Teacher, I CAN'T!"

The class laughed.

John laughed too. The specific warm teacher-laugh that said: you have given me something honest, and I accept it.

"It's okay," he said. "You can try again tomorrow."

And he turned to the board. He picked up the chalk. And — with the specific considered care of a teacher giving a class its real, final sentence of the day — he wrote, beneath the list of talents, one more line:

I can try.

The class repeated it. In full layered village chorus. Including Mansoor — who said it loudest of all, because he had, in the last ninety seconds, discovered that trying was a kind of can he could, in fact, manage.

"I can try."


## Scene Six — The Sewing Shop


When the bell rang, Safiya said, "Today was good."

John nodded. "Very good."

They walked out.

Near the road — the small stretch that passed the edge of the market and ran toward the houses — they saw Sadoo again.

This time, he was not in a tree. This time, he was sitting on a low wooden stool outside the small front door of his shop. And he was sewing.

Not just mending. Not just poking a needle through a tear. He was sewing — the specific careful, practised, entirely artisan sewing of a man whose hands had, for many years, been restoring shirts. His small dark-eyed face was bent over the cloth. A long piece of white thread ran from his needle to the spool. Beside him, on the step, sat two more shirts, folded, waiting their turn.

Safiya's mouth opened.

"He really does everything."

John smiled. The specific settled smile of a man who had, in one morning, rebuilt his entire concept of the village economy.

"Sadoo can climb. Sadoo can sew. Sadoo can sell. Sadoo can…"

He paused — because he was about to continue, and because he had realised, even as he said it, that he could not possibly finish the list. Sadoo could, as far as John was now concerned, do everything. The list had no end.

Sadoo looked up. He saw them. He smiled — proudly, with the specific self-satisfaction of a man whose list of skills had just been the subject of classroom instruction.

John almost said something in Arabic again.

He could feel it. The small rising sentence. The specific tightening at the back of his throat that meant his mouth had prepared a phrase his brain did not recognise.

He stopped himself.

Deliberately. With the specific small discipline of a man who had now decided to be a witness to his own mouth rather than a victim of it. He closed his lips. He nodded at Sadoo. In English. With only English.

"Good work, Sadoo."

Sadoo beamed. He held up the shirt he was working on, as though to show it. He gave a small polite inclination of the head.

Safiya looked at John. She looked suspiciously at John.

"You're thinking in Arabic again," she said, flatly. "Aren't you?"

John considered the question. He considered it honestly, as a man who had, over the last several weeks, developed a new relationship with his own mouth.

"No," he said. A pause. "Maybe." Another pause. "I don't know."

Thuraya laughed. Warmly. The specific teacherly laugh she used when she was about to deliver a sentence that was both a joke and, underneath the joke, a true observation.

"Don't worry, Mr. John. Slowly, slowly, Oman is teaching you."

John looked up.

He looked at the palm trees along the road. He looked at the mountains, low and brown-blue, holding their ancient line against the sky. He looked at the school, small and white, already empty for the afternoon. He looked at Sadoo, sewing his small shirt on his small stool, the man who could do, apparently, every single thing this village required.

He looked at his wife. At Thuraya. At the small dusty road home.

And he said, quietly — as much to himself as to anyone listening — "Maybe I can learn more than I think."

Malood, somewhere, invisible but present, chose that exact moment to kick something small over in someone's yard. A small crash. A small bleat. The distant shout of Aunt Azizah calling his name.

John did not even flinch.

He was, Safiya realised — quietly, in the pink-gold late afternoon — becoming a man who flinched less.


# Palm Fifteen — When Marhoon Lifted His Hand



## Scene One — The Missing Things


Some days in Samail started quietly.

And some days… waited for trouble — the way trouble waited, sometimes, in the cool hour before the sun climbed — like something hiding behind the palm trees.

That day started with fear.

Not the large fear. The small, ordinary, lost-object fear of the village schoolyard. It began there — at the small low wall where the children usually gathered before the bell, where the dust lay soft against the clay, where most of the village's lost-and-found-and-lost-again objects passed through at some point in any given week.

Ali was jumping. Not happily. On one foot. Angry, and confused, with the specific narrow-browed urgency of a boy whose morning had just acquired a crisis.

"My zanoubah is GONE!" he shouted. "My mother will KILL me!"

Rahma was crying. Not the small crying of a small wound. Big crying. The specific full-chested crying of a small girl whose new, freshly-given, buckle-equipped sandals — the sandals her father had bought her from Samail market only three days earlier — had, somehow, been taken by the universe.

"My new sandals!" she wept. "I just got them! With a BUCKLE!"

Mansoor — because Mansoor considered every small village drama to be, in some sense, his drama — looked around with wide, theatrical eyes. He composed his face into the specific low-browed seriousness of a boy about to deliver a terrible explanation. He lowered his voice. He let the sentence carry.

"It is," he said, "the work of Shayb Khalaf."

The children froze.

Khalil whispered — with the small anxious awe that the village's favourite sahir-theory produced — "The wizard?"

Mansoor nodded, seriously. "He makes hura. Things disappear."

Noura grabbed Salima's arm. "Maybe he took them to his clay house…"

Ahmed — the only voice of order in a schoolyard that was, once again, about to go myth-first — adjusted his books. His small wise face took on the specific patient weariness of a boy who had, by the age of nine, understood that the only reliable pleasure of being rational was watching everyone else be otherwise.

"That," he said, "doesn't make sense."

But nobody listened.

Only one boy knew the truth.

Saeed.


## Scene Two — What Saeed Saw


Saeed had seen it all.

He had seen Ali, earlier that morning, playing near the falaj — the specific small loud splashing game he played with the younger boys, in which one's zanoubah had a tendency to end up somewhere on the stone bank, and sometimes, if one were not careful, very near the edge.

He had seen Rahma, crossing the yard earlier, with her new buckled sandals — had seen her, in the specific careless way of a small girl whose shoes were still new, leave them near the tree while she played with Salima.

And he had seen Malood.

Yes.

Malood.

The baby goat. Moving — with the specific casual, acquisitive, entirely unbothered air of a goat who had been stealing small pieces of the village for weeks — toward both items. Taking them. Quietly. In his mouth. Disappearing behind the low wall with them, one at a time.

Saeed had seen all of it.

But Saeed said nothing.

He just watched.

He watched, as he watched everything, from the specific quiet place he had carved for himself at the edge of the group. He watched the children panic. He watched Mansoor deliver his sahir-theory. He watched Khalil absorb it. He watched Noura grip Salima's arm. He watched Ahmed try to be reasonable. And he watched, as he had so many mornings, the small pattern repeat itself: thing lost → old man blamed. It happened, as Thuraya had once said, a lot.

He could have spoken.

He did not.

And then — all at once, like the small punch of an old memory returning — he remembered.

He should be at the mal.

The small farm. His father's small strip of date palms, at the edge of the village, where the falaj channel crossed the stony patch of ground they had been renting for two years.

Watering.

He should have been watering an hour ago.

His stomach turned. It turned the way a child's stomach turns when the child has remembered, too late, something important. The specific dropping feeling — low, interior — that is the first form fear takes in a nine-year-old body.

He stood up. He did not run. He did not yet. He walked — briskly, the way a small boy walks when he is pretending he is not in a hurry — away from the school, down the small road, toward the farms.

He had, perhaps, ten minutes of walking between the schoolyard and the mal. His father, Marhoon, might not have arrived yet. He might have another half hour. He might make it.

He was not going to make it.


## Scene Three — At the Mal


The sun was high when Marhoon, Saeed's father, arrived at the " data-m="Omani Arabic: a small farm or piece of agricultural land, typically with palm trees and fed by a falaj channel" data-s="small-farm, date-plot" data-x="The mal was the family's small piece of earth. Every family in Samail had — or hoped to have — a mal.">mal.

He was a tall man. Thin. Sun-weathered, in the specific accumulated way of a man who had been working outside every day since he was twelve. He wore a plain dishdasha. He carried, over his shoulder, a small cloth pouch with his tobacco and the small rolled-up paper he used when he needed to smoke.

He stood in the middle of the small farm. He looked at the dry ground.

The channels were empty.

The plants were thirsty.

The small curved earth-banks that he had, himself, over several years, dug to bring the falaj water evenly to the base of each young date palm — those banks were dry. Bone-dry. The specific pale-cracked dry of ground that had not felt water since yesterday afternoon, at the earliest, and more likely the day before that.

His face became dark.

Not the dark of rage, not yet — the dark, rather, of a man whose entire investment in the patient future of his family was, at that moment, being evaluated in front of him, and found, in the most elementary way, to have been betrayed.

"SAEED!"

No answer.

He walked around the mal. Past the dry channels. Past the small rope that hung from the wooden frame over the water source. Past the iron bucket — the one Saeed was supposed to have been using, dipping down and pulling up, twenty, thirty, forty times by now, until the channels had filled and the water could find, by its own slow gravity, its way to the thirsty roots.

Nothing.

Then he saw it.

The rope. The bucket. Untouched.

The rope, in fact — he noticed — had been coiled, neatly, the way Saeed had coiled it the night before. Nobody had touched it since.

His jaw tightened. The specific small hardening of a man who had, in that instant, moved from the dark of concern to the dark of understanding.

"He went," he said — quietly, not to anyone, only to the dry earth and the hot air — "to watch those school children again."

He said it with the specific heavy disappointment of a father who had had this exact argument with his son before. The specific small tiredness of a man who had, many times, tried to explain that the mal was real and the school was for other boys.

He picked up a thin stick from the ground.

A kheyzaran. Like most fathers in the village carried, if they needed it. Like most fathers had been carried by their own fathers, if they had needed it. A thin piece of cane. Light. Flexible.

He walked.

Fast.

In the direction of the school.


## Scene Four — The Road


Saeed was sitting near the school road.

He had not, in the end, made it to the mal. He had made it halfway — then turned, drawn, as he always was, to the sound of the children. He had found his usual spot: a low stone wall beside a small ghaf tree, near the last bend before the school, where he could see the children at play through the low openings of the wall, without being seen by them.

He always watched.

He did not come close.

He just sat.

He was nine. He had never been inside a classroom. His father had, three years earlier, when the village had first built its school, forbidden him to go. His father had reasons. His father had, like most men of his generation, inherited them — the belief that formal school would make a son soft, would make him unfit for the mal, would teach him to say yes and no in a foreign tongue and forget the language of the falaj and the land. His father had not been wrong, exactly. He had simply been, about this one particular boy, misreading what the boy had been born to do. Saeed was born to collect. Not things. Stories.

Then he heard footsteps.

Heavy footsteps.

He turned.

His father.

Marhoon.

Saeed stood up. Slowly. The slowness of a small boy who had not yet decided whether to run or to face it, and who was, in that moment, deciding — with the specific small resignation of a child who had done this calculation before — to face it.

"Why are you HERE?" Marhoon shouted. "Why is the mal DRY?"

Saeed looked at the ground. The specific small looking-at-the-ground of a boy who was about to speak, and who knew, even before he spoke, that the sentence would not be enough.

"I just…" he said, "wanted to see…"

He did not finish.

The stick came down.

Once.

Twice.

Saeed cried out. He fell to his knees. The fall was not dramatic. It was the small, collapsed fall of a small body whose instinct was to curl.

Marhoon was shaking with anger. His whole tall thin frame was shaking. Not with the shake of a man losing control — with the shake of a man holding himself in place, against a current of something older than the morning.


> أهالك باه! تبا تتعلم yes و no كما الكفّار؟ تباني أربطك في ذي النخلة؟!


(Damn you! You want to learn "yes" and "no" like the unbelievers? You want me to tie you to this palm tree?!)

The children at the end of the road had stopped.

They had heard the shouting. They had stepped out from the schoolyard. They had gathered at the edge of the path — a small loose group of seven, eight, ten children — and they were watching, frozen, from far away.

Nobody moved.

Then — a voice, from further back along the road, came like thunder.

"STOP!"

It was Thuraya.

She was running forward — running in her long abaya, running with the specific full-body urgency of a woman who had seen something she was not going to allow — and Safiya was running behind her, and John was running behind Safiya, and the three of them crossed the space in a specific compressed several seconds that seemed, to everyone present, to last much longer.

Thuraya stood between Marhoon and Saeed. Like a wall.

"That is enough!"

Safiya — without any decision — knelt on the dust. She pulled Saeed into her. She held him the way one holds any small wounded thing: with a full, protective, entirely unnegotiable grip. He was shaking against her shoulder.

John looked at Marhoon. Angry. Confused. The specific English confusion of a man who had not yet had the Arabic word translated for him.

Then John asked, in English — carefully, because he did not want to be wrong — "What does koffar mean?"

Thuraya turned to him. Her face was tight. Her voice was level. "It means… unbelievers."


## Scene Five — The Arabic That Came


Thuraya took a deep breath. The specific long breath of a teacher gathering herself before entering a conversation she had not, at breakfast, expected to be in.

"Let me," she said, "talk to him."

John raised his hand. "Sabriiii!"

Everyone looked at him.

Sabri — wait — was, for a man like John who had been in the country only two months, a surprisingly confident request.

Then John started speaking.

In Arabic.

Without knowing.

Again.

Except this time, the Arabic was different. It was not the small shocked exclamation he had produced for Khalaf. It was not the small respectful reply he had offered at Sadoo's feet. This was a defence. A whole small speech, forming itself, syllable by syllable, out of his English mouth:


> أوووي، هااا! ما كفار حنوه! احنا مسلمين! نصلّي ونصوم كماكم باه! لا تحسب!


(Whoa, hey! We are not unbelievers! We are Muslims! We pray and we fast just like you! Don't think otherwise!)

Silence.

Pure silence. The specific layered silence of a road where every mouth was, simultaneously, closed in shock.

Marhoon's mouth opened. Not to speak. Simply to hang there — the specific physical shock of a man who had just been addressed, by a tall Englishman, in the full vernacular Arabic of his own village, claiming a religion that the Englishman did not, in strict fact, have.

The children's eyes became huge.

Safiya — still kneeling on the dust, still holding Saeed — whispered, in English, mostly to herself, in the specific small baffled tone of a wife who had just been included, without her consent, in a religious claim she had no memory of acquiring —

"When did we pray? Or fast?"

Then — arriving at a run, with Fatkat beside him — Mohammadeen.

He had heard. The Egyptian math teacher had heard the specific sound of an Englishman producing village Arabic, and had arrived at full speed to witness it. He took in the scene in one professional scan — the stick, the father, the shaking boy, the kneeling English woman, the standing English man, the frozen children.

He pointed at John. He shouted in his full, rich, unmistakable Egyptian:


> مش انتو يا أختي! ده الجني اللي فيه! يا خرابي، الواد اتلبس!


(It's not you, my sister! It's the jinni in him! Oh ruin of mine — the fellow has been possessed!)

Fatkat screamed.
