Not the small scream of surprise. The big, committed, operatic scream of a woman who had, a second earlier, decided that the situation required a full reaction and had, in the specific Egyptian way, produced one.

And then — she fainted.

She fell like a sack of rice.

Everyone jumped.

Safiya screamed, "Oh no!" — one hand still on Saeed's back, one hand reaching toward Fatkat.

Thuraya — Thuraya, in a moment that would, for decades afterwards, live in the private memory of everyone present — tried not to laugh. She tried with the specific full-body discipline of a teacher who understood that laughter, at this exact moment, was inappropriate. She failed. A small laugh — muffled — escaped her.

John blinked. He looked around — at his shaking wife, at the fainted sports teacher, at the frozen Egyptian husband, at the frozen village father, at the frozen children, at the kneeling boy.

"Why," he said, quietly, in English, "is everyone looking at me?"

Marhoon sat down. Slowly. Very slowly. The specific slow sitting-down of a man whose legs had, all at once, stopped agreeing to hold him.

He did not speak. He did not shout.

He just stared.

At John. At Saeed. At the world — the small piece of it he had, this morning, thought he understood.


## Scene Six — The Goat, and What Changed


It was Saeed who broke the silence.

He was still kneeling in the dust, inside Safiya's arm. He was still shaking — the small, receding shake of a body returning, slowly, to itself. His voice, when it came, was very small.

"I didn't steal anything…" he whispered. "Malood took them…"

They all turned.

Malood stood near the tree.

He had, at some point during the commotion — nobody had seen when — arrived. He had positioned himself, with the specific unconcerned proprietary air he brought to every scene, in the small strip of shade beneath the ghaf tree. And he was holding — visibly, without shame, without any comprehension of what he had just caused — Ali's zanoubah in his mouth.

And beside him, on the ground: Rahma's buckled sandal. Slightly chewed.

The children screamed.

"THE GOAT!"

Thuraya closed her eyes. The specific long weary closing of a woman who had, in her teaching career, witnessed the village blame a great many old men for a great many things, and who had now — yet again — seen one of her favourite small truths confirmed: that the guilty party, in this village, was almost always small, horned, and entirely unrepentant.

"Of course."

Later, when they walked back to the school — Saeed between Safiya and Thuraya, his small arm looped through Safiya's, his face streaked but calm — Marhoon did not follow them. He sat, for a long time, on the dusty patch of ground where he had landed. He did not pick the stick back up. He did not go to the mal. He simply sat.

And it was, Thuraya would tell Safiya that night, the specific sitting of a father who had just been shown, unmistakably, the gap between what he had believed and what had actually been true.

Back at school, Fatkat had woken up. She had been revived with water and a great deal of fanning by the other women. She was now drinking sweet tea and explaining, at high volume, to anyone who would listen, that she had known, she had known, that the English teacher's mouth was not normal. Mohammadeen, beside her, was still quietly telling everyone the boy was possessed. The classroom had divided, by now, into the pro-possession camp and the pro-confused-miracle camp, and the debate was, on Fatkat's side, lively.

John sat in the corner of the classroom. Holding his head.

Safiya looked at him. Quietly. With the specific small warm affection of a wife whose husband had, in the space of one morning, saved a boy and terrified the village, without quite knowing how he had done either.

"You," she said, "are dangerous in Arabic."

John whispered — into his palms, the specific muffled whisper of a man who had not yet decided how to feel about himself —

"I don't remember anything."

Thuraya looked across the classroom — past John, past Fatkat, past Mohammadeen — to the door.

Saeed was sitting there.

He was sitting quietly, on the small wooden step just inside the door. His arms around his knees. His face calm now, the small tear-tracks drying in the afternoon light. His eyes open. Watching.

Not a student.

Not a guest.

But — for the first time — not invisible anymore.

Thuraya watched him for a long moment. Then — very quietly, almost to herself — she said, "Tomorrow."

Safiya looked at her. "Tomorrow, what?"

Thuraya did not take her eyes off the boy.

"Tomorrow," she said, "we will go to the Sheikh's house. And we will fix this."

She paused. A small, considered, entirely settled pause.

"Not just for Saeed. For all of them."


# Palm Sixteen — The Sheikh's House and the Mandoos



## Scene One — The Invitation


In Samail, when something big happened, people did not talk about it in the street.

They talked about it in the Sheikh's house.

And that day, something big had happened.

Safiya had understood, over the course of the afternoon — slowly, the way she now understood most village matters, through the small layered sediment of small glances and half-translations — that the morning had shifted something. Marhoon's stick. John's uncontrolled Arabic. Fatkat fainting. Thuraya standing between a father and a son. The village had absorbed it all by noon. By afternoon, the village had begun to metabolise it. And by evening — as it turned out — the village had decided.

The message came in the afternoon.

A boy ran from house to house — the specific house-to-house small boy that every Omani village of that period kept in supply — saying the same sentence at each doorway.

"After Maghrib prayer. Everyone to the Sheikh's house."

He did not explain. He did not need to. The village already knew. By the time the boy reached the third doorway, the reason had travelled ahead of him.

Thuraya had been sitting on the low wooden bench outside the small clay house, folding a piece of cloth. She looked up when the boy arrived at the gate. She heard the sentence. She returned to her folding.

But her hands — Safiya noticed — had stopped.

She stood, quietly. She set the cloth aside. She said, without ceremony, with the specific calm voice she used when she was giving a piece of information that had weight —

"They will talk about Saeed. And about school."

John closed his notebook. He had been, all afternoon, writing nothing — holding the pen over the page without letting it touch down. His mind had not been settled enough to produce a sentence.

"Is that," he asked, carefully, "good or bad?"

Thuraya looked at him. She did not smile. She did not frown. She gave him, instead, the specific even honest look she gave John when she was about to translate a village concept into English and knew, in advance, that the English version would not quite capture it.

"In Samail," she said, "it is serious."

The word serious — in the specific way Thuraya used it — covered both good and bad. It covered every outcome that a village meeting was capable of producing. It was the correct word. John, hearing it, did not ask further.

Safiya looked up. "Do we need to prepare something?"

Thuraya smiled — a small, careful smile. "Just your hearts. And maybe… empty stomachs."

Safiya, who did not yet understand why empty stomachs would be useful, nodded anyway. She had, by now, learned that Thuraya's sentences usually explained themselves an hour or two later.


## Scene Two — The House of the Sheikh


The Sheikh's house was not small.

It was not fancy either.

It was, Safiya realised as they approached it in the last gold light after Maghrib, a house that had been built, deliberately, to be the house where large quantities of people could sit on the floor at the same time without knocking into each other.

It had a long front room. A wide low sitting-room along one wall. Incense smoke was already rising, in soft slow columns, from two censers at the two ends of the room.

But it was full.

Men sat on carpets.

Old men — the silver-bearded men of the village, the ones who no longer worked the farms but who weighed in, when it was time to weigh in. Young men — fathers of young children, men in their thirties and forties, men who still had skin in the game. Fathers. Uncles. A few men Safiya recognised from the school gate. A few she did not recognise at all.

The air smelled like coffee — the fresh cardamom-heavy coffee that had just been prepared in a room at the back — and incense, and the specific layered smell of serious talk that men's gatherings everywhere produce.

At the side of the compound, through a low open doorway, women were busy.

Very busy.

Big pots. Big cauldrons. Big plates. And big plans, already underway, for lunch — or rather, for dinner — after the meeting.

Safiya whispered to Thuraya, "Why is there so much food?"

Thuraya answered, without hesitation, with the specific calm certainty of a village truth —

"Because no Omani meeting ends without food."

Safiya nodded. "Important rule."

It was, she realised, an actual rule. Not just a custom. A rule. In the way that the falaj has laws was a rule. Every significant village gathering ended, no matter what else had happened, with the full communal meal — and the meal was, in itself, the ratification of the gathering.


## Scene Three — The Food Fear


Safiya looked around at the kitchen end of the compound. Her eye moved — idly at first — from the pots, to the plates, to the rising steam, to the faint aromatic cloud of cooking meat.

Then — without warning — her face changed.

"Thuraya…"

"Yes?"

"Where is Malood?"

Thuraya blinked. She had, like everyone else, been focused on the gathering, and had not thought about the goat in — she considered — perhaps the last hour and a half. "He was outside."

Safiya's eyes became big.

"Outside," she said, slowly, the specific slow English of a wife approaching a dreadful hypothesis, "or… in the kitchen?"

She looked at the big knives.

At the big pots.

At the big plates.

The logic, in Safiya's head, assembled itself with terrible speed. A village meeting. A big meal. Lamb, presumably. Where did lamb come from, on a day of no warning, with no time to go to the butcher? From a goat, presumably. A goat available locally. A small, horned, white goat who had, for the last two months, been making himself conspicuous in every courtyard of the village.

"Oh no," she whispered. "They killed Malood."

She held her heart. Literally. Her hand went, without her planning it, to her chest.

"The goat…"

At exactly that moment — the specific timing that only village comedy produces — Fatkat came around the corner. She was laughing. She had heard the last sentence.

She cupped her hands around her mouth — as though Safiya were across a courtyard instead of three metres away — and shouted, in her full Egyptian Arabic:


> إيه بس بقى يا أختي! مالود إيه اللي يتدبح؟ ده بره مع الحريم! قاعد ياكل ويعمل دوشة عند أم مثّلى!


(What are you on about, my sister! Malood — who would slaughter him? He is outside with the women! Sitting and eating and making noise at Umm Mathila's!)

Safiya froze. "He is… alive?"

Fatkat nodded. She put her hands on her hips — the specific wide-set stance of a woman who had just been asked a question that had a very satisfying answer. "More than alive."

Outside, as though he had heard — and as though he had decided, from wherever he was, to make himself audible — Malood's bell rang loudly. The specific bright small bell that Aunt Azizah had tied to his neck two weeks ago in an attempt to track him.

And someone, from the courtyard, shouted —

"STOP EATING THAT!"

Safiya almost cried. From happiness. From the specific full relief of a woman whose small adopted animal had not, after all, been sacrificed for the communal meal.

She sat down. On a small wooden stool. She held her heart again — this time in gratitude rather than grief.


## Scene Four — The Meeting Begins


Inside, the meeting had begun to gather itself.

The Sheikh sat in the middle — at the far end of the majlis, on a small raised cushion that raised him, by the width of a hand, above the rest of the room. He was, Safiya saw now — looking at him properly for the first time — an older man, perhaps seventy, with a white beard neatly combed, calm eyes, and the specific settled presence of a man who had spent forty years listening to villagers argue with each other and who had, at some point, stopped needing to raise his own voice.

Marhoon — Saeed's father — sat near the wall.

Quiet.

Not angry.

Just… heavy.

The specific heaviness of a man who had, that morning, been publicly shown the gap between what he had believed and what was actually true, and who had been carrying that gap around, inside his body, for the whole afternoon.

Saeed sat behind a pillar. Half hidden. The specific small half-hidden sitting of a boy who had been brought to the gathering, had been told he could stay, and who had chosen the exact seat that offered both attendance and, if needed, concealment.

John, Safiya, and Thuraya sat together. Near the door. In the specific slightly-set-apart way that was reserved for guests who were part of the conversation but not part of the lineage.

The Sheikh cleared his throat.

The room quieted. Immediately.

"We are here," he said — speaking in Arabic, slowly, with the specific practised clarity of a man who knew that his words would be translated in several heads around the room — "to talk about our children. And about learning."

A voice, from somewhere along the carpet, said: "School is new."

Someone else: "Work is more important."

Small nods. Small head-shakes. The specific small motion of a room in which several opinions had just silently surfaced.

Marhoon looked at the floor.

He did not speak.

He simply, for the first time in that long afternoon, looked down — and did not look up.

Thuraya stood up.

She did not shout.

She did not cry.

She just spoke — in her clear, clean, village-teacher Arabic, the specific Arabic she had developed over six years of working in a school, the Arabic that carried the dignity of having been used, many times, to translate small English words for small Omani ears.

"Knowledge," she said, "does not take your sons away from you. It gives them a way."

The sentence landed.

Not loudly. Not dramatically. But it landed, the way certain sentences land when they have been waiting, for a long time, for exactly the right room to be said in.

John wanted to help. Safiya could see him, beside her, the specific small tightening of a man whose mouth had, twice that day, gone off without his permission. He took in a small breath. He was about to speak.

Safiya held his arm. Firmly. Under the sleeve, her fingers closed around his wrist.

"No Arabic today."

He nodded. Slowly. With the specific, grateful, managed nod of a man who had, after three separate unauthorised Arabic episodes, begun to trust his wife's grip more than his own mouth.

Men talked.

Argued.

Listened.

The specific rotation of a majlis in action — one man giving his view, pausing, another man picking up the thread, the Sheikh inclining his head at intervals, a small interruption from a younger uncle, a small correction from an older one. Safiya could not follow all of it. But she could follow the shape.

And then, from somewhere near the wall, came a voice she did not, at first, recognise.

"I was afraid," it said — quietly, in simple Arabic that even John, leaning forward, could partially follow. "I thought school will change him."

Safiya turned her head.

Marhoon.

He had spoken. His head was still partly down. His voice was not loud. But every man in the room had heard the sentence.

The Sheikh nodded. Slowly. The specific slow nod of a man who had been waiting — patiently, without expecting — for this exact sentence.

"It will," he said. "But not in a bad way."

And across the room, behind the pillar, Saeed — the quiet boy, the collector of secrets, the nine-year-old who had, that morning, carried bruises from his own father's stick — looked up. For the first time. His eyes met the Sheikh's. Then they met his father's. Then — very slowly — they came to rest on Thuraya.


## Scene Five — And Then — Lunch


And like magic…

The serious faces changed.

It was the specific small wonder of Omani gatherings, which Safiya was witnessing for the first time — the instant at which the talking phase was understood to have ended, and the eating phase was understood to have begun, without anyone announcing the transition out loud.

Plates came.

The specific enormous communal plates of Omani hospitality, each carried by two people, each the size of a small table on its own.

Rice — the specific long-grain rice of the village, cooked with cardamom pods and saffron strands that had turned the grains a pale gold, with small slivers of fried almond and sweet raisins scattered across the top.

Meat.

More rice.

More meat.

And the men — in the specific coordinated way of men who had done this together many times — moved closer to the plates, tucked the edges of their dishdashas under their knees, washed their right hands in the small basins that had been passed around, and began, without announcement, to eat.

John looked at the food.

He looked at it the way a man looks at a thing he has, until this moment, not understood the scale of. His eyes were wide. His face was, Safiya noted, the specific face of an Englishman who had just recognised that the civilised portion sizes of his upbringing were, in the larger grammar of global hospitality, a modest dialect.

He ate.

And ate.

And ate.

Safiya, beside him, watched him work his way through a small mountain of rice and a generous layer of lamb, with the specific sustained appetite of a man who had decided, at some early point in the meal, that not to eat fully would be culturally rude.

After some time — John having now reached what Safiya would later describe to her sister as the fifth plateau — she stopped him. Quietly. With her hand on his wrist. Again.

"John…"

He said — with a full mouth, with the specific cheerful muffled delivery of a man who had, that evening, found his proper vocation — "This is history. I must respect history."

After the meal, the specific slow after-meal arrangement began. The tall young men collected the plates. The old men leaned back on cushions. John did not lean back. John had already leaned back, and was now holding his stomach with both hands, and was, Safiya could see, in a state of physical wonder at what had just happened to him.

He said — loudly, in English, with the specific small philosophical clarity that came to him after he had eaten far too much —

"Now I understand why Omani men have big belly."

Silence.

A small, appalled silence, on Safiya's side, at having a husband who said such things at such moments.

Then Thuraya — Thuraya, from across the room, laughing before she had even spoken — shouted back:

"It's good for wearing khanjar!"

The men laughed. Loudly. The specific warm, forgiving, welcoming laughter of a majlis that had just, collectively, agreed to take John's rudeness as a compliment.

Safiya blinked. "What is… khanjar?"

Thuraya smiled — the specific Thuraya-knows-something smile.

"You will see. Very soon."


## Scene Six — Outside, and That Night


Outside, Malood was still alive.

Very alive.

He was, by some combination of goat-cunning and lucky circumstance, in the back courtyard with the women and the children. He was eating. He was stealing food. He was making children scream — both from delight and from the specific fear of having their own small portion taken by a small white thief.

And next to him — close enough that his small hand, once or twice, settled on Malood's warm flank — sat Saeed.

Smiling.

Not widely. Not loudly. But — for the first time that Safiya had ever seen — smiling.

The specific quiet smile of a nine-year-old who had just, in his own small way, been inside a village decision. Had heard his father speak. Had heard the Sheikh reply. Had heard the room not reject him. Had watched his father, across the room, not look at him with anger — and, eventually, not look at him at all, because the matter had been, in the unspoken village way, resolved.

Tomorrow, Saeed would, at long last, come to school.

Nobody had said it in those words. Nobody had put it on paper. But it had been, somewhere between Thuraya's sentence and the Sheikh's nod and Marhoon's quiet admission, decided. And every person in that majlis knew it.

That night, in their simple house, John lay on the bisat with his hand on his stomach. He was still feeling, faintly, the weight of the rice. He would, he had announced at the door, not eat again for two days. Safiya had smiled at this — the small, knowing smile of a wife who had heard that exact declaration before, and who knew, with complete confidence, how long it would last.

He reached for his notebook.

He had been writing more, over the last week. The notebook was now perhaps a third full. He had, without planning, begun to keep a kind of journal — not a professional record, not a teaching log, but the specific accumulated personal record of a man being quietly, steadily, made by a village.

He wrote, slowly:

Today, a village talked. And something changed.

Safiya, sitting across from him on her own small part of the bisat, had her own notebook open. She was, she had decided some weeks earlier, going to keep her own record. She wrote her sentence beneath his — in her smaller, rounder English hand:

Also, Malood is not food.

John read the sentence. He laughed — the specific quiet full-stomach laugh of a man who had been worn out by the day and was now, at the end of it, content.

Outside, Thuraya stood in the small courtyard of her parents' house, looking at the stars.

The stars in Samail, in October of 1973, were a specific dense pattern that the village had been watching since long before there had been a village. They had watched, that evening, as the village reasoned with itself. They had watched the Sheikh nod. They had watched Marhoon admit his fear. They had watched Saeed look up.

Thuraya looked at them for a long time. And then she said — quietly, to no one, only to the stars — the specific small sentence a teacher says when she is, in private, admitting to herself that something she has been hoping for has finally happened:

"Tomorrow," she said, "we start something new."


# Palm Seventeen — Spicy Food and Saeed's First English Sentences



## Scene One — The Visit


Some houses in Samail had secrets.

Not bad secrets.

Beautiful ones.

And most of them lived inside a mandoos.

That morning, Thuraya invited John and Safiya to her parents' house.

"My mother wants to show you something," she said, smiling in the specific way that — Safiya had learned to read — meant many somethings.

Safiya was excited. "Is it food?"

Thuraya laughed. "Not this time."

John looked suspicious. The specific wary look of a man who had, by now, been surprised enough times by Omani invitations to know that the word something carried weight.

"Is it," he asked, "something that will make me eat again?"

His stomach — from the previous night's meal at the Sheikh's house — had, he would later admit, still not fully recovered. He had eaten, in fact, only a single small piece of bread for breakfast, in an attempt to let his body reach a neutral state.

"Maybe," Thuraya said. The specific teasing maybe. "But first… culture."

They walked together to Thuraya's parents' house — along the small path that ran behind the school, past two palm groves, past the old well that had not been used since the falaj had been extended in 1968, to the low clay house with its blue door and its small front yard swept clean every morning.

The house was simple and warm. The walls were light-coloured — the specific warm pale ochre of unpainted village clay — and the rooms smelled of oud — burning quietly in a small clay censer by the door — and of coffee.

Thuraya's mother welcomed them kindly. She was a small sturdy woman with hennaed fingertips and the specific steady warmth of a village woman who had, by her sixties, become entirely at home with strangers.

"Come, come," she said. "Sit."

They sat on the low cushions arranged along the wall. She brought coffee — in the small traditional cups, the specific finjans of Omani hospitality — and a small dish of dates. She spoke to them, through Thuraya's gentle translation, about the weather, about their health, about John's leg, which had, by now, fully recovered from the wasp.

Then she disappeared into another room.

She came back — moving slowly, careful — with something held in both arms.

A big wooden box.

Old.

Heavy.

Beautiful.

It was perhaps the size of a small coffee table. Its dark wood had been polished, over decades, by the specific careful weekly rubbing of someone's hands. Its front and sides were studded with small brass nails — dozens and dozens of them, arranged in geometric patterns, in the specific traditional designs that Safiya had begun to recognise on door lintels and cupboard fronts around the village.

The lid had a small brass lock. The key — a heavy, old, ornate key — hung on a thin silver chain from Thuraya's mother's wrist.

"What is that?" Safiya asked. Her voice had gone quiet. The specific quiet voice of a woman who had recognised, in advance of knowing anything else, that she was about to be shown something important.

Thuraya said — proudly, warmly, with the specific affection of a daughter referring to a family object — "That is the mandoos."

John reached forward. He touched it — carefully, with two fingers, on one of the brass studs. "It looks like a pirate treasure box."

Thuraya's mother, when this was translated for her, smiled. The specific small proud smile of a woman who had heard her own beloved chest compared to a pirate's box, and who had, with immediate amused grace, decided to accept the compliment and improve it.

"Better," she said.


## Scene Two — The Treasure Inside


The mandoos opened with a slow, dramatic sound — the specific soft creak of wood that has been closed for a long time, of old hinges, of a lock that had been turned by only a few specific hands over several decades.

Inside…

Light.

Silver.

Gold.

Stories.

Safiya, without meaning to, leaned forward. Her breath caught. The inside of the mandoos was not just a container. It was, as she would write in her notebook that night, a whole small museum of someone's life.

Thuraya's mother took out the first thing.

A khanjar.

John's eyes became round. The specific round-eyed look of a man who had, in one week, heard the word khanjar several times, and was now — finally — seeing it in the actual hand of an actual Omani woman.

"Oh! The curved knife!"

Thuraya looked at him. Seriously. With the specific patient seriousness she used when she was about to correct a foreign visitor's understanding of a national symbol.

"It is not just a knife."

She paused. To let the correction land.

"It is a symbol."

The khanjar was perhaps thirty centimetres from the curved tip of its sheath to the top of its hilt. The sheath itself was curved upward in the specific crescent shape that was the national signature of Oman. Fine silver wire, worked in intricate patterns, wrapped the sheath from its tip almost to its mouth. Small hand-hammered rosettes decorated the silver. The belt that came with it was a wide leather strap, embossed with further silver threads.

Safiya asked, quietly, "Can I touch it?"

Thuraya's mother nodded.

Safiya held it. She held it the way one holds a thing that might, at any moment, turn out to be made of glass. Her hands, she noticed, had gone very careful.

"It's beautiful."

Then came the silver jewellery.

The specific silver jewellery of old Oman, the kind that had been made, piece by piece, by small specialist silversmiths in the markets of Nizwa and Muscat and Rustaq over the last sixty years, and which Thuraya's grandmother had, over the course of her married life, collected.

Big necklaces — the specific wide pectoral necklaces that women wore at weddings, with long silver pendants that hung down the chest and reached almost to the waist.

Heavy bracelets — Safiya lifted one and was surprised by its weight. A single bracelet, worn on a wrist for a day, would remind its wearer of its presence every time she raised her arm.

Shiny earrings — small and large, some with drops of silver, some with tiny coloured stones, some with the specific cone-shaped silver ornaments that had been made in Nizwa for perhaps two hundred years.

And — something else. Something that, when Thuraya's mother lifted it from the mandoos, moved.

A small, very specific, cascading movement. And a sound. The small soft shimmer of small silver pieces moving against each other.

Thuraya smiled. "That," she said, "is martaasha."

Safiya said the word slowly, carefully. "Mar… ta… asha."

The jewel moved in Thuraya's mother's hand. It made the small soft sound again.

"Oh!" Safiya laughed — the specific small delighted laugh of a woman who had just understood. "It is shaking!"

Thuraya nodded. "That's why."

Then came the banjari.

Safiya looked confused. "What is this?"

Thuraya's mother showed her. "Bracelets. Many of them."

She gestured for Safiya's arm. Safiya, slightly hesitant, extended it. Thuraya's mother slid the banjari, one at a time, onto her wrist.

One.

Cling.

Two.

Cling. Cling.

Three.

Cling. Cling. Cling.

By the time she had finished, Safiya's wrist carried perhaps twelve thin silver bracelets. She moved her hand. They all moved with her.

It sounded like music.

John clapped. "You," he said, "are now a walking instrument."


## Scene Three — Silver or Gold?


Thuraya took out two necklaces from the mandoos. She laid them, carefully, on a small white cloth on the low table in front of them.

"One is silver," she said. "One is gold."

John, inspecting both, said — with the specific careful observation of a man trying to contribute meaningfully to a culture lesson — "Silver is… gray. Gold is… yellow."

Safiya, across from him, could not resist.

"Like John," she said — with the specific small warm teasing of a wife who had been, all week, saving up this observation — "after lunch, and John before lunch."

John ignored her. The specific dignified English ignoring that had been, over the course of his marriage, refined into a small art form.

Thuraya smiled. She had heard the joke. She would, she decided, let John keep his dignity.

"In Oman," she said — shifting back to her teacher voice, the specific voice she used when she was about to explain something with cultural weight — "silver is very important. Especially for women."

She lifted the silver necklace. The pieces of it caught the light from the small high window — the light had reached, by this hour, the oblique mid-morning angle that was, Safiya thought, the exact right angle for showing silver off.

The silver had a different quality to it than the silver she had known in London. It was handmade silver — the kind that had small inconsistencies, small hammer marks, small visible signs of the specific hand that had shaped it. It was not polished to factory brightness. It was polished to the softer shine of a thing that had been loved and used.

Safiya looked at the jewelry on the table.

"They are heavy," she said.

Heavy was the word. A single necklace, if worn, would sit on a woman's chest with real, sustained, remembering weight. You would not forget you were wearing it.

Thuraya's mother nodded — the specific calm confirming nod of a woman who had, many times, worn exactly these pieces.

"Yes. But beautiful."

John — holding one of the larger necklaces across his own palm, testing the weight — said, with genuine wonder, "I think, if I wear this, I will fall."

Thuraya laughed. "We don't put everything on one person."

The jewelry, she explained — as her mother began lifting out smaller items, one by one, placing them on the cloth — was distributed across a woman's body on the specific day of her wedding. The martaasha at the throat. The banjari at the wrist. The earrings. The rings. The belt. The headpiece. The whole thing, together, had its own name, its own order, its own meaning.

A bride, dressed in all of it, was — for one day — carrying her own family's entire saved history on her body.


## Scene Four — Trying Clothes


Then Thuraya's mother brought traditional clothes.

She came out from the inner room carrying them carefully over both arms — the specific reverent carrying of clothes that have been, for years, waiting in a dark cupboard for exactly this kind of morning.

A dress for Safiya. The specific embroidered wedding dress of an Omani woman, with gold threadwork along the cuffs and across the chest, in the specific geometric patterns that had been, in this region, woven for perhaps a hundred years.

A dishdasha for John. Plain white. Freshly pressed. The specific clean cotton whiteness of a dishdasha that had been saved for guests.

And a belt.

And something long and shiny.

John pointed — with the specific careful pointing of a man who had understood, the instant it appeared, what the long shiny thing was, and who wanted — but had not quite dared — to ask.

"Is that… for me?"

Thuraya smiled. Slowly. The specific slow smile of a woman about to give a tall Englishman, for the first time in his life, a khanjar.

"Yes."

John wore the dishdasha.

It fell to his ankles. It was, Safiya noted, surprisingly flattering — the loose white cotton hung from his tall thin frame the way that a dishdasha was designed to hang, and the effect was to make him look, for the first time since they had arrived in the country, not like a foreigner but like a recognisable kind of man.

Then Thuraya helped him with the belt. She wound it twice around his waist, adjusted the buckle, tightened it in the specific correct way. Then she picked up the khanjar. She attached it to the front of the belt — at the specific angle, tipped slightly outward, that Omani men had been attaching their khanjars for several centuries.

John looked at himself — in a small mirror that Thuraya's mother had brought out specifically for this.

Straight.

Serious.

Important.

The specific transformation that a khanjar produced, on the body of a man who had never worn one before, was not subtle. He stood differently. His shoulders had squared. His chin had lifted.

Safiya clapped. She could not help herself.

"You look," she said, "like a very hungry sultan."

John stood taller. He had decided to accept the title. "I feel powerful."

Thuraya laughed. "Be careful. Power," she said — with the specific small wisdom of a sentence her mother had probably said to her when she was ten — "comes with responsibility."

Safiya tried the dress. And then — piece by piece — the jewelry.

Banjari. Twelve of them. Cling. Cling. Cling.

Necklace.

Earrings.

Martaasha.

She walked. Carefully. The specific slow walk of a woman who was, for the first time in her life, wearing enough metal on her body to be audibly visible from ten metres away.

Cling cling cling.

She turned. She looked at herself in the same small mirror.

"Do I," she asked, softly, "look Omani?"

Thuraya smiled warmly. The specific small warm smile of a woman who had, many times, imagined this exact moment — the English teacher's wife, in her mother's bridal silver, in the specific soft oblique light of her mother's small living room.

"Very."


## Scene Five — The Lesson Idea


Later, they sat back down on the cushions. Thuraya's mother brought fresh coffee. The specific small ceremonial refill that happened, in every Omani household, at the exact point in a visit when the conversation had moved from formal hospitality into genuine companionship.

John, still in his dishdasha, had reached for his notebook. He was, Safiya could see, doing that specific small thing he did when he had found a lesson — the small furrowed-brow concentration of a teacher converting lived experience into classroom material.

"We can," he said, slowly, writing, "make this a lesson."

Safiya nodded. "Yes! Clothes. And jewelry."

Thuraya said — with the specific calm certainty of a teacher who had already, in her head, seen the lesson plan — "Vocabulary."

She took a small piece of paper. She wrote, in her clear Arabic-and-English hand:

GoldSilverWearingNecklaceBraceletKhanjarDress

Safiya leaned forward. "We can teach them: 'I am wearing…' 'She is wearing…'"

John added — with a small teacher-satisfaction — "And: 'It is made of silver.' 'It is made of gold.'"

Thuraya nodded. "Good."

They sat for a while longer in the warm small room. The oud continued to burn. Thuraya's mother, who had been watching them with the specific slow contentment of a woman whose household had just delivered a small cultural success, lifted the mandoos — now slightly lighter than it had been — and carried it back into the inner room.

They walked home through the afternoon light. Safiya was, by now, unadorned again — her own ordinary ring back on her finger, her own ordinary scarf around her neck. But she moved, for the rest of the afternoon, with a small specific awareness of her own wrists. As though some part of her still carried, faintly, the memory of the banjari.

And John — who had, with great reluctance, given back the dishdasha and the belt and the khanjar at the door — walked a little straighter all the way home. His shoulders, Safiya would later note, stayed squared for approximately three more hours, until the cumulative effect of teaching, lunch, and being tall finally returned him to his ordinary English slouch.


## Scene Six — At School: I Am Wearing


The next day, the classroom was full of whispers.

Safiya came in wearing only one bracelet — a single silver banjari that Thuraya's mother had, that morning, gently slipped onto her wrist at the door and refused to accept back.

John came in with only the belt. Not the khanjar — Thuraya had said, very firmly, that the khanjar itself should not be brought into the classroom; a khanjar was a thing, not a teaching aid — but the belt, with its beautiful silver work, he was permitted to wear.

Thuraya carried a small cloth bag.

She opened it on her desk.

And the children gasped.

The specific full collective gasp of twelve small Omani children who had, for most of their lives, seen their own mothers wear such jewelry only at weddings — at distance, from across a crowded hall, on special occasions — and who had never, until this exact moment, seen the same pieces laid out carefully on a teacher's desk.

Mansoor shouted, "Wow!"

Mathila smiled. "Pretty!"

Ahmed — studying one of the pieces, with the specific careful attention of a boy who considered himself an expert in most household objects — "Is that real silver?"

Thuraya smiled. She did not answer directly. Instead, she turned to the board and wrote, in her clear capitals:

I am wearing…

She pointed to Safiya. "Sophia. What are you wearing?"

Safiya, lifting her wrist — the single silver banjari catching the morning light — said slowly, "I am wearing… a bracelet."

The class repeated. In their usual layered chorus.

"I am wearing a bracelet."

John pointed to himself. To the silver-worked belt. "I am wearing… a belt."

The class repeated. Ahmed — Safiya noticed — wrote the sentence down twice, with two different decorative underlinings.

Then Thuraya held up the large silver necklace. The one that had, the day before, hung on her own mother's hand.

"What is this?"

Noura — first, as always — said, "Necklace."

"Good. And what is it made of?"

Silence.

The specific small classroom silence of a set of children who had, for years, seen this exact material on their mothers' necks and chests, and who knew — absolutely knew — what it was, but who had never been asked to name it in English.

Thuraya said, helpfully, "It is made of… silver."

The class repeated. "It is made of silver."

Then Thuraya held up another piece — a small gold ring that her mother had, specifically for this lesson, lent to the day.

"And this?"

Khalil shouted — immediately, with the confidence of a boy who had, this time, not had to think —

"Gold!"

Thuraya smiled. "Yes. It is made of gold."

Mansoor raised his hand. "Teacher… you are wearing… many things?"

Thuraya laughed. The specific warm laugh of a teacher whose best student of pragmatics had just shown, once again, his gift for asking the exact question she had been hoping someone would ask.

"Not today."

At the back of the class, Saeed was sitting quietly.

He was new. Still careful. He had arrived that morning — following the meeting at the Sheikh's house, following the evening's decisions — and had taken a seat at the back, near the door, where the light was cool and where he could, as always, watch without being watched.

He looked at the jewelry on the desk as though it had come from another world.

In a sense, it had. His family did not have such pieces. His mother's wrists were bare. His grandmother's had been, at the end of her life, also bare. The having of a mandoos full of silver required, as a precondition, several generations of modest prosperity — which his family had been, for one reason and another, steadily missing.

Safiya noticed. She walked — quietly, without fuss — to the back of the classroom. She knelt beside his small desk. She held out her wrist. The single silver banjari caught the morning light.

"Saeed," she asked, gently, in the specific careful English of a teacher who had chosen a simple sentence, "what am I wearing?"

Saeed thought.

His small serious face concentrated. His eyes — still the eyes of a boy who had, two days earlier, been hit with a stick — now had, across them, the specific small clarity of a child being asked, for the first time in his life, to produce a sentence in English.

He said — slowly, carefully — "You… are wearing… bracelet."

Safiya smiled. Warmly. The specific warm teacher-smile she had been waiting all week to produce.

"Good!"

John — who had come over to stand behind her — added, equally gently, "And what is it made of?"

Saeed whispered — the specific small shy whisper of a boy trying out a new word — "Silver."

John nodded. "Excellent."

The word — excellent — was not one Saeed had heard applied to himself before. He sat very still. His shoulders, almost without his permission, squared slightly.

From two desks away, Mansoor — who could not, even now, manage more than thirty seconds of uninterrupted attention — whispered to Khalil, "One day, I will wear khanjar."

Khalil — without hesitation, with the specific small affectionate cruelty of a nine-year-old friend — whispered back, "One day, you will lose it."

That evening, in their simple house, John wrote in his notebook:

Today, we taught silver and gold. Also, I want my own khanjar.

Safiya, without looking up, said flatly — "No."

Thuraya, who had come over for tea, laughed.

Outside, in the small yard, Malood — who had, for reasons of his own, decided that today's target would be a piece of cloth drying on the washing line — was attempting to pull it down.

Thuraya saw, through the window. She shouted — in her full teacher-voice — "NO, MALOOD!"

Malood ignored her.

As always.


# Palm Eighteen — Mountains, Sea, and the Magic Box of Muttrah



## Scene One — The Tired Fan


The classroom was already hot before the sun reached the middle of the sky.

This was a specific kind of Samaili hot. Not the hot of an English summer afternoon, which came and went and permitted one to take a jacket off and feel immediately better. Not the hot of a hotel room in August. This was the accumulated hot of an October morning in the interior of Oman — a hot that began in the walls, that had been saved inside the clay all night long from yesterday's sun, that radiated quietly out of the packed mud and made everything — the desks, the chalkboard, the small piles of notebooks, Safiya's own chair — feel as though they had been, for a long time, bathed in a warmth they would not be letting go of any time soon.

The fan on the ceiling turned slowly. It turned, Safiya thought, the way a tired workhorse turned — not because it believed in the turning, but because nobody had released it from the job.

It made a dry, weak sound.

Tak… tak… tak…

The specific dry mechanical clack of a metal bearing that had been, for several years, quietly petitioning for the small amount of oil that nobody had ever thought to give it. The fan, Safiya realised, was in much the same state as most of the village's small machines — serving faithfully, undermaintained, uncomplaining.

But the air did not change its mind.

It stayed warm. It stayed heavy. It stayed full of the specific dust-and-chalk smell of a classroom that had been, for some months now, Safiya's second home — full of chalk from the board, dust from the packed earth floor, and the faint small sweetness of the dates that some of the children had tucked into their pockets for their morning break.

John stood near the window, blinking. His eyes were still a little red from yesterday — the residue of the saffron, the smoke, the accumulated small indignities of a week in which his face had been, by his own later count, the target of at least four separate local hazards.

Safiya sat on the teacher's chair, fanning herself with a notebook.

The notebook was doing, in her hand, what the ceiling fan had failed to do all morning. Its small rapid paper movements, an inch from her warm face, produced the memory of cool air, if not cool air itself.

"Next time," she said — with the specific flat dry delivery of a wife who had, in the last hour, stopped pretending to be a professional — "we teach English in the fridge."

Thuraya smiled. The specific settled smile of a woman whose country had been, for several thousand years, exactly this hot. "Welcome to Samail."

The students were already inside — or, more accurately, the students had been inside before the adults had arrived, which was Safiya had come to understand, the usual order of things in a village where children had no commutes, no traffic, no schedules other than the bell is about to ring.

Ahmed sat straight — the specific straight of a ruler laid along a page. He had, Safiya noticed, developed over the last few weeks a small posture that she associated with Omani boys who had decided, early, that they were going to be doctors.

Mansoor was not sitting straight at all. Mansoor was in a specific curved, half-slumped, partly-kneeling configuration that suggested his body had been, in the last ten minutes, several different configurations.

Khalil was whispering something to Ali. Whatever he was whispering was funny — Ali's shoulders were shaking, silently, in the specific restrained shake of a boy who knew he was not supposed to be laughing.

Rahma and Salima shared a pencil. The specific peaceful pencil-sharing of two girls whose friendship required no words.

Marwa was drawing small flowers in the corner of her book. Noura raised her hand even before the lesson had started — the specific pre-emptive hand-raising of a girl who wanted to ensure she would be the first one called on, even if the teacher had not yet asked a question.

And at the back of the class — in his new, still-tentative, officially-his seat, the seat the Sheikh's house had, two days earlier, given him — a boy sat quietly, watching everything.

Saeed.

He did not sit with the others. He did not talk. He just watched.

But the watching was different now. Safiya could see it. It had moved — subtly, over two days — from the specific collector's watching of an outsider to the slightly different watching of a boy who was, now, legally and publicly inside. The village had decided. The Sheikh had nodded. His father had, in a room full of other fathers, spoken the hardest sentence of his life. And Saeed, this morning, was still learning how to hold his new status inside his small nine-year-old body.


## Scene Two — We Ate Lamb


Under the desk, something small moved.

Malood.

The baby goat had decided — in the specific unilateral way that Malood made all his decisions — that the classroom was also his classroom. He had, by now, established a small territorial arrangement beneath the second row — a patch of cooler floor, partly hidden by the shadow of the desk, from which he could exit and re-enter the classroom at his own discretion.

Safiya looked down at him. She whispered — to John, mostly — "We really need to talk about your student."

Malood answered her — without looking up, without apology — by chewing, audibly, a piece of paper. The specific small wet chewing sound of a goat who had, again, made a contribution to the early-morning deficit of small pieces of paper in the classroom.

Before John could start the lesson, Mansoor suddenly stood up.

The standing up was not gradual. Mansoor did not rise. Mansoor launched. In the space of half a second, his small body was upright, both arms already up, the specific urgent announcement-posture of a boy who had, in the last four seconds, remembered the single most important thing about his own life.

"Teacher! Teacher!"

"Yes, Mansoor?"

"We eat lamb yesterday."

John blinked. The specific small blink of a teacher translating tense in real time. "You… ate lamb."

"Yes! And with chili."

The word chili — when Mansoor said it, with the specific breath of a boy who had, over his short life, developed a very serious relationship with the pod — triggered the class.

At exactly the same moment — in the specific overlapping chorus that a village classroom produced when a shared experience had just been named aloud —

"Hot!"

"Very hot!"

"My mouth burn!"

"I cry!"

Khalil added, proudly — the specific small proud contribution of a boy whose pride lay in his ability to survive things other boys did not — "I eat more."

Ali shook his head. He shook it with the specific small unmoved scepticism of a boy who considered himself too rational to compete in chili-eating contests.

"You crazy."

Safiya laughed. "So… everyone ate spicy food?"

The students nodded. Collectively. The specific layered-nodding of twelve small heads confirming, simultaneously, that yes — a shared cultural experience had, the previous evening, taken place across the entire village.

John smiled. Slowly.

A teacher's smile. The specific dangerous kind — the small private smile that meant the lesson has just walked through the door on its own legs and volunteered for duty.

"Good," he said. "Very good. Today… we talk about taste."


## Scene Three — The Five Senses


He turned to the board. He wrote — in his clear large capitals —

THE FIVE SENSES

Then, underneath, with a smaller flourish:

TASTE

He looked at the class. "Repeat after me. Taste."

"Taaaste," the class said. Layered. A little stretched. The specific elongated vowel that village children produced when they were giving a new English word the full respect of being, for the first time, actually tasted in the mouth.

"Good. Now… what words do we use for taste?"

He wrote, one under the other, in a neat vertical column:

SweetSourSaltySpicy

He pointed at each word, in order, and the class — with the specific delight of a class learning four new adjectives they could actually use — repeated.

"Sweet."

"Sweet."

"Sour."

"Sour."

"Salty."

"Salty."

"Spicy."

"Spaiiiicy," Mansoor said — with the specific too-much emphasis of a boy who had decided that his version of the word was going to be the correct version.

John nodded. Patiently. "Yes. Spicy."

Safiya clapped her hands. "Okay! Examples! Who can give me an example?"

Noura's hand went up like a rocket. It had been, in fact, already up for about two minutes — but it now went up higher, the specific further-up-hand-raise of a girl who felt her existing hand-raise was at risk of being missed.

"Yes, Noura."

"Sugar is sweet."

"Excellent!"

Ahmed raised his hand — carefully, at exactly the correct height, neither too high nor too low. The specific calibrated hand-raise of a boy who had, by the age of nine, understood that the dignity of one's contribution was partly determined by the restraint of one's arm.

"Lemon is sour."

"Perfect."

Khalil, not to be outdone — "Fish is salty."

"Good."

And then — because Mansoor had been, for two and a half minutes, trying to find a way into the sentence — Mansoor jumped from his seat. The jump had no warning. It had no apparent reason. It was simply, Safiya had begun to understand, what Mansoor did when his contribution could no longer be held inside his body.

"Chili is… spicy and KILL YOU!"

The class laughed.

John laughed too. "Yes. Chili is spicy. But it does not kill you."

Under the desk, Malood — who had been, up to this point, invisible — made a sound. A small sad sound. The specific small huh-sound that he had been producing, at intervals, since the previous morning.

Safiya looked down. She saw him properly, for the first time that morning. He was, she realised, somewhat subdued. His usual proprietary sparkle was missing. His eyes, when he looked up at her, had the specific small suffering of a goat who had recently regretted a food choice.

"Unless," Safiya added, softly, "you are a small goat."

Thuraya laughed. "Malood learned the lesson the hard way yesterday."

The details — as Safiya would later piece together — involved a red pepper left unattended on a low windowsill at Aunt Azizah's house, and a small goat who had, as always, confused everything with food.


## Scene Four — I Like, I Don't Like — And Saeed Speaks


John turned back to the board. He wrote, beneath the four taste-words, in slightly different lettering:

I like…I don't like…

"Repeat," he said. "I like sweet."

"I like sweet."

"I don't like spicy."

"I don't like spicy," said half the class — very seriously, with the specific small conviction of children declaring a personal truth they had been waiting for permission to speak.

Mansoor crossed his arms. He crossed them across his small proud chest, in the specific wide stance of a boy preparing to register a public disagreement.

"I like spicy."

"Of course you do," said Khalil.

Safiya walked between the desks. She walked the specific slow teacher-walk she had, over the last two months, been practising — the walk that let her pause, without interrupting, at each small desk, and make her presence available to the shy children as well as the loud ones.

"Okay. You. Ali."

Ali stood. "I like salty."

"Good. Rahma?"

Rahma smiled — the specific small satisfied smile of a girl who had been, in her head, composing this exact sentence for forty seconds.

"I like sweet."

"Marwa?"

"I don't like sour."

"Excellent!"

And then — because Safiya's slow walk had, without announcement, brought her to the back of the classroom — she looked up.

To the back seat.

To the quiet boy.

"And you…" she said — gently, without fuss, in the specific gentle way she had been waiting for exactly this moment — "what is your name?"

The quiet boy froze.

The class turned. Slowly. Every small head. The specific slow collective turn of twelve children who had just registered, simultaneously, that something significant was about to happen.

Thuraya looked surprised — and then, a fraction of a second later, looked moved. The specific small settling of a face as it understood what was happening.

"His name," she said, quietly, "is Saeed."

She said it the way Safiya herself might have said, in another life, this is our son. Not loudly. Just — with the specific warm claim of a teacher who was, at last, saying his name for the record.

Saeed stood up.

Slowly.

The specific slow small standing-up of a boy who had never, in his life, had to stand up in a classroom before — who was, in fact, trying to copy, in his body, the standing-up he had seen Ali do thirty seconds earlier, the standing-up he had watched for weeks from his seat on the low wall outside.

His voice was small.

But clear.

"I…" he said, "I like sweet."

The room went quiet for one second.

One full second of the specific small silence that sometimes settles over a classroom when something ordinary has just happened in an extraordinary way. A boy had stood. He had spoken. In English. For the first time. The sentence itself was nothing — I like sweet, the kind of sentence any six-year-old could manage — but the saying of it, in that small careful voice, in that specific back-of-the-room posture, in this specific boy, was everything. Safiya, standing in the middle aisle, felt her throat tighten. She felt, somewhere behind her sternum, the specific small physical warmth that she had learned, over a teaching career that had been, until this moment, relatively ordinary, to recognise as the reason she had become a teacher in the first place.

Then Safiya smiled. Warmly. She said — with the specific full careful warmth she had been saving for exactly this answer — "Very good, Saeed."

Saeed sat down again. His heart — Safiya could see it from where she stood, in the small way his small shirt moved — was beating fast. The specific fast beat of a boy who had, in one sentence, crossed a threshold he had been watching from the other side for three years.

Mansoor whispered — the specific loud village-whisper that reached the whole room — "He talk!"

Khalil whispered back — with a small amazement that Safiya would, for the rest of her life, remember — "He talk ENGLISH!"


## Scene Five — The Window


Under the desk, Malood moved again.

Not his usual restless move. A different move. A small distressed shuffle. The specific shuffle of a small animal whose nose had detected something that his body, without consulting him, had decided to react to.

Suddenly, John felt something strange.

A small, specific, unfamiliar presence in the air. Not a smell exactly — or not yet. The first whisper of a smell. The faint forerunner of a smell. Something sharp, something red, something that had been, somewhere nearby, very recently opened.

"Why," John said, carefully — the specific slow careful investigation-voice he used when he suspected something — "do I smell chili?"

Safiya's eyes widened. The specific wide-eye of a wife who had, at the exact same moment, registered the same faint sharp chemical on her own tongue.

"Oh no."

She turned to the window.

Outside, in the school yard — through the small gap between the classroom wall and the low outer wall that marked the edge of the compound — another class was eating.

Lamb.

With chili.

Very red chili — the specific deep arterial red of Samaili chili that had been, the previous afternoon, dried in the sun, crushed by hand, mixed with oil, and was now, this very moment, being spread on pieces of warm lamb by small hands Safiya could not see but could — in her head — perfectly imagine.

And the smell was coming inside.

Slowly. Deliberately. In the specific way that capsaicin in warm oil travels — not straight, but insinuating itself into a room through any small opening available, and by the time a person had registered its presence the air was already full of it.

John's eyes started to water.

Not dramatically. Just the small involuntary water of tear ducts that had decided, without his permission, to begin defending themselves.

Mansoor smiled. The specific small amused smile of a boy who was having a good morning. "Teacher… smell is spicy."

"Yes," John said — slowly, as though confirming a diagnosis — "the smell is… spicy."

He was — Safiya could see — committing to the lesson. The specific commitment of a teacher who had just realised that the airborne chemical now invading his classroom was, in fact, pedagogical material.

Malood — who could not commit to the lesson, because he was a goat with a very sensitive nose — suddenly stood up. He walked, proudly, in the specific small businesslike walk of an animal who had decided that a situation required immediate management.

He walked to the door. He stopped.

He sneezed.

Then coughed.

Then made a very sad sound — the specific small despairing bleat of a goat who had, in two consecutive days, been ambushed by chili in two different ways, and who was beginning to suspect that the universe had something specific against him.

Safiya rushed. "Oh no, Malood!"

The students laughed.

But then — something worse happened.

John — because John had not yet learned, and would require several more such moments before he did learn, that curiosity in this country had a specific tax — walked to the window.

He said, "Let me see."

It was, Safiya would later reflect — for many years later, every time she told this story — a big mistake.

A wave of chili air hit his face.

Not a smell. A wave. The specific hot chemical wave of capsaicin-soaked air that had been, for the last forty seconds, pooling just outside the window, waiting for a foreigner to lean into it.

His eyes closed — involuntarily, the specific protective snap of eyelids that had, in all their prior English life, never encountered anything this aggressive.

His mouth opened.

He inhaled.

Then —

"AAAAAA!"

He jumped back. Both hands to his face. The specific backward-stumble of a tall Englishman whose entire respiratory system had just filed, simultaneously, several complaints.

"My mouth," he managed, "is ON FIRE!"


## Scene Six — The News from Dhofar


Safiya rushed to help him. But then — as she reached him — she smelled it too. The specific small moment of wifely solidarity in chili.

"Oh no—"

She coughed.

Thuraya tried to be strong. She tried — she really tried — with the full professional discipline of an Omani teacher. But even she started to laugh and cough at the same time. The specific uncontrolled double-action of a woman whose village pride had finally met a situation her village pride could not contain.

The class exploded.

Mansoor laughed so much that he fell from his chair — actually fell, sideways, onto the packed-earth floor, where he lay laughing for a full eight seconds before Ahmed quietly helped him up.

Khalil held his stomach.

Ahmed tried to stay serious. He failed. The specific rare failure of Ahmed's composure — the failure that happened, perhaps, once a month — crumbled his normal straight-ruler posture and produced, for a full ten seconds, a small wheezing laugh.

Malood ran in a circle. Then — having re-evaluated his strategy — hid under the desk again.

John grabbed the water bottle. He drank. Not a sip. A long commitment of a drink — the specific emergency-hydration of a man attempting to extinguish his own mouth from the inside.

"It is…" he said, crying actual small tears, "spicy air."

Safiya — seeing, even through her own coughing, that a classroom had just produced the exact conditions for a perfect English lesson — stepped to the board. She wrote, fast, still laughing:

HotBurnMy mouth is burning!

She turned to the class. "Repeat!"

"My mouth is burning!"

John pointed at himself — the specific clear indicative point of a teacher using his own suffering as instructional material. "My mouth is burning!"

Mansoor shouted — because of course he did — "TEACHER JOHN IS BURNING!"

John looked at him. With the specific flat-faced gratitude of a man whose own student had just correctly used the present continuous.

"Thank you, Mansoor. Very helpful."

Then he looked back at the board. "Okay. New sentence."

He wrote: It is too spicy.

"Repeat."

"It is too spicy."

"Good."

He wiped his eyes. "Today… we learn English. And we learn… not to open windows near chili."

The class laughed again.

Then John clapped his hands. "Now. Past simple."

He wrote: Yesterday, I ate…

He pointed at Mansoor. "Mansoor."

Mansoor stood proudly. "Yesterday, I eat lamb."

John smiled. "Say: I ate."

"Yesterday, I ate lamb."

"With chili!"

"With chili," the class added.

John pointed at Malood. "Yesterday, Malood…"

Khalil, quick — "Yesterday, Malood ate paper."

Safiya laughed. "Say: Malood ate paper."

"Malood ate paper."

Then John pointed to himself.

"Yesterday, I…"

He thought for a second. The specific small teacher's pause of a man realising that yesterday he had done many things, but only one of them made a good sentence.

"…I opened the window."

Safiya added, quietly — "And today, you learned a big lesson."

John nodded. The specific full accepting nod of a husband who had, once again, been summarised by his wife. "Yes. Today, I learned a big lesson."

At the back of the class, a small hand went up.

Everyone turned.

Saeed.

For the second time that morning.

"Yes, Saeed?" John said, gently — the specific gentle teacher-voice he used when he understood that the next sentence out of a student's mouth was, for that student, historic.

"Yesterday…" Saeed said, slowly, carefully, "I ate… dates. Sweet."

John smiled. Warmly. The specific warm full smile of a teacher whose day had just, quietly, without announcement, become the best day of his teaching career.

"Excellent sentence."

Saeed sat down. His face was a little brighter. The specific small internal brightness of a boy who had, in the course of one English lesson, produced two complete sentences in a language he had, four days ago, never used.

Thuraya watched him quietly.

She did not speak. She did not clap. She did not, in any visible way, react. She only watched — the specific still watching of a teacher who was, at that exact moment, witnessing the small slow beginning of the reason she had entered her profession.

The fan kept turning.

Tak… tak… tak…

The heat stayed.

The lesson continued.

At the end of the class, John wrote on the board — under the day's heading — the full accumulated vocabulary list. Taste. Sweet. Sour. Salty. Spicy. Hot. Burn. I like… I don't like… Yesterday, I ate… Ten items. The specific honest vocabulary of a morning that had produced every one of those words through lived experience rather than through a textbook. Safiya announced homework: "Write three sentences about what you like and what you don't like." Mansoor sighed, dramatically, as he always did — the specific theatrical sigh of a boy who had, over his school career, cultivated the small art of visible suffering at the assignment of any homework.

Then — something changed.

From outside — from the small school office where Abu Salim the caretaker kept the one shared radio that served the whole school — a sound came. A sound that had not been present ten seconds earlier. It had been turned on. Someone had, in the office, decided it was time.

Low. Old. Serious.

A man's voice. In Arabic. The specific formal careful Arabic of state radio.

"…this is the latest news from Dhofar…"

The class did not understand the words.

But they felt the tone.

It was not funny.

Not light.

Not like chili.

Safiya slowly looked at John.

John looked back at her.

For one moment, the classroom was quiet.

Even the fan — even Tak… tak… tak… — sounded smaller, somehow. As though the fan itself had recognised the shift in the air.

Even Malood stopped moving.

Oman was not only a place of funny stories. It was not only a village where Englishmen fell into falaj channels and were saffron-painted by well-meaning women and bitten by wasps and ambushed by chili-air. It was also a country — in that October, in that particular year — in the middle of a long quiet war, far to the south, that every family in the village had a relative in or a neighbour who was connected to. A war that had been, for eight years now, eating the country's young men. A war the radio spoke of each morning at this hour, always in the same level serious voice, always in the same careful official words. Safiya had not, until this moment, fully understood that the village knew. That every adult in that small compound around her had, this morning, already heard the earlier broadcast. That the children who would, eventually, run home to their mothers, would pass men on the road whose faces would be serious in the specific way that a piece of news made a face serious.

Oman was also a place of big stories.

John closed his notebook. Slowly.

"Okay," he said softly. "Class is over."

The children stood. The specific small orderly standing of children who had understood, without needing to be told, that the morning had shifted into something else.

They collected their notebooks.

They walked out.

Quietly.

Saeed, at the back, stood up too. He lifted his small bag. He did not hurry. He did not speak. But as he passed Safiya at the front of the room, he looked up at her — briefly, just the one look — and his eyes said, thank you.

He walked out into the hot late-morning yard. Where the other class was, by now, finishing its lamb and chili. Where the radio, from the office, was still speaking quietly in its level serious voice about a war that was, for all of them, the specific heavy shadow that sat at the edge of every ordinary day.


# Palm Nineteen — The Zanzibari Trader and the Saffron Mask



## Scene One — An Honest Morning


The sky was still sleepy when Aunt Azizah's rooster shouted his opinion to the whole village.

It was the specific opinion that he shouted every morning — a long, declarative, unapologetic announcement that the night was over, that the neighbours should get up, that the world was not going to wait for them to be ready. There was no negotiation in this rooster's voice. There was no suggestion. There was only the old village certainty that morning had come and anyone still asleep was behind schedule.

John opened his eyes slowly — as if the morning itself had pushed a heavy door on his face.

He listened for a second. He was still, even after weeks of this, listening on the old English frequency — listening for the quiet polite mornings of a Norwich suburb, for the distant soft whirr of an early milk van, for the absent sound of birds that had been, in his English childhood, the only thing you heard before eight.

But Samail did not do polite mornings.

Samail did honest mornings.

Outside, the specific dense sound-layer of a village already awake — the rooster, of course, and a small urgent clang of metal pans being arranged on a hearth, and — underneath it all — the specific soft tssss of something beginning to fry in oil.

Then — the smell.

Fresh bread. The specific warm yeast-and-smoke smell of khubz being made, three courtyards away, on a hot metal pan over small coals — the bread whose smell, in any Omani village, functioned as a kind of village alarm-clock — soft, warm, impossible to ignore, making every person within smelling-distance remember, at exactly the same moment, that they were hungry.

Safiya sat up. She fixed her scarf. She smiled quietly — the specific dreamy morning-smile of a woman whose first sensation of the day had been a good one.

"Something," she whispered, "smells amazing."

John sniffed the air. "I think your neighbour is making breakfast for the entire planet."

Thuraya knocked on the wooden door frame and walked in — without waiting — because in the seventies, in villages, people did not knock on doors the way they knocked on doors of museums. They knocked the way a friend knocks: as a courtesy, not as a request.

"Good morning," she said, brightly. "Are you ready?"

John looked around their simple home. The walls were plain — clay, unpainted, warm in tone. Their bags were still half-open. A corner of Safiya's dress hung from a single nail — as though the house itself were trying to help them unpack, by offering, free of charge, all the small hooks and nails it had been collecting for the last forty years.

"I'm ready," he said — pretending, unconvincingly, that he was not nervous. "Where are we going again?"

Thuraya's eyes sparkled. "Muscat. Muttrah Souq."

Safiya's mouth opened. "A real souq?"

"A real souq," Thuraya confirmed.

John rubbed his hands. The specific small professional hand-rub of a teacher preparing to turn a day out into classroom material. "Okay. New vocabulary. New adventure."


## Scene Two — The Pickup and the Mountains


Behind Thuraya, something tiny and determined slipped through the doorway like a thief with hooves.

Malood.

He walked in as if the house belonged to him. He sniffed John's shoe. He considered the shoe. He decided the shoe was not interesting. He aimed — directly, without any further investigation — for Safiya's cloth bag.

"No," Safiya said. Gently. Pulling her bag up out of his reach. "Not today."

Malood stared at her — as though he had understood the word today and was, internally, calculating whether this specific morning made him a genuine exception to the rule — and then tried again.

John pointed at him. The specific strict teacher-point of a man who had, by now, had enough personal experience with Malood to know that strictness was his only available defence.

"Malood. You are not invited."

Malood's ears flicked. He was clearly offended.

Thuraya laughed. "He is coming from the neighbour's yard every morning. He thinks you are his family now."

Safiya sighed — the specific small sighing of a woman who had, at some point, given up on the question of whether Malood was or was not their goat. "He is too confident."

Thuraya clapped her hands once — business mode. "Come. Uncle Nasser is waiting."

Outside, the pickup truck waited — like a strong old animal. It was one of those boxy, tough, slightly-battered pickup models of that period — dusty, proud, the specific Japanese pickup that had, Safiya was coming to understand, become the workhorse of every interior Omani village in the years since the roads had begun.

Uncle Nasser sat behind the steering wheel. The specific calm face of a man who had driven, in his life, on roads that were sometimes roads and sometimes — when the wadi had decided otherwise — only hopes.

In the back of the truck there was a wooden bench and a rope tied neatly, ready for passengers to hold. Uncle Nasser did not say much. He did not need to. His bamboo stick — the long thin driver-stick that rested beside him — was, in its way, an extra seat.

John climbed into the back first — trying to look confident. The pickup moved, just a little, under his weight. He grabbed the side. Fast.

Safiya climbed in more carefully — holding her scarf, laughing at John's earlier panic, settling onto the bench with the specific careful movement of a woman whose Omani clothes were not yet fully natural to her body.

Thuraya jumped in — as though she had done this, literally, a thousand times. Which, Safiya realised, she almost certainly had.

"Hold tight," Thuraya said. "And don't scream. Like Mansoor."

John blinked. "Mansoor screams?"

Thuraya smiled. "Mansoor screams when the wind touches him."

The pickup rolled forward — a slow, heavy, accepting start — and the village opened behind them like a storybook. Palm shadows. Small paths. A woman at a doorway lifting her water pot. Two goats watching them from the top of a low wall, with the specific assessing gaze of village security.

Then the land began to change.

Mountains appeared.

Not polite hills. Not the gentle slopes of southern England. Not the green rolling kindnesses that Safiya's English geography books had prepared her for. Real mountains. Tall and bold, rising like giant brown walls around them. The specific layered brown-on-brown stone of the Hajar range — the range that had, for tens of millions of years, been slowly pushed up out of the ancient sea floor by the collision of tectonic plates, the range that carried, in its specific red-brown colour, the literal fossils of the ocean that Oman had once been. They rose from the plain not at a gentle angle, but at cliff. They rose like they had been, a long time ago, extremely angry — and had frozen, mid-rage, into stone.

John leaned forward. His eyes wide.

"Safiya. Look."

Safiya stared.

"John. I've never seen mountains like this."

Thuraya glanced at them both. She smiled — the specific small patient smile of an Omani woman who had just seen two English faces undergo the specific small overwhelm that all visitors' faces underwent on their first sighting of the Hajar.

"You don't have mountains in Britain?"

"We have…" John admitted — carefully, because he did not want to betray his own homeland too obviously — "small ones."

He paused. He was thinking of Snowdon, which he had climbed once as a student. He was thinking of the Scottish Highlands, which he had seen in photographs. He was comparing them, honestly, in his head, to the silent brown giants currently sliding past the side of the pickup.

"But these," he said, "these look like they belong in a movie."

Safiya whispered — almost reverently, almost against her will — "They look ancient."


## Scene Three — The First Glimpse of the Sea


As the road continued, the air changed.

It became salty. The specific unmistakable maritime salt that had been, since Safiya was a child in an English coastal town, the specific smell she most associated with freedom. A different kind of wind than the inland wind of Samail. A different smell.

Cooler. More — in some specific way she could not yet name — mobile.

Safiya lifted her chin. As though she could taste it.

"Sea," she said.

John's eyes lit up. The specific boyish lighting-up of a man whose heart had, at the moment of scenting salt, become ten years younger.

"The sea!"

Thuraya nodded. "We are close to Muttrah now."

When the first glimpse of the water appeared — between two low bluffs, in a sudden small gap between the coastal hills — Safiya made a small sound. The small sound that a person makes when they have been, without quite realising it, holding their breath, and the sight that releases the breath is a sight they did not expect.

The sea stretched wide.

Shining.

The specific shining of the Gulf of Oman at mid-morning, in late October, in 1973 — before the age of plastic, before the age of oil spills, before the age of the mass tourism that would, in decades to come, change the specific clarity of the water. The sea she was looking at had been looking exactly like this for, perhaps, two thousand years. It was the same sea that Sinbad's Arabs had sailed. The same sea the Portuguese had arrived on in the sixteenth century. The same sea that Ibn Battuta had crossed. The same sea whose trade winds had, for more than a thousand years, carried wooden dhows between Muscat and Mombasa and Bombay and Basra and Aden and back. The same sea.

Boats and ships rested in the distance. Small white fishing launches. A few dhows, further out, with their specific lateen sails gathered at the mast. A larger cargo ship, far on the horizon, with the specific dark silhouette of a post-war freighter waiting for its turn at the Muttrah port.

The harbour was busy — alive — like a giant classroom in which everyone was doing a different job at the same time, and each job was, somehow, necessary to every other job.

John pointed. His face had the specific delighted face of an Englishman who had grown up on Kipling and had finally — in real life, with real eyes — found himself at one of Kipling's specific places.

"Ships!"

Thuraya smiled. "Yes. And today, you will learn words you cannot learn in a classroom."

Safiya opened her notebook. The specific small quick opening of a woman who had decided that this day was going to be worth documenting. "I'm ready."

The pickup stopped near the harbour. They climbed down.

John's legs felt strange. The specific phantom motion of a body that had been, for forty minutes, absorbing the vibrations of an Omani road surface, and whose nervous system had not yet updated itself to the fact that it was now standing on solid ground.

Uncle Nasser said — quietly, without drama — "Stay close."

Then he added, even more quietly, "And no trouble."
