John nodded quickly. "No trouble."

Thuraya raised one eyebrow. The specific amused Omani eyebrow of a woman who had just heard a foreigner say a sentence that the country was, in her experience, likely to make impossible.

"That sentence," she said, "is dangerous in Oman."


## Scene Four — The Zanzibari Trader


They walked toward the souq area. But first — before they reached the main entrance — they passed a small loose group of sellers near the port.

Some had baskets. Some had cloths laid on the ground, with items arranged, carefully, in small rows.

And then they saw him.

A simple Zanzibari trader — sitting, cross-legged, beside a wooden box that looked like it had travelled, in its small wooden life, more than any passport in John's England had ever travelled. His skin was sun-browned. His clothes were plain — a simple cotton shirt, loose trousers, a small cap. His smile was the specific sharp smile of a man who knew a joke that the world had, so far, not heard.

In front of him, arranged on a small cotton cloth, were the specific small items that a Zanzibari trader sold at the edge of the harbour in 1973 — small beads, tiny perfume bottles with cork stoppers, a small mirror in a beaten-metal frame, and a few shiny silver-coloured objects whose purpose was not, at first glance, obvious.

Safiya slowed down. "Oh… look."

John leaned in. "What is he selling?"

Thuraya whispered — with the specific small warning-whisper of an Omani woman trying to protect two foreign friends from a trader she had recognised, from six paces, as a professional — "Many things. But be careful. Some sellers in Muttrah have strong… talking skills."

The trader looked up. His eyes landed on John and Safiya the way a fisherman's eyes land on two fish who have just, without quite knowing it, entered his net.

He smiled. A broad, welcoming, entirely commercial smile.

"Welcome, welcome! Come! Look! Very good things!"

John froze. He heard English. Not perfect English. But clear enough. Good enough for negotiation.

Safiya smiled politely. "Hello."

The trader nodded — proudly — the specific proud nod of a man who had, over a trading career, collected the small useful English phrases of every kind of European customer who had ever passed his cloth.

"Hello! Hello! You want? I have nice things. Small price. Big happiness."

John laughed softly. "Big happiness? That sounds good."

The trader tapped the wooden box — with love, with the specific small affectionate tap of a man who considered his box a character in the negotiation.

"This box? Magic box. It has… everything."

Thuraya tried not to smile. "Magic?"

The trader leaned forward. Dramatically. The specific small leaning-in of a professional storyteller about to deliver the critical line. "Yes. Magic."

Safiya whispered to John — quietly, with a small delight — "He is a storyteller."

John, being John, decided — the specific irrepressible decision of a man who could not, under any circumstances, stop being an English teacher — that this was a learning moment.

He cleared his throat. Teacher voice. He turned to Safiya and Thuraya.

"Okay. Vocabulary."

Then he turned back to the trader, with a friendly smile. "What do you sell?"

The trader's eyes shone — the specific shining of a trader who had just realised that his customer was going to buy him a whole conversation before buying anything else. "I sell… things. Good things."

John nodded. "How much?"

The trader blinked. Then smiled wider. The specific pleased widening of a man whose customer had just, by using the right second sentence, identified himself as somebody who was not a complete amateur. "Ah. You know. You learn fast."

Safiya's eyes were bright. "John. Ask him the names."

John pointed to a string of beads. "What is this?"

The trader answered — quickly, with the specific small pride of a man naming his own merchandise — "Beads."

"Good. And this?"

John pointed to a tiny bottle.

"Perfume," the trader said. "Very strong. One drop, you smell like… rich person."

Safiya giggled. "One drop?"

The trader nodded. Very seriously — the specific small deadpan seriousness of a professional salesman about to deliver his best line. "One drop. Two drops, you become problem."

Thuraya's shoulders shook with laughter.


## Scene Five — The Ship Key


John pointed to a small silver object — a short, shiny, vaguely key-shaped thing with a worked handle at one end.

"And this?"

The trader hesitated. The specific small hesitation of a man who had, until this exact question, not yet decided what this particular object was going to be.

Then he said — confidently, the specific sudden full confidence of a man who had, in the space of a quarter-second, made an entirely new decision about an object's identity —

"A… ship key."

John blinked. "A ship key?"

Safiya leaned in. The specific small leaning-in of a woman who was, entirely against her better judgement, charmed. "A key for a ship?"

The trader nodded — the specific full confident nod of a man who had decided that yes, of course, the ship key, everybody knows the ship key. "Yes yes. For ship. You open ship."

Thuraya turned away. Laughing, audibly now, into her scarf — the specific helpless scarf-laughter of a woman who had, for the last thirty seconds, been trying to preserve the dignity of her country in front of her English friends and was now, finally, giving up.

John looked at her. "Is that real?"

Thuraya tried to be serious. She failed. But she managed to speak.

"No, Mr. John. That is not real."

The trader — sensing, with the specific professional radar of a trader who had been selling things for thirty years, that danger had entered the negotiation — jumped in quickly.

"Okay okay! Not ship key. It is… decoration!"

John laughed. "Ahh. Decoration. Good."

Safiya whispered to Thuraya, with the specific delighted small whisper of a woman who had just understood something important — "He is trying to sell a story. Not an object."

Thuraya nodded. The specific settled nod of a woman confirming a village truth she had known all her life. "Yes. That is Muttrah."

John looked at the trader. He decided — teacher-mode still fully engaged — to teach. Politely.

"In English," he said, carefully, "we say… 'I want.'"

The trader's eyes widened. He repeated the sentence — with the specific small pleasure of a man adding a new tool to his negotiation-toolkit. "I want."

John smiled. "Good. 'I want beads.'"

The trader repeated, "I want beads."

Safiya laughed. "He sounds like the customer now."

The trader — without warning, as though a button had been pressed somewhere deep inside him — pointed suddenly at John.

"You! You want beads!"

John laughed. "No. Not me."

The trader pointed at Safiya.

"You want perfume!"

Safiya shook her head, quickly. "No. Thank you."

The trader held the perfume bottle up — like an award he was presenting to a champion. "One drop, you smell like QUEEN!"

Thuraya cleared her throat. Stepping in. With the specific diplomatic firmness of an Omani woman intervening on behalf of her guests.

"We are going to the souq. Thank you."

But the trader leaned forward. Determined now. The specific determined lean of a man who had, in his head, not yet given up on the sale.

"Wait! Wait! Gift. Small gift."

He opened the wooden box. He reached inside — with the specific small theatrical reach of a man producing a rabbit from a hat — and pulled out the small mirror with its beaten-metal frame.

He offered it to Safiya.

Safiya hesitated. Her polite English heart wanted, genuinely, to accept — a gift, freely offered, was not a thing she wanted to refuse. Her slightly smarter English brain, however, was screaming, somewhere at the back of her skull, the specific loud warning DON'T ACCEPT GIFTS FROM STRANGERS.

John tried to help. "No. It's okay. Thank you."

The trader pushed the mirror gently closer. "Free. Welcome gift."

Safiya — finally — took it. Carefully. "Thank you."

The trader smiled. Like he had just won.

Then he pointed at John.

"Now you buy."

John froze.

"…What?"

Thuraya's eyes danced — the specific small dancing of a woman whose country had, once again, delivered exactly the lesson she had been waiting for it to deliver.

"Welcome," she said, "to Muttrah."

John turned to Safiya. "Safiya!" — the specific betrayed tone of a husband whose wife had just walked them both into a small commercial trap.

Safiya held up the mirror. Sheepishly. "I thought it was kindness."

The trader nodded proudly. "Yes. Kindness business."


## Scene Six — I Don't Want To Buy


John sighed.

The specific full sigh of a man who had, six minutes earlier, approached a small Zanzibari trader for a language lesson and had now — to his own chagrin — been manoeuvred into a small obligatory purchase.

He decided — because John was John, and because there was always, for John, another learning moment available — that this moment, too, was going to be turned into one.

He faced the trader. He spoke slowly. Clearly. With the specific measured diplomacy of a man who had decided to use the exact sentence as a sentence.

"I don't want to buy."

The trader frowned. "You don't want to… buy?"

John repeated — with the same measured deliberateness —

"I don't want to buy."

Thuraya whispered to Safiya, with the specific small educational whisper of an Omani friend — "Good sentence."

Safiya whispered back, with the specific grateful full approval of a wife — "Very good sentence."

The trader stared at John. For one full second. Then — smiling, suddenly, with the specific professional-respect smile of a trader who had just lost a specific small negotiation and was, in his mind, already moving on to the next customer —

"Okay okay. You don't want to buy. But you can… look."

John exhaled. "Yes. Look."

They started walking away — toward the souq — but John could not stop thinking about one thing.

He turned to Thuraya. Voice low.

"Was that… a trick?"

Thuraya smiled. Sweetly. The specific small sweet Thuraya smile, the smile that John had, in his notebook, been warned about.

"It is not a trick. It is… a lesson."

Safiya scribbled in her notebook — fast, still walking, her pen moving in the specific rapid diagonal of a woman trying to capture ten new words before they escaped.

New words:mountain, sea, ship, port, seller, beads, perfume, gift, mirror, price.

John looked at the entrance of the souq ahead. The narrow lanes. The shadows. The sounds — the overlapping shouts of traders, the specific rhythm of bargaining in three different languages, the high-pitched calls of porters moving through crowds with trays on their heads. The smells — spices and incense and old leather and something fried he could not identify. The voices.

He swallowed.

"I think," he said, quietly, "this place is going to teach me English again."

Thuraya nodded. "And patience."

Safiya smiled. "And survival."

Uncle Nasser called from behind them — the specific efficient single word of a driver who had already identified the next phase of the outing and intended to move them into it — "Yalla!"

John squared his shoulders. "Okay. Next task."

Safiya teased — quietly, with the specific warm teasing of a wife who had just recognised her husband slipping back into his professional vocabulary — "Task-Based Learning… in real life."

Thuraya smiled. "Exactly."

They stepped closer to the souq. Behind them, the trader — who had already, in the specific quick way of all successful salespeople, reset his face and his small cotton cloth for the next customer — waved happily. As if nothing had happened.

"Welcome! Welcome! Big happiness!"

John muttered under his breath, "Small price. Big trouble."

Thuraya heard. Laughed.

And just before they entered the first narrow lane of Muttrah Souq — just before the specific dark-cool shadow of the covered arcade swallowed them, just before the full sensory ambush of the souq began — John heard, somewhere in the crowd ahead of them, a familiar voice.

Loud. Confident. Entirely, unmistakably — Sadoo.

John stopped walking.

Thuraya's smile slowly widened. The specific slow widening of a smile that had, at the sound of that voice, fully recognised the morning's next trouble.

"Oh no."

Safiya blinked. "What?"

Thuraya pointed toward the sound — toward the overlapping crowd, toward the small dark opening of the main souq lane.

"That voice… is going to start our next problem."

John whispered, with the specific small pleading whisper of a man hoping — against all village evidence — that the sentence he was about to hear was not going to be the sentence he was afraid of — "Please tell me Sadoo is not here."

Thuraya whispered back. Almost lovingly.

"Sadoo is everywhere."

And the three of them — Safiya with her notebook already open, John with his shoulders squared, Thuraya with her specific small prepared-for-anything smile — walked into the souq…

Right toward the trouble.


# Palm Twenty — Of Goats and Grammar



## Scene One — Everything, All At Once


The first thing that entered John's nose inside Muttrah Souq was… everything.

It was not a smell. It was a census of smells. Spices — cardamom warming in the small open sacks, cumin catching in the late-morning light like small brown pollen, the peppery sharpness of turmeric, the dark sweet resin of frankincense that had been, for three thousand years, carried out of these same streets on wooden ships bound for every port the sea could reach. Fish — the unapologetic salt-and-scale of fresh fish resting on beds of ice at the back of the dark covered walkways, mixed with the faint dried-fish smell that clung, permanently, to certain wooden stalls that had been selling fish for six generations. Incense — oud smoke curling from small braziers outside shops, burning slowly, inviting customers. Leather — the specific warm animal smell of a tanner's stall where sandals hung from wooden pegs. Old wood — the ancient cedar beams of the covered arcade, darkened by decades of passing hands. Perfume — attars and musks and amber, layered and competing. And something fried — whose name John did not know and whose smell, somewhere between garlic and onion and hot oil, made him, against his will, slightly hungry.

The second thing that entered his ears was noise.

Not the noise of Samail. Not the layered gentle hush of a village. This was the specific dense, layered, mercantile noise of a port city that had been trading with the world since the time of the Portuguese.

Voices calling — in Arabic, in Swahili, in Baluchi, in Hindi, in broken English. People bargaining — the specific rhythmic up-and-down of numbers being thrown back and forth, of feigned outrage, of slow reconsideration. Metal sounds — copper pots being tapped, a brass scale clinking, a blacksmith at the far end of a lane hammering the specific slow even beat of a man who had been, that morning, making the same type of nail he had been making on this same anvil for twenty years. Footsteps. Donkeys somewhere behind them. And, somewhere in the middle of all that — rising above the rest, the way a single instrument rises from an orchestra —

Sadoo.

His voice, unmistakable, full-throated, addressed to someone named Salim across a narrow lane:


> يا سالم يا سالم! اليوم تجارتنا نار! نار!


(O Salim! O Salim! Today our business is fire! Fire!)

John stopped walking.

He stopped the way a man stops when he hears, unexpectedly, a voice he had, only an hour earlier, hoped to avoid. The specific small frozen stop of a traveller who had been warned about something, and who had just realised that the warning was, at this exact moment, becoming true.

Safiya bumped into his back. "Why did you stop?"

John pointed slowly — the specific slow measured point of someone pointing at a wild animal in a zoo. "That… voice."

Thuraya closed her eyes for one second. Just one. The specific small eye-closing of a woman gathering herself for the next hour of her life.

"Yes. That is Sadoo."

Safiya blinked. "Your Sadoo?"

"Our Sadoo," Thuraya corrected — quietly, with the specific small village claim of a woman who had, long ago, accepted that certain people were no longer individual property but village property.


## Scene Two — The Happy Storm


They moved carefully through the narrow path — weaving past porters carrying trays of dates on their heads, past small clusters of Indian traders in white shirts, past a tall Baluchi man with a coiled rope over his shoulder — until they saw him.

Sadoo stood in front of a small shop.

Wearing his usual clothes — the same slightly-dusty dishdasha he had worn up a palm tree, the same small cap, the same sandals — and talking with his hands, his feet, and, Safiya would have sworn, probably his eyebrows too. Around him were bags. Boxes. Ropes. And a very confused man holding a basket.

Sadoo saw them.

His face became a celebration.

The specific sudden full face-celebration of a village man who had, only seconds earlier, been performing commerce at full volume, and had now been handed, free of charge, three of his favourite people in the middle of a city where he had not expected to see them.


> هااااه! الأستاذ جون! صفيه! مس ثريا! وينكم من زمان!


(Haaah! Teacher John! Safiya! Miss Thuraya! Where have you been all this time!)

He rushed toward them — like a happy storm, the specific barrelling small-man-at-full-speed movement of a Samaili shopkeeper who had decided that the reunion required immediate physical contact.

John smiled. Carefully. The specific careful smile of a man who had, minutes earlier, been hoping that Sadoo was not in this city, and who was now committing to being polite about having been wrong.

"Hello, Sadoo."

Sadoo shook his hand. Strongly. With the specific full-forearm handshake of a man who considered weak handshakes a small moral failing. Then he shook Safiya's hand. Equally strongly. Then he tried to shake Thuraya's hand.

Thuraya stepped back. Quickly. The specific practised village step-back of an Omani woman who had, on principle, not been shaking men's hands since she was fifteen, and who had, over her teaching career, developed the reflex to a small art.

She asked, dryly — in her clear Samaili Arabic —


> سادو، ويش تسوي هنا؟


(Sadoo, what are you doing here?)

Sadoo puffed his chest. The specific small full puffing of a man whose entire public dignity was about to be expressed through a single sentence.


> أنا؟ أنا تاجر كبير تو! جيت أشتري أغراض للدكان. مطرح! كله خير!


(Me? I am a big trader now! I came to buy goods for the shop. Muttrah! All is well!)

Safiya looked around at the small hill of bags and boxes beside him.

"You have… many bags."

Sadoo nodded. Proudly.


> كلها لي!


(All of them are mine!)

John looked at one of the bags.

It moved.

He froze.

The specific full frozen stop of a man whose eye had just registered a piece of movement that his brain was not ready, at ten in the morning, in a new country, to process.

"…Why is your bag moving?"

Sadoo looked. He looked down at the bag with the specific mild surprise of a man who had, until that moment, forgotten it was a bag that moved.


> آه. هذا؟ سمك.


(Ah. This? Fish.)

John stepped back. "Alive fish?"

Sadoo nodded. Placidly.


> طازج.


(Fresh.)

John whispered to Safiya — with the specific small desperate English whisper of a man whose cultural reserves had, that morning, finally run out — "I am not ready for this country."


## Scene Three — The Bargaining


Before Thuraya could control the situation — and she had, Safiya noted, already opened her mouth to try — Sadoo grabbed John's arm. With the specific proprietary village-grip of a man who had decided that he was now, for the next fifteen minutes, John's personal business partner.


> تعال تعال! هذا محل زين!


(Come, come! This is a good shop!)

He pulled John toward a shop. The specific pulling of a man who was not, under any circumstance, going to accept no for an answer.

The shop was full of metal things.

Copper pots — stacked in small towers, each one hand-hammered, each one bearing, on its base, the specific small maker's mark of a coppersmith somewhere in the interior. Plates — thick brass plates of the kind that were traditionally given as wedding gifts, that a bride would receive and keep in her kitchen for sixty years. Trays. Round trays, rectangular trays, the small circular sinyah on which communal meals were served. Everything arranged with the specific careful stacking of a shopkeeper who knew, exactly, where every piece was, and who would be privately offended if a customer picked something up and did not put it back correctly.

In the middle stood a man with crossed arms and a very serious face.

Sadoo pointed proudly.


> هذا صديقي. بيعطينا سعر خاص.


(This is my friend. He will give us a special price.)

The man looked at John. Then at Safiya. Then at Thuraya. Then back at Sadoo. His face, throughout all four inspections, remained a single consistent expression of Muttrah neutrality.

He said something. Fast. In Arabic.

Sadoo answered. Faster.

They started talking. Loudly. With hands — the specific full Arabic hand-talk of a bargaining negotiation, in which the palms opened, closed, indicated prices, indicated rejection, indicated final offers, indicated small modifications. A whole second-language running simultaneously alongside the words.

John turned to Thuraya. "What is happening?"

Thuraya sighed. The specific long sigh of a woman who had, many times, watched exactly this conversation. "Sadoo is bargaining."

Safiya whispered, leaning toward John, "It looks like a fight."

"It is a kind of fight," Thuraya whispered back. "Both sides enjoy it."

Sadoo, without warning, turned to John.


> جون! قول له! قول له!


(John! Tell him! Tell him!)

John blinked. "Say… what?"

Sadoo pushed a big metal tray toward him — the specific wide sinyah that, in a Samaili home, would have held rice for six people. He pushed it the way a lawyer pushes evidence toward a jury.


> قول له:


"I want this. But cheap."

John cleared his throat. Teacher mode. He looked at the seller.

"I want this," he said, carefully, pointing. "But… cheap."

The seller's eyebrow went up. Slowly. The specific slow deliberate eyebrow-raise of a professional merchant who had, in thirty years of selling trays in Muttrah, never once been asked to make something cheap by a customer who had not, first, worked their way through at least four ritual opening moves of refusal and amusement.

He answered — in fast Arabic.

Thuraya translated. "He says: 'This is not cheap.'"

John nodded, seriously. "Okay. This is not cheap."

Sadoo slapped his forehead. Hard. With the specific full-palm forehead-slap of a man whose carefully-prepared negotiation had, in one sentence, been demolished.


> لا لا! مو كذا! لازم تقول:


"I don't want. Too expensive!"

John tried again. Obediently. "I don't want. Too expensive."

The seller crossed his arms. Tighter.

He answered again.

Thuraya translated — with the specific small amused-translator tone of a woman who was enjoying this a great deal. "Now he says: 'Then don't buy.'"

Sadoo stared at John.

Like John had just, with one English sentence, broken the entire world.


> يا رجل! خربتها!


(Man! You ruined it!)


## Scene Four — Sometimes English Is Magic


Safiya tried not to laugh. She tried — the specific doomed effort of a wife whose laugh was already, somewhere deep in her chest, in the process of escaping.

John defended himself. "I said exactly what you told me."

Sadoo waved his hands. The specific full hand-wave of a man whose pupil had, in the critical moment, misused the lesson.


> بس ما كذا!


(But not like that!)

The seller suddenly picked up the tray. He put it inside the shop. He turned his back.

The specific three-part surgical retreat of a Muttrah shopkeeper who had decided that this particular transaction was over.

Sadoo gasped.


> راح!


(He's gone!)

He turned, desperately, to Thuraya.


> مس ثريا! قولي له شي!


(Miss Thuraya! Say something to him!)

Thuraya crossed her arms. The specific calm crossed-arms of a woman who had, at this exact moment, decided to let Sadoo experience the specific consequences of his own commerce.

"You started this."

Sadoo looked around. Panicking. Then he saw Safiya.

The specific lit-up face of a man who had, in his desperation, just identified his remaining asset.


> صفيه! انتِ طيبه. قولي له شي.


(Safiya! You are kind. Say something to him.)

Safiya panicked. The specific small wifely panic of a woman who had just been promoted, without consultation, into a diplomatic role.

"Me? I don't know Arabic!"

Sadoo smiled. The specific instant-recovery smile of a man who had, two seconds ago, been desperate and who had just, in the space of one thought, identified a new strategy.


> قولّي بالإنجليزي!


(Tell him in English!)

Safiya looked at the seller's back. She took a breath. She tried — with the specific small careful attempt of a woman who had decided to produce a polite English sentence and hope that politeness, as a quality, translated.

"Excuse me… we are… just looking."

The seller turned. Slowly.

He looked at her. The specific slow evaluation of a professional whose ears had just caught the specific respectful register of an English customer not attempting to insult him.

Then he said — in broken English — "Looking… no buying?"

Safiya nodded. Fast. "Yes. Just looking."

The seller shrugged. The specific small Muttrah shrug that released all parties from the negotiation. "Okay."

Sadoo whispered — amazed —


> نجحت!


(It worked!)

John blinked. "That worked?"

Thuraya smiled. The specific small teacher's smile of a woman who had just, in real time, witnessed the specific moment when English had accomplished, in one sentence, what Arabic had failed to accomplish in thirty.

"Sometimes," she said, "English is magic."


## Scene Five — Where Is Mansoor?


They moved away — before Sadoo could cause another disaster.

They walked deeper into the souq. More shops. More sounds. More colours — the specific accumulating visual overload of a covered market in which every stall wanted the eye. Silk scarves in impossible pink. Stacks of yellow lemons. Dark red carpets hanging from wooden beams. A butcher's stall with bright pomegranate juice running from the cutting board.

Suddenly —


> وين منصووور؟


(Where is Mansoor?)

Thuraya stopped walking.

The specific full stop of a teacher whose brain had just, in one instant, performed the headcount that every teacher on every field trip performs involuntarily — and which had, for the first time since they had left the pickup truck, produced the wrong number.

Safiya's heart jumped. "What?"

Thuraya looked around. Fast. "Mansoor is not here."

John's stomach dropped. The specific sudden low drop of a man whose brain had just registered, at the same moment, that the school trip had included the village's most irrepressible eight-year-old — and that he had not seen that eight-year-old for, he now realised, at least fifteen minutes.

"What do you mean… not here?"

They turned. They counted. The specific slow accusing counting of three adults realising, at the same time, that they had, somewhere, failed.

One. Two. Three.

No Mansoor.

Sadoo looked around too. He suggested — with the specific easygoing village optimism of a man who had, in his own childhood, disappeared at markets many times without consequence —


> يمكن راح يشوف شي.


(Maybe he went to look at something.)

John's face became white. The specific full English whiteness of a teacher whose professional responsibility had just collided with a specific Omani geography.

"He is a child. In this place."

Thuraya's voice became sharp. The specific small knife-sharp teacher-voice that went on only in emergencies.

"We must find him. Now."

They split.

John and Safiya went one way. Thuraya and Sadoo went another. The specific immediate silent co-ordination of four adults who had, without needing instructions, divided the search quadrants between them.

John walked fast. Calling. "Mansoor! Mansoor!"

People looked at him. The specific puzzled looking of passers-by who had just seen a tall foreigner calling a name that, in their ears, was the name of every third boy in the country.

Safiya checked every small shop. "Mansoor!"

She checked a fruit seller's corner. She checked a scarf shop. She checked a narrow alley between two tailors. She ducked under a hanging rug. She called his name again — her voice, in the specific full-volume teacher-voice she had developed over the last two months, carrying further than she had expected in the enclosed space.

Then — they heard it.

Laughter.

Very familiar laughter.

The specific high-pitched, eight-year-old, entirely unaware-of-the-panic laughter that could, in any country, be identified from fifty metres away as the laughter of a boy who was having a good time.

They followed the sound.

And found him.

Mansoor stood in front of a man selling toys. He was holding a small wooden sword — a traditional Omani toy-sword, with a short curved blade of painted wood, about the size of his forearm.

His eyes were shining.

The specific full shining of an eight-year-old boy who had, without any of the supervising adults knowing, discovered exactly what he had, without telling anyone, come to this market to find.

"Mansoor!" Thuraya shouted — arriving, with Sadoo, behind them.

Mansoor turned. He turned with the specific surprised happy turning of a boy who had not, until that moment, realised that anybody had been looking for him.


> مس ثريا! شوف!


(Miss Thuraya! Look!)

John ran. He grabbed Mansoor's shoulder — not roughly, but firmly, the specific firm teacher-grip of a man whose relief had, in the same instant, become a need to communicate something important.

"You cannot disappear like that!"

Mansoor looked confused. The specific small innocent confusion of a boy who did not, in his own experience, understand the concept of disappearing. He had, from his point of view, simply been here — next to the swords — and the adults had, inconsiderately, left.


> بس أنا شفت سيوف.


(But I saw swords.)

Safiya bent down. Her knees hit the dirty souq floor and she did not, in that moment, care. She was at his eye level now. Her hand on his arm.

"You scared us."

Mansoor looked guilty. Properly guilty. The specific small contrite face of a boy who had just — for the first time in his short life — registered that his own good time had cost other people real fear.


> آسف.


(Sorry.)

The toy seller smiled. "He likes sword."

Sadoo arrived. Breathing hard.


> هذا مجنون.


(This one is crazy.)

Thuraya took Mansoor's hand. Firmly. The specific small firm hand-taking of a teacher who had decided that, for the rest of the trip, the boy's fingers were going to be inside hers.

"Stay with the group. Always."

Mansoor nodded.

John wiped his face. "New rule. Nobody moves alone."


## Scene Six — The Shouting


They continued walking.

Then —

They heard shouting.

Angry shouting.

It was Sadoo's voice.

They turned.

Sadoo was arguing with another seller. Loudly. Very loudly. The specific full-volume public argument of a Samaili man whose village pride had just been, in a city he did not belong to, publicly wronged.


> هذا مو اللي عطيتني!


(This is not what you gave me!)

The seller shouted back.

People started looking.

Thuraya whispered — with the specific heavy whisper of a woman who could already see how this was going to end — "Oh no. What did he do?"

Sadoo turned and saw them.


> هذا الرجال أعطاني شي غير!


(This man gave me something different!)

The seller shouted something.

Thuraya translated, fast. "He says you changed it."

Sadoo put his hands on his head.


> أنا؟ لا!


(Me? No!)

The seller pointed at a bag. Inside was something broken.

The crowd grew.

John felt the heat. Not the chili-heat of the previous week. A different, colder heat — the specific administrative heat of a situation that had, in the last thirty seconds, crossed from commercial into legal.

"This is becoming… big."

Safiya whispered, "Very big."

The seller shouted again. Sadoo shouted louder. The argument had entered its volume-spiral.

Then —

A police officer appeared.

Everything became quiet.

The specific instant hush of a Muttrah crowd recognising the specific uniform of the Royal Oman Police.

The officer looked at Sadoo. Then at the seller. Then at the bag.

Thuraya's heart sank.

The officer asked questions. Fast Arabic.

Sadoo answered. Also fast Arabic.

Thuraya translated, in a low voice, for John and Safiya. "The seller says Sadoo broke something and does not want to pay."

Sadoo protested —


> ما كسرت شي!


(I didn't break anything!)

The officer looked at Sadoo. Seriously.

John stepped forward — without thinking, the specific unthinking forward-step of a man who had, all his English life, been quietly trained to intervene politely when an institution appeared.

"Excuse me."

Everyone looked at him.

He swallowed. "We… are teachers. He is… our friend."

The officer looked at him. Long.

Then said — slowly, in English — "Problem?"

John nodded. "Yes. But… maybe misunderstanding."

The officer thought. The specific slow Omani-police thinking that John, at that moment, could not read — was he about to be arrested? was Sadoo? was the whole thing about to become paperwork?

Then he said something to the seller.

The seller sighed. He said something back.

Thuraya's eyes widened. "He says… okay. He will not make problem. But Sadoo must pay a small amount."

Sadoo's face fell.


> حتى لو ما كسرت؟


(Even if I didn't break?)

Thuraya looked at him. "Yes. Even if."

Sadoo sighed. Deeply. The specific deep defeated sigh of a village man whose morning pride had just been, in a formal legal way, abridged. Then he paid.

The crowd disappeared. The specific fast Muttrah-crowd dispersal of an audience whose show had ended.

John exhaled. "That… was scary."

Safiya nodded. "Very."

Thuraya looked at Sadoo. The specific small rueful look of a teacher whose pupil had, once again, confirmed the lesson. "I told you. In Muttrah, your mouth is dangerous."

Sadoo smiled sheepishly.


> بس حماس.


(Just enthusiasm.)

They walked back toward the bus. Everyone was tired. The specific full tiredness of four adults and a small boy who had, in one morning, done too many things.

Uncle Nasser was waiting. Counting them like precious boxes.


> كلكم موجودين؟


(Are you all here?)

Thuraya nodded. "Yes."

They climbed in.

As the pickup moved — rolling slowly out of the Muttrah back-lane toward the coast road that would carry them back through the mountains to Samail — the students talked and laughed and showed what they had bought.

Mansoor slept in two minutes. Holding his small wooden sword. The specific instant-sleep of a small boy whose morning had been, by all his own measures, the greatest morning of his life.

John leaned back. He closed his eyes.

Safiya smiled. "So… what did we learn today?"

John answered without opening his eyes — with the specific tired full-body delivery of a man whose vocabulary lesson had become a life lesson.

"How to say 'I don't want to buy'… and how to survive Muttrah."

Thuraya laughed. "And you still have many lessons."

From the front, Sadoo — unconquered by his small legal defeat, the specific irrepressible good cheer of a man who would never, under any circumstance, let a day be anything other than beautiful — said, proudly —


> رحلة جميلة.


(Beautiful trip.)

John opened one eye. "Beautiful… and dangerous."

Safiya laughed.

And Muttrah stayed behind them — the city fading into the late-morning haze, the sea fading after it, the small lanes fading, the sound of the trader who had tried to sell them a ship key fading into the specific small accumulated memory of a day that would, in all the years to come, live in their English-English conversations under the short phrase remember Muttrah. Safiya opened her notebook. She wrote, quickly, in the bouncing hand that the moving pickup allowed her — Today we learned: how markets work. How Arabic works. How English is sometimes magic. How Sadoo is always Sadoo. How Mansoor is always Mansoor. And how Oman will, in one morning, give you more story than an English year. She closed the notebook. The mountains rose ahead of them again — the brown silent giants, the same ones that had surprised her on the way out — and she found, without meaning to, that she was looking at them now as though they were, somehow, on her side.


# Palm Twenty-One — Tofes 17 — The Day Sky Almost Fell on Samail



## Scene One — The Mountains in His Head


The village looked ordinary again.

No smoke. No shouting. No running behind trucks. Just sun, dust, and the specific quiet footsteps of Samail mornings that always sounded, to John's newly-adjusted ear, louder than they should. After the Muttrah trip — after the souq, the seller, the ship key, the mislaid Mansoor, the abridged pride of Sadoo in front of a police officer — Samail felt smaller, like the mountains had followed John back and were now hiding inside his head.

John stood in the yard early, holding his cup of tea with both hands.

The tea was the specific strong black tea of Samail mornings — brewed, in their small kitchen, the way Thuraya's mother had taught Safiya to brew it, with cardamom pods cracked and dropped in at the boil, with a small piece of cinnamon stick, with enough sugar that an English throat required two sips to recover from the first. His eyes were still slightly red — not from smoke this time, not from saffron, not from pesticide, not from any of the specific Samaili chemical assaults of the previous weeks — but from too much thinking.

The thinking had begun on the drive back from Muttrah. It had continued through dinner. It had continued, quietly, during the night — in the specific way that thoughts continue during Omani nights, when the air is still warm enough to keep the body just below sleep, and the mind is free to arrange, and re-arrange, the questions of the day.

Safiya — Safiya to everyone now except John and Thuraya — was folding clothes on a rope line. The specific careful folding of a woman who had learned, over the last two months, to fold her own English clothes in the Omani way — corners first, then the third fold, then the press against the thigh that locked the fold in place. She paused every few seconds to stare at the mountains far away, as though she expected a ship — a wooden ship, with a lateen sail — to appear, somehow, from behind them.

Muttrah was doing this. To both of them. It had installed itself as a small permanent soundtrack behind every ordinary morning.

Ms. Thuraya arrived carrying a small basket with papers and chalk. She looked bright. Too bright. The specific too-bright smile of a woman who knew a secret joke and was waiting for the perfect moment to release it.

John watched her carefully. "You are smiling," he said, "like you already know what I will say."

Thuraya's smile grew wider. "Maybe I do."

Safiya leaned toward him. "John. You have been staring at the door since sunrise. Are you waiting for a letter? A miracle? A goat?"

John pointed at the road — with the specific small serious point of a man who had, over coffee, decided that this morning was going to be the morning.

"I'm waiting for… answers."

"What answers?" Safiya asked.

John lowered his voice. As though the date palms at the edge of the yard could understand English and might, if he spoke too loudly, report him.

"Muttrah. The market. The sea. That man with the box. And how everyone kept saying things that sounded like warnings and jokes at the same time."

Thuraya sat on the edge of the small stone wall. She folded her hands in her lap — the specific small village-woman hand-fold that meant I am going to say one sentence, and you should listen to it.

"In Samail," she said, "warnings and jokes are cousins."

John nodded slowly. "Exactly. And now I want to know if… if this village is normal."

Safiya laughed. "John. You chose Oman in 1973. Normal is on vacation."


## Scene Two — John Decides


Before John could reply, a familiar little shadow hopped into the yard.

Malood.

The baby goat marched in — like a king, the specific short-legged marching of an animal who had, long ago, decided that this house was as much his as anyone else's. He sniffed Safiya's clean clothes. He tried to chew the corner of a scarf.

Safiya pulled it away — gently — and sighed. "This goat has no shame."

Malood stared at her — as though he had been, in fact, offended by the accusation — then ran straight for John's bag. The same bag he always attacked. The specific old English canvas bag that John had brought from Norwich, and which contained, in Malood's private cosmology, the most delicious secrets in the world.

John lifted the bag. "No, Malood. Not today. I need this bag for school."

Malood jumped. Failed. Looked angry.

Thuraya chuckled. "He is practising. For future crimes."

John looked at Thuraya. The specific focused look of a man returning, with effort, to the question he had been, all morning, trying to ask.

"Speaking of crimes…" he said, "I have to ask you something."

Thuraya blinked — innocently. "Ask."

John took a breath. The specific careful preparatory breath of a man who had, over many weeks, not quite dared the question he was about to ask.

"Who is Shayb Khalaf. Really?"

Safiya froze mid-fold.

Malood stopped chewing.

Even the air — Safiya would later remember — felt like it waited. The specific small waiting of an Omani morning that had, at the utterance of a specific name, become attentive.

Thuraya did not answer right away.

She looked, instead, toward the path that led to the edge of the village — where the clay houses grew older, and the trees grew wilder, and the specific quiet that attached itself to the furthest houses became audible even from here.

Then she said, calmly —

"He is an old man. He lives alone. People fear him because people fear what they don't understand."

John's voice dropped. "But why do they think he is… a wizard?"

Thuraya shrugged. The specific small shrug of a village woman whose country's folk-imagination had, in her experience, been generating wizards out of lonely old men for approximately two thousand years.

"Stories grow faster than palm trees. One day he is just a lonely man. Next day he is a wizard. The next day… he is something worse."

Safiya spoke softly. "Is he dangerous?"

Thuraya looked at her.

"No. But… he likes silence. And he doesn't like people watching him like he is a story."

John nodded. Then he whispered — the specific small private whisper of a man who had, in the last two minutes, made up his mind — "Good. Because I want to talk to him."

Safiya snapped her head toward him. "John… why?"

"Because," John said — standing straighter, with the specific small straight-shoulder posture of a man taking a decision — "I feel like he knows things. And I don't like mysteries. In places where I live."

Thuraya's smile returned. Small and sharp. "Then go. But don't blame me if you run."

Safiya narrowed her eyes. "Thuraya. That sounded like a threat."

Thuraya raised her hands. The specific innocent double-palm raise of a woman committing to plausible deniability. "It is not a threat. It is… a prediction."

And just like that, John decided.


## Scene Three — Under the Shawab Tree


The path to Shayb Khalaf's house felt longer than it looked.

The village sounds faded behind them. The rooster fell away. The small bickering of children at the far wells fell away. Even the continuous hush of the falaj — which was, for Samail, what the distant sea was for coastal towns, a permanent sound — thinned to a faint whisper. Even Malood followed. Bouncing along. As though this was a grand adventure.

Safiya tried to keep her voice light. "Maybe we should not bother him."

John walked faster. "We are not bothering. We are greeting. We are… friendly teachers."

Thuraya walked beside them. With calm confidence. The specific calm of a woman who had, three times in her life, had this exact conversation with this exact old man, and who knew, already, what its shape would be.

"Remember your greeting lesson."

John forced a smile — the specific small nervous smile of a man rehearsing, in the final metres before arrival, the sentences he had memorised.

"Hello. Good morning. How are you?"

Safiya added, "Nice to meet you."

Thuraya nodded. "Good. Don't forget 'We are sorry to disturb you.' Just in case."

John gave her a look. "You prepared that sentence too quickly."

Thuraya pretended not to hear.

Soon, they saw it — a small, old clay house, with a rough wooden door. In front of it stood the famous shawab tree. Wide. Heavy. Like it had been watching the village for a hundred years without blinking.

Under the tree, a man sat on a mat.

Shayb Khalaf.

He held a small cup of coffee — the specific finjan of the Gulf, with its small round base, its slightly flared rim. His walking stick rested beside him. And in front of him — laid out neatly, in their small interlocking pattern, as if waiting for a guest — a set of dominoes. The specific anticipatory arrangement of a man who had been, somehow, expecting company.

John slowed down.

His stomach did a strange little flip. The specific small flip of a man whose rational English mind had just registered an arrangement of dominoes that could not have been prepared for him, because nobody had been told he was coming, and which had, nonetheless, been prepared for him.

Safiya whispered, "Why are the dominoes already ready?"

Thuraya whispered back — with the specific small calm village-whisper of a woman whose answer was genuine but also deliberately unhelpful —

"Because he is always ready."

Malood tried to step forward. Thuraya gently held him back by the little rope around his neck.

"Not now. Trouble-maker."

John cleared his throat.

Shayb Khalaf did not look up.

John tried again. Louder.

"Good morning."

Still nothing.

Safiya leaned closer. "Maybe he didn't hear."

Then the old man spoke. Without looking up.

"Good morning," he said.

His English was not perfect.

But it was clear enough.

Clear enough to hit John like a cold bucket of water, poured, without warning, over his head in the middle of a warm village morning.


## Scene Four — Sit


John froze.

Safiya's mouth opened slightly, then closed — the specific small English double-move of a woman who had prepared a sentence, and who had, in the half-second between preparing and speaking, realised the sentence was no longer going to work.

Thuraya's eyes sparkled. She looked, in fact, like a woman who was trying, very hard, not to laugh.

John managed to speak. "You… you speak English."

Shayb Khalaf finally looked up.

His eyes were sharp. Sharp like a hawk's. But tired — the specific kind of tired that belonged to someone who carried heavy thoughts.

He nodded. Once.

"Little."

John's voice became polite. And nervous. The specific careful politeness of a man addressing a figure whose existence, ten seconds ago, he had partially relegated to village folklore and who was, now, in front of him, speaking English.

"We are teachers. New teachers. We came from Britain."

Shayb Khalaf stared at him for a long moment.

Then he pointed at the dominoes.

"Sit," he said.

John blinked. "Sit?"

"Sit," Shayb Khalaf repeated — like it was the only word that mattered.

Safiya touched John's arm. "John… do we sit?"

John swallowed. "We sit."

They sat under the shawab tree. The shade felt cooler than the rest of the village — the specific deeper cool of the oldest tree in a small place, a tree whose canopy had, over a hundred years, accumulated its own micro-climate. As though the tree were protecting secrets.

Shayb Khalaf pushed the dominoes forward.

John stared at them. "You want to… play?"

Shayb Khalaf nodded. "Play."

Thuraya sat to the side. Watching — the specific small referee-watching of a woman who already knew who was going to win, and who was looking forward to watching how the loser would lose.

Safiya whispered, "Do you know how to play?"

John whispered back. "I know the rules. I just… didn't know I would play dominoes with a village wizard under a sacred tree."

Thuraya whispered. "He is not a wizard."

John whispered — urgently, the specific look-at-the-evidence whisper of a man making his case — "He spoke English."

Thuraya smiled. "Some people learn languages. That doesn't make them wizards."

John lifted a domino piece. Carefully. As if it could bite.

Shayb Khalaf watched him. Silent.

John placed a piece down.

Shayb Khalaf placed a better piece down. Immediately. Like he had been, since before John and Safiya and Thuraya had started walking, waiting for John to make that exact opening move.

Safiya leaned back. Slowly. "He is good."

John nodded — stiffly. "Too good."


## Scene Five — Muttrah Is Noisy


After a few moves, Shayb Khalaf spoke again. Still calm. Still, apparently, not interested.

"Muttrah is noisy," he said.

John's heart jumped.

The specific full jump of a man whose day had just, without warning, acquired an uncanny edge.

"Yes…" he said. Carefully. "It is."

Shayb Khalaf continued — placing another piece, without looking at it —

"You went there. You saw ships."

Safiya's face changed. "How do you know?"

Shayb Khalaf looked at her.

"News travels."

John's hands tightened on his dominoes. "Who told you?"

Shayb Khalaf pointed loosely — expansively — toward the village behind them.

"People."

Then he looked directly at John.

"And you got into trouble."

John's face went pale. "We… we did not get into trouble."

Shayb Khalaf raised one eyebrow.

John felt the air press down on him. The specific heavy descent of a warm October afternoon converging on a man whose lie had, in less than three seconds, collapsed.

Safiya whispered — sharply, the specific small wifely emergency-whisper of a woman who had decided to intervene — "John. Your face is confessing."

John tried to laugh. It came out like a cough. "Small trouble. Only… small."

Thuraya covered her mouth. Her shoulders shook.

Shayb Khalaf placed another domino piece.

Click.

The sound felt loud. Louder than it should have been. The specific small acoustically-enhanced click that happened when a domino piece was placed, in total silence, under the oldest tree in the village.

Then Shayb Khalaf said — still in English —

"Next time, listen."

John's throat became dry. "Listen to who?"

Shayb Khalaf's eyes flicked toward Thuraya. For half a second. Then back to John.

"Listen," he repeated.

John stared at him. Then looked at Thuraya. "Why does he keep saying 'listen' like it is… a warning?"

Thuraya smiled. Sweetly. "Maybe because you don't listen."

John opened his mouth to argue. But Shayb Khalaf suddenly said — casually, without looking at anyone —

"Your goat likes your bag."

Safiya turned her head so fast she almost hurt her neck. "How do you know about Malood?"

Shayb Khalaf looked at Malood — who was currently chewing the corner of John's bag again. With full confidence.

Shayb Khalaf said — calmly —

"Because he is doing it now."

John looked at Malood. Then back at Shayb Khalaf.

Then he whispered, "This is impossible."

Shayb Khalaf placed his last domino.

He won.

John stared at the domino line like it was an official stamp of defeat.

Safiya exhaled. "He beat you."

John — half in shock — stood up. Quickly. "Thank you. We… we should go."

Shayb Khalaf lifted his cup. Drank slowly.

Then he said — almost lightly —

"You are afraid."

John forced a smile. "No. I am not afraid."

Shayb Khalaf nodded. Once. "Yes. You are."

John felt his cheeks heat. He turned away.

Then Shayb Khalaf added — quietly, in Arabic this time, like a knife slipping out of its sheath —


> واحليلك باه… تفزع.


(Oh you poor dear… you're scared.)

John froze.

Safiya's eyes widened.

Thuraya's smile became dangerous.

John spun back. "You… you said that before."

Shayb Khalaf looked at him. "Did I?"

John's voice shook. "Yes."

Shayb Khalaf leaned back. Calm. "Maybe you imagined it."


## Scene Six — If I…, I Will…


John grabbed his sandals. Suddenly. The specific sudden grab of a man who needed to move, needed air.

Safiya stood too. "Thank you, Shayb Khalaf."

Shayb Khalaf did not answer.

John took two steps… then turned. And blurted, in English, the specific question that had been, under all the small English sentences, the real question all along.

"Why do people say you are a sorcerer?"

The air changed again.

Thuraya stopped smiling.

Safiya held her breath.

Shayb Khalaf's face did not change. But his eyes did.

He said — quietly — "People need a story."

John swallowed. "And the story about… the 'maghsoob'?"

Thuraya's head snapped toward John. "Why would you say that here?"

John whispered, "Because… because I heard it."

Shayb Khalaf stared at him. For a long time.

And in that long stare, Safiya would later understand, was the entire private history of a man who had, over forty or fifty years, been the subject of a village story that was not true — a story about giving a son to the devils, a story about sorcery, a story about his son Obaid, who had simply gone to Bahrain to work, who had returned eventually as an ordinary man, and who had, because of his father's reputation, been treated by the village ever since as a ghost. The maghsoob story was a village invention. Khalaf knew it was an invention. The villagers, somewhere deep down, also knew. But the story was too useful — too satisfying — to be allowed to die. And Khalaf had lived his life inside it.

Then he said one sentence. Slow. Heavy.

"Don't play with fear. Fear plays back."

John's stomach dropped.

He nodded. Quickly. "Okay. Thank you. Goodbye."

And then he did what Thuraya had predicted.

He ran.

Barefoot. Holding his sandals like a strange trophy. The specific full-commitment run of a man whose legs had, without consulting him, decided that the path away from the shawab tree needed to be traversed as quickly as possible.

Safiya followed. Half laughing. Half worried. Calling — "John! John, slow down!"

Behind them, Thuraya walked. Calmly. Like she owned the whole scene.

She glanced back. Once.

Shayb Khalaf was still sitting under the shawab tree. Quiet. Unmoving. As if nothing had happened. As if they were the ones who were strange.

By the time they reached the classroom, John was still breathing like he had escaped a tiger.

The students noticed his face immediately.

Mansoor whispered — loudly, the specific stage-whisper of a boy who wanted the whole class to hear — "Teacher John… you run!"

John tried to stand tall. "Yes. I ran."

Thuraya wrote the target structure on the board. In her clear clean lettering:

IF I …, I WILL …

Safiya clapped her hands. "Today. We use our trip to learn English!"

John pointed at himself. Slowly. The specific slow deliberate self-pointing of a man who had decided to turn his own fear, for the second time that week, into pedagogical material.

"If I see Shayb Khalaf… I will run."

The class burst into laughter.

Thuraya smiled. "Good sentence. True sentence."

Safiya pointed to a student. "Your turn. If you eat too much chili…"

A boy grinned. "If I eat too much chili, I will cry."

John nodded. "Excellent."

Thuraya pointed at Mansoor. "If Mansoor makes noise…"

The students shouted together — the specific joyful full-class chorus of children who knew, exactly, what the next sentence was — "Teacher will shout!"

Mohammadeen — passing by the door, unable to resist — added in his Egyptian accent, "No, no, no. Teacher will make you quiet!"

Fatkat laughed. Loudly. From the corridor.

Safiya continued. "If we go to Muttrah…"

The students repeated — "We will see ships."

John's eyes drifted to the window.

He could almost hear Shayb Khalaf's voice again —

Don't play with fear.

Thuraya noticed his expression. She leaned closer — whispering in English, so only he could hear.

"Now you understand. People here can make a story inside your head. And you will believe it."

John swallowed. "Did you… did you tell him about Muttrah?"

Thuraya blinked. Innocently. "Me?"

John stared at her. "Thuraya."

She smiled. "If you accuse me… I will laugh."

Safiya called from the front. "John! Your turn. Give the students one more sentence."

John forced a smile. He faced the class.

"If I hear a strange English voice under a tree," he said, "I will… drink tea and pretend it is normal."

The class laughed again.

But John didn't. Not fully.

Because deep inside, he was still wondering the same thing he had been wondering since the smoke day —

How did that old man learn English?

And why did he feel like Shayb Khalaf knew more than he should?

Outside, the wind moved softly through the palm farms.

And somewhere in the village — under the shawab tree, in the shade that had been accumulating for a hundred years, in the small patient circle of dominoes laid out carefully on the mat — silence kept its secrets. Shayb Khalaf sat, alone now, with his small cup of coffee, and a second game of dominoes already being arranged by his own hands. As though, somewhere in the village, another visitor was already on the way.


# Palm Twenty-Two — The Sky Falls on Samail — A Village in Rain



## Scene One — The Sound That Did Not Belong


There are sounds a place teaches you, and sounds it does not. After enough mornings in a single house, the body learns the village before the mind does — the rooster who calls always at the same hour, the woman whose footsteps pass at the same angle, the door of the neighbour that closes always with the same particular complaint of wood against wood. The ear builds an inventory. And anything outside that inventory arrives, when it arrives, with the specific small alarm of an unexpected guest.

That morning, the sound was outside the inventory.

Not the rooster. Not the bread being clapped between palms in the next house. Not the small tin sound of Sadoo opening his shop. Not Mansoor shouting somewhere in the lanes — that voice which had become, over the past weeks, almost a clock.

This sound was something else. Soft. Patient. Repeating with the small wet certainty of a thing that has decided it is going to continue.

Tup… tup… tup…

John opened one eye. The room was dim — that specific blue-grey dimness of a Samaili dawn, when the sun had begun its work but had not yet committed to it. He listened. The sound continued. Tup… tup… tup…

Sophia was already sitting up — not startled, not afraid, simply alert in the careful, listening way of a person who has been awake longer than the room knows.

"John,"

she whispered, and her voice carried the small wonder of someone reporting a fact that contained a question.

"I think… the sky is leaking."

Before he could answer her — before his mind had even fully assembled the sentence she had just said — a single cold drop fell on his nose.

He sat up in one motion.

"The sky is WHAT?"

Another drop. Then another. Each one finding a slightly different patch of skin, of pillow, of wooden floor — not many, not threatening, simply arriving, with the unhurried persistence of a thing that had been doing this for longer than the house had existed.

The roof was a clay roof. Old. Patient. Maintained the way Omani village roofs were maintained — not against rain, because rain did not come, but against time. And now time and rain had arrived together, on the same morning, and the roof was discovering, along with its inhabitants, that its preparations had been incomplete.

Sophia looked up. Then around. Then down at the floor, where a small dark mark was forming on the bisat.

"The roof… it's dripping!"

She moved with the specific full speed of a woman who had decided, in a single second, that this was an emergency and that she was going to manage it. She ran to the kitchen. She came back with bowls. With a cup. With a small tin plate. With anything that could hold water and had not been previously assigned the task.

"Emergency! Emergency!"

John followed her — half-asleep, still trying to understand the syntax of a sky that was, apparently, capable of leaking.

"Is this normal? Is this… a flood?"


## Scene Two — Welcome to Oman Rain


Ms. Thuraya appeared at the door.

She did not appear the way a guest appears. She did not appear the way a neighbour might appear. She appeared the way the morning itself appeared in this village — without announcement, without surprise, with the calm specific completeness of a person who had known, somehow, that her presence would shortly be required and had simply arranged her steps to coincide with that requirement. Her scarf was already on. Her face was already calm. The rain was already, in some private way, hers.

She looked at the dripping ceiling. Then at Sophia, running between bowls. Then at John, who was standing in the middle of the room holding a cup with the dignity of a man who has not yet been informed of the situation.

She smiled. The specific small calm smile of a woman watching foreigners encounter their first Omani rain.

"Relax. It's rain."

John froze.

"Rain?"

Sophia stopped. A bowl in each hand. The specific stopping of a person who has been told that the emergency she has been managing is, in fact, not an emergency at all.

"Rain?"

And then — without warning, without the courtesy of a small build-up —

BOOOOOM.

The thunder did not come from the sky. It came from inside the sky. It came from a place in the sky that the village did not normally have access to, and which on this particular morning had decided to make itself heard. The walls of the house — clay walls, patient walls, the same walls that had stood for generations — felt it before the ear did. The floor felt it before the walls. There was a small specific second in which everything that had ever been still in this room remembered that stillness was a temporary condition.

Sophia screamed.

John screamed a small, slightly delayed second later — the polite scream of a man whose body had decided to participate before his mind had finished assessing whether participation was warranted.

Malood screamed too, from somewhere outside the door, with the specific full-bodied protest of a goat whose morning had been interrupted by something he could not negotiate with.

Thuraya laughed.

The specific small full laugh of a woman who had been waiting, all morning, for this particular cultural exchange.

"Welcome to Oman rain."


## Scene Three — A Village Transformed


They went outside.

And the village they had known yesterday — the dry village, the dust-coloured village, the village whose air carried the small specific aged scent of stone and heat — was no longer that village.

The ground was dark. Not muddy yet — simply changed, the colour of earth that had remembered, for the first time in months, what water was. The smell had transformed completely. Not the slow ancient scent of dust waiting. Something green. Something new. The specific full wet-earth smell of a place that had been holding its breath and had, in the last hour, finally exhaled. Sophia breathed it in with her whole chest. The way you breathe in something you had not known, until that moment, you had been missing.

Water was running everywhere. Not in floods — in small careful lines. Down the sides of houses. Along the edges of paths. Following the small grooves the falaj had cut over the centuries, but also finding new grooves of its own, places it had not been before, claiming territory the way water always claims territory: with patience, and with the absolute certainty that it had time on its side.

And the children.

The children were already outside.

Not in coats. Not in boots. Not in any of the careful preparations a Northern child would have been wrapped in before being permitted to enter such weather. They were in their normal dishdashas, their normal small girl-dresses, their normal bare feet — and they had taken the rain into themselves the way a desert takes water: completely, joyfully, without any concern at all for the consequences. The mud was a gift. The puddles were a celebration. The specific full wet shining of every small face in the village had the quality of children who had, for the first time in many months, been given an entirely new toy by the sky itself.

Mansoor was the wettest. Mansoor was always the most of whatever was available, but in this specific moment, his commitment to the rain had reached the level of vocation. He jumped — not in any particular pattern, simply jumped, his feet landing in puddles that had not been puddles thirty seconds earlier, sending sprays of muddy water in every direction including, occasionally, his own face.

"Maaaaaan! It's raining! It's REALLY raining!"

Ali stood differently. Carefully. His face lifted, his eyes on the sky, the specific full attention of a boy whose mind worked in the careful slow way of a person trying to record something he understood was rare.

"It's beautiful."

Noura lifted her face too. But where Ali's lifted face had been all observation, hers was all sensation.

"Cold! But nice!"

Ahmed, predictably, was already worried. He stood under a small clay overhang, holding his book against his chest with the careful protective posture of a boy who had calculated, immediately and without sentiment, that the rain was a threat to literacy.

"My book… my book is getting wet…"

Khalil, of course, had found the largest puddle in the immediate vicinity and was investigating its capacity for displacement. He threw a stone. The stone landed. A small brown wave rose and broke against the side of the path.

"Look! River!"

Sophia stood under the rain holding a bowl on her head — she had brought it out with her without thinking, and now it was protecting nothing because she had stopped caring whether anything was protected. Her hair was wet. Her dress was wet. Her face was full of the specific clean joy of a Northern woman who had been told her whole life that rain was an inconvenience and was discovering, for the first time, that in some places it was a celebration.

"This is… wonderful,"

she said. "And my hair is already ruined."

Another BOOM. Closer this time. The thunder rolling over the mountains and arriving at the village with the small specific delay of a guest who had decided to make sure he was noticed.

John jumped again. "The sky is angry."

Thuraya pointed.

"Look."


## Scene Four — The Wadi Coming Down


They all looked.

And far away, between the mountains — between the specific brown shoulders of the wadi-walls that Sophia had walked past every day since she had arrived without ever once thinking of them as anything other than scenery — something had begun to move.

Not a flicker. Not a small shift. A movement of scale. Something brown and full and entirely alive was coming down the dry valley, filling it the way breath fills a chest, advancing with the specific full slow inevitability of something that had decided, somewhere up in the high mountains where rain had been falling for hours, that it was time. The riverbed that had been dry yesterday — that had been dry for so many months that even the children had forgotten it was a riverbed — was no longer dry. It was a river. A real river. With current and weight and direction. And it was approaching.

Ali shouted. A specific small ancient shout — the shout of an Omani child who had been born knowing the meaning of this particular kind of brown movement between mountains.

"Halaaaa! The wadi is coming down!"

The wadi.

A wide riverbed that was usually dry.

Now it was full.

Full of fast, brown, strong water — water that had been, two hours ago, raindrops on rocks at three thousand metres altitude, and was now, having gathered itself together with the specific full patience of all mountain water everywhere, descending toward the sea by the only route available to it: this specific small valley, between these specific small mountains, past this specific small village.

John stared. His mouth slightly open. The specific full open-mouthed staring of a man who had been told, theoretically, that dry valleys could become rivers, and was now watching the theory become true in front of his eyes.

"That is not a river. That is a moving mountain."

Sophia whispered.

"It's beautiful… and scary."

Thuraya nodded. Her face had the specific small calm competence of a woman who had seen many wadis come down in her life and who had developed, over those years, the right specific small relationship with the phenomenon — neither afraid nor casual, simply respectful.

"When the wadi comes like this, it means good rain in the mountains."

She paused. And then a small specific smile crossed her face — the kind of smile that meant a plan was already forming.

"It also means… we can go to Tawi Zahir."

The children's eyes lit up. All of them. At the same time. In a single small specific village explosion of joy.

Mansoor shouted, "Swimming!"

Noura shouted, "Picnic!"

Khalil shouted, "No school!"

Ahmed shouted, "Careful!"

The specific small full simultaneous four-direction shouting of four small Omani children whose expectations of the next day had just been completely transformed.

John blinked. "What is Tawi Zahir?"

Thuraya explained — patiently, the way she had explained letj and nahsher and maghbar and every other piece of village life that John's English-trained mind had not previously had a category for.

"It's a place where clean water stays after the rain. Pools. Clear water. People go to swim, wash, relax."

Sophia clapped. The full small clap of a woman whose travel-instincts had just been activated.

"A trip!"

John was already excited. "A trip!"

Thunder answered again. BOOM.

Malood ran between their legs and almost killed everyone — a small wet white missile of goat, fully soaked, fully delighted, fully unconcerned about whether his trajectory through human ankles was welcome. John, for his part, did not protest. He had reached, in this village, the specific small acceptance of a man who had decided that some things were simply going to keep happening, and that the most efficient response was to remain upright.


## Scene Five — In the Classroom — Past Continuous


The rain stopped.

Not slowly. Not with a long courteous tapering off. Simply stopped — the way Omani rain always stopped, with the same suddenness with which it had begun, as if the sky had decided it had made its point and had no further business with the village that morning.

The sky became clean and blue. So blue that Sophia, walking back toward the school, had to stop for a moment and just look at it. The specific full deep washed blue of a sky that had, for one morning, allowed itself to be opened, and had now closed itself again — clean, calm, restored.

The mountains looked washed too. As if the rain had worked through them with the same care a careful person works through silver — polishing each surface, restoring each colour to its full intensity, removing the small specific accumulated dust of months without water. The whole landscape had the specific freshness of a face that has just been splashed with cold water — alert, awake, slightly surprised by its own brightness. The air smelled like new beginnings. There was no other phrase for it. Sophia tried, in her head, to find a more sophisticated description, and could not. New beginnings. The smell of a place that had, on this particular morning, agreed to start over.

In class, everyone was still talking about the rain. The energy of it had not yet drained. The students sat in their normal places but their bodies had not quite returned to their normal stillness — there was still rainwater in the small private parts of their attention, still mud somewhere on their feet, still thunder echoing somewhere in the back of their ears.

John wrote on the board:

WEATHER

Then under it, in his careful chalk hand:

sun  —  rain  —  thunder  —  lightning  —  hot  —  cold  —  cloudy  —  rainbow  —  wadi

The last word he wrote slowly. Carefully. With the specific small respect of a teacher who had, that morning, been introduced to the specific Arabic word for a phenomenon his English vocabulary had not previously contained, and who was now placing it, with appropriate ceremony, on his board.

He turned around. "Today, we talk about the weather."

Sophia added — "And about what we were doing."

John wrote:

PAST CONTINUOUSI was…  /  He was…  /  They were… + verb-ing

He pointed at Mansoor. "Mansoor. What were you doing when it started raining?"

Mansoor stood proudly. With the specific full standing-up of a small boy who had, that morning, performed the action he was about to describe with all the energy his small body possessed.

"I was… jumping."

John smiled. "Good. You were jumping."

He pointed to Noura. "And you?"

Noura thought. The specific small thinking-pause of a girl whose morning had contained so many distinct activities that she had to choose between them.

"I was… running."

"Excellent."

He pointed to Sophia. "And you, Sophia?"

Sophia laughed. The small full self-aware laugh of a woman who was about to confess a personal indignity to her students.

"I was putting bowls under the roof."

The class laughed. John pointed to himself. "I was sleeping."

The class laughed more. Thuraya helped — adding more sentences in the same pattern, building each one carefully so the structure could be heard.

"Say: The children were playing. The rain was falling. The wadi was coming down."

Ali raised his hand. "Teacher… the rainbow?"

Outside — and they had not noticed it until this moment, because it had appeared with the small slow patience of all rainbows, occupying its corner of the sky without any announcement — a rainbow was forming. Slowly. Each colour declaring itself in turn. The specific full quiet emergence of a rainbow that had been waiting, all morning, for someone to notice it.

Everyone ran to the window.

"Wow…"

"Look!"

"Two colours!"

"No, many colours!"

John smiled. "Rainbow. When rain and sun meet."

Sophia whispered. The specific small whisper of a woman who had heard a sentence and recognised, in it, the small unexpected presence of poetry.

"That is… poetry."

John cleared his throat — the specific full English cleared throat of a man who had been, for half a second, accidentally lyrical, and who was now returning to grammar.

He wrote:

When it started raining…I was…

They practiced together — the small full classroom-chant of children repeating sentences they had just lived:

"When it started raining, I was sleeping."

"When it started raining, I was playing."

"When it started raining, I was eating."

And then — quietly, from the back of the room, in the specific small careful English of a boy who had spent more time listening than speaking — Saeed raised his hand.

"When it started raining,"

he said, slowly,

"I was… watching."

Thuraya looked at him. With the specific full soft eyes of a teacher who had recognised, in a single sentence, both the truth of a particular boy's whole life — that he had spent it at the edges, watching — and the small specific quiet courage of his having said so. Not I was running. Not I was jumping. I was watching. The most honest English sentence in the room, and the most carefully constructed. Thuraya did not correct it. Thuraya did not embellish it. Thuraya simply, with her eyes, received it — and that was the whole of Saeed's reward, and it was, by his own private calculation, more than enough.


## Scene Six — Planning the Trip — The Village After Rain


After school, everyone gathered outside.

The wadi was still running. Loud and strong. The brown water that had arrived from the mountains that morning had not yet finished its journey — it would continue for hours, perhaps for the rest of the day, the specific full slow draining of an entire mountain range through this specific small village's specific small valley.

John said, watching it:

"If the wadi is like this… Tawi Zahir must be amazing."

Thuraya nodded. "We should go tomorrow."

Sophia clapped again. "Yes! A real Omani picnic!"

They went to see Uncle Nasser. He stood next to his pickup, drinking tea — the specific full calm tea-drinking of a man who had spent his life next to this particular pickup and who had, by long practice, mastered the art of drinking tea while leaning on its side.

"You want to go Tawi Zahir?"

he asked. "Tomorrow? Good. I will take you."

Mansoor jumped. "With the pickup?"

"Yes."

Mansoor jumped more.

They started planning, the way villages plan such things — not by lists, but by the small specific accumulation of who-volunteered-for-what.

Who brings food?Who brings water?Who brings firewood?

Sophia and Thuraya decided to cook. John decided to "help" — a decision which, when it was announced, produced a small specific worried silence among the women, the kind of silence that meant his help would be received politely and then quietly redirected.

The children ran to tell their families. And soon — within the small full hour that all village news takes to travel — people started coming.

Rahma's mother. Marwa's aunt. Salimah's brothers. The full extended kinship-network of the village converging, over the course of an afternoon, on the small specific project of going together to Tawi Zahir tomorrow.

Even Uncle Marhoon said, "I will bring meat."

John's eyes became big. "Meat?"

"Meshkak,"

Marhoon said proudly.

Malood heard the word "food" and followed him immediately.

That evening, Samail was different.

Everything was green. Clean. Fresh. Birds were singing like they were paid for it — the specific full noisy joy of village birds whose territory had, in a single morning, been completely transformed into a softer version of itself. The mountains looked closer. They always did, after rain. The dust that normally hung between the village and the mountains had been washed out of the air, and so the mountains stood now in the specific full sharp clarity of mountains that had been, for one evening, restored to their original geometry. The air was cool. The houses smelled like wet clay. The children's voices, in the lanes, had a different quality — slower, fuller, the way voices sound on evenings when nothing more needs to be done.

Sophia stood outside the house, breathing deeply. "This is… the most beautiful day since we came."

John nodded. "I think Oman is showing us its secret."

They looked at the wadi one more time. It was still running. Strong. Loud. Alive.

Thuraya said quietly:

"Tomorrow… Tawi Zahir."

From far down the lane, Mansoor shouted — "Teacher! I am bringing my swimming clothes!"

John shouted back — "Good! I am bringing… courage!"

Sophia laughed. "You will need it."

Malood ran past them, slipped in the mud, stood up, and continued like nothing happened — the specific full goat-resilience of a creature who had decided long ago that mud was simply one of the surfaces, and that all surfaces were, in the final analysis, equivalent.

John looked at him. "You… are not normal."

Malood answered by eating a leaf.

And in the small specific evening of that specific small Samaili day — between the still-running wadi and the still-cooling mountains and the still-soft scent of new beginnings — the village settled, slowly, into its own rest. Tomorrow they would go to Tawi Zahir. Tomorrow they would swim, and eat meshkak, and sit by clear pools, and learn whatever Tawi Zahir was going to teach them. Tonight — tonight was simply the small specific quiet space between the rain that had arrived and the day that was coming. The space in which a village, briefly, was allowed to know that it had been blessed.


# Palm Twenty-Three — Tawi Zahir — The Pools, the Picnic, and the Mountain Cousin
