## Scene One — Before the Sun Finished Waking


There are mornings when a village wakes faster than its sun. When the people, by collective decision the night before, have agreed to be ready before the day begins — and the day, finding them already in motion, has to hurry to catch up. This was such a morning. The sky was still dark blue at the edges, still that specific full pre-dawn blue of an Omani village in October, when the first headlight of Uncle Nasser's pickup truck cut into the lane outside John and Sophia's house.

The pickup truck was already making noise before the sun finished waking up.

Uncle Nasser was proud of his engine — even if it sounded, in the careful judgement of John's English ear, like it was arguing with itself. The engine had its own particular conversation, a kind of metallic disagreement between its various small components that never quite resolved but also never quite broke down. Uncle Nasser had decided long ago that this was a feature, not a problem. Engines that argued were engines that were thinking.

John and Sophia stood in the back, holding the side carefully — the specific full careful holding of two foreigners who had never before stood in the back of an Omani pickup truck and were not entirely sure what protocol to follow.

Sophia whispered, with the small specific dramatic whisper of a woman about to commit to an unfamiliar kind of risk:

"If I fall, please tell my mother I tried."

John answered bravely — the specific small full husband-bravery of a man who had decided that whatever was about to happen would happen to both of them together.

"If you fall, I fall with you."

Ms. Thuraya sat in the front, calm and confident, like always — the specific full passenger-seat composure of a teacher who had ridden in many pickups in her life and who had developed, over those rides, the particular relationship with a moving truck-cab that allowed her to read, think, and occasionally translate all at once.

Behind them came the children. Full of noise and excitement. Carrying the small accumulated kinetic energy of an entire night's anticipation — having barely slept, having woken before their mothers, having dressed in whatever combination of clothes their hands had found in the dark.

Mansoor was jumping without moving — a feat which, until John had encountered Mansoor, he had not believed was physically possible. The specific full vertical-only motion of a small Omani boy whose body had decided that running in a direction was insufficient and that pure vertical celebration was required.

"We are going! We are going! We are really going!"

Ahmed was checking the bags — the specific small administrative checking of a boy who had appointed himself, without consulting anyone, as the trip's quartermaster.

"Food is here. Water is here. Plates are here. Good."

Khalil was already planning trouble. He always was. It was less an activity than a posture — the specific full forward-leaning ease of a boy who had not yet identified the specific trouble he intended to cause but was certain that, at some point during the day, the right opportunity would present itself.

Malood was there too. Because Malood was always there. The principle had been established months ago: where the village went, Malood went. The principle was no longer subject to debate.


## Scene Two — A Place That Was a Dream


The road to Tawi Zahir did not announce its destination. It began the way all village roads began — in dust, in heat, in the specific small anonymous brown of an Omani road that had not yet decided what it was going to show you. They passed the last houses of Samail. They passed the last palm farms. They passed an old falaj-channel running silver in the early light. The land flattened. Then rose. Then narrowed. The pickup, taking each curve at Uncle Nasser's practiced unhurried speed, climbed gradually into the small specific landscape that lay between Samail and its quiet neighbour.

The road was dusty, then rocky, then suddenly green.

It was the suddenness that Sophia would remember. Not the gradual transition from one landscape to another. The simple clean specific moment when the brown gave way and the green arrived — full and shocking, the way certain truths arrive after a long silence. There had been water here. The wadi had run last night, and before it, the high mountains had received hours of rain, and now — in this small specific cup of land between the rocks — the consequence of all that water was visible. Trees. Real trees, not the dust-tolerant village palms but full green trees, leaves heavy with the small full memory of the moisture that had passed through them. Grass at the edges of the path. Wild flowers, of small careful varieties that Sophia did not know the names of. The whole place was the specific small full demonstration of what Oman was, when Oman had water.

When they reached Tawi Zahir, everyone became quiet.

The place was beautiful.

Small clear pools of water lay between the rocks — pools that had not been there a week ago, pools that would not be there in a month. Pools that existed only in the specific small window after rain when the high mountain water had finished its descending and had collected, briefly, in the careful small basins that the rocks of Tawi Zahir had been quietly preparing, over centuries, to receive it. The water was clear. So clear that Sophia, looking at it, could see the small specific brown stones at the bottom, each one shifting slightly in the small movement of the water above. Palm trees stood around the pools — not the dusty palms of the village, but palms whose roots had reached the water and whose fronds, as a consequence, hung heavier and greener than any palms Sophia had previously seen. The air was cool. Not cold — cool, in the specific full sense of an Omani micro-climate that had organised itself, by water and shade, into a small private pocket of relief from the surrounding heat. Birds were singing — the specific full unselfconscious singing of birds whose territory had, that morning, been visited by something good.

Sophia covered her mouth.

"This is… a dream."

John looked at the water. The specific small careful look of a man calculating exactly what depth of cold he was about to commit himself to.

"This is… cold."

The children did not care.

Mansoor was already taking off his shoes — the specific full single-second shoe-removal of a boy whose mind had decided, before the truck had fully stopped, that his feet were going to be in water within the next ten seconds and that any obstacle to that goal was going to be eliminated immediately.

Ali shouted, "Swimming!"

Noura shouted, "Wait!"

Khalil shouted, "Last one is a chicken!"

And suddenly — the specific small full collective village-children's suddenness that no foreign visitor had ever yet successfully predicted — everyone was running.

Thuraya clapped her hands. "Careful! Don't run on the rocks!"

Nobody listened.


## Scene Three — Setting Up the Picnic


The families arrived slowly.

Not in one group. The village did not move that way. They arrived in the specific full unhurried way villages always arrive at picnics — one car, then another, then a small group on foot from the closer farms, then a final family in a borrowed truck whose horn announced their arrival before their faces did. The site filled gradually. Mats appeared. Coolers appeared. Stacks of qafeer baskets appeared, each containing the small specific contributions of a different household — bread from this house, dates from that, fruit from another. The unspoken protocol of the village potluck, which no one had needed to organise because everyone already knew their part.

Rahma's mother arrived. Marwa's aunt arrived. Salimah's brothers arrived, carrying firewood. Even Uncle Marhoon came, bringing a bag of meat.

"Meshkak,"

he said proudly.

John's eyes became happy immediately. The specific full instinctive happiness of a man whose body had, over the past months, learned that the word meshkak was always followed, sooner or later, by the specific small joy of meat that had been marinated overnight and grilled over a fire that had been built specifically for it.

Sophia and Thuraya started working.

They put stones in a circle — three flat stones, balanced carefully, so that the cooking pots could rest stably on top of them. The specific small ancient hearth-design that Omani women had been using for generations, that needed no instruction manual, that Sophia was learning by watching Thuraya's hands. They brought firewood. They started the fire — Thuraya doing the actual lighting with the practiced quick small wrist-movements of a woman who had started a thousand fires in her life and would start a thousand more.

Sophia was cutting vegetables. The specific small full concentration of a foreign woman who had decided that her contribution today would be precise vegetable-cutting, even if her precision was being measured against a Northern standard that Omani village cooking had never required.

Thuraya was washing rice. Both hands in the small metal bowl, the rice rotating between her palms, the cloudy water being poured off, fresh water being added, the rotation beginning again. The full small rice-washing ritual of an Omani kitchen, which Thuraya had been doing since she was nine and which her hands no longer needed to think about.

They were both very serious.

John walked around like a boss.

"I am… supervising,"

he said.

Thuraya looked at him. The specific full unimpressed look of a woman who had identified, without ambiguity, that John's contribution to the cooking was at this moment subtractive rather than additive.

"You are in the way."

John moved.

Malood moved too.

Unfortunately, Malood moved into the food bag.

"NO!"

Sophia shouted.

Malood ran away with something green in his mouth — a specific full green leaf-cluster that had, ten seconds earlier, been part of Sophia's carefully washed picnic-vegetables.

John chased him. "Come back here, criminal!"

The children laughed — the specific full village-children's laugh of small spectators who had been waiting, all morning, for the moment when the goat would inevitably commit his picnic-crime.


## Scene Four — In the Water


The boys were already in the pools.

Each boy had found his own kind of water. Ali was swimming calmly — the specific full slow underwater stroke of a boy who had been taught, by an older brother or an uncle, that water was something you moved through rather than fought with. Mansoor was jumping again and again — from the same rock, into the same pool, with no apparent diminishing of enthusiasm between jumps; the specific full repetitive joy of a boy whose body had decided that the act of leaving a rock and entering water was the most satisfying single action available to a human being. Khalil was splashing everyone within range — the strategic splashing of a boy who had identified water's military potential. Ahmed was sitting at the side, touching the water carefully, as though it might be a kind of liquid that he had not yet been formally introduced to.

"It's cold,"

he said.

"Yes,"

Mansoor answered, mid-jump. "That's the point."

John put one foot in.

Then he took it out.

Then he put it again.

Then he took it out again.

The specific full diplomatic foot-in-foot-out of a Northern man whose body had decided that the temperature of this particular pool was unacceptable but whose pride had decided that retreat was not an option. He was negotiating. Sophia, watching from the bank, had identified the negotiation immediately, the way wives identify negotiations they have witnessed many times.

Sophia laughed.

"You are not swimming. You are negotiating."

Finally — perhaps because the boys were watching, perhaps because his pride had completed its calculations, perhaps because the cold water had decided that further negotiation was futile — John jumped.

He screamed.

The specific full body-shock scream of a man whose entire body-temperature had been recalibrated in a single second.

Everyone clapped.

"I am alive!"

he announced.

The specific small full announcement of a man who had been, in his careful Northern life, never quite this cold and never quite this happy at the same time.


## Scene Five — The Lesson Without a Blackboard


Thuraya looked at Sophia and smiled. The specific small full conspiratorial smile of two teachers who had identified, simultaneously, the same teachable moment.

"Let's teach them something."

Sophia nodded. She stood and said loudly:

"Okay! English time!"

The children groaned a little — the specific small ritual groan of children whose play had been interrupted by the word English — but they came. They always came. By now, the relationship between the foreign teachers and the village children had developed into the specific small full friendship in which a small inconvenience was always followed by a small reward.

John stood next to her. He said:

"Today… we use Present Continuous."

He said it slowly and clearly, the way he had learned to say everything in this village. The specific full clarity of a man who had discovered that pace and clarity were more valuable than vocabulary range.

"I am swimming."

"He is swimming."

"They are swimming."

He pointed at Ali. "Ali, what are you doing?"

Ali answered, calmly, the specific full calm answer of a boy who knew the structure perfectly:

"I am swimming."

"Good!"

He pointed at Mansoor. "Mansoor?"

Mansoor shouted, mid-jump:

"I am jumping!"

"Excellent."

He pointed at Sophia. "Sophia?"

She said proudly, holding her wooden spoon as if it were credentials:

"I am cooking."

Thuraya added — "I am washing."

They pointed at Uncle Marhoon, standing over his small specific village-fire, turning the meshkak with the careful concentration of a man who had been turning meshkak for thirty years and intended to turn it for thirty more.

"What is he doing?"

The children laughed.

"He is cooking meat!"

"He is making meshkak!"

John wrote in the sand with a stick — the specific full sand-blackboard of an outdoor teacher who had decided that the absence of chalk was not going to prevent the lesson:

I am…  /  He is…  /  They are… + verb-ing

Khalil raised his hand. "Teacher… Malood?"

Everyone looked.

Malood was eating leaves from a bag. A specific bag. A bag that had not, until thirty seconds ago, been within his reach. The specific full goat-determination of a creature who had identified, by smell alone, that this particular bag contained the specific full picnic-leaves that he had decided constituted his afternoon meal.

John sighed. "Malood is… always eating."

They all repeated:

"Malood is always eating."

The specific full grammatical formulation of a sentence which, the children would soon learn, applied to Malood in every possible tense.


## Scene Six — Disasters, Afternoon, and the Quiet of Saeed


Sophia was mixing something in a pot.

She tasted it.

Her face changed.

She ran to the water.

"HOT! HOT! VERY HOT!"

She was fanning her mouth — the specific full international gesture of a person whose mouth had, in the last three seconds, been introduced to a quantity of chili it had not been prepared for.

Thuraya tasted it. She laughed.

"You put too much chili."

John tasted it.

He did not speak.

He just opened his mouth and stood in front of the wind — the specific full silent mouth-opening of a man whose vocal cords had been temporarily disabled by a quantity of chili that had not previously been documented in any English-language cookbook.

The children copied him.

Soon, John, Sophia, and three students were standing in a line, mouths open, facing the air — the specific full village-tableau of five people who had committed, simultaneously, to the same cooling strategy.

Mansoor said: "You look like fish."

The meshkak was ready.

Uncle Marhoon was proud — the specific full quiet pride of a man whose meshkak was, by general village agreement, the best meshkak available to the residents of this particular wadi.

John ate one. Then another. Then another.

Sophia said, "Slowly."

John said, "I am enjoying Oman."

Malood tried to steal one. He failed. He tried again. He succeeded. And ran.

John shouted: "That goat is impossible!"

The afternoon settled.

People sat. Talked. Laughed. Some were swimming. Some were sleeping. Some were eating. The specific full unhurried Omani-picnic afternoon, in which time had agreed to slow down to the specific small pace of a wadi-pool, and in which no one was responsible for moving anything along. The fire smoked gently. The children kept practicing English without knowing — using the structure they had been taught not because anyone had asked them to, but because the words had entered their mouths and were now coming back out, in small full sentences scattered across the rocks: I am swimming! I am eating! He is sleeping! They are playing!

Saeed was sitting on a rock, watching.

Of course he was. Of course Saeed was the boy who had selected, in this particular paradise of pools and meshkak and joy, the specific small high rock that gave him the specific small full view of everything without requiring him to be in the middle of any of it. The boy whose first English sentence, the previous day, had been I was watching. The boy whose whole life had been organised around this particular position — at the edge, taking it in, missing nothing.

Thuraya went to him.

"What are you doing?"

He smiled shyly.

"I am… thinking."

She smiled back. The specific full quiet teacher-smile of a woman who had recognised, in those two English words, both the truth of Saeed's whole interior life and the small specific fact that he had given her the words for it.

The sun started to go down.

Everyone was tired and happy. They cleaned the place. They packed everything. They moved with the specific small full slow-motion of a village-group that had spent itself on a single excellent day and had nothing left to give but the energy required to drive home.

John looked at the pools one more time.

"This is… one of the best days of my life."

Sophia nodded. "Mine too."

Malood was sleeping inside a bag. Nobody knew how he got there. Nobody questioned it. By this point in the village's relationship with this particular goat, the question of how had been replaced, permanently, by the simpler practice of acceptance.

The pickup turned. The wadi shrank in the rear-view. The pools, which had been there for three days and would be gone in three weeks, glittered one more time and then disappeared behind a rock — and the village, full of meshkak and tired children and the specific full sleep-warmth of Malood's breathing, returned home.


# Palm Twenty-Four — The Camera, the Tools, and the Fastest Goat in Samail



## Scene One — Something Special


There are mornings in a classroom when the air itself becomes the lesson — when something has been brought into the room that the room has not previously contained, and the children, before they have been told what it is, have already begun to lean. The lean comes first. The recognition follows. And by the time the teacher speaks, the lesson has already, in some small invisible way, begun.

The classroom was unusually quiet.

Not because Mansoor was absent. Not because Khalil was sleeping. Both were present. Both were occupying their normal seats. Both were even, on this particular morning, theoretically obeying the rule about not making noise.

The classroom was quiet because John was standing near the blackboard with a very serious face and a very important-looking bag. The specific small theatrical bag-holding of a teacher who had decided that the morning's lesson was going to be staged — that the bag itself was, before any object came out of it, already part of the pedagogy.

Sophia sat next to Thuraya. Watching him with a small smile — the specific small wifely smile of a woman who knew exactly what was about to happen, and who was looking forward, with a small shared private pleasure, to watching it happen.

"Today," John said. Slowly. Proudly. "…we have… something special."

The children leaned forward.

The specific full collective lean of twelve small bodies who had, in the first three syllables of something special, recognised that the lesson was about to be unusual. The lean had its own grammar. It was not the same as the lean of curiosity about a difficult question, nor the lean of attention before an explanation. It was the small specific anticipatory lean reserved for moments when the teacher's voice carried a different weight, and the children, expert readers of teacher-weight, had already understood that today was different.

Mansoor whispered, "Maybe sweets."

Ahmed whispered, "Maybe books."

Khalil whispered, "Maybe trouble."

The specific three small whispers that, together, summarised the three different worldviews of the back row — Mansoor hoped for sugar, Ahmed hoped for knowledge, Khalil simply hoped, with no specifications, the way certain boys hope: vaguely, eternally, ready to celebrate whatever the day produced.

John opened the bag carefully.

And took out a black camera.

The specific small black camera of a 1973 English schoolteacher — a Kodak, perhaps, or a small German box-style camera, with its leather strap, its small front lens, its specific body of stamped metal that had, in the village's eyes, the unmistakable look of a foreign object. It was, perhaps, the first camera that had ever been displayed openly in this specific classroom. The children had seen photographs before — there were photographs in the village, brought back by uncles who had worked in Bahrain or Kuwait, framed photographs of weddings in city studios — but the device that made photographs had until this moment existed only as rumour, as theory, as something that happened in other places to other people.

The class exploded.

"Abooooy!"

"What is that?"

"Does it bite?"

"Is it a radio?"

The four questions arriving simultaneously, from four different small corners of the room, with the specific small full classroom-democracy of a moment in which every child had decided, at the same instant, that their question was the most important.

Sophia laughed. "No, no. This is a camera."

Ali raised his hand. "Teacher… it steals faces?"

John blinked. The specific small startled blink of a man whose own English-language framing of the camera had just collided with a different framing entirely — a framing he had not expected, but which, the more he thought about it, was not unreasonable. The camera did, in some sense, steal faces. The English word for what it did was simply less honest than Ali's.

"No. It… keeps memories."

He lifted it. He looked through it.

CLICK.

The sound alone made half the class jump. The specific full village-classroom jump of children whose ears had, until this moment, been used to softer instruments — chalk, paper, the small wooden complaints of school furniture — and who had now, without warning, been introduced to a small mechanical sound that contained, somewhere in its tiny shutter-mechanism, the entire foreign promise of modernity.

Mansoor shouted, "It shot me!"

Thuraya laughed. "It's not a gun. It's a camera."


## Scene Two — We Use … For …


John wrote on the board:

We use … for …

He turned around. "Repeat: We use…"

The class: "We use…"

He pointed at the camera. "We use a camera for…?"

Ahmed thought carefully. The specific careful Ahmed-thinking of a boy who, before answering, conducted a small internal English audit, comparing the structure on the board against the small inventory of verb-forms he had been quietly compiling in his head since the school year began.

"For… taking pictures."

"Excellent!"

John smiled. He wrote:

We use a camera for taking pictures.

Then Thuraya stood up. She brought a small basket — a small woven palm-leaf basket of the specific shape that Omani households had been weaving, by hand, for centuries. John had seen many of these baskets. He had not understood, until this moment, that he was about to see one used as a teaching aid.

Inside were strange things.

Strange to John. Entirely ordinary to the children.

This was the small specific quiet pedagogy that Thuraya had been planning, perhaps for days. John had brought the foreign object — the camera, the symbol of the world the children did not yet know. Thuraya had brought the local objects — the symbols of the world the children knew so completely that they had perhaps stopped seeing them. And the lesson would weave the two together, so that by the end the children would have used English to describe both the strange foreign and the familiar local, and would have learned, without anyone saying it explicitly, that English was a language capable of carrying their own world as well as someone else's.

She took out a ma'wal. A hoe.

"We use a ma'wal for…?"

Ali said, proudly, "For farming."

Then she took out a heeb. A small sickle.

"And this?"

Noura said, "For cutting grass."

Then came the majz.

Some students laughed. The specific nervous laugh of children who had, all of them, encountered the majz at some point in their short lives — usually in the form of a story their grandmother had told them, sometimes in the form of an actual treatment that they remembered with the specific small clarity of a small physical event that had not been negotiated with.

"We use a majz for… cutting and… treatment," Thuraya said. Carefully. The specific small careful English of a teacher who had decided, in real time, to honour the village practice without overstating its scientific basis. Treatment was a sufficient word. Cure would have been too much.

John nodded. Slowly. "Oman has… very brave medicine."

Then came the mowqa'a. A garlic crusher.

Sophia pressed it. The class laughed.

"We use this for garlic!"

Then a silver kohl stick — the specific small silver mirwad of an Omani woman's collection.

"We use this for… eyes."

Then a marjal. A big pot.

Then a qafeer. A basket.

The board was full:

We use a ma'wal for farming.We use a heeb for cutting grass.We use a majz for cutting.We use a camera for taking pictures.

John was very happy. The specific full quiet teacher-happiness of a man who had watched his lesson plan and his colleague's lesson plan converge into a single coherent classroom moment.

"This is… a perfect lesson."

That was the moment Malood chose to become famous.


## Scene Three — The Fastest Goat in Samail


Nobody knew how.

Nobody saw how.

But suddenly — the camera was not in John's hand.

It was in Malood's mouth.

The specific magical small disappearance-trick of a goat who had, over months in this classroom, perfected the small art of being beneath everyone's awareness exactly when the most valuable object in the room was within his reach. Sophia would later swear she had been looking at the camera. Thuraya would later swear she had been looking at Malood. The two facts, somehow, had not overlapped. There was, somewhere in the small specific spatial geometry of the classroom that morning, an invisible window of half a second in which both pairs of teacher-eyes had been pointed elsewhere — and through that window, with the specific full surgical precision of a creature who had been waiting his whole life for exactly this opportunity, Malood had executed the most ambitious theft of his career.

Malood turned.

Malood ran.

Sophia screamed. "THE CAMERA!"

John shouted. "STOP THAT GOAT!"

The class exploded into chaos.

Mansoor ran first.

Then Khalil.

Then Ali.

Then half the class.

Malood ran. Like a champion. The specific full focused running of a small white goat who had identified, with absolute clarity, that he was carrying the most valuable object he had ever stolen and that the entire human population of the building was now organised around recovering it from him.

He jumped over a bag.

He passed under a table.

He escaped through the door.

Everyone followed.

In the yard, Malood ran in circles. The specific zigzag goat-circles of a small thief who had calculated, correctly, that a moving target was harder to catch than a straight one — and who was, in addition, enjoying himself.

John ran after him. Holding his bag.

Sophia ran after John.

Thuraya ran after Sophia.

Ahmed ran after everyone — still holding a notebook. The specific small administrative running of a boy who had, even mid-chase, refused to abandon his classroom equipment.

And then…

A hero appeared.

Sadou.

Sadou the shop owner.

He was passing by. Carrying a box.

He saw the goat.

He saw the camera.

He understood everything in one second.

The specific full one-second comprehension of a man who had spent his entire life as Sadou the shop owner, in this specific village, with this specific goat, and who had developed — through long observation — the capacity to read any situation involving Malood with the speed of a man reading his own handwriting. He did not need explanation. He did not need context. The goat had something. The teachers wanted it back. He was the available adult. The mathematics of the situation resolved itself in his head in the time it took him to put down his box.

He put the box down.

He opened his arms.

Malood ran straight into him.

Sadou caught him like a football — the specific full triumphant football-catch of a man who had performed exactly this manoeuvre, with exactly this goat, on at least three previous occasions and had developed, by this point, the specific technique it required.

The yard clapped.

John arrived. Breathing like he had run from Britain.

He took the camera. He hugged it.

"Thank you, Sadou. You saved my memories."

Sadou smiled. Proudly.

"Malood always want be star."

The class laughed — the specific full village-laughter of children who had recognised, in Sadou's small careful English sentence, that the goat's whole life had finally been named.


## Scene Four — Many Pictures


That day, John took many pictures.

The students.

The tools.

Malood.

Mansoor jumping.

Khalil making faces.

Ahmed fixing his hair.

Even Thuraya, secretly.

The specific small secret photograph of a teacher he had, by now, come to know well — the photograph he took without her permission, with the specific small thief-photographer's instinct that all good photographers possessed, when she was looking somewhere else, when her face was caught at a small angle that revealed something true about her. The photograph he would, when developed, hang on a small hook in their kitchen, and which, fifty years later, would be one of the few surviving photographs of Thuraya at the age of forty-two, in her clean white scarf, watching the small troubles of her village classroom with the specific patient amused eye of the person who had, in all of John's life, taught him most about a country.


## Scene Five — The Darkroom


That evening, in the house, the lights were low.

The windows were closed.

The room was dark.

John was very serious.

"This," he said, "is the darkroom."

Sophia held a paper. Carefully.

Thuraya watched. With curiosity. The specific small full curiosity of a woman who had, until this evening, only ever encountered photographs as finished objects, and who was now being shown the specific small alchemical process by which a chemical reaction produced an image.

They put the paper in liquid.

Nothing.

The specific patient nothing of a developing tray in its first ten seconds — when the chemistry was, invisibly, beginning to act on the silver halide crystals embedded in the paper, but had not yet produced any visible image. The specific holding-of-breath that all photographers knew, in those years before digital, when every photograph had to be waited for. Thuraya had not understood, until that exact moment, that photography was a slower process than she had imagined. The image was being delivered by chemistry. Chemistry took its time.

Then slowly…

A face appeared.

Sophia gasped. "It's Mansoor!"

Then another.

"It's Malood!"

Then another.

"It's you!" Thuraya laughed.

They hung the photos. To dry.

The wall was full of memories.

John sat down. Tired but happy.

"These," he said, "are our days."

Sophia smiled. Softly. "And we almost lost them to a goat."

From outside, they heard:

"Me-e-e-e!"

John closed his eyes. "That goat will be in every picture."


## Scene Six — A Wall of Days


They hung the photos one by one along the small string they had tied between two nails on the kitchen wall.

The specific small careful hanging of a photo-line — each photograph attached with a small wooden clothes-peg, each one slightly damp still, each one carrying its own specific small story. The first photograph: Mansoor mid-jump, both feet off the ground, his face the specific full unrestrained joy that was, in that village, his trademark. The second: Malood, with the camera in his mouth, looking at John with what appeared to be — though it could not have been — satisfaction. The third: Sadou holding Malood like a football, the specific full triumphant face of a man who had been, for one minute, a hero. The fourth: Ahmed in his careful straight ruler-posture. The fifth: Khalil making a face. The sixth: Noura pointing at something the camera had not captured, her finger frozen at the specific exact angle of a girl whose hand had been her whole life faster than her thoughts. The seventh: Saeed. In the corner. Watching.

Thuraya stopped at the seventh photograph.

She looked at it. For a long moment. The specific full long teacher-look of a woman who had, in that single small image, recognised, in advance of anyone else, the small future of a small boy.

"Saeed," she said, quietly.

Sophia nodded.

"He is in many of them," she said. "Always at the side. Always watching."

Thuraya smiled. The specific small full smile of a woman who had, for the third time that month, witnessed the small private satisfaction of seeing her quietest student claim a small permanent place in the village's photographic record.

"He is collecting," she said, "again. But this time… he is collecting in the picture, not from outside it."

John, drying his hands on a small cloth, came over.

He looked at the photograph of Saeed. He thought for a moment. Then he said — quietly, in the specific small reflective voice he used when he had just understood something he had not, until that moment, fully grasped —

"In the next pictures," he said, "he will be in the middle. Not the side."

Sophia smiled. "You think?"

"I will make sure," John said.

The specific small full husband-promise of a man who had, after a tiring photographic day and a small darkroom revelation, decided that one of his small future projects in this village was going to be the deliberate, careful documentation of the specific shy boy who had, for too long, been at the edges.

Outside, in the small yard, Malood bleated once.

Then went back to whatever Malood went back to.

The wall of photographs glowed quietly in the small yellow lamplight of the kitchen. And John, looking at it, understood — with a small specific clarity that he would carry with him for the rest of his life — that he had, that day, taken the first photographs of a place that would, slowly, become his.


# Palm Twenty-Five — The Day Nobody Spoke Arabic



## Scene One — The Tired Fan and the Important Envelope


The classroom was louder than usual.

Not because the students were talking.

Because the fan was.

It stood in the corner. Shaking. The specific small full-body shake of an old standing fan whose internal balance had, after several years of Samaili use, drifted slightly out of true — so that every rotation produced a small wobble, and the metal cage rattled against the small frame, and the whole machine sounded like it had a fever. rrrrrrrrrrr… rrrrrrrr… The specific tired motor-sound of a fan that had been working since dawn and was, by now, in the specific small middle-aged afternoon of its electrical life. The students no longer registered it consciously. It had been the soundtrack of their classroom for years. But on this particular morning, in the small specific quiet that was about to be required, the fan would emerge — briefly, accidentally — as the loudest thing in the room.

John stood near his desk. Holding a big envelope — like it was a treasure chest. The specific small careful holding of a brown manila envelope that had been, the previous evening, filled with the freshly-developed photographs from the kitchen darkroom, and which now contained, in its quiet manila weight, the entire visual history of the previous month.

Sophia stood next to him. Trying very hard not to smile.

The specific small wifely effort of a woman who knew exactly what was about to happen — who had, in fact, helped John design the rule the previous evening — and who was looking forward, with the specific contained pleasure of a teacher waiting for her own joke to land, to watching it.

Thuraya leaned against the wall. Arms crossed. Watching the students' faces.

All the children were sitting… very… very… straight.

The specific full upright posture of twelve small bodies who had registered, in the specific solemn way John was holding the envelope, that this lesson was — somehow — already beginning, even though no one had yet announced its beginning.

Malood was also in the classroom.

Nobody knew how he had entered.

Nobody questioned it anymore.

Malood sat near the window. Like a student who had failed many years but never left school.

The specific small permanent classroom-presence of a small white animal who had, by the unstated agreement of the entire school staff, been quietly accepted as a non-paying auditor. The principle had been settled long ago. The principle was no longer subject to debate.

John cleared his throat.

"Today," he said, slowly, "is a very special day."

The students looked at the envelope.

Mansoor whispered, "Pictures?"

John raised one finger. "Yes. But… there is a rule."

Everyone leaned forward.


## Scene Two — English Only


"No Arabic," John said. Seriously. "Only English. If anyone speaks Arabic…"

He pointed at the door.

"…outside."

The classroom froze.

The specific full chamber-of-frozen-bodies stillness of twelve small Omani children who had just, in one short rule, been told that the specific language of their grandmothers, of their food, of their mothers' bedtime songs, of the entire dense domestic life of every small body in the room, was, for the next forty minutes, illegal. They had been asked to do many strange things by these foreign teachers. They had been asked to repeat words that had no meaning to them. They had been asked to write letters that ran in the wrong direction. But they had never been asked to refuse their own language. The rule landed in the room with the particular small weight of a rule that no one had previously imposed on these particular small mouths.

Ali swallowed.

Noura put her hand over her mouth — the specific small instinctive defensive cover of a girl whose mouth was, by long habit, faster than her thinking, and who had decided, in the half-second since the rule, to install a small physical barrier between her lips and her native language.

Khalil looked like he was preparing for war.

Ahmed sat straight like a ruler. Ready. The specific full focused readiness of a boy who had, in fact, been waiting for exactly this kind of challenge — the kind that rewarded careful preparation.

Saeed was quiet. Watching everything carefully.

Matla smiled. Confidently. The specific small confident smile of a girl whose private opinion was that she would, of course, be the first to obey the rule successfully.

Rahma and Salma looked at each other. Nervous.

Marwa whispered, "Oh no."

John wrote on the board. In big letters:

ENGLISH ONLY TODAY

Then he added:

This is…  /  He is…  /  She is…

Sophia clapped her hands. "Okay! Who wants to start?"

Silence.

Even Malood was silent.

John opened the envelope. He took out the first photo.

He lifted it.

It was Malood.

Malood in the yard. Malood on the table. Malood in front of the door. Malood eating. Malood sleeping.

Malood was everywhere.

The class exploded with laughter.

John smiled. "Okay. Who?"

Mansoor jumped up. "Me! Me!"

John nodded. "English."

Mansoor took a deep breath. The specific full preparatory deep-breath of a small boy who was, for the first time, about to attempt a complete English sentence under specific external pressure.

"This is… Malood."

John clapped. "Good! Very good!"

Mansoor pointed at the picture. "He is… he is… eating!"

Sophia added, "Yes! He is eating."

Malood looked proud.


## Scene Three — Smoke, Maghbar, and the First Slip


The next photo.

It was Sheikh Khalaf.

He was standing near his clay house. Throwing smoke in the air. Doing maghbar.

Some students gasped. The specific small village-children's gasp of a class whose collective childhood folklore had assigned Sheikh Khalaf a specific small mythic place, and who had now been asked to look at a photograph of him performing a ritual that, according to all the stories they had heard, was supposed to be private.

Ali whispered. In Arabic.


> «هوه…»


John pointed. "Outside."

Ali froze. The specific full small-boy frozen face of a student who had just, in two syllables of his own native language, lost the rule. The specific small full self-betrayal of a child whose mouth had moved before his mind had remembered.

"No no no! Sorry! Sorry!"

He tried again. Slowly. "This is… Sheikh Khalaf."

Thuraya smiled. "Good."

Khalil added, "He is… making smoke."

John nodded. "Yes. He is making smoke."

Sophia explained — "In English: He is doing something. He is making something."

Thuraya added — first in Arabic for the children, then translating for John — "They used to do this to chase bad luck."

The next photo.

John.

His eyes were red and swollen. Dust on his face. Looking like a very tired ghost.

The class laughed again. The specific full village-classroom laugh of children whose teacher had, in one photograph, been reduced to a recognisable subspecies of comedy.

Noura raised her hand. "Teacher… English."

She stood. "This is… Mr. John."

She pointed at his eyes. "He is… not happy."

The class laughed more.

John sighed. "He was not happy."

Ahmed corrected — politely, the specific full polite correction of a boy who had been waiting all morning for a chance to use his good grammar — "He was not happy."

John looked at him. "Thank you, Ahmed."


## Scene Four — Yellow Ladies and the Door


The next photo.

Safiya and Fatkat.

Both of them.

Yellow.

Very yellow.

Like lemons.

The specific small full saffron-mask catastrophe of a previous afternoon was now permanently preserved on photographic paper, hanging in John's hand for the entire classroom to inspect. Sophia would, in later years, when she became a real Muslim called Safiya not just by the village but by herself, look back at this photograph and laugh — but in this specific moment, in this specific classroom, the photograph was still raw, and so was the indignity it documented.

Fatkat's mouth was open. In shock.

Safiya was trying to smile.

The class fell on the floor laughing.

Marwa stood up. "This is… Miss Sophia and Miss Fatkat."

She thought. The specific careful Marwa-thinking of a girl who had, in the half-second of consideration, decided that the safest and most accurate description was the most direct.

"They are… yellow."

Fatkat — who was standing near the door — shouted in Arabic:


> «يا شيخة فضيحة!»


(Oh dear, what a scandal!)

Safiya laughed. "Skin care."

Thuraya added, laughing — "But the skin doesn't care."

Even Mohammadeen — passing by the door — laughed and said in his Egyptian Arabic:


> «والله عاملين زي الليمون.»


(I swear they look just like lemons.)

The next photos.

One by one.

Ali holding a fish.

Khalil making a silly face.

Matla standing like a queen.

Rahma and Salma together. Shy.

Marwa falling near the falaj.

Noura pointing at something and shouting.

Saeed in the corner of many photos.

Watching.

Always watching.

Ahmed helping others.

Mansoor… in almost every picture… running.


## Scene Five — A Whole Class in English


Each student stood. Each student said something.

"This is Ali.""He is holding a fish."

"This is Matla.""She is standing."

"This is Khalil.""He is… uh… funny."

Sometimes they made mistakes. Sometimes they stopped and thought. Sometimes Ahmed helped. Sometimes Thuraya helped.

But nobody spoke Arabic.

Not even Malood.

Malood only made a "meeeeh" sound. The specific small low-volume goat-vocalisation that did not, in the strictest grammatical sense, qualify as either Arabic or English, and which therefore remained, throughout the lesson, the only legal non-English utterance in the room.

Then John showed a photo.

It was Sadoo. Holding Malood. Like a hero.

Safiya said, "This is Sadoo. He is saving the camera."

John nodded. "Yes. Very brave."

Then one more photo.

It was taken secretly.

The class in the shop.

The class in the street.

The class playing.

The class running.

The students stared.

The specific full collective stare of children who had just realised, with a small mixed feeling of pleasure and small indignation, that they had been being photographed, secretly, for some time. The teacher, who had introduced the camera to them as a tool for taking pictures, had — without telling them — been taking pictures all along.

Matla asked, in English, slowly, "Teacher… when?"

Thuraya smiled. "He took them secretly."

The class looked at John.

John pretended to be innocent.

"I am a photographer," he said. Proudly.


## Scene Six — The Bell, the Tunisian, and Saeed's Smile


The bell rang.

Drrrrrrrrrriiiiiiing!

Everyone jumped.

Mansoor shouted — without thinking, the specific instinctive Arabic exclamation of a boy whose excitement had, in the half-second after the bell, broken every rule of the morning —


> «طلاااقة!»


(Tlaaagah! — A shot/discharge!)

Rabaa — the Tunisian music teacher, who happened to be passing by — screamed.


> «يا ربّ! من ضرب؟!»


(Ya Rab! Who shot?!)

Thuraya laughed. "Not gun. School finish."

Rabaa held her heart — the specific full heart-grasping of a Tunisian woman whose nerves had, in the moment of Mansoor's mistranslation, registered a real urban-emergency. "You scared me!"

The students ran out.

Shouting. Laughing. Singing — the specific small Omani schoolyard song that Safiya would, by now, have heard half a dozen times that month —


> «يا سايقنا دوس دوسالله يبعث لك عروس!»


(Ya saeeegna doos doos / Allah yeb'ath lak arooos! — Hey driver, press, press / May God send you a bride!)

The specific full village-children's running-song that had, somewhere in the last several decades, been composed by some unknown small mouth and which had, since then, been sung by every generation of small Omani children running out of school — passed down without a teacher, without a textbook, with the small specific cultural precision of children who learned by hearing and who never forgot.

John sat on his chair. Tired but happy.

Safiya sat next to him. "Today… they spoke English."

Thuraya smiled. "And nobody died."

Malood jumped on the desk. He tried to eat a photo.

John shouted. "No!"

Malood ran.

Everyone laughed.

Outside, Saeed stood for a moment.

The specific small standing-still of a quiet boy who had, throughout the morning, been part of the lesson — who had, when his turn had come, said one of his careful small English sentences ("This is Saeed. He is watching.") — and who was, now, in the doorway, taking a small last look back. Looking at the classroom. Looking at the photos still spread across John's desk. Looking at Thuraya, who had, throughout the lesson, given him exactly the small attention he needed — not too much, not too little, the specific calibrated amount of attention that, in his nine-year-old life, had only ever been calibrated for him by her.

Then he smiled.

The specific small private smile of a boy whose private internal records had, that morning, registered the specific moment when he had — for the first time — not been at the side of a photograph, but in the middle of it.

And ran after the others.


# Palm Twenty-Six — The Detective Game and the Missing Treasure



## Scene One — When Thuraya Smiles Like That


The classroom was unusually quiet.

Not because the students were well-behaved. No. That almost never happened.

It was quiet because Ms. Thuraya was smiling.

And when Ms. Thuraya smiled like that, something dangerous was coming.

The specific small Thuraya-smile of which the village had — over years — learned to be careful: not the open warm smile she gave to grandmothers at the falaj, not the small teacher-smile she used for grammar corrections, but the third, more conspiratorial smile that meant: I have, in the last twelve hours, designed a small game, and I am about to release it onto the world. The students had learned to read this smile the way village children learn to read weather. The smile predicted the climate of the next forty minutes. And that climate, today, was going to involve effort.

John was standing near the blackboard, holding a piece of chalk. Safiya was sitting at the small teacher's desk, fixing her notebook. Mohammadeen was leaning against the wall with his arms crossed. Fatkat was fanning herself with a book.

Malood, the baby goat, was sitting near the window, chewing something that nobody wanted to know the name of.

Thuraya clapped her hands once.

"Today," she said, in English, "we will not sit and write."

The students' eyes lit up.

Mansoor almost fell from his chair. "Teacher… we go home?"

John laughed. "No, Mansoor. Worse."

The class groaned.

Thuraya continued. "Today, we play a game."

Now the class exploded.

"A game?" Noura said.

"A real game?" Marwa asked.

"A running game?" Mansoor shouted.

Thuraya raised her hand. "A detective game."

She went to her bag. She took out a big, old-looking paper. She opened it carefully — the specific small ceremonial unfolding of a paper that had been, the previous evening, drawn with deliberate care by a teacher who had decided to give the village a small adventure.

It was a map.

"This," she said, "is the map of today's mystery."

Safiya leaned forward. "Oh, I like this already."

John looked at the map. Seriously. "Is there… danger?"

Thuraya smiled innocently. "Always."


## Scene Two — The Treasure That Is Hidden


The students came closer. Even Ahmed left his perfect straight sitting position and leaned forward — the specific small full Ahmed-lean of a boy whose normal posture did not permit such concessions, but whose curiosity had, on this occasion, overruled his discipline.

"Somewhere," Thuraya said, pointing to a drawing of a tall palm tree, "there is a treasure."

"Treasure?" Khalil whispered. Dramatically.

"Yes. But it is missing."

Mansoor stood up. "Who stole it?"

Thuraya looked at Malood.

Malood blinked.

Everyone laughed — the specific full classroom-laugh of children who had immediately understood the joke without needing it explained.

"No," Thuraya said. "The treasure is not stolen. It is hidden. And you must find it."

John clapped his hands. "Okay! Today's lesson is instructions."

He wrote on the board:

Go straightTurn leftTurn rightStopDon't runLook under…Look behind…

Safiya added, "And remember: Only English."

The students looked at each other. In fear. The specific small full village-children's fear of children who had, just yesterday, lived through the small specific torture of an English-only morning, and who had not, until this moment, prepared themselves for a sequel.

Mohammadeen lifted his stick. Slowly. "Today… I also speak only English."

The class went silent — the specific small full Egyptian-shock-silence of a class whose Egyptian Arabic teacher, who was technically not even supposed to be participating in this lesson, had just announced his alignment with the rule.

Fatkat smiled. Sweetly. "Good luck, my children."

Thuraya divided them into two teams.

"Team One," she said, "the boys."

The boys cheered.

"Team Two, the girls."

The girls cheered louder. The specific full village-girls'-cheer of a group that had, by long experience, calibrated their volume specifically to be slightly louder than the boys' cheer, regardless of how loud the boys had been.

"Team One leader," Thuraya said, "is Mr. John and Mr. Mohammadeen."

John saluted. Mohammadeen nodded seriously.

"Team Two leader is Safiya and Fatkat."

Safiya smiled proudly. Fatkat cracked her knuckles — the specific small full Fatkat-preparation of an Omani auntie who had decided that this game was going to be won by her team and was beginning her preparations accordingly.

"And Malood," Thuraya added, "is with me. For safety."

Malood made a small sound that meant: I will still cause problems.


## Scene Three — The Old Wall and the Falaj


They went outside.

The village was warm. The sun was high. The palm trees were quiet — like they were watching the show. The specific small full afternoon-stillness of an Omani village whose adults were inside, whose children had been let out into the lanes, and whose ancient slow palms had, by long custom, settled into the specific small spectator-position of trees that had seen many generations of village children play many generations of village games. The trees were not surprised. The trees, over the centuries, had stopped being surprised by anything.

Thuraya gave each team a paper.

"Follow the instructions," she said. "Don't cheat. Don't fight. And don't die."

John looked worried. "Is that last one part of the rules?"

"Yes," Thuraya said.

The game started.

Team One ran.

"No running!" Mohammadeen shouted.

They walked fast instead. The specific small village-boys'-fast-walking of children who had been told they could not run but had not been told they could not approximate running with their entire bodies.

Ahmed read the paper. "Go straight. Stop at the old wall. Look under the stone."

Mansoor jumped. "I found it!"

It was another paper.

Khalil read it. "Turn right. Walk ten steps. Look behind the tree."

Meanwhile, Team Two was also moving.

Safiya was reading. "Go left. Stop near the falaj. Don't touch the water."

Fatkat laughed. "Why always don't touch, don't touch?"

They found their next paper near a rock.

Marwa read slowly. "Go… straight… until… you see… a house."

Noura pointed. "That one?"

But when they arrived, everyone stopped.

It was the house of Shayb Khalaf.

The air suddenly felt… colder. The specific small village-shudder of a group that had walked, by following a series of innocent English instructions, into the small specific cold-zone that surrounded the home of the village's most folkloric inhabitant.

"Why here?" Salima whispered.

Fatkat swallowed. "Who made this map?"

Thuraya, far away, was pretending not to see anything — the specific small full innocent-teacher-pretence of a woman who had, in fact, drawn the map deliberately, with the knowledge that this small village-cold would be part of the lesson.

The door of the house was closed.

"Look under the tree," Safiya said.

They ran. Found the paper. Ran away. Very fast. Nobody wanted to stay there.


## Scene Four — The Pickup and the Bees


Team One arrived near Uncle Nasser's pickup.

He was sitting and drinking tea. The specific calm tea-drinking of Uncle Nasser on any village afternoon — the same tea, the same pickup, the same small specific tin-cup whose handle had been bent slightly to fit his particular grip.

"What are you doing?" he asked.

John smiled. "Learning English."

Uncle Nasser nodded. Like that explained everything. The specific small full village-uncle-nod of a man who had decided long ago that the specific activities of the foreign teachers were to be accepted without examination.

The paper said: "Ask the driver. Then go behind the fence."

Mohammadeen tried. "Please… sir… where is… the fence?"

Uncle Nasser laughed. "Behind you, ya ustadh."

They went, and found another paper.

Team Two arrived near a big tree.

They heard a sound.

Bzzzzzz.

Fatkat stopped. "No. No. No. I know this sound."

Safiya read the paper. "Look near the tree."

The girls looked at each other.

Mansoor's voice came from far away. "Maybe it is empty!"

Then they heard him scream.

"BEEEEES!"

Everyone ran.

The bees stayed where they were.

Thuraya wrote something in her notebook: Good drama.


## Scene Five — Palm Al-Fard


Both teams arrived near the big palm tree called " data-m="Arabic: a single, individual palm tree; here, the specific tall solitary palm that the villagers had named for being the most prominent in this stretch of land" data-s="lone-palm, single-palm" data-x="Palm Al-Fard was the village landmark. Tall, solitary, taller than its neighbours.">Palm Al-Fard.

The specific full single solitary palm whose name in Arabic — al-fard — meant the only one. It stood at the edge of one of the larger palm farms, taller than its neighbours by a clear margin, with the small specific authority of a tree that had decided long ago that it was going to outgrow every palm around it. Generations of village children had used it as a landmark. Generations of village adults had given directions in relation to it. Past Palm Al-Fard. Before Palm Al-Fard. Just at Palm Al-Fard. The specific small village-cartography of a place that had learned, before any modern map existed, how to organise itself around a single tall tree.

Team Two arrived first.

Noura looked up. "There!"

High. Very high. Was a small bag.

Fatkat clapped. "We win!"

Safiya looked worried. "How do we get it?"

They waited.

Team One arrived.

Khalil looked up. "It's too high."

John scratched his head. "We need a hero."

Thuraya walked slowly to them.

"So," she said, "you found it. But…"

"But?" Mansoor asked.

"But the game is not finished."

She smiled. "The treasure is there. But how will you get it?"

Everyone looked up.

Very high. Too high.

Inside the bag, something moved.

Mansoor whispered, "Maybe it is a snake."

John stepped back. "Then the game is finished."

Thuraya laughed. "No, no. It is food."

Safiya looked at Fatkat. "Food?"

Thuraya nodded. "Honey. And ghee."

The students cheered.

Mohammadeen rubbed his hands. "Healthy food."


## Scene Six — Who Will Climb?


John looked at the tree. "We need… someone who can climb."

Everyone slowly turned.

Toward one direction.

The road.

Footsteps.

A voice.

"Who needs help?"

Sadou was standing there. Carrying a bag. Smiling like he knew everything.

The specific small full timing of Sadou's arrival was, in retrospect, suspicious — the kind of timing that, in a different village, might have prompted the question of whether Thuraya had perhaps mentioned the location of Palm Al-Fard to him in advance. But Sadou was always passing by. Sadou was always carrying a bag. Sadou always knew everything. The question of how he had appeared at exactly this moment was, by the village's long experience of Sadou, no longer a question worth asking.

Thuraya closed her notebook. She looked at both teams.

"Tomorrow," she said, "we will continue."

The students shouted. "Tomorrow?!"

"Yes," Thuraya said. "Because tomorrow… you will learn how to ask and promise."

She looked at Sadou. "And maybe… how to convince."

Sadou smiled bigger.

Malood suddenly ran to the tree and tried to climb.

He failed.

Everyone laughed.

John looked at the tree, then at Sadou, then at the class.

"Tomorrow," he said seriously, "will be dangerous."

Far away, from the shade of another tree, a thin shadow moved. Someone was watching. Someone old. Someone quiet. The door of Shayb Khalaf's house closed slowly. The specific small full village-mystery of a man who had, throughout this small game, been watching from a distance — and who, by the specific small careful closing of his door, had now signalled that whatever opinion he held of the foreign teachers' games, he had decided not to share it. Not yet. Not today.


# Palm Twenty-Seven — Convincing Sadou



## Scene One — I Will


The next morning, everyone arrived at school early.

Too early.

Even Ahmed.

Even Mansoor.

Even Malood.

The reason was simple.

The treasure.

Honey and ghee were still hanging on Palm Al-Fard. Like a royal secret that refused to come down. The specific small unmoving stillness of a knotted bag at fifteen metres altitude — visible from the school yard, visible from the path, visible from every small angle of the village — and yet, against the laws of childhood, untouched. The whole village had spent the night thinking about that bag. Some of the children had dreamed about it. Mansoor had, by his own later admission, woken up at three in the morning to check that it was still there, and had then walked back to bed with the specific full disappointment of a boy who had hoped, briefly, that gravity might have done his work for him.

John stood in front of the class and wrote in big letters:

I WILL

He turned around. "Today, we learn how to promise."

Safiya added, "And how to ask nicely."

Mohammadeen crossed his arms. "And how to not fight."

Fatkat smiled. "And how to not cry."

Mansoor looked worried. The specific small full Mansoor-worry of a boy who had identified, in the four announcements, four different categories of behaviour at which he was simultaneously expert.

Thuraya clapped her hands. "Today's hero is… Sadou."

The students cheered.

Outside, Sadou was sitting in the shade. Fixing something with a small knife. Looking very proud of himself.

The specific small full Sadou-pride of a man who had, the previous afternoon, been informed by Thuraya herself that the entire next school day would centre, in some specific small way, on him. He had not asked for the role. He had not negotiated for it. But he had, with the particular small village-confidence of a man whose specific physical talent was finally going to be officially recognised by the foreign teachers, accepted it. Malood was standing near him — staring at him like he was planning a crime, the specific small full goat-stare of a creature who had detected, in Sadou's posture, an opponent worth observing closely.


## Scene Two — The Refusal


Thuraya took the class outside.

Sadou looked up. "Ah. The English army."

John smiled. "Good morning, Sadou."

Sadou nodded. "Morning… mister teacher… mister long."

The students laughed. The specific full village-children's laugh of a class that had been waiting, since they first met him, for someone to officially comment on John's height — and which had decided, immediately, that mister long was now John's village name.

John pointed to the palm tree. "We need your help."

Sadou looked up. "Aha. The bag."

Safiya stepped forward. "Inside the bag is honey and ghee."

Sadou's eyes became a little brighter. The specific small full village-shopkeeper's-eye-brightening of a man who had immediately calculated the value, in his shop's small economy, of the specific objects being described.

Fatkat whispered, "He is interested."

Thuraya said, "But Sadou, we cannot climb."

Sadou crossed his arms. "And?"

John took a deep breath. "We want you to climb and get it."

Sadou looked at the class. Then at the tree. Then at Malood.

"No."

Everyone froze.

"No?" Mansoor shouted. "Why no?!"

Sadou smiled. "Because."

The specific small full village-shopkeeper's-because of a man who had decided that this morning's negotiations were not going to begin in his favour, and who was — with the small specific calm enjoyment of a person who has the upper hand — going to make the children work for it.

Khalil whispered, "He is enjoying this."

Thuraya smiled. Slowly. The specific full slow teacher-smile of a woman who had recognised, in Sadou's deliberate refusal, the small specific opportunity she had been waiting for.

"Okay. Let's begin the lesson."

She turned to the students. "Remember the sentence?"

The students shouted. Together. "I will!"

John nodded. "Good. Now… we try."


## Scene Three — The Great Persuasion


Noura was the first.

She walked to Sadou. She said carefully, "Sadou… I will give you samboosa."

Sadou didn't even look at her. "No."

The girls gasped.

Ali tried next. "Sadou… I will give you bakoorah."

Sadou shook his head. "No."

Marwa said, "I will give you dates."

"No."

Rahma tried. "I will give you bread."

"No."

Salima said, "I will give you tea."

Sadou smiled. "I don't like tea."

The boys started.

Khalil said, "I will give you fish."

Sadou laughed. "No."

Ahmed said politely, "I will help you in the shop."

Sadou looked at him. "For how long?"

Ahmed froze. The specific small full Ahmed-freeze of a boy whose careful planning had not extended to a follow-up question. "One… day?"

Sadou shook his head. "No."

Mansoor jumped forward. "I will give you Malood!"

Everyone screamed.

Malood screamed louder.

Thuraya shouted. "NO!"

Sadou laughed so hard he almost fell. "I don't want this trouble."

Malood looked offended.

Safiya sighed. "Nothing works."

Mohammadeen said, "In Egypt, we solve this with shouting."

Fatkat nodded. "And sometimes shoes."


## Scene Four — Saeed's Whisper


John was thinking. Very hard.

He looked at the class.

Then at Sadou.

Then at the tree.

Then at Saeed.

Saeed was standing quietly. Watching everything. As he always did. As his whole life had been organised around doing. The specific small full Saeed-watching of a boy whose silence was, in that moment, the most attentive silence in the entire school yard. John had, by this point in the term, learned to recognise the small specific quality of Saeed's silence — the way you learn to read the silence of a person who knows things they have not yet said. Saeed had been watching Sadou his whole life. Saeed, by his own private long observation, knew what Sadou wanted.

John walked to him. He whispered something in his ear.

Saeed's eyes became wide.

He smiled. Slowly.

Then he walked to Sadou.

Everyone became quiet.

Saeed looked up. He said, in his simple English:

"Sadou… I will give you… chilli. Very hot."

The class froze.

Thuraya corrected quickly. "Chilli, not filfil yohroak."

Everyone laughed.

Sadou's eyes opened wide. "Chilli?"

Saeed nodded. Seriously. "Yes. Big chilli. Very strong."

Sadou's face changed.

A slow, dangerous smile appeared. The specific small full chilli-lover's smile of a man whose entire dietary identity revolved around the search for the strongest chilli available, and who had just been informed that an unexpected source had been identified.

"You have chilli?"

Saeed nodded.

Sadou stood up. "Why you not say this from beginning?"

The class exploded.

"He agreed!"

"He agreed!"

"We win!"

But Fatkat shouted, "Wait! Which team wins?"

The girls crossed their arms.

The boys crossed their arms.

Thuraya raised her hand. "Both teams found the treasure. But…"

She smiled.

"The boys convinced Sadou."

The girls groaned.

Mansoor danced.


## Scene Five — The Great Climb


Sadou tied his cloth tighter around his waist.

He looked at the palm tree.

He cracked his fingers.

The specific small full village-climber's preparation of a man who had, since he was nine years old, been the boy his uncle sent up the tallest palm in the family farm. The specific small full Omani body-knowledge of how to climb a palm — feet flat against the trunk, hips outward, knees bent, the small rhythmic up-and-up of a person who had decided long ago that gravity was a negotiable proposition. The specific physical literacy of an entire generation of village men who had, by long custom, made themselves the architects of the palm-canopy.

Then he started climbing. Like a cat.

Up. Up. Up.

The students looked. With open mouths.

Safiya whispered, "He is not human."

John whispered, "He is… many talents."

In one minute, Sadou reached the top.

He took the bag.

He looked down. "Ready?"

"YES!" everyone shouted.

He threw the bag.

Mohammadeen tried to catch it. He missed.

Fatkat tried. She missed.

Malood jumped.

The bag hit Malood.

Malood fell.

The bag opened.

The honey jar rolled.

The ghee jar rolled.

Everyone screamed.

John ran.

Thuraya ran.

Mansoor ran.

Somehow, nothing broke.

They all sat on the ground. Breathing like they ran a marathon.

Safiya looked at the jars. "We are alive."


## Scene Six — Healthy Food — and a Small Watching Shadow


They went back to school.

In the classroom, Thuraya put the jars on the table.

"Today's lesson," she said, "is Healthy Food."

John wrote on the board:

Honey is healthy.Ghee is healthy.Too much sugar is not healthy.

Mansoor raised his hand. "Teacher… honey and ghee… together?"

Fatkat laughed. "That is not a lesson. That is a crime."

They made small bread and tasted a little.

John closed his eyes. "Now I understand… why Omani men have big belly."

Thuraya shouted, "It's good for wearing khanjar!"

Safiya laughed. "What is khanjar?"

Everyone started talking at the same time. The specific full village-classroom-cacophony of a moment in which the lesson had finished and the social hour had begun, with the small specific quality that no one was now in charge.

Sadou stood at the door.

Saeed gave him a small chilli.

Sadou smiled.

Then he bit it.

Then he screamed.

Then he ran.

Everyone laughed — the specific full village-classroom laugh of a small audience who had, in the last forty minutes, watched their grown-up shopkeeper-neighbour go from refusing all bribes to climbing a fifteen-metre palm tree to running away from a small village-chilli, and who now, in the small specific completed-arc of the morning's narrative, were laughing in the way one laughs when a story has ended exactly as it should have.

In the afternoon, when the class was empty, Thuraya wrote in her notebook:

"Today, they learned how to promise.And how to convince.And how to work together."

Far away, near the old house, a shadow watched.

Shayb Khalaf.

The specific small full shadow-watching of a man who had, throughout the morning's English-language adventure, observed from the shade of his clay-house with the specific full quiet patience of a person who had decided that whatever he understood about these foreign teachers' games — and he understood more than they suspected — he was going to keep, for now, to himself.


# Palm Twenty-Eight — We Must and We Mustn't — The Confession Lesson



## Scene One — The Punching Sun


The sun in Samail did not shine. It punched.

It was the kind of heat that made even the palm leaves look tired. The school yard felt quiet, but not because the children were calm. It was quiet because everyone was trying to save their energy for later — like camels storing water. The specific small village-quiet of an Omani late-morning when the body's preferred response to the temperature was the small specific stillness of a creature that had decided, by long evolutionary practice, that motion was for cooler hours.

Mr. John walked slowly across the yard. Holding a small chalkboard. His face was serious — like he was carrying something very important. The specific small full slow-walking of a foreign teacher whose body had, after several months in this heat, stopped fighting it and had begun, instead, to negotiate with it. He moved at the speed Omani men moved at this hour. The speed of conservation.

Safiya — and yes, the village still called her Safiya — followed him with a notebook, a water bottle, and the specific expression of a woman who had accepted that Oman was going to roast her, politely, every single day. The acceptance had taken months to arrive. The acceptance, now that it had arrived, had freed her to stop fighting the heat and start, instead, dressing for it.

Ms. Thuraya was already under the palm tree. Sitting on a woven mat. Her chalk was ready. Her eyes were ready. Her teacher-heart was very, very ready.

A small shadow ran between their feet.

Malood.

The baby goat stopped, stared at John's chalkboard like it personally offended him, and then tried to nibble the corner.

"Malood," John said slowly, "this is not salad."

Malood chewed anyway.

Thuraya clapped once. "Okay, class! Sit down. Circle. Under the palm tree. Today we speak English."

The students came running in a messy line — Ali, Ahmed, Khalil, Noura, Marwa, Rahma, Salimah, Matla, Mansoor… and a few smaller children who always followed behind like curious birds.

They sat down. Legs crossed. Backs half-straight. Eyes shining. Some held notebooks. Some held nothing but confidence.

John placed the chalkboard on a wooden stand. He wiped the sweat from his forehead. He wrote in big letters:

WE MUST / WE MUSTN'T

Safiya nodded. Proudly. "This lesson is important."

Thuraya smiled. "Also… it will be funny."


## Scene Two — John Confesses First


John turned to the class. "Today… we do something brave."

The students leaned in.

John pointed to himself. "I will start. I confess."

The children gasped — like he said he stole a camel. The specific small full village-children's gasp of a class who had been told, in the first three syllables, that the foreign teacher was about to admit a personal moral failing.

John took a deep breath. "Yesterday… I tried to carry a bucket of water… and I spilled it on my shoe."

The class stared.

Then Mansoor whispered. Loudly. "That's it?"

Everyone laughed.

John sighed. "Okay. Not a big crime. But today, we practice speaking. We practice honesty."

Thuraya added, softly, "Sometimes we learn English with our mistakes."

The specific small full pedagogical principle that Thuraya had been quietly teaching John all year — that language was not best learned through perfection but through the small specific willingness to be wrong out loud. John, in his old Northern teacher-training, had been taught the opposite — that error was something to be corrected, smoothed over, replaced with the right answer as quickly as possible. Thuraya's instinct was different. Error, in her view, was the substance of the lesson. Without error, no truth-telling could occur.

Safiya clapped once. "Yes! Today you say: I did… and I feel… Then we say: We must… or We mustn't…"

John nodded. "Exactly."

He pointed to the board. "Repeat after me."

He spoke clearly. Slowly.

"We must tell the truth."

The class echoed. "We must tell the truth."

John smiled. "Good. Now… who will confess first?"

Silence.

A dramatic silence.

Then Khalil raised his hand. His face looked innocent — which was always a dangerous sign.

Thuraya narrowed her eyes. "Khalil… what did you do?"

Khalil stood up. Hands behind his back. Trying to look like a serious man in a tiny body.

"I… I did… a bad thing."


## Scene Three — The Bucket Crime


John encouraged him. "Say: I did a bad thing."

Khalil repeated. Proud. "I did a bad thing."

He pointed to the classroom door. With one finger. Like he was showing evidence in court.

"Yesterday… Teacher Thuraya… she came… to open the door."

Thuraya already knew.

Safiya already knew too. She leaned forward. With interest. "Oh no."

Khalil continued. "And I put… a bucket… above the door."

Mansoor exploded laughing before he could stop himself. The specific full Mansoor-laugh of a boy who had been waiting all morning for this specific public confession to occur.

Thuraya gasped. "You!"

Khalil lifted his chin. "And water… went… BOOSH… on her head."

The whole group laughed. Even the younger kids who didn't fully understand. They laughed because Khalil's BOOSH was too perfect. The specific small full Khalil-onomatopoeia of a boy whose grasp of English vocabulary was limited but whose mastery of sound effects was unparalleled.

Thuraya tried to look angry. But her mouth betrayed her. The corner of her lips twitched.

John held up his hand. "Okay! Now we use must and mustn't."

He wrote quickly:

We mustn't play dangerous jokes.We must respect our teachers.

He looked at Khalil. "Repeat: We mustn't play dangerous jokes."

Khalil repeated. Careful. "We mustn't play dangerous jokes."

John nodded. "Good. And: We must respect our teachers."

The students echoed it. Like a chant.

Thuraya folded her arms. "Khalil… you hear?"

Khalil nodded fast. "Yes teacher. Last time."

Malood bleated loudly — like he was adding his own opinion.

Safiya laughed. "Even Malood agrees."


## Scene Four — Bob Jamid


John pointed to the class. "Next confession."

Mansoor raised his hand before anyone else could breathe. He stood up so fast he almost tripped over Rahma's notebook.

His eyes were wide. Face dramatic. Mansoor always looked like life was happening to him loudly.

"I… I confess," he said, in broken English. But brave.

John nodded kindly. "Good. Tell us."

Mansoor swallowed. "I… go… to Sadoo shop."

Thuraya's eyes flicked toward him. "Uh-oh."

Mansoor continued quickly. Like he wanted to throw the words out and run away. "I take… Bob Jamid."

Safiya blinked. "Bob… what?"

Thuraya whispered to her, "A cold drink. Fizzy."

Mansoor nodded hard. "Yes! Cold! I take… without pay."

The class made a sound — "OHHHH." The specific small full village-classroom collective ohhh of children whose moral compass had immediately registered that the crime being described was, by the small specific economy of their village, a real crime.

Ahmed sat straight as a ruler. Looking shocked. Ali raised his eyebrows like he was calculating the crime.

John stayed calm. "Okay. Now we say rules."

He wrote:

We must pay for things.We mustn't steal.

He spoke clearly. "Repeat: We must pay for things."

The class echoed.

"We mustn't steal."

The class echoed. Louder this time.

John looked at Mansoor. "Now you make a promise. Say: I mustn't steal again."

Mansoor breathed in deeply. "I mustn't… steal again."

Safiya added gently, "And you can fix it. You can say sorry."

Mansoor nodded. Like a soldier. "I say sorry to Sadoo."

Thuraya pointed at him. "And you pay."

Mansoor said quickly, "And I pay!"

The kids clapped — because it felt like a happy ending.


## Scene Five — More Confessions


Next, Noura raised her hand. Of course Noura did. She didn't just ask questions. She lived inside questions.

She stood up. "Teacher… I confess… I talk too much."

The class laughed — because even Noura laughed at herself.

John smiled. "That is a good confession."

Noura added, "I… I don't stop."

Safiya nodded dramatically. "That is true."

Thuraya laughed. "Okay. Rule?"

John wrote:

We must listen.We mustn't interrupt.

The students repeated. Noura put her hand on her chest — like she was taking an oath.

Then Rahma stood up. Shy. But determined.

"I confess… I broke… my sister's comb."

She looked down. "And I said… cat did it."

Everyone giggled.

John wrote:

We must take responsibility.We mustn't blame others.

Rahma repeated the sentences softly. She sat down — looking lighter, like the confession took a stone out of her pocket.

The specific small full village-confession-relief of a girl who had been carrying — for an unknown number of days — the small specific guilt of a broken comb that had not been hers. The English-language exercise had given her a small permitted occasion to set the guilt down. The grammar had become, accidentally, a small ceremony of release. Thuraya, watching, recognised the moment for what it was. The lesson on the board was about must and mustn't. The lesson actually being learned was about how to live with one's own small mistakes.

One by one, they confessed.

Marwa admitted she hid her homework under a mat so her mother wouldn't see it.

Salimah admitted she told her brother his ears were "too big" just to make him cry.

Ali confessed he corrected people so much he sometimes sounded rude. And Ahmed quietly said, "That is true," without smiling.
