Matla confessed she once kept two pencils when she promised to share. Then she offered the class her best pencil as "compensation" — like a tiny businesswoman.

Even the small children copied them. Confessing strange little things —

"I eat sugar… before lunch."

"I kick… a stone… to Mansoor."

"I throw… date seed… at goat."

Malood lifted his head proudly — as if he was the goat representative in the meeting.


## Scene Six — The Strong Ending


Finally, John clapped his hands.

"Excellent. Today you used English for real life. You spoke truth. You learned rules."

He pointed to the board. He made them say the full set again — like a strong ending:

We must tell the truth.We must respect our teachers.We must pay for things.We mustn't steal.We must listen.We mustn't interrupt.

Safiya smiled at Thuraya. "This lesson is… powerful."

Thuraya nodded. "And funny."

John picked up the chalkboard. "Now… everyone go wash your hands."

The students repeated. Automatically. Laughing. "We must wash our hands!"

Malood tried to bite the chalk. Again.

John pulled it away. Fast. "And we mustn't eat chalk!"

The children roared with laughter as the bell rang in the distance. Thin and metallic.

"Tolqah!" someone shouted, and the students jumped up like springs — running toward the classroom, toward shade, toward the next little adventure.

Under the palm tree, Thuraya watched them go. She whispered, almost to herself:

"They are learning English… but they are also learning how to be good humans."

Safiya leaned closer. "And Malood?"

Thuraya smiled. "Malood is learning… how to cause trouble in two languages."

John sighed. "That goat will become fluent before me."

And for once, even the palm leaves seemed to laugh quietly above them.


# Palm Twenty-Nine — We Should and We Shouldn't — And the Grasshopper Wing



## Scene One — A Kinder Sun


The afternoon in Samail had a different personality.

The sun was still hot — but not angry anymore. It was the kind of light that made the village look golden, like someone had painted everything with honey. The specific small late-afternoon honey-light of a hot place that, after eight hours of punching, had finally relaxed. The shadows had grown long. The clay walls of the houses had begun to release the heat they had absorbed since dawn — and the air, which had been a substance to be endured, had become, for a few small hours, a substance to be enjoyed.

Under the big palm tree, the same woven mats were waiting.

Mr. John arrived first. Carrying the chalkboard and a small cup of water. He looked at the tree.

"This tree is becoming our second classroom."

Safiya smiled. "And a very windy one."

Ms. Thuraya came with her usual calm steps. And behind her came the students. Laughing and talking. And of course…

Malood.

Malood walked like he owned the place. He went straight to the mat. He sat down. He started chewing something nobody knew the source of.

"Malood," John said tiredly, "you should not eat mystery food."

Malood did not care.

The students sat in a circle: Ali, Ahmed, Khalil, Noura, Marwa, Rahma, Salimah, Matla, Mansoor… and a few smaller ones who always followed like shadows.

Safiya clapped her hands. "Okay everyone. Yesterday we talked about bad things and rules. Today…"

John wrote in big letters:

WE SHOULD / WE SHOULDN'T

Thuraya smiled. "Today we talk about good things."

The students looked at each other. This felt… nicer. The specific small full village-children's relief of a class that had been told they would not, today, have to confess anything bad about themselves — and that today's grammar would be in the small specific direction of pride rather than guilt.


## Scene Two — Things to Be Proud Of


John turned to them. "Yesterday you confessed bad things. Today you will tell us good things. Things you are proud of."

He pointed to himself. "I start."

He thought for a moment. "I am proud that… I learned how to drink Omani coffee without crying."

The class laughed. The specific full classroom-laugh of children who had, in the early months, watched the foreign teacher's small specific facial reactions to Omani kahwa with the cardamom-saffron strength that no Northern coffee training had prepared him for.

Safiya added, "And you should stop putting three spoons of sugar in it."

John nodded. Seriously. "Yes. I should."

Thuraya said, "Now students. Who wants to start?"

Matla raised her hand immediately. She always did when the topic was generosity.

She stood up. Very straight. "I am proud that… I give my pencils to Rahma when she forget."

Safiya smiled. "Good! Say: I am proud that I gave my pencils."

Matla repeated carefully. "I am proud that I gave my pencils."

John wrote on the board:

We should help others.

The class repeated together. "We should help others."

Rahma looked at Matla. She whispered, "Thank you."

Next, Ali stood up.

"I am proud that… I help my father read letters."

The specific small full village-confession of a boy whose father — like many men of the older generation in Samail in 1973 — had not had access to literacy. The whole specific small private routine of a small son sitting next to a tall father in the evening, opening the small specific government letters that arrived occasionally, reading them aloud carefully, helping his father understand what the state had decided to communicate. Thuraya, hearing the sentence, recognised it for what it was — not just an English-language exercise, but a small specific portrait of a generational shift. The fathers had not learned to read. The sons would. And the school, this small specific school, was where the shift was happening.

John nodded. Impressed. "Excellent."

Thuraya added, "Many people need help with reading."

John wrote:

We should help our family.

The students repeated. Ali sat down — looking taller.

Then Ahmed stood up. Hands behind his back. Like always.

"I am proud that… I do my homework… before playing."

The class made a sound — "Ooooooh."

Mansoor whispered. Loudly. "Impossible."

Everyone laughed.

John wrote: We should do our homework before playing.

Mansoor looked at the ground — like the sentence personally attacked him.


## Scene Three — Sharing, Talking, Stealing


Then Marwa stood up. Shy. But smiling.

"I am proud that… I share my food with my little brother."

Safiya nodded. "Good. Very good."

She wrote on the board:

We should share.

Next, Noura stood up. She said, proudly, "I am proud that… yesterday… I did not talk too much."

The class burst out laughing.

Thuraya said, "That is a very big achievement."

John added, jokingly, "You should do that again tomorrow."

Noura laughed and sat down.

Then Mansoor stood up.

Everyone waited.

He scratched his head. "I am proud that… I did not steal today."

The class clapped.

John smiled. "That is progress."

He wrote: We should be honest.

Then Khalil stood up. He said, "I am proud that… I did not put any bucket today."

Thuraya crossed her arms. "Good. Keep it like that."

Now Safiya stepped forward. "Okay. Now we also say things we shouldn't do."

She wrote:

We shouldn't hurt others.We shouldn't lie.We shouldn't laugh at people.

They repeated together.

The lesson was going perfectly.

Too perfectly.

That is when something green jumped.


## Scene Four — The Grasshopper


A big green insect flew. And landed…

On Safiya's head.

For half a second, nobody moved.

Then Safiya screamed.

"Aaaaaaaaah!"

She jumped up. Waving her arms. "Something is alive on my head! Something is ALIVE!"

The students froze… then laughed.

Thuraya ran to her. "Don't move! Don't move!"

Safiya ran anyway.

The insect fell on the mat.

Ali shouted, "Jaradah! Grasshopper!"

Safiya looked at it. Like it was a monster from another planet. "It is… huge!"

John tried to be brave. "It is just… an insect."

The grasshopper jumped again.

Safiya screamed again. And ran. Behind Thuraya.

"I should not come to Oman," she said. Dramatically. "I should live in a fridge."

The students were laughing so hard some of them were holding their stomachs.

Suddenly, a shadow fell over the group.

A familiar, thin, quiet figure appeared.

Shayb Khalaf.

The children gasped.

Some of them moved back.

He bent down calmly. He picked up the grasshopper with his hand — like it was nothing — and showed them… a bag.

A bag full of grasshoppers.

"Who wants mashawi?" he asked. In a quiet voice.

Silence.

Absolute silence.

Safiya's face turned white.

"You… you eat… that?" she asked. Slowly.

Thuraya nodded. "Yes. People grill them."

Safiya put her hand on her mouth. "I should not ask questions in this country."

Shayb Khalaf walked away. Like nothing happened.

The class was still shocked.


## Scene Five — The Wing


The lesson ended soon after. Everyone went home.

That evening, in John and Safiya's house —

Safiya was still traumatized.

"I cannot believe people eat grasshoppers," she said.

John was very quiet.

Too quiet.

They sat down to eat.

Thuraya brought food.

John chewed.

Safiya looked at him.

She stopped.

She leaned closer.

"…John."

He looked at her innocently.

"…John, why is there a wing… here?"

She pointed at the corner of his mouth.

John froze.

He slowly wiped his mouth. Looked at the napkin. It was… a wing.

Safiya's eyes became very big. "You ate it."

John tried to lie. "No."

Safiya crossed her arms. "John."

He sighed. "…Yes."

She screamed and ran to the sink.

"You ate mashawi grasshopper!"

John smiled. Proud. "Actually… not bad."


## Scene Six — A Cup, a Smile, a Lesson Done


Safiya shouted from the kitchen. "You should see a doctor!"

John laughed. "You should try it!"

She almost threw a cup at him.

The cup did not get thrown — though, for a fraction of a second, Safiya's grip had decided that it would. The specific small wifely calculation of a woman whose cup-throwing impulse had, at the last possible moment, been overridden by the specific small wifely understanding that the cup was, in fact, one of two matching cups they had brought from England, and that breaking it would be a small private tragedy that her morning could not absorb.

She put the cup down. Carefully.

She glared at John.

Then — eventually — she smiled. The specific small reluctant smile of a wife who had, against her better judgement, just been impressed.

"Okay," she said. "Tell me. Honestly. Was it bad?"

John considered.

"It was salty," he said. "Like fish. But… crunchy."

Safiya made a face. "I am not ready."

Thuraya, who had been watching the whole scene from the doorway with the specific calm satisfaction of a teacher whose lesson had — through no fault of her own — extended into the dinner of her foreign friends, smiled.

"Today," she said, "you learned should and shouldn't. And you also learned: in Oman, you should never refuse food."

John wrote something in his notebook before going to bed:

Today, I learned:I should be brave with food.I should not lie to my wife.And I should never sit too close to a grasshopper bag again.

Outside, in the warm village night, somewhere along the small alleys of Samail, a goat — almost certainly Malood — bleated once.

And went back to whatever Malood went back to.


# Palm Thirty — Good Manners, Black Cats, and a Very Full Stomach



## Scene One — The Folded Paper


There are mornings when an envelope arrives at a school and the entire shape of the day rearranges itself around it. The teacher who carries it walks differently. The children, who have not yet been told what is in it, sense its arrival the way an old village senses news — through the small specific shifts in adult posture that always, always, precede an announcement.

The invitation came in the morning.

Ms. Thuraya was holding a small folded paper when she entered the classroom. Smiling. In a special way.

"Today," she said, "we are invited to lunch."

The students' eyes became big immediately.

"Where?" Mansoor asked.

"To the house of Al-Mu'allim Hamad," Thuraya said. "The village Quran teacher."

The specific small full village-respect that the name Al-Mu'allim Hamad commanded. He was not the kind of man who needed introduction. He was the kind of man who taught the Quran from morning prayer until the hour of late afternoon — patient, white-bearded, with the small specific quietness of a person whose entire life had been organised around a single ancient text. Every child in the room had, at some point, sat in his small specific morning-circle, the white kufiyya on each child's head, the wooden tablet in each lap, the careful slow rocking of small bodies as they recited the verses in the specific small village-rhythm that had been passed down without break for fourteen centuries. To be invited to his house for lunch was a small specific honour.

Safiya clapped her hands. "Oh! A real Omani lunch!"

John smiled politely. But his stomach smiled more.

Before they left, Safiya stood in front of the class. "Okay, today is not only about food. Today is about etiquette."

She wrote the word on the board:

ETIQUETTE

John added under it:

• Wash your hands• Sit properly• Say "please" and "thank you"• Don't eat like Mansoor

Mansoor protested. "I eat normally!"

Ali whispered, "Like a camel."

Safiya continued, "We should be polite guests."

The class repeated, "We should be polite guests."

Thuraya nodded. "Very important."


## Scene Two — The Black Cat


They walked in two groups.

Thuraya, Safiya, and Fatkat with the girls.

John and the boys in front.

The village was quiet. The sun was soft. The smell of bread was coming from somewhere far. The specific small full lunchtime-stillness of a village where every kitchen, at this exact hour, was producing its own private smoke and its own private bread, and where the air had filled, over the course of the late morning, with the small specific scent of a community feeding itself simultaneously. The lanes were empty. The children walked through the slowness of an Omani afternoon's first hour with the specific small full processional dignity of guests who were, themselves, about to be received.

Suddenly, something black crossed the road.

A black cat.

The boys stopped.

Some of them stepped back.

Mansoor whispered, "Sennoor aswad…"

Khalil said, "Bad luck…"

Ali said, "Maybe sorcery…"

John looked at the cat. He laughed.

"In Britain, this is just… a cat."

The black cat sat. Looked at them. Walked away. Like a king.

John said, jokingly, "Maybe this is Shayb Khalaf."

The boys laughed.

Suddenly, a voice came from behind.

"Why you say that, John?"

Everyone jumped.

It was Shayb Khalaf himself.

Standing there. Looking at John.

The specific small full appearance-out-of-nowhere that Shayb Khalaf had perfected over a long life of being precisely the right specific old man, in precisely the right specific shaded village corner, at precisely the specific moment when his name had been mentioned by a foreigner. John's mind, in that small specific second, registered three things simultaneously — the quality of the late-morning light on Khalaf's face, the absolute silence of his own boys around him, and the small specific cold-water-down-the-spine sensation that the entire previous Smoke-Day, with all its small folkloric absurdity, was now being asked, by the universe, to be paid for.

"I look like a cat now?" he asked. Calmly.

John's face turned white.

"No, no, no, no… I mean… cat is… nice… and you are… also… nice?"

Shayb Khalaf smiled. A small strange smile.

"Good," he said. "Because cat don't fear."

He walked away. Slowly.

John looked around. "Where did everyone go?"

The boys were already ten meters away.

Mansoor shouted, "We waiting you there, teacher!"

John ran after them. "You left me with him!"


## Scene Three — Safiya and the Lizard


Meanwhile…

Safiya, Thuraya, and Fatkat were walking with the girls.

Safiya suddenly stopped.

"Oh my God…"

She pointed to a wall.

"It is… so beautiful."

Thuraya looked. "What?"

Safiya smiled. "The lizard!"

Thuraya's face froze.

"Maah…"

She took two steps back.

"No. No. No."

The specific small full village-Omani-woman's-relationship with house lizards — a relationship that, contrary to Safiya's Northern assumption, was not one of affection. The lizard was not a creature one admired. It was a creature one removed, when one had to remove it, with the specific small full disgust that Thuraya's body was now communicating with absolute clarity. Thuraya, who climbed mountain paths without complaint, who delivered babies in the village when needed, who had once stood between a grown man and a beating he was about to give a small boy — Thuraya was, on the subject of house lizards, unambiguously not brave.

It was a small house lizard on the wall.

Fatkat said, "Ya satir… what is this disgusting thing?"

The girls started laughing.

Safiya walked closer. Happily. "It is cute!"

She took it. Gently. In her hand.

The girls screamed.

"Throw it! Throw it!"

Safiya said, "Why? It is a living creature."

Fatkat said, "The grasshopper also is a living creature, but you didn't like it, right?"

Before Safiya answered, the lizard jumped.

Straight to the wall.

And ran away.

Thuraya screamed and ran. The specific small full uncharacteristic high-pitched scream of a teacher whose composure had, on this single matter, completely collapsed.

The girls laughed so much they almost fell.

Safiya said, "Okay… maybe not so beautiful."


## Scene Four — At Al-Mu'allim Hamad's House


The house was simple. Clean. Calm.

The specific full small Omani-religious-teacher's-house of a man whose entire small economy had been organised around the specific principle of enough. The walls were whitewashed clay. The floor was covered with woven palm-leaf mats. There was a small wooden shelf with three or four books on it, all of them religious texts. There was a small specific brass coffee-pot in a corner. There was no decoration that was not also useful. The room was the specific small material expression of a man who had decided, long ago, that what he had was sufficient — and who had, by that decision, achieved a quality of small interior peace that John, walking in, immediately recognised as something he had never himself possessed.

They sat on mats.

Water was brought.

Thuraya whispered, "First… wash hands."

Everyone went to wash their hands.

John washed his hands three times. "Just in case," he said.

They came back and sat.

Safiya whispered, "Back straight. Legs in. Don't touch food yet."

Mansoor was already touching food.

John pulled his hand back. "No."

Mansoor whispered, "But I am hungry."

Then Al-Mu'allim Hamad came in.

A calm man. White beard. Kind eyes.

The specific full small village-Quran-teacher's quietness of a man whose voice — when he spoke, which was rarely — carried the specific small weight of a person who had spent his life carrying ancient words, and whose body had, over decades, been organised by those words into a specific small posture of receptive attention. He did not enter a room. He arrived in it. The atmosphere of any space he occupied shifted, very slightly, in the direction of stillness.

"Welcome," he said.

Everyone answered. Together. "Welcome."

He smiled. "Today, you are our guests. And guests must be honored."

Food came.

Rice. Meat. Bread. Dates.

Safiya looked amazed. "So beautiful…"

Before eating, Thuraya said softly, "We say bismillah."

John heard the word. He repeated it. Quietly. "Bismillah…"

The first time the word had ever passed his lips. The specific small full first-time-uttering of a small ancient sacred phrase by a man whose Northern Christian upbringing had not previously had occasion for it. He noticed, as he said it, that the word fitted his mouth more easily than he had expected. As though it had been waiting for him. As though it had been, all his life, a small linguistic shape his lips had been ready to make.


## Scene Five — The Meal and the Quiet After


They ate.

Slowly. Politely. At first.

Then John forgot etiquette.

Then he forgot the world.

He ate. And ate. And ate.

The specific full small foreign-guest's-collapse of a man whose careful Northern manners had, after the third specific small handful of kabsa, surrendered completely to the specific full small village-meal protocol — which was, at heart, the protocol of more. The host's small specific village-honour required him to keep offering. The guest's small specific village-honour required him to keep accepting. Both protocols, working together, produced the specific small full inevitable result of the foreign guest at his fourth helping with his eyes slightly glazed, communicating in monosyllables, and beyond the reach of any further conversational obligation.

Fatkat whispered to Safiya, "He eat like he never see food before."

After lunch, tea came.

They sat.

Al-Mu'allim Hamad talked about life. Kindness. Respect.

John listened.

He did not understand everything. But he felt something… peaceful.

The specific small full first-encounter of a Northern man with the specific small atmosphere of a quiet Omani religious household — the specific small quality that all the books he had read about Islam in his university library had not prepared him for. There was a thing in this room that the books had not described. The thing was not in the words. The thing was in the small specific way the words sat in the room. The small specific way the host did not interrupt himself. The small specific way the cup was placed on the mat. The small specific space — between sentences — that was not silence but presence. The thing had a name in Arabic that John did not yet know but would, in the years that followed, learn — and he would, by long study, eventually understand that the thing was not a feeling but a discipline.

On the way back, John was very quiet.

Safiya looked at him. "Are you okay?"

John touched his stomach. "My stomach is very okay."

Then he said. Slowly. "But… today was… different."

Thuraya looked at him. "Different how?"

John thought. "The way… he lives. The way… he talks. The calm. The respect. The prayer…"

He stopped.

Safiya looked at him.

John said, quietly, "I think… I want to learn more about Islam."

The specific small full quiet sentence of a man who had, in one specific lunch, encountered a specific small atmosphere of being-in-the-world that had — without warning, without preparation — registered with him as real. The sentence had been forming in him, perhaps, since the bismillah. Perhaps since the meshkak afternoon at Tawi Zahir. Perhaps since his first small specific encounter with the smell of Thuraya's morning prayer, drifting through the courtyard of their shared house, when he had been told it was kahwa being made and had only later learned that the smell was actually oud, and that Thuraya burned it after she prayed. The sentence had been forming a long time. Today it had simply, finally, found its words.

Thuraya did not smile. She did not joke. She just said, softly, "When you are ready… I will explain."


## Scene Six — A Stomach Like a Separate Country


John entered the house.

He tried to sit.

He failed.

He fell on the mat.

"My stomach… is now… a separate country."

Safiya laughed. "You should not eat like that."

John closed his eyes. "But we should accept hospitality."

She threw a pillow at him.

The pillow landed softly on his chest. John did not even open his eyes. The afternoon light was coming in low through the small window, painting one wall of the room in the specific small full late-Omani-light of golden and dust. Thuraya's footsteps could be heard in the next room — the small specific tea-making sounds of a woman who had decided that the appropriate end to a heavy lunch was a small specific cup of strong unsweetened tea. The whole house had settled into the small full quiet of a household digesting.

And in the small specific golden silence, John, lying on his back with a pillow on his chest and his eyes closed, said one more thing — quietly, more to himself than to the room —

"He is a good man," he said. "Al-Mu'allim Hamad. He is a good man."

Safiya sat near him. She did not say anything at first. Then —

"Yes," she said, softly. "He is."

The specific small full wifely confirmation of a woman who had, in the same afternoon, registered the same thing — and who had decided, without saying so explicitly, that whatever small specific shift had happened to John in Al-Mu'allim Hamad's house, she was not going to question it. She was going to wait. The way Thuraya waited.

Outside, a goat — almost certainly Malood — bleated once.

The afternoon settled into its small specific golden completion. And the village, full of fed bodies and quiet houses and the small specific lingering scent of after-meal cardamom, slowed, briefly, into the specific small full peace of a place that had — for one afternoon at least — done its hospitality correctly, and was now allowed to rest.


# Palm Thirty-One — I Am Afraid Of — And Thuraya's Secret



## Scene One — The Tired Fan and the Topic of Fear


Some lessons are decided in advance. Some lessons arrive when the teacher walks into the room. And some lessons — the most interesting kind — are produced by the room itself, by the small specific atmosphere of a particular morning, by the small accumulated trivia of the previous days. The morning of I am afraid of… was that third kind. Safiya had not woken up planning to teach the topic of fear. The topic had simply, quietly, arrived in the room with her — born of the previous afternoon's grasshopper, of the small specific lizard Thuraya had not stopped mentioning, of the small specific quality the village had been carrying ever since Shayb Khalaf's appearance with his bag of mashawi. The whole village had been thinking about fear for two days. The teachers had simply decided, that morning, to give the village permission to talk about it.

The fan was making its usual tired sound.

The classroom was hot.

The chalk was squeaking.

And John was already sweating before the lesson even started.

Safiya stood near the board. Holding a small paper. Smiling.

"Today," she said happily, "we will talk about fear."

The students looked at each other.

Mansoor immediately said, "I am not afraid of anything."

Khalil whispered, "Liar."

John wrote on the board:

I am afraid of …

He turned. "Repeat, everyone."

The class said together. "I am afraid of…"

Safiya clapped. "Good! Now we will learn insect words."

She drew small bad pictures on the board. The specific small full Safiya-drawing of a foreign teacher whose chalk-skill in still-life rendering was approximately at the level of a six-year-old, but whose enthusiasm compensated for accuracy. A bee. A grasshopper. A butterfly. A lizard.

Mansoor shouted, "This one looks like Ali!"

Ali threw chalk at him.


## Scene Two — The Confession Game


Safiya said, "Okay. One by one. You say your name and what you are afraid of."

She pointed at Noura.

Noura stood up. She said shyly, "I am afraid of… bee."

John smiled. "Good! Say: I am afraid of bees."

Noura repeated. "I am afraid of bees."

Khalil said proudly, "I am afraid of nothing."

At that moment, a fly passed near his ear.

He screamed.

The class exploded with laughter. The specific full village-classroom-laugh of a class that had been waiting all morning for exactly this small specific contradiction.

Ali said, "I am afraid of… snake."

Ahmed said. Seriously. "I am afraid of… failing exam."

Everyone respected that. The specific full silent classroom-respect of a group of children who had recognised, in Ahmed's answer, a fear so unambiguously real that no one was prepared to mock it.

Then Mansoor stood up. Smiling. Like a hero.

"I am afraid of… butterfly."

The class went silent.

Then BOOM.

Laughter.

"Butterfly???" Khalil shouted. "You fight boys but afraid of butterfly?"

Mansoor shouted, "They fly… without warning!"

John wrote on the board: Mansoor is afraid of butterflies.

Mansoor crossed his arms. "They are dangerous."

Safiya smiled. "Okay. My turn. I am afraid of… grasshoppers."

The students looked at her in shock.

"You eat it," Khalil said.

Safiya made a face. "Yes. But I don't want to meet it alive."

Fatkat, passing by the door, said, "Normal. I am afraid of rats."

Then John said proudly, "I am afraid of nothing."

Thuraya looked at him. "Really?"

John nodded. "I am a brave man."

At that moment, Malood jumped inside the class. Straight to John.

John jumped on the table. "I am afraid of goats!" he shouted.

The class died laughing.

Safiya wrote: Mr. John is afraid of Malood.


## Scene Three — Thuraya's Secret


Safiya looked at Thuraya. "And you?"

Thuraya answered fast. "I am not afraid of anything."

Too fast.

John looked at her suspiciously.

"Really?"

She nodded. "Really."

But her eyes moved to the window.

John followed her eyes.

On the wall outside…

A small lizard was sitting quietly.

John smiled. A dangerous smile.

"Ohhh," he said. "Interesting…"

He walked outside.

The students watched. Like they were watching a movie.

He came back. Holding a small box.

"Thuraya," he said sweetly, "I have something for you."

Thuraya stepped back. "No."

John opened the box.

The lizard jumped out.

Thuraya screamed.

A real scream.

The specific small full uncharacteristic high-pitched scream of a teacher whose composure had — for one second — completely abandoned her. The students, who had never in their lives heard Thuraya scream, registered the sound with the small specific full classroom-shock of children whose authority figure had, in front of them, demonstrated a small specific weakness. The discovery was, in their developing minds, a small specific permanent file. Ms. Thuraya was not, after all, indestructible. Ms. Thuraya had a small specific human edge. The information would be remembered. The information would be respected. The information would be — for the rest of the school year — used very, very carefully, and only by John.

She ran.

The girls screamed.

Safiya screamed.

Mansoor climbed on the desk.

The lizard ran.

The class became a war zone.

Thuraya shouted, "JOHN!"

John laughed so much he couldn't breathe.

"I am afraid of nothing," he said, "but you are afraid of lizards!"

Thuraya pointed at him. "You will pay for this."

Malood tried to eat the lizard.

The lizard escaped.

Order came back after ten minutes.

Safiya wrote on the board: Ms. Thuraya is afraid of lizards.

Thuraya crossed her arms. "Delete it."


## Scene Four — The Big Question


When the class calmed down, Ali raised his hand.

"Teacher… what about Shayb Khalaf?"

The class became quiet.

"What is he afraid of?" Mansoor asked.

Everyone looked at the door.

The specific small full collective village-classroom turn of every small head, simultaneously, toward the closed door — as if the act of saying Khalaf's name had a small physical weight, and the weight had been pulled toward the room's only entrance, and the heads had instinctively followed. The fan continued. The chalk dust drifted. The specific small full village-superstition of a group of children who had been raised, all of them, on the small specific certainty that to mention the sahir was — at minimum — to invite his attention.

John swallowed.

Safiya whispered, "Good question…"

Thuraya smiled. Slowly. The specific small full slow teacher-smile of a woman who knew the answer to the question and who was deciding, in real time, whether she was going to share it.

"No one knows," she said.

The fan continued its tired sound.

Outside, somewhere far away…

Someone was laughing.


## Scene Five — Saeed's Quiet Watching


The class went out for break.

Most of the children ran. Mansoor ran first, of course. Khalil chased him. Noura walked with Marwa talking about something Safiya could not hear but which involved several small bursts of laughter.

Saeed stayed.

He stood near the small specific window through which the lizard had earlier been visible, looking out at the empty wall.

Thuraya walked to him.

"Saeed," she said softly, "and you? What are you afraid of?"

Saeed did not answer immediately.

The specific small full Saeed-thinking of a boy whose answers were always the result of consideration, and who had decided, by long practice, that a teacher's question deserved his actual answer rather than his easiest one.

Finally, he said, in his careful small English:

"I am afraid of… not seeing."

Thuraya looked at him. "Not seeing what?"

Saeed thought again. He could not find the English. Eventually he said, simply:

"What is true."

Thuraya stood with him, then, for a small specific long moment without speaking. The classroom around them was empty. The fan continued its tired conversation with itself. Outside, the village's small specific lunchtime had begun — the smell of something onion-and-spice cooking somewhere down the lane. Thuraya, watching her quietest student, recognised that he had just told her — without quite meaning to — what his entire life had been organised around. The specific small full village-watching of a child who had spent his nine years collecting the specific small textures of what is true, because he had decided, very young, that the only protection against being deceived was to see clearly. Thuraya, with her own small specific quiet life, understood. She did not need to say so.

Eventually she said, quietly, "That is a very brave fear, Saeed."

Saeed nodded.

And ran out, finally, after the others.


## Scene Six — The Small Closing of the Door


The classroom was empty.

Thuraya sat down at the teacher's desk. She wrote, in her small private notebook, in Arabic — the specific small full private Arabic she only ever wrote in her own notebook, the language of her own private thinking:


> «اليوم تعلّموا كلمة الخوف.وأنا تعلّمت أن الصغير سعيد… يعرف أكثر مني.»


(Today they learned the word "fear." And I learned that little Saeed… knows more than I do.)

She closed the notebook.

She sat for a small full minute, listening to the fan.

Then she stood up and went outside, into the bright small village afternoon, where John and Safiya were waiting under the big palm and where the children — her children, in the small specific full sense that this village's children had become hers — were doing, as always, exactly what children do at lunchtime in a small Omani village in 1973: shouting, running, sharing dates, kicking small stones, and existing in the small specific full unrepeatable joy of being nine years old in a place whose entire life still moved at the speed of palm trees and falaj water.

Far away, on the path toward his clay-house, Shayb Khalaf's door closed slowly.

And for once — only for this small specific afternoon — no one in the village wondered, quite as much as they usually did, what the closing of that door might mean.


# Palm Thirty-Two — The Only Thing Shayb Khalaf Was Afraid Of



## Scene One — Tap, Tap, Tap


There are mornings in Samail when the village announces itself slowly, in small specific layers. The rooster first. Then the woman next door clapping her dough between her palms. Then the smoke from a fire being kindled three houses away. Then, finally, the whole sky deciding to admit that it was light. John's body had, by now, learned this particular small specific layered awakening — and on this particular morning, lying in the grey-blue dawn-dimness, his ear collected the layers in their proper order, and registered, somewhere in his half-sleep, that the day was beginning correctly.

The morning in Samail began before the sun fully decided to show itself.

A rooster screamed like it was late for work. Somewhere nearby, a woman clapped dough between her palms, and the sound traveled through the quiet lanes like soft drumbeats. John opened his eyes to the smell of wood smoke, warm bread, and that familiar village dust that somehow made every day feel like a new page.

Sophia — or Safiya as the villagers insisted on calling her — sat on the edge of the bed tying her scarf with careful patience.

"I feel like I'm dressing for a documentary," she whispered, then smiled. "A very hot documentary."

John rubbed his eyes. "At least documentaries have fans. We have… heat."

A tiny hoof tapped the door.

Tap. Tap. Tap.

Safiya froze. "Please tell me that is not… him."

The door creaked open — because it was never properly closed in the first place — and Malood walked in like he owned the entire house.

The baby goat looked at them with calm confidence. Then marched straight to John's shoe.

"No," John warned, pointing. "No biting."

Malood ignored the warning. He chewed the shoelace with the seriousness of a professional worker.

Safiya sighed. "He has a mission."

John leaned down. Trying to free his shoe with dignity. Malood pulled harder. John pulled harder. The shoelace snapped.

John stared at the broken lace in his hand. Malood stared at him.

It was a silent war.

Outside, Ms. Thuraya's voice called. Bright and practical.

"Good morning! Hurry, or the students will arrive before you."

John stepped into the yard — still holding the dead shoelace like a defeated soldier. Thuraya stood at the gate with her chalk and her notebook. She looked ready to fight the whole world using only grammar and confidence.


## Scene Two — The Schoolyard and the Workers


The school in 1973 did not look like the schools in John's mind.

It was simple. Chalk dust lived everywhere. The bell was not a modern bell — it was more like a sound that said, Enough talking, start suffering quietly. Students arrived in groups, barefoot or wearing worn sandals, carrying small notebooks. Some carried nothing except energy and curiosity. The specific full small village-school of an Oman that was still — in 1973 — at the very beginning of its modern educational ambitions. The first national curriculum was new. The first textbooks were new. The first batch of properly trained Omani teachers, of which Thuraya was one, had only recently graduated. The whole system was being built, in this specific small village, brick by brick — and every morning was, in some small specific sense, a small contribution to that building.

And then… the shouting started.

"Teacher John!"

"Teacher!"

"Safiya!"

"Miss Thuraya!"

Mansoor ran first, as usual — like his legs were powered by nonsense. He nearly crashed into John, then stopped at the last second and saluted dramatically.

John blinked. "Good morning, Mansoor."

Mansoor grinned and yelled the only English greeting he trusted:

"HAAAAI!"

Behind him came Ali with calm eyes, Ahmed sitting upright even while walking, Khalil already laughing about something he hadn't done yet, and the girls — Matla, Noura, Rahma, Marwa, and Salimah — all watching with the curious bravery of people who had never seen a foreign teacher before this year.

Saeed stood at the far side. Half-hidden near the wall. Watching. Like a quiet shadow. He still didn't join fully, but his eyes collected every detail.

Thuraya clapped once. "Okay! Everyone, listen. Today we practice morning assembly."

John picked up a piece of chalk. He wrote large words on a small board:

LINE UPSTAND STILLGOOD MORNINGEXERCISESANTHEM

A small group of men appeared at the corner of the yard — carrying measuring tools. One held a rolled paper. Another carried a hammer. They looked like they were here to change the world one brick at a time.

Safiya pointed. "Who are they?"

Thuraya lowered her voice. Sounding proud. "Workers. The school will be rebuilt. New classrooms. Better rooms. Better future. This is the new time."

John watched them with awe. "So this is what it looks like when a country starts to grow."

Thuraya's face softened. "Yes. Slowly. But it will grow."

The anthem began — sung in voices that were not perfect but were full of something bigger than music.

John stood quietly. Listening. Feeling like he was standing inside history. The specific full small full witnessing-position of a foreign man who had arrived in this country at the precise small moment when its people were singing, for the first time in their generation, a national anthem of a national renaissance that had not, only three years earlier, existed. The new sultan, Qaboos, had only recently taken his place. The nahda — the renaissance — had only recently been declared. The schools were only beginning to be built. The roads were only beginning to be paved. John, who had been hired as an English teacher and had thought of his contract as a small foreign adventure, was beginning to understand that he had — by accident — been placed inside one of the small specific full pivotal moments of a small specific full nation's becoming.


## Scene Three — The Fear Lesson and the Match


Then his brain remembered something else.

Shayb Khalaf.

The mysterious old man. The village's "wizard." The man who had scared children without even trying.

John's eyes narrowed. An idea sparked like a dangerous match.

Later, in class, John decided to do what teachers do best — turn chaos into a lesson.

He wrote on the chalkboard:

I AM AFRAID OF…HE IS AFRAID OF…HE IS NOT AFRAID OF…

Safiya clapped softly. "Good topic. Everyone has fears."

Thuraya smirked. "Even you, Mr. John."

John pretended not to hear.

He pointed at Noura. "Noura. I am afraid of…?"

Noura stood up confidently. "I am afraid of… bee."

John corrected gently. "A bee. Good."

Mansoor raised his hand like his arm was on fire.

John sighed. "Yes, Mansoor."

Mansoor stood and announced. Proudly. "I am afraid of… butterfly."

The room exploded. Khalil laughed so hard he fell sideways. Ahmed tried to stay serious and failed. Even Ali's mouth twitched.

John stared. "A butterfly?"

Mansoor nodded quickly. "Yes. It… fly… in my face. Big problem."

Safiya wiped tears from her eyes. "That is the cutest fear I have ever heard."

John tapped the board. "Okay. We respect all fears."

Then John leaned forward.

"Now… let's talk about Shayb Khalaf."

Half the class gasped.

Some students looked at the door — as if Khalaf might appear just because his name was said.

John lowered his voice. Like a storyteller. "Is Shayb Khalaf afraid of anything?"

Silence.

Then Ali whispered, "He is not afraid."

Khalil added, softly, "He is… the fear."

Thuraya watched John carefully. "Why you ask?"

John tried to look casual and failed. "Just… teacher curiosity."

Safiya narrowed her eyes. She knew that face. That face meant: John is about to do something stupid but educational.


## Scene Four — Dukkan Al-Hallah and the Old Man's Secret


After school, John walked to Dukkan Al-Hallah — determined to collect information like a detective.

Safiya and Thuraya followed. Suspicious.

Inside, the shop smelled like dried fish, spices, and childhood. The specific full Omani-village-shop smell of a small specific commercial space whose stock had never been catalogued, had never been organised by category, had been arranged instead by the specific small full personal logic of a shopkeeper who knew, exactly, where everything was — sacks of rice next to crates of dates, soap stacked next to packets of cardamom, the small specific brass coffee-pots in the corner where his cousin had left them six months ago and which he had not yet decided how to display. The shop was, in 1973, the village's social centre. The news exchange. The credit office. The small specific full informal court where village disputes were sometimes adjudicated by collective laughter.

Sadu stood behind the counter. Half-selling and half-arguing with a boy who wanted something for free.

Sadu spotted John and grinned. "Jooohen! You come! You want what?"

John leaned on the counter. "Sadu. I need village information."

Sadu's eyes lit up. He loved information. Especially dramatic information.

Before Sadu could speak, an old man entered slowly. Carrying a small bag. His beard was white and his eyes were bright like they still had mischief in them.

Sadu whispered, "That is Shayb Zahran."

John turned politely. "Hello, sir."

Zahran looked at him and smiled slowly. "Hello, teacher."

John blinked. "You speak English."

Zahran shrugged. "Little. Life teach."

John leaned closer. "Sir… can I ask something?"

Zahran's smile widened. "You want ask about Khalaf."

Safiya's head snapped toward John. "John!"

Thuraya's eyebrows lifted. "Mr. John…"

John pretended this was totally normal. "Yes. About Khalaf."

Zahran lowered his voice. Enjoying himself. "People think he fear nothing."

John nodded eagerly. "Yes!"

Zahran pointed one finger up. Like a lecturer. "Wrong."

John's eyes widened. "He is afraid?"

Zahran's smile turned wicked. "He was afraid of one thing."

Safiya leaned in. Even Thuraya stopped breathing for a moment.

Zahran whispered. Like it was a secret that could burn the air:

"His wife."

John blinked. "His wife?"

Zahran nodded. Seriously. "Name…"


> «منوّه»


The moment Zahran said the name, the shop felt colder for half a second — as if the air itself remembered it.

John swallowed. "And… what happens if you say her name?"

Zahran chuckled. "He change. He shake. He… fly."

Safiya whispered, "This is too good."

Thuraya looked at John — already knowing the disaster coming.

John straightened up. Eyes shining. "Thank you, sir. Very helpful."

Thuraya grabbed his sleeve. "No. Absolutely no."

John smiled innocently. "I'm just… practicing English."

Safiya muttered, "Liar."


## Scene Five — The Clay House


The sun was dropping when John reached the path to Khalaf's clay house.

Safiya and Thuraya followed at a distance. Whispering:

"He will do it."

"He will definitely do it."

"He will die."

Malood followed too — because Malood followed everything interesting.

John stood at the door. His heart beat fast.

He raised his hand. Ready to knock.

Then he remembered… fear lesson.

He whispered to himself in English, "He is not afraid of… anything."

He pushed the door open dramatically.

The wooden door swung wide.

John stepped in and shouted —


> «منوّ—!»


He stopped.

His voice died in his throat.

The house was silent. Too silent. The specific small full wrong-silence of a place that had been, until very recently, occupied — and which had, in the small specific window of the last hour, been suddenly emptied. The walking stick was on the ground. Not propped against the wall. Not placed on the small specific shelf where Khalaf always placed it. On the ground. The dallah — the small specific brass coffee pot — was tipped over. The dark coffee had spread. Like a stain. Across the earth floor. The specific small full village-tragedy of an Omani household whose dallah had been knocked over — because no Omani household, by the small specific careful daily protocols of these specific small lives, ever knocked over its dallah. A dallah was tipped over only when something had gone very wrong.

John's mouth went dry.

Safiya appeared behind him. Her face suddenly serious. "John…"

Thuraya stepped in slowly. Her laughter completely gone. She looked around once. Then whispered, "No…"

Malood made a small nervous sound.

From outside, the wind moved through the palm fronds — like a quiet warning.

John swallowed and spoke softly. No jokes now.

"He was here…"

Thuraya's eyes fixed on the spilled coffee. And for the first time, John saw real worry in her face.


## Scene Six — A Village Suddenly Different


Then, far away, from the village road, a child's voice called:

"Teacher John!"

The sound snapped the moment like a thread.

But the house stayed quiet.

And the coffee stayed spilled.

And the stick stayed on the floor.

John backed out slowly. His mind racing.

Safiya whispered, "Where is he?"

Thuraya didn't answer. She stared at the doorway like the village itself had just changed shape.

The specific small full Thuraya-stare of a woman who had, until that moment, considered herself — by long village familiarity — to be relatively unmoved by Khalaf's small specific folkloric reputation. Thuraya had grown up in this village. Thuraya had known Khalaf all her life. Thuraya had, until this small specific evening, believed that the small specific village-mythology around him was almost entirely nonsense — small village-stories accumulated over years, exaggerated by repetition, harmless. But the specific small empty house with the spilled dallah and the abandoned stick had introduced, for the first time in her adult life, a small specific possibility: that some part of the mythology might, in some small specific way, be true. The stare was not about Khalaf. The stare was about her own small specific certainty, which had — in one small evening — quietly cracked.

John looked at the dark stain again, then at Thuraya.

In a low voice, he said:

"Tomorrow… we must find out."

Thuraya finally spoke. Very quietly:

"Yes."

And somewhere behind them, Malood chewed a leaf — like nothing in the world was wrong.

But for once… Malood looked uncertain.

The three of them walked back to the small village in silence. The lamps had begun to come on in the houses. Smoke rose from the small specific kitchens of small specific evening meals. Children's laughter sounded from one small lane. A donkey, somewhere, brayed once. The village continued its specific full unbroken evening pattern — and yet, for the three of them, the village had — by the small specific spilled dallah of a single old man's empty house — suddenly become a place that contained a small specific question they did not yet know how to answer.


# Palm Thirty-Three — A Son from Bahrain — The Truth Behind the Story



## Scene One — A Morning That Felt Different


That morning, Samail felt different.

The air was softer. The sun was kinder. Even the palm trees looked like they were waiting for something. The specific small full village-anticipation of a place that had — over the previous evening — been collectively informed of an event that was about to occur, and that had decided, by collective unspoken agreement, to be ready for it. The houses had been swept. The yards had been cleaned. Smoke from the morning fires rose more carefully than usual. Even the goats — even Malood — moved more slowly than they usually did, as if the animals themselves had received the village's message and were honouring it.

John noticed it before anyone else.

He stood at the door of the small house. Holding his notebook. He said quietly, "Today feels like… something is coming."

Safiya, tying her scarf, smiled. "In Oman, something is always coming. Usually heat."

Thuraya walked in from the path. Holding her bag. Her face thoughtful.

"Not heat today," she said. "News."

John looked at her. "Good news or village-news?"

Thuraya smiled. "Both."

They walked together toward the school. Malood followed, of course — like a loyal cloud with legs.

The students were already there. Sitting under the palm tree. Not running. Not shouting.

Ali was quiet. Ahmed was quiet. Even Mansoor was quiet.

Something was definitely wrong.

Thuraya clapped her hands. "Why are you all sitting like this?"

Khalil answered softly, "We are waiting."

"Waiting for what?" Safiya asked.

Saeed, who usually stayed at the back, stepped forward.

"For Obaid," he said.

John frowned. "Who is Obaid?"

Thuraya's face changed. Softer. Warmer.

"Obaid is Shayb Khalaf's son."

Safiya gasped. "He has a son?"

Thuraya nodded. "He left years ago. To Bahrain. To work. No one thought he would come back."

John felt something move in his chest. "And now?"

Thuraya smiled. "Now he is coming."


## Scene Two — The Return


The sound came first.

A car.

Not the pickup of Uncle Nasser. Not a donkey. A real car.

Dust rose at the end of the road.

The students stood up. Like one body.

Then they saw him.

A man in clean clothes. Carrying two bags. Walking fast. His eyes searching. The specific full small returned-son posture of a man who had, twenty years earlier, walked away from this same village as a boy — and who was now walking back into it as a person his old neighbours did not, at the first glance, fully recognise. The years in Bahrain had given him a different small specific carriage. Cleaner clothes. A small specific city-trimmed beard. A walk that knew, by long muscle-memory, the wider streets of a Gulf capital. But the small specific way he searched the road for the small specific old door of a small specific clay house was the same searching that he had done as a child — when he had come home from the falaj at sunset, and had looked up the same road, and had seen the same door, and had run toward it.

"Baba!" someone shouted.

Shayb Khalaf came out of his clay house. Slowly. His stick in his hand.

He stopped.

He looked.

The stick fell.

For a second, the village stopped breathing.

Then Obaid ran.

He didn't walk. He didn't think.

He ran. And hugged the old man so hard that Khalaf almost fell.

"Baba," Obaid said, his voice breaking. "I am back."

Shayb Khalaf did not speak.

He just held him.

And held him.

And held him.

The specific full small twenty-year embrace of a father who had — every morning for twenty years, with the small specific quiet stubbornness that the village had mistaken for sorcery — refused to give up the small private hope that this exact moment would, one day, occur. The whole specific small village-folklore of the maghsoob, the magbar smoke, the small specific reputation of Khalaf as a frightening figure — had been, the whole time, the small accidental cover that a grieving father had grown around himself, because grief in a village was not a thing a man could simply have. Grief had to be disguised. The small specific village-reputation had been the disguise. And now — at this moment, in front of his son — the disguise had become unnecessary. Khalaf, who had not wept in twenty years, did not weep now either. But the small specific holding-of-his-son was, in its complete physical commitment, more eloquent than any weeping would have been.

Some of the women wiped their eyes.

Safiya's eyes were full.

John swallowed.

Thuraya whispered, "He waited many years."

Even Malood was quiet.


## Scene Three — Gifts and Stories


Later, Obaid came to the school with his father.

He brought small gifts for the children.

Simple things. But precious.

For each child, a smile.

For each smile, a memory.

John shook Obaid's hand. "Welcome back."

Obaid smiled. "Thank you, teacher."

Safiya asked, "What do you do in Bahrain?"

Obaid's eyes shone. "I work with herbs. Medicine. Old knowledge."

He showed them small bags.

"This is for stomach."

"This for headache."

"This for sleep."

Thuraya nodded. "Your grandfather used to do this."

Obaid smiled sadly. "Yes. And my mother…"

He stopped.

No one spoke.

Shayb Khalaf looked away.

The specific small full village-silence of a moment in which the unsaid name of a long-lost mother — Khalaf's wife, Obaid's mother, the woman the village had lost so many years ago that some of the children had no idea she had ever existed — became audible without being spoken. The specific small understanding of every adult present that the small specific hard quietness of Khalaf's last twenty years had been, at least in part, a quietness around her absence. The folkloric reputation of sahir had been the village's way of explaining a man whose grief had been too large to look at directly. The folkloric reputation of maghsoob for his missing son had been a similar small accommodation — a folktale to fill the small specific gap of an actual son who had simply gone to Bahrain and, until today, not come back.


## Scene Four — The Truth at Sadu's Shop


That afternoon, John went to Dukkan Al-Hallah.

He found Sadu laughing.

Too much laughing.

"What?" John asked.

Sadu almost fell from the bench.

"You! You and 'Manooh'!"

John froze. "What?"

Sadu wiped his eyes.

"Zahran. He is crazy. Manooh is HIS wife. Not Khalaf's!"

John's mouth opened.

"Zahran mixes names. He forgets. He tells stories. Everyone knows."

John whispered, "So… Shayb Khalaf was never afraid of Manooh?"

Sadu laughed harder. "No. He is afraid of nothing."

John closed his eyes.

Slowly.

Very slowly.

The specific full small slow closing-of-the-eyes of a man who had, in the previous twenty-four hours, organised an entire elaborate detective-investigation, broken into an empty house, encountered a spilled dallah and an abandoned walking stick, and constructed a small private hypothesis about the small specific psychological vulnerabilities of the village's most folkloric figure — only to discover that the entire chain of reasoning had been founded on the small specific confusion of an old man who could no longer reliably remember whose wife was whose. The whole detective story had been a small specific village-fiction. The whole small specific terror that John had constructed in his Northern foreign-teacher's imagination had been, at heart, the small specific gossip of one specific old man whose memory had begun, over the years, to assign other people's wives to other people's husbands.


## Scene Five — The Big Embarrassment


In the evening, John walked to Shayb Khalaf's house.

He stood straight.

He cleared his throat.

Khalaf looked at him. "You were coming yesterday?"

John nodded. "Yes. I wanted to say something."

Khalaf leaned on his stick. "Say."

John took a deep breath.

"I wanted to say… Man—"

He stopped.

He waited.

Nothing happened.

No shaking.

No flying.

No fear.

Khalaf raised an eyebrow. "Say what?"

John tried again. "I wanted to say… M—"

Suddenly:

WHACK.

The stick hit John's back.


> «عجب! أنا أخاف منوّوه نوبه؟»


('Ajab! Ana akhaaf min Manooh nawbah?! — Amazing! Me, afraid of Manooh, of all people?!)

John jumped. "OW!"

Khalaf growled. "You think I am stupid?"

John's face turned red.

Then Khalaf added, calmly, "Zahran is crazy."

John looked at the ground.

Very interested in the ground.


## Scene Six — The Legendary Chase — and the Light in the Window


From behind the house, Sadu appeared. Laughing. Like a drum.

"I told you! I told you!"

John saw the stick in Khalaf's hand.

Bad idea.

Very bad idea.

He grabbed Sadu and ran.

"HEY!" Khalaf shouted and ran after them.

Malood saw running and decided this was a game.

He ran too.

The children saw running and joined.

Soon the whole village saw:

Sadu running.

Behind him John.

Behind him Khalaf.

Behind him Malood.

Behind him the children.

Someone shouted, "It's a festival!"

That night, John sat at home. Rubbing his back.

Safiya looked at him. "So… what did we learn today?"

John sighed. "Never trust village gossip."

Thuraya smiled. "And?"

John thought.

Then said, softly:

"Some people wait years for love."

The specific full small late-evening sentence that, of everything John had said that day, was the only one that had any actual weight. The whole comic-village-chase of the afternoon — Sadu running, the stick swinging, the children turning the chase into a small specific village-festival — had been the small surface theatre. The actual content of the day had been the small specific twenty-year embrace at the door of Khalaf's clay house. The folktale had been about a sahir and a maghsoob. The truth, which had been waiting under the folktale all along, was about a father waiting for his son. The whole village had needed the folktale because the village had not had the small specific emotional vocabulary, in those years, to name the actual thing. The actual thing was love. The folktale had been love's small specific Omani disguise.

Outside, Shayb Khalaf's house had light again.

And for the first time…

The village slept peacefully.


# Palm Thirty-Four — Then and Now — A Trip to Jibreen Castle



## Scene One — A Long Dusty Road and a New Guide


The road to Jibreen Castle was long, dusty, and full of quiet excitement.

There are roads in Oman that announce their destinations long before you arrive — and there are roads that, like this one, hold their secrets close. The interior road from Samail toward Bahla in 1973 was not paved end-to-end; it was a small specific surface of compacted earth and gravel that had been graded only the previous year, that ran between specific small palm farms and through specific small wadis, and that delivered, eventually, to anyone willing to follow it for two hours, the small specific full revelation of what an Omani castle actually looked like in person.

The pickup of Uncle Nasser moved slowly between palm trees and open land. The children sat in the back, holding their bags, their notebooks, and their small dreams.

John sat in front, looking at the horizon — like a man about to meet history.

Safiya sat beside him. Her camera resting on her lap.

Thuraya sat behind them, explaining the plan again and again — because teachers never trust that students really listen.

And with them this time was Obaid.

Not as a visitor.

Not as a guest.

But as a guide.

The specific small full quiet new role of Obaid — who had been, only a few days earlier, the long-lost son returned from Bahrain, and who had now, by the small specific fast small village-economy of belonging, become the person whose knowledge the school day required. He had spent his Bahraini years working with herbs and old knowledge. But before Bahrain, he had been the village boy who had grown up in the small specific shadow of the interior castles, who had visited Jibreen a dozen times as a child with his uncles, who carried — as Thuraya herself did not carry — the small specific full inherited memory of these specific small interior places. The school had needed him for this exact day. The school had not, until Thuraya had specifically asked, known it needed him.

He stood in the back of the pickup. Holding the side with one hand. Pointing at the land with the other.

"There," he said. "That road did not exist before."

John turned. "So… how did people travel?"

Obaid smiled. "On animals. On feet. Slowly. Very slowly."

Mansoor opened his eyes wide. "Even to Nizwa?"

Obaid nodded. "Especially to Nizwa."

The children looked at each other — like they were trying to imagine a world without roads.

Safiya whispered to Thuraya, "It's strange. We are living inside a story."

Thuraya smiled. "Yes. And today, we will teach them how to tell it."


## Scene Two — The Castle Appears


Jibreen Castle appeared like something from a dream.

Tall.

Strong.

Quiet.

Its walls looked like they were holding secrets.

The specific full small first-encounter that any Omani-interior visitor had with Jibreen — the small specific moment when the road curved, and the palms thinned, and the castle simply was there, unannounced, vast, the colour of the desert it had been made from. Three storeys of carefully balanced stonework, four corner-towers, the small specific ornamental wooden window-frames that even three centuries of sun had not entirely faded. The castle did not advertise itself. The castle did not need to. The castle was, simply, the small specific architectural expression of a moment in Omani history when the Yarubi imams had built — across the entire interior — a network of small specific full administrative-fortress-palaces from which the country had been governed.

Ali stood very still. "It looks like a king's house."

Obaid nodded. "It was."

They entered slowly.

Their footsteps echoed.

Obaid spoke in a calm, respectful voice.

"Long time ago, people lived here. They studied here. They fought here. They planned here."

John wrote some words in his notebook:

lived  —  studied  —  fought  —  planned

Safiya whispered to the students, "Repeat after me: They lived here."

The class said together. "They lived here."

Obaid continued. "In the past, Oman did not have schools like today. People learned in mosques and houses. They used oil lamps, not electricity."

Thuraya added, gently, "And water… they used falaj. No pipes."

John turned to the students. He wrote on a small board he carried:

In the past, they used to…Now, we…

He pointed at Ali. "Ali. In the past, they used to…?"

Ali thought. "They used to… walk… long."

John smiled. "Good! They used to walk long distances."

Ahmed added, "Now, we… use car."

Safiya clapped. "Excellent!"

Mansoor raised his hand. "In the past, they used to… no fan."

The class laughed.

Thuraya nodded. "True. No fan. Only patience."


## Scene Three — From Room to Room


They walked from room to room.

The specific full slow-pacing of a small school-group through a 17th-century Omani castle — the specific small careful step of children whose feet, instinctively, knew that the floors they were walking on were old, that the small specific painted wooden ceilings above their heads were not to be touched, that the small specific carved wooden doors had been, three centuries earlier, the doors of imams. Obaid moved between the rooms with the small specific full quiet authority of a man whose own grandfather had told him, long ago, what each specific small space in this castle had been for. The children followed in a small specific full informal cluster, asking questions in Arabic, receiving answers in Obaid's slow careful village-Arabic, and absorbing — without quite noticing — the small specific full lesson that their own country was old.

Obaid showed them the ceilings. The small windows. The secret passages.

"In the past," he said, "people protected their families here. Now, we protect history."

John felt something heavy and beautiful in his chest.

He looked at Thuraya. "Your country has strong roots."

She answered quietly, "And now… it is growing."


## Scene Four — Ali's Drawing


They sat in the shade near the castle wall.

Safiya took out colors and papers.

"Now," she said, "you draw."

The students looked at her. "Draw?"

"Yes," she smiled. "Draw the castle. Or what you feel."

Thuraya added, "And write one sentence."

John wrote on a big paper:

In the past…Now…In the future, I will…

Ali sat very quietly.

Very focused.

His hand moved slowly. Carefully.

John passed by and stopped.

He leaned down.

Did not speak.

Just watched.

Ali was not drawing only the castle. He was drawing the castle, the land, and a big flag above it. The lines were confident. The colors were strong. The specific full small unmistakable confidence of a child whose hand had — without anyone in the village having previously noticed it — been quietly developing the small specific competence of a real artist. The flag was the new flag of Oman, the flag that had been adopted only three years earlier, with its red field and its white and green panels and its small specific khanjar at the top. Ali had, with the small specific patience of a boy who had been watching the world more carefully than the world had been watching him, memorised every detail of that flag. He was now putting it, very carefully, above the castle.

Safiya came and froze.

She whispered, "Thuraya…"

Thuraya looked.

And smiled in surprise.

"He has talent," she said softly.

Ali wrote under his drawing. Slowly:

In the past, Oman was strong.Now, Oman is growing.In the future, I will help my country.

Safiya felt her eyes burn.

She lifted the drawing. She showed it to John.

He looked at it for a long time.

Then he said, quietly, "Ali… you are an artist."

Ali looked down. Shy.

Mansoor's drawing was… a castle with five suns and two goats.

Malood walked over and tried to eat the corner.


## Scene Five — In the Future, I Will…


Before leaving, John stood and said, "Okay. Let's speak."

He pointed to Noura. "One sentence."

Noura said, "In the past, they used to… live in castle."

John nodded. "Good."

Khalil said, "Now, we have… school."

Ahmed said, "In the future, I will… be teacher."

Saeed, from the back, said quietly, "In the future, I will… learn more."

Thuraya looked at him.

And smiled.

The specific small full teacher-smile of a woman who had — over the course of the past several months — watched Saeed's small English-language confidence grow from I was watching in October to I am thinking in November to I am afraid of not seeing what is true last month, and now to In the future, I will learn more at Jibreen. The trajectory of the year was visible in the specific small full grammatical journey of one boy. Thuraya, who had taught for years and who had — in earlier classes — watched many small bright children fade as their grammar got better but their courage got smaller, recognised that the opposite was happening with Saeed. His grammar was getting better. And his courage, in proportion, was getting larger. The small specific outcome that every teacher hopes for, but that no teacher gets to count on.


## Scene Six — The Flag Receding


On the way back, the sun was lower.

The castle stayed behind them like a memory.

The specific full small late-afternoon light on the small specific honey-coloured stone of Jibreen as the pickup pulled away. The castle had been there for three centuries. The castle would be there for many more. The pickup, with its small specific cargo of children and teachers and one small folded paper drawing, would be remembered — by the people inside it — for the rest of their lives. Safiya held Ali's drawing on her lap, the colours still wet at the edges, the small specific village-paper slightly curled. She had decided, without asking, that this drawing was going home with them. She had decided, somewhere between the castle and the truck, that this small specific image — a castle in the past, a country growing in the present, a flag promising the future — was the small specific completion of her year here. She would keep it. She would frame it, eventually. She would, in many years, hang it on a wall in a different country and would, on certain mornings, look at it and feel a specific small full warmth that no other object in her possession would be able to produce.

Safiya held Ali's drawing on her lap.

She said softly, "Sometimes… a lesson is not in the book."

John nodded. "Sometimes, it's in a child."

Thuraya looked at the road ahead.

"And sometimes," she said, "it is in a country."

The pickup moved forward.

And history moved with it.


# Palm Thirty-Five — The Month When Everything Changed — Ramadan in Samail



## Scene One — The First Adhan of the Month


The first sound came before the light.

It was not the sun that woke Samail.

It was the adhan.

The voice floated from far away. Soft and strong at the same time. Moving between the mountains, over the palm trees, into the houses, into the hearts. The specific full small Omani-village adhan of a small specific muezzin who had, for thirty-eight years, climbed the small specific narrow stairway of the small specific village mosque before the first hint of dawn, and had — into the small specific quiet of every Samaili pre-morning — released the same fourteen ancient lines that had, over the centuries, anchored every single Muslim awakening in this part of the world. The adhan was not, on a normal day, news. The adhan was the small specific full background-fact of life. But on this specific morning, in this specific season, the adhan carried — for the first time in the year — the small specific full additional weight of Ramadan. The whole village, hearing it, recognised the difference instantly.

John opened his eyes slowly.

"What… is that?" he whispered.

Safiya was already awake. She was sitting on the edge of the bed. Listening.

"It's beautiful," she said. "It sounds like… the sky is speaking."

Outside, the village was different.

Quieter.

Kinder.

Like it was holding its breath.

Thuraya walked past their house. Her steps light. Her face calm. She carried a small bag of dates and bread. She stopped, looked at the sky, and whispered a short prayer before continuing her way.

That day, no children came to school.

No noise.

No running.

No shouting.

Even Sadoo's shop was closed. A wooden sign hung on the door:

Closed for Ramadan.

Mansoor passed by slowly. His face tired but proud.

"I am fasting," he said to Malood, who was trying to eat a leaf. "You… you don't understand religion."

Malood ate the leaf.


## Scene Two — The Travelling Food


By afternoon, something strange happened.

The village started to smell different.

From one house: fried onions. From another: bread. From another: spices, soup, meat. The specific small full layered scent-architecture of an Omani village preparing iftar — the small specific late-afternoon collective accumulation of every household's small specific evening-meal smoke rising at the same time, blending, drifting through the lanes, settling into the small specific full village-air with the small specific layered density that Safiya, standing at her window, had not — until this exact afternoon — ever encountered. The village did not smell like itself. The village smelled like thirty households cooking simultaneously, all of them with the same small specific full cardamom-saffron-onion logic, all of them aimed at the same small specific full magrib moment that was still — by John's wristwatch — three hours away.

Plates moved from door to door.

Women walked in groups, carrying food, laughing quietly.

Safiya stood at the window. "The food… it's travelling," she said.

John laughed. "Like a food parade."

But the sun was still high.

And nobody was eating.

John looked at his watch. "So… they don't eat. They don't drink. All day?"

Thuraya, who had just come to check on them, nodded. "From dawn to sunset."

Safiya blinked. "Even water?"

"Yes."

John sat slowly. "That is… serious."

Safiya smiled. "We read about this. In books. Remember?"

John nodded. "That was… one of the reasons we wanted to come here."

They stayed quiet for a moment. The specific small full silence of a married couple who had — three years earlier, in their small specific Northern flat, before the contract, before the journey — discussed Islam many times. Had read books about it. Had wondered, both of them privately, about what it would feel like to live somewhere it was the air. They had not, in those early conversations, used the word convert. They had used the word understand. But the small specific layered quietness of this exact afternoon was beginning to suggest, to both of them, that understanding had — somewhere along the months — been replaced by something else.

Then Safiya said, suddenly, "Let's try."

John turned slowly. "Try what?"

"Fasting."

He stared at her. "Are you… serious?"

She smiled. "Come on. One day."


## Scene Three — The Longest Day


By ten in the morning, John was already dramatic.

"I think… my mouth is becoming a desert."

Safiya looked at him. "Stop it."

By twelve, he was lying on the floor.

"I can hear… water calling me."

Safiya sat beside him. "Drama queen."

By two, he tried to stand up and failed. "I see… light."

Safiya laughed. "That's the fan."

Malood came, looked at him, and stepped over his leg.

By four, John was on the floor again. "I am not built for this."

Safiya sat cross-legged. "You wanted to understand the culture. Congratulations."

Then… knock knock.

The door opened.

Thuraya stood there. With a big tray.

"I brought you iftar," she said.

John tried to sit. "But… we are not fasting."

Thuraya smiled. "I know. But you should taste Ramadan."

She put the tray down.

Dates. Soup. Bread. Samosas. Rice.

Safiya looked at the food. Then at Thuraya. Then at John.

Then she laughed.

"Look at us. Not fasting. And dying."

Thuraya looked at them both and laughed.

"Poor you. One day only, and you almost die."

She looked at John seriously and said in Arabic. Smiling:


> «صيام بدون صلاة؟»


(Siyam bidoon salat? — Fasting without prayer?)

Then she laughed again.


## Scene Four — The Disappearing Afternoons


Days passed.

Ramadan continued.

John and Safiya started… disappearing in the afternoons.

The specific small full pattern that the village had begun to notice — and not yet quite explain. Around three in the afternoon, every day, the foreign teachers would leave their small house. They would walk down the lane. They would not go to the school. They would not go to the shop. They would not go to the falaj. They would simply, in a specific small full direction the children could not quite track, disappear. They would, an hour or two later, return — quietly, with the small specific full quietness of two people who had done something that had required quietness — and would, for the rest of the day, be the same John and Safiya the village had grown accustomed to. But the afternoon disappearance had become daily. And the village, by long village-instinct, was paying attention.

They left quietly.

They came back quietly.

The children noticed.

Mansoor whispered, "They go to the teacher's house."

Khalil said, "Every day."

Noura said, "Why?"

They tried to follow them.

But Shayb Khalaf appeared. Like a wall.

He stood in the road. Holding his stick.

"Any one follow them," he said, "this bamboo talks."

He looked at Thuraya. "First you, Thraywa."

Thuraya crossed her arms. "You like drama."

He did not move.

They turned back.


## Scene Five — A Different Adhan


Half of Ramadan passed.

One evening, the adhan was different.

Stronger.

Closer.

John and Safiya walked with Thuraya.

Not to their house.

Not to the teacher's house.

To the mosque.

The village noticed.

People stopped.

Looked.

Whispered.

The specific small full village-pause of an Omani community whose entire small specific reading of the foreign teachers had — over a year — gradually shifted, but had not, until that exact small evening-walk, expressed itself in a single visible small specific public action. The walk to the mosque was not, by itself, an announcement. It was a small specific full direction-of-travel. John walked at a normal pace. Safiya walked beside him. Thuraya walked slightly ahead, the way a guide walks. None of them looked left or right. None of them needed to. The village's collective small specific full attention was on them — and their feet, the three pairs of feet, were quietly carrying the small specific full visible answer to a question that the village had been asking, for many months, in silence.

That night, after iftar, Shayb Khalaf opened his house.

He did something nobody expected.

He made a big table. For everyone.

Rice. Meat. Bread. Dates. Tea.

He stood and said, simply:

"Today… we celebrate."

John stood. His voice was not strong. But it was clear.

"I came to Oman to teach English," he said. "But Oman taught me… something bigger."

Safiya held his hand.

"We did not come only for work," she said. "We came because we were… searching."

John looked at Thuraya. "At your people. Your life. Your faith."

He took a breath.

"Today… we say it clearly."

They looked at each other.

And said:

"We are Muslims."

For a second, nobody spoke.

Then… the village exploded.

Smiles. Tears. Voices.

Mansoor shouted, "Teacher John is one of us!"

Malood jumped on the table.

Shayb Khalaf wiped his eyes with his sleeve and said, "Late… but good."


## Scene Six — The Night of Light


They ate together.

They laughed.

They talked.

Safiya tasted everything and said, "This is dangerous. I will never be thin again."

John looked at the people praying.

Quiet.

Peaceful.

And said softly, "I think… this is home."

Thuraya looked at them.

And said nothing.

Because some moments… do not need words.

The specific full small small village-night that Thuraya would, in later years, remember with the specific small clear photographic precision of the most important nights of a person's life. Khalaf's open house. The small full specific lamp on the wooden shelf casting yellow light on every specific face.
