The specific small smile of Saeed, who had shown up — uninvited, unannounced, in the small specific quiet way only Saeed showed up — and was sitting in his usual corner, watching, but for the first time without watching from outside the moment. He was inside it. The whole village was inside it. The small specific full evening had been the small specific completion of a small specific full year — the year a man and his wife had come from Britain to teach English, and had ended, instead, by becoming the village's small specific full additional Muslims. The small specific full grammar of I am, which Safiya had taught the children all year, had — on this small specific night — been spoken back to her, by herself and her husband, in its most consequential conjugation: We are Muslims.

Later, at home, John lay on the floor.

Full.

Happy.

Safiya looked at him.

"You survived fasting. You survived food. Congratulations."

He smiled.

"This… was the month that changed everything."


# Palm Thirty-Six — The Day the Palm Tree Witnessed Everything — Samail, 1976 → 2026



## Scene One — The Quiet Morning of Exams


The classroom was not noisy that morning.

It was… nervous.

Even Mansoor was quiet.

The specific full small village-classroom-quietness of an exam morning — different in texture from any other school-quietness, because it was a quietness made of accumulated small individual nerves rather than absence of activity. Each child carried, that morning, the small specific full private weight of their own private examination-anxiety. Ahmed had been awake since five. Ali had eaten only half his breakfast. Noura had reorganised her pencil case three times. The specific small full preparation-rituals of small village-children who had decided, individually, that today was the small specific day they had been being prepared for, all year.

The old fan turned slowly. The chalkboard was clean. The desks were too straight — like they were also waiting.

"Today… exams," John said, trying to sound calm.

Safiya smiled. "Don't look at me like I am the judge."

Thuraya walked between the desks. "Just do your best. That's all."

The papers were given.

Silence fell like a blanket.

The specific full small examination-silence that, in any classroom anywhere, has the same small specific layered quality — pencils on paper, the small specific scratching of focused thought, an occasional small specific full sigh from a corner where a particular student had encountered a particular question and was, for a small specific moment, reorganising their understanding of what they had been taught. Thuraya, walking slowly between the desks, had — over many years of teaching — learned to read these small examination-silences with the small specific precision of a doctor reading a heartbeat. The silence was good. The silence was the silence of children doing their work.

Ahmed wrote like a machine.

Ali stopped sometimes, looked up, then continued.

Noura whispered the question to herself.

Khalil bit his pencil.

Mansoor stared at the paper like it was an enemy.

Malood slept near the door.


## Scene Two — The Longest Week


The week of correction was… dangerous.

The specific small full village-teacher's correction-week of three teachers who had committed themselves, over many months, to a small specific full project — and who had now, with the exam papers in front of them, the small specific full task of measuring whether the project had worked. The correction was not, by itself, a difficult task. It was emotionally difficult. Every paper was the small specific full record of a particular small child's accumulated small year. Thuraya, who had taught these specific children since they were five, knew their handwriting better than her own. She could, by the third sentence of any paper, tell which student had written it without reading the name. The papers were not anonymous to her. The papers were small specific personal things.

John was tired.

Safiya was tired.

Thuraya was pretending she was not nervous.

At night, they corrected papers in the small house. The single oil lamp on the small wooden table. Three teachers leaning over papers. A pot of tea between them, occasionally being refilled. The small specific full late-evening sound of pencil-marks and small specific full teacher-comments and the occasional quiet sigh.

"Look at Ali," Safiya said. "His writing improved so much."

"And Ahmed," John said. "Perfect grammar."

"And Mansoor…" Thuraya laughed. "At least he wrote something."


## Scene Three — Results Day


The children stood in lines under the palm tree.

The same palm tree.

The one that saw everything.

The specific full small symbolic palm tree under which — during the past school year — every important small specific village-school moment had occurred. The lessons under it. The confessions under it. The grasshopper under it. The pride-list under it. The small specific full village-tree that had stood there, patient, since long before any of these specific small children had been born, and that would stand there, patient, long after they were gone. The tree had not changed. The children had. The whole specific full pedagogical contract of the school year had been, in some small specific full unspoken sense, witnessed by this exact tree.

John held the paper.

Safiya held her breath.

Thuraya smiled like a mother.

"Ahmed," John said. "Excellent."

Ahmed stood straight. Like a soldier.

"Ali," Safiya said. "Excellent."

Ali's eyes shined.

"Noura… Very good."

"Mithla… Very good."

"Salima… Good."

"Marwa… Good."

"Rahma… Good."

Then…

"Mansoor…"

He closed his eyes.

"…Passed."

The village heard his scream before the mountains did.

The specific full small full Mansoor-scream of pure joy that was, in its own specific small way, the small specific village-anthem of the entire school year. Mansoor, who had run more than any other child, who had stolen one Bob Jamid from Sadoo, who had been afraid of butterflies, who had jumped in puddles, who had attempted to bribe Sadou with a goat — had, against his own private long expectations and against several teachers' private quiet doubts, passed. The pass had not been spectacular. The pass had been narrow. But the pass had been, by every relevant village-metric, a victory. And Mansoor, who had decided long ago that he was going to register every small specific full village-emotion at maximum volume, screamed accordingly.


## Scene Four — Graduation Day


The school was decorated with simple cloth and palm leaves.

The specific full small village-decoration of an Omani school's graduation day in 1976 — small specific lengths of bright cloth tied between palm fronds, small specific paper flowers made by the girls of the older class, a single small specific cardboard banner with hand-painted Arabic and English. The decoration was modest. The decoration was, by the small specific village-economy of those years, the most that could be done. And the decoration was, in its small specific full modesty, exactly right — because the day was not, in any sense, about decoration. The day was about the children.

Mothers brought food.

Fathers came with proud faces.

Even Sadoo closed his shop early.

Shayb Khalaf sat in the front, holding his stick like a medal.

The children stood in a line.

They sang.

They laughed.

They received small certificates.

Safiya cried.

John pretended not to cry.

Thuraya cried openly.

When it ended, John looked at the children and said:

"You came here not knowing even one word. Now… you can speak. You can read. You can dream."


## Scene Five — The Goodbye That Was Not a Goodbye


That evening, they sat under the palm tree.

The same one.

Malood jumped between them.

The sun went down slowly.

The specific full small Omani-evening of a school year that had ended. The lanes had emptied. The mothers had taken the food back to their kitchens. The fathers had returned to their farms. The children had gone home — most of them, by now, with their small certificates rolled and tied with palm-leaf string, the small specific full first formal recognition any of them had ever received from any institution. The teachers, the three of them, had stayed. They were not in a hurry. The day had not yet completed itself. It had — by the small specific full quiet pull of a year that did not want to be put away too quickly — required that they sit, the three of them, under this exact palm tree, and look at the small specific village they had, together, taught.

Safiya said, quietly, "Do you remember our first day here?"

John smiled. "I fell in the smoke."

They laughed.

Thuraya looked at the tree.

"This place… changed us all."

The three of them sat in silence then. The small specific full late-Omani-evening with the colour of the sky moving slowly through pink and orange and finally into the small specific full purple that meant night. Somewhere down the lane, a child laughed. Somewhere a mother called another child home. Somewhere a goat — almost certainly Malood — bleated once. The village continued. The village had always continued. The village would continue.

And in the small specific full quiet of that exact evening, the school year ended.


## Scene Six — Suddenly… 2026


Half a century passes. The reader of this book — like the writer of it — knows that fifty years cannot be folded into a single page. And yet the small specific full ending of a story sometimes requires exactly that fold. The pickup of Uncle Nasser rusts and is replaced by another, and another. Sadoo's shop closes and reopens under his nephew. The school, which was simple in 1973, is rebuilt — twice. Shayb Khalaf dies in 1981, an old man, peaceful. His son Obaid stays in the village. Al-Mu'allim Hamad dies in 1984, in the same calm small specific way he had lived. Mansoor grows up, becomes a teacher himself, and tells the story of Bob Jamid at every staffroom in the country. Ali becomes an architect, and his small drawings of castles become real buildings. Saeed becomes a doctor — quiet, observant, the small specific full physician whose patients always say he sees what is true. Noura talks her way into a radio station. Ahmed does, in fact, become a teacher. Khalil becomes — to no one's surprise — a small specific full successful village-businessman who, on certain mornings, sets up a bucket above his own shop door for fun.

And Thuraya?

Thuraya teaches for forty more years.

She marries. She raises children. Her children grow up. Her children have children. The small specific full long quiet life of an Omani teacher whose small specific full career was — by every small specific village-measurement that mattered — exactly the career she had wanted.

And John and Safiya?

They stay in Samail for many years. Then they leave. Then they come back. Then they leave again. Then — eventually — the small specific full slow forgetting that takes them away from this story, but not from this village's memory.

The notebooks of that year stay with Thuraya. In a small specific full wooden chest. In a small specific full corner of her house. For fifty years.

And then, on a specific small full morning in the year 2026 —

The sound of a pen.

A notebook.

A woman sitting in her room.

Writing.

She closes the notebook slowly.

A voice from outside:


> «ثرياااا… تعالي تغدّي! الساعة صارت 2!»


(Thuraaaaa… come for lunch! It's already 2 o'clock!)

Thuraya answers:


> «إنزين ماه… جايّه.»


(Okay Mom… I'm coming.)

She looks at the notebook.

On the last page:

"And this was how it all started… under the palm tree."

She closes it.

On the cover:

123 Let's Learn English Under the Palm Tree

She holds the notebook for a small specific full moment. It is heavy. It is full of fifty years. It is — by the small specific full impossible accumulating density that any well-kept notebook acquires — both a small physical object and a small specific full life. The fifty-year-old woman who is now Thuraya looks down at the small specific full hands that wrote it, and recognises that those hands are — though they have changed — the same hands that once held a piece of village chalk, under a palm tree, in 1973, and taught a small group of barefoot children the present continuous tense.

She places the notebook on the shelf.

She walks toward the kitchen — toward her mother's voice, toward the small specific full Omani-lunch that is waiting, toward the small specific full quiet ordinary continuation of every life that has ever, against the odds, been allowed to continue.

And somewhere — in the small specific full long memory of the village of Samail, Al-Qurawashiyah neighbourhood, in the year 1973 — a palm tree, the same palm tree, continues to stand. Patient. Tall. Older now than most people remember. Witness still to the small specific full long conversation between a teacher and her place. The conversation has not ended. The conversation will not end.
